## XV Any Cure? / 十五 可有良方？

### I / 一

===Original===
How shall it all be set right? For it must be, of course. A people that did not wait to be pushed off its seat by the Kaiser is not likely now to turn its face to the wall and die inertly of shortage of faith and general moral debility. Some day soon we shall have to cease squatting among the potsherds and crabbing each other, and give all the strength we have left to the job of regaining the old control of ourselves and our fate which, in the days of our health, could only be kept by putting forth constantly the whole force of the will. “Not to be done,” you may say. And, of course, it will be a miracle. But only the everyday miracle done in somebody’s body, or else in his soul. When the skin shines white and tight over the joints, and the face is only a skull with some varieties of expression, and the very flame flickers and jumps in the lamp, the body will bend itself up to expel a disease that it could not, in all its first splendour of health, keep from the door. In all the breeds of cowardly livers﻿—drunkards, thieves, liars, sorners, drug-takers, all the kinds that have run from the enemy, throwing away as they ran every weapon that better men use to repel him﻿—you will find some that turn in the end and rend with their bare hands the fiend that they could not face with their bow and their spear.

===Chinese===
这一切该如何拨回正轨？因为那当然是必须做到的。一个当初不曾坐等德皇（Kaiser）来把自己推下座位的民族，如今也不至于转身面壁，因信仰匮乏与全面的道德衰弱而惰怠地死去。不久之后的某一天，我们就得停止蹲坐在碎瓦之间互相怨詈，把仅剩的全部气力都投入那件工作：重新赢回我们对自身与命运的旧日掌控——在健康的日子里，这份掌控唯有靠不断使出意志的全部力量方能维持。“办不到，”你也许会说。当然，那会是一个奇迹。但不过是发生在某人身体里、或者他灵魂里的那种日日常见的奇迹。当皮肤在关节处绷得发白发亮，当面孔只剩一具尚有几种表情的骷髅，当连灯里的火苗本身也摇曳跳动起来，身体却会奋然弓起，把一种疾病驱逐出去——想当年它以全盛的健康，尚且未能把这种病挡在门外。在一切懦怯度日的品类——酒徒、窃贼、说谎者、吃白食的、吞麻醉药的，所有那些临敌而逃、一路把较好的人用以御敌的件件武器都抛却的人——之中，你总会发现有一些到了最后掉转头来，赤手空拳，撕碎那个他们当年持弓仗矛也不敢面对的恶魔。

===Original===
But these recoveries only come upon terms: no going back to heaven except through a certain purgatorial passage. There, while it lasts, the invalid must not expect to enjoy either the heady visions of the fever that is now taking its leave or the more temperate beatitude of the health that may presently come. He lies reduced to animal, almost vegetable, matter, quite joyless and unthrilled, and has to abide in numb passivity, like an unborn child’s, whatever may come of the million minute molecular changes going on unseen in the enigmatic darkness of his tissues, where tiny cell is adding itself to tiny cell to build he knows not what. And then some day the real thing, the second birth as wonderful as the first, comes of itself and the stars are singing together all right and the sons of God shouting for joy. The same way with the spirit, except that the body faints, and so is eased, at some point in any rising scale of torment: the spirit has to go on through the mill without such anaesthetics as fainting. So the man who has gone far off the rails in matters of conduct, and tries to get back to them, has such hells of patience to live through, and out of, as no liquid fire known to the war chemists could make for the flesh. To possess your soul in patience, with all the skin and some of the flesh burnt off your face and hands, is a job for a boy compared with the pains of a man who has lived pretty long in the exhilarating world that drugs or strong waters seem to create and is trying to live now in the first bald desolation created by knocking them off, the time in which

> The dulled heart feels
> That somewhere, sealed with hopeless seals,
> The unmeaning heaven about him reels,
> And he lies hurled
> Beyond the roar of all the wheels
> Of all the world.

===Chinese===
但这些康复都是附有条件的：不经一段炼狱般的历程，便无路重回天国。在那段历程持续期间，病人既别指望享受正在告退的高烧所赐的醉人幻象，也别指望领略或许不久将至的健康那种较为平和的至福。他卧着，被削减为动物性、几乎植物性的物质，全无欢愉，全无悸动，只能麻木而被动地承受——像未出世的胎儿那般——任凭千百万次微细的分子变化在他组织幽暗难测的深处悄然进行，小小的细胞正把自己叠加到小小的细胞之上，建造着连他自己也无从知晓的东西。然后某一天，真正的康复——与第一次诞生同样奇妙的第二次诞生——自行降临，晨星确然一同歌唱，神的众子也都欢呼。灵魂的康复也是同一回事，只除了一点：在任何步步升级的酷刑之中，身体到了某个节点便会昏厥，并由此得到宽解；灵魂却必须在没有昏厥这类麻醉的情况下，硬生生闯过磨坊的全程。所以，一个在行为上脱轨太远而试图归轨的人，必得活着熬过并且活着走出一座座忍耐的地狱——战时化学家所知的任何液火（liquid fire），都无法为肉体造出这样的地狱。与那样一种人的疼痛相比，带着烧掉了全部皮肤和几分皮肉的脸与手而“常存忍耐，保全灵魂”，不过是小孩子的活计：那种人在麻醉品或烈酒（strong waters）似乎营造的令人亢奋的世界里活了颇久，如今正努力活在戒掉它们之后最初那片光秃秃的荒凉里，活在那样的一段时日里——

> 迟钝的心只觉得：
> 在某处，盖着无望的封印，
> 无意义的天穹绕着他晕眩，
> 他被远远掷倒，
> 抛在这世界一切车轮的
> 轰鸣之外。

===Original===
And yet no other way out. Disease and imbecility and an early and ignoble death, or else that stoic facing, through interminable days, of an easily escapable dullness that may be anything from an ache up to an agony.

===Chinese===
然而此外别无出路。要么是疾病、痴愚，加一场早至而不体面的死亡；要么便是斯多噶式的直面——在一个个无尽的白昼里，直面一种本可轻易脱身的沉闷，它轻则如隐痛，重则如剧痛。

### II / 二

===Original===
That is about where we stand as a nation. Of course, a few fortunates mailed in a happy, indefeasible genius of wonder and delight at everything round them are all right. And so are a few clods of whole-hog insensibility. Most of us, on the whole, find that effort is less fun than it was, and many things somewhat dull that used to sparkle with interest; the salt has lost, not all, but some of its savour; the grasshopper is a bit of a burden; old hobbies of politics, social causes, liberal comradeships, the loves and wars of letters and art, which used to excite, look at times as if they might only have been, at the best, rather a much ado about nothing; buzzing about our heads there come importunate suspicions that much of what we used to do so keenly was hardly worth doing, and that the dim, far goals we used to struggle towards were only possibly worth trying for and are, anyhow, out of reach now. That is the somewhat sick spirit’s condition. The limp apathy that we see at elections, the curious indifference in presence of public wrongs and horrors, the epidemic of sneaking pilferage, the slackening of sexual self-control﻿—all these are symptomatic like the furred tongue, subnormal heat, and muddy eye.

===Chinese===
作为一个民族，我们的大致处境便是如此。当然，少数幸运儿身披一层快乐而不可剥夺的天才甲胄，对周遭一切都能惊奇与欣喜，他们安然无恙；少数感觉彻底迟钝的木头人，也安然无恙。而我们当中的大多数人，总的来看，发觉出力已不如从前有趣，许多旧日闪着兴味之光的事物多少显得乏味了；盐失了几分味——不是全失；蚱蜢也成了几分重担；政治、社会事业、自由的同道之谊、文字与艺术的爱憎与论战，这些曾令人振奋的旧日爱好，有时看上去充其量不过是一场无事忙；绕着我们头顶嗡嗡盘旋的，是种种纠缠不去的疑心：我们从前做得那么起劲的许多事，恐怕本不值得做；我们曾奋力以求的那些朦胧遥远的目标，至多是或许值得一试，而且无论如何，如今已够不着了。这便是一个抱恙灵魂的境况。选举中我们看到的有气无力的冷漠，面对公共的罪错与惨祸时那种古怪的漠然，偷偷摸摸的小偷小摸蔚成风气，性方面自我约束的松弛——这一切都是症候，好比厚腻的舌苔、偏低的体温、浑浊的眼神。

===Original===
Like the hard drinker next morning, we suffer a touch of Hamlet’s complaint, the malady of the dyspeptic soul, of indolent kings and of pampered youth before it has found any man’s work to try itself on﻿—

> How weary, stale, flat, and unprofitable
> To me are all the uses of the world!

===Chinese===
像豪饮者次日清晨那样，我们也染上了几分哈姆雷特（Hamlet）的宿疾——消化不良的灵魂的痼疾，怠惰君王的痼疾，也是尚未寻得任何男子汉的事业来一试身手的娇惯青春的痼疾——

> 在我看来，人世间的一切行止
> 是何等可厌、陈腐、乏味而无聊！

===Original===
Not the despair of the battered, vanquished, or oppressed, but the moping of the relaxed, the surfeited, or the morbid. Glad as we all were to be done with the war, its ends left even the strongest of us a little let down, as the ends of other long and intense excitements, good or bad, do. As Ibsen’s young woman out in search of thrills would have said, there were harps in the air during the war. Many of them were disagreeable in their timbre, but still they were harps. Since the war a good many of the weaker vessels have somehow failed to find harps in the air, though there are really plenty of them in full vibration. So they have run about looking for little pick-me-ups and nips of something mildly exciting to keep up to par their sagging sense of the adventuresomeness of life. Derby sweeps never had such a vogue; every kind of gamble has boomed; dealers in public entertainment have found that the rawest sensationalism pays better than ever﻿—anything that will give a fillip, any poor new-whisky fillip, to jaded nerves.

===Chinese===
这不是被摧折者、战败者或受压迫者的绝望，而是松弛者、饫足者或病态者的悒悒寡欢。我们人人都乐于同战争作个了断，但战争的收场，正如其他或善或恶的漫长而剧烈的亢奋的收场一样，也使我们当中最坚强的人感到几分颓落。用易卜生（Ibsen）笔下那位出门寻刺激的年轻女子的话来说：战争期间，空中有竖琴。其中许多架音色刺耳，但终究是竖琴。停战以来，相当一部分意志薄弱者，不知怎的再也寻不见空中的竖琴了，尽管满架竖琴分明都在全声振动。于是他们四处奔走，寻些小小的提神饮子和轻度兴奋的浅酌，好让他们下垂的人生历险感维持在及格线上。德比抽彩（Derby sweeps）从未这般风行；形形色色的赌事一派兴旺；公众娱乐的经营者们发现，最生猛的耸动噱头比以往任何时候都更能生财——什么都行，只要能给疲惫的神经来一下刺激，哪怕像新出的威士忌那样蹩脚的一下。

### III / 三

===Original===
Of course, life itself is all right. It never grows dull. All dullness is in the mind; it comes out thence and diffuses itself over everything round the dull person, and then he terms everything dull, and thinks himself the victim of the impact of dull things. In stupid rich people, in boys and girls deadeningly taught at dead-alive schools, in all disappointed weaklings and in declining nations, this loss of power to shed anything but dullness upon what one sees and hears is common enough. Second-rate academic people, Victorian official art, the French Second Empire drama, late Latin literature exhibit its ravages well. In healthy children, in men and women of high mental vitality, in places where any of the radioactivity of gifted teaching breaks out for a while, and in swiftly and worthily rising nations the mind is easily delighted and absorbed by almost any atom of ordinary experience and its relation to the rest. The wonder and beauty and humour of life go on just the same as ever whether Spain or Holland or Italy feel them or miss them; youth would somewhere hear the chimes at midnight with the stir they made in Shakespeare’s wits although all England were peopled for ages with dullards whose pastors and masters had trained them to find the divine Falstaffiad as dull as a thaw.

===Chinese===
当然，生活本身是没有问题的。它从不变得乏味。一切乏味都在心智里；它从那里生出来，弥散到乏味之人四周的万事万物上，于是他便把一切都称作乏味，还自命为乏味之物撞击之下的受害者。在愚蠢的富人身上，在死气沉沉的学校里被教得日渐麻木的男孩女孩身上，在一切失意的弱者身上，在衰颓中的民族身上，这种能力的丧失——只往所见所闻之上敷设乏味、再也敷不出别样东西的能力的丧失——相当常见。二流学人、维多利亚时代的官方艺术、法兰西第二帝国的戏剧、晚期拉丁文学，都很好地陈列着这场浩劫的创痕。而在健康的儿童身上，在心智活力充沛的男女身上，在天才教学的放射性得以短时喷发的地方，在迅速而体面地崛起的民族身上，心智几乎能被寻常经验的任何一个原子、以及它与其余一切的关联所愉悦、所摄住。生活的奇妙、美与幽默一如既往地进行着，无论西班牙、荷兰或意大利感受到它们，还是错失了它们；纵使整个英格兰千秋万代住满蠢人，他们的牧师与师长把他们训练得把神圣的“福斯塔夫剧”（Falstaffiad）看得像化雪天气一般乏味，青春总会在什么地方听见午夜的钟声，并感到那钟声曾在莎士比亚的机敏头脑里激起的悸动。

===Original===
It need not come to that. Sick as we are, we have still in reserve the last resource of the sick, that saving miracle of recuperative force with which I have bored you. To let the sick part of our soul just be still and recover; to make our alcoholized tissues just do their work long enough on plain water﻿—that, if we can but do it, is all the sweeping and garnishing needed to make us possible dwelling-places again for the vitalizing spirit of sane delight in whatever adventure befalls us. How, then, to do it? Not, I fancy, by any kind of powwow or palaver of congress, conference, general committee, subcommittee, or other expedient for talking in company instead of working alone. This is an individual’s job, and a somewhat lonely one, though a nation has to be saved by it. To get down to work, whoever else idles; to tell no lies, whoever else may thrive on their uses; to keep fit, and the beast in you down; to help any who need it; to take less from your world than you give it; to go without the old drams to the nerves﻿—the hero stunt, the sob story, all the darling liqueurs of war emotionalism, war vanity, war spite, war rant and cant of every kind; and to do it all, not in a sentimental mood of self-pity like some actor mounting in an empty theatre and thinking what treasures the absent audience has lost, but like a man on a sheep-farm in the mountains, as much alone and at peace with his work of maintaining the world as God was when he made it.

===Chinese===
事情未必就会落到那步田地。我们虽然病了，却还保有病人的最后一项资源，那救命的康复力奇迹——这一层我已经说得你们生厌了。让我们灵魂中患病的那部分只管静下来，自行复原；让我们酒精浸透的组织只在清水上勉力履职足够长的一段时间——只要我们做得到，这便是全部所需的洒扫与陈设，足以使我们重新成为合宜的居所，迎回那种赋生活力的、神志清明的欢愉精神，无论遭遇什么际遇都能乐在其中。那么，该怎么做呢？我想，决不是靠任何大会、会议、全体委员会、小组委员会式的帕瓦聚谈或冗长空议，或任何借群处清谈以逃避独自劳作的法子。这是个人的活计，而且是相当孤独的活计，尽管一个民族的得救正系于它。别人尽可闲荡，自己埋头苦干；别人尽可靠说谎得利，自己一句谎也不说；保持康健，压住体内的兽；帮助任何需要帮助的人；从你的世界取得的，少于你给予它的；不再给神经灌那些旧日的烈酒——英雄戏码、苦情戏，以及战争感伤主义、战争虚荣、战争怨毒、种种战争的狂言与伪善所酿的全部心爱甜酒；而且做这一切时，不要怀着自怜的感伤情绪，像某个登上空无一人的剧场舞台、暗想缺席的观众错失了多少瑰宝的演员；而要像山间牧羊场上的汉子，那样孤独，又那样与自己的工作相安——他维系着这个世界，一如上帝创造它时那般。

===Original===
You remember the little French towns which the pestle and mortar of war had so ground into dust, red and white, that each separate brick went back at last, dust to dust, to mix with the earth from which it had come. The very clay of them has to be put into moulds and fired again. To some such remaking of bricks, some shaping and hardening anew of the most elementary, plainest units of rightness in action, we have to get back. Humdrum decencies, patiently practised through millions of undistinguished lives, were the myriad bricks out of which all the advanced architecture of conduct was built﻿—the solemn temples of creeds, gorgeous palaces of romantic heroism, cloud-capped towers of patriotic exaltation. And now, just when there seems to be such a babble as never before about these grandiose structures, bricks have run short.

Something simple, minute, and obscure, wholly good and not pulled up at all, something almost atomic﻿—a grain of wheat, a thread of wool, a crystal of clean salt, figures best the kind of human excellence of which our world has now most need. We would seem to have plunged on too fast and too far, like boys who have taken to spouting six-syllabled words until they forget what they had learnt of the alphabet. The moral beauty of perfect contrition is preached to a beaten enemy by our Press while the vitals of England are rotting with unprecedented growths of venereal disease: an England of boundlessly advertised heroes and saints has ousted the England in which you would never, wherever you travelled, be given wrong change on a bus.

===Chinese===
你们还记得那些法国小镇吧：战争的杵与臼把它们碾磨得那样碎，红的白的，尽成尘土，以致每一块砖最终都尘归于尘，回到它所从来的泥土里去。连它们的泥料，也须重新入模，重新烧制。我们必须回到某种这样的重新制砖的工作上来，回到对行为之正当性那些最基本、最朴素单元的重新塑形与重新硬化上来。千百万默默无闻的生命耐心践行的平凡德行，曾是亿万块砖，一切高级的行为建筑皆由它们砌成——信条的庄严神殿，浪漫英雄主义的瑰丽宫殿，爱国豪情的入云高塔。而此刻，正当这些宏构巨制似乎前所未有地被众口喧腾地议论着，砖却短缺了。

某种简单的、细小的、不显眼的东西，全然的好，全然不事拔高，某种近乎原子性的东西——一粒麦子，一缕羊毛，一粒洁净的盐的结晶——最好地写照了今日我们的世界最需要的那种人的卓越。我们似乎猛进得太快太远，像一些惯于吐诵六音节大词的男孩，直到把字母表上学过的东西忘了个干净。我国的报界正向一个被打垮的敌人宣讲完美痛悔的道德之美，而英格兰的脏腑正因前所未见的性病赘瘤而溃烂：一个英雄与圣徒被无限炒作的英格兰，已经逐走了那个你无论旅行到哪里、都不会在公共汽车上被找错零钱的英格兰。

===Original===
The wise man saved his little city, “yet no man remembered that same poor man,” and no one had better take to this way of saving England if what he wants is public distinction. It will be a career as undistinguished as that of one of the extra corpuscles formed in the blood to enable a lowland man to live on Himalayan heights. Our best friends for a long time to come will not be any of the standing cynosures of reporters’ eyes; they will find a part of their satisfaction in being nobodies; assured of the truth of the saying that there is no limit to what a man can do so long as he does not care a straw who gets the credit for it. Working apart from the whole overblown world of war valuations, the scramble for honours earned and unearned, the plotting and jostling for front places on the stage and larger letters on the bill, the whole life that is commonly held up to admiration as great and enviable, they will live in a kind of retreat almost cloistral; plenty of work for the faculties, plenty of rest for the nerves, control for desire and atrophy for conceit. Hard?﻿—yes, but England is worth it.

===Chinese===
那智慧人救了他的小城，“却没有人记念那穷人”；谁若图的是公众的显名，就最好不要拣这条拯救英格兰的路。那将是一份同样无声无闻的履历，一如低地人血液中为使其能居于喜马拉雅高处而生出的那些额外红血球的履历。往后很长一段时日，我们最好的朋友，将不会是记者眼中那些常年的瞩目焦点；他们将在做无名之辈之中寻得一部分满足；他们确信那句格言的真确：只要一个人对功劳归于何人毫不在乎，他能做成的事便没有限度。置身于整个被吹胀的战争估值世界之外——那争抢挣得的与未挣得的荣衔、为台上的前排座位和海报上更大的字母而钩心斗角推推搡搡的世界，那整套通常被推尊为伟大而可羡的生活——之外，他们将过着一种近乎隐修的退隐生活：才干不乏工作，神经不乏歇息，欲望得其节制，自负归于萎缩。难吗？——难，但英格兰值得。

### IV / 四

===Original===
Among the mind’s powers is one that comes of itself to many children and artists. It need not be lost, to the end of his days, by anyone who has ever had it. This is the power of taking delight in a thing, or rather in anything, everything, not as a means to some other end, but just because it is what it is, as the lover dotes on whatever may be the traits of the beloved object. A child in the full health of his mind will put his hand flat on the summer turf, feel it, and give a little shiver of private glee at the elastic firmness of the globe. He is not thinking how well it will do for some game or to feed sheep upon. That would be the way of the wooer whose mind runs on his mistress’s money. The child’s is sheer affection, the true ecstatic sense of the thing’s inherent characteristics. No matter what the things may be, no matter what they are good or no good for, there they are, each with a thrilling unique look and feel of its own, like a face; the iron astringently cool under its paint, the painted wood familiarly warmer, the clod crumbling enchantingly down in the hands, with its little dry smell of the sun and of hot nettles; each common thing a personality marked by delicious differences.

===Chinese===
在心智的种种能力之中，有一种会自行来到许多儿童和艺术家身上。凡是曾经拥有过它的人，直到生命的尽头都不必将它失去。这便是对一件事物、或者毋宁说对任何事物、一切事物感到欢喜的能力——不是把事物当作通往其他目的的手段，而仅仅因为它就是它自己，一如恋爱中人疼爱着所爱对象的无论哪一样品性。一个心智全然健康的孩子会把手掌平摊在夏日的草皮上，去感觉它，为这颗星球弹性而坚实的触感而微微打一个只有自己知道的欢颤。他并不在想这块草地拿来做哪种游戏有多合适，或在上面牧羊有多合适。那样想的话，便是那种满脑子惦着情人钱财的追求者的路数。孩子的欢喜是纯粹的情感，是对事物固有禀性的真正出神的感知。无论事物可能是什么，无论它们有用无用，它们就在那里，各各带着一种令人战栗的、独一无二的模样与触感，像一张脸：铁器在漆皮下沉着一股清冽的凉，上过漆的木头则亲切地更暖几分；土块在掌心里迷人地散碎，散出阳光和烫荨麻那一小股干燥的气味；每一件寻常之物都是一个人格，以种种可意的差异为标记。

===Original===
This joy of an Adam new to the garden and just looking round is brought by the normal child to the things that he does as well as those that he sees. To be suffered to do some plain work with the real spade used by mankind can give him a mystical exaltation: to come home with his legs, as the French say, reentering his body from the fatigue of helping the gardener to weed beds sends him to sleep in the glow of a beatitude that is an end in itself. Then the paradoxes of conduct begin to twinkle into sight; sugar is good, but there is a time to refrain from taking it though you can; a lie will easily get you out of a scrape, and yet, strangely and beautifully, rapture possesses you when you have taken the scrape and left out the lie. Divine unreason, as little scrutable and yet as surely a friend as the star that hangs a lamp out from the Pole to show you the way across gorse-covered commons in Surrey. So he will toe the line of a duty, not with a mere release from dismay, but exultantly, with the fire and lifting of heart of the strong man and the bridegroom, feeling always the same secret and almost sensuous transport, while he suppresses a base impulse, that he felt when he pressed the warm turf with his hand or the crumbling clay trickled warm between his fingers.

===Chinese===
这种初入园林、方才举目四顾的亚当式的欢喜，正常的孩子不但带到他所见的事物上，也带到他所做的事情上。获准用人类真正使用的那种铁锹做一点平凡的活计，能给他一种神秘的昂扬：正如法国人所说，帮着园丁锄完草畦里的草，带着两条累得直往身体里缩的腿回到家，他会在一种自为其目的的至福光辉中入睡。于是行为的种种悖论开始闪烁着显形：糖是好的，但纵然拿得到，也有应当克制不去拿的时候；一句谎话能轻易把你从窘境里捞出来，然而，奇妙而美丽的是，当你把窘境吞下、把谎话撇开时，狂喜会占据你。神圣的非理性——它像从天极悬出一盏灯、为你照亮穿越萨里郡（Surrey）长满金雀花的公共荒地之路的那颗星一样难于参透，却也同样确然是朋友。于是他会去踏上一条责任的准绳，不是仅仅如释重负，而是意气昂扬，带着大力者与新郎的心火与心扬；在克制一个卑下冲动时，他始终怀着那同一种隐秘的、近乎肉感的销魂，一如他当初把手掌压上温热的草皮、或看着酥散的黏土在指间温热地簌簌流泻时所感到的。

===Original===
The right education, if we could find it, would work up this creative faculty of delight into all its branching possibilities of knowledge, wisdom, and nobility. Of all three it is the beginning, condition, or raw material. At present it almost seems to be the aim of the commonplace teacher to take it firmly away from any pupil so blessed as to possess it. How we all know the kind of public school master whose manner expresses breezy comradeship with the boys in facing jointly the boredom of admittedly beastly but still unavoidable lessons! And the assumption that life out of school is too dull to be faced without the aid of infinitely elaborated games! And the girl schools where it seems to be feared that evil must come in any space of free time in which neither a game nor a dance nor a concert nor a lecture with a lantern intervenes to rescue the girls from the presumed tedium of mere youth and health! Everywhere the assumption that simple things have failed; that anything like hardy mental living and looking about for oneself, to find interests, is destined to end ill; that the only hope is to keep up the full dose of drugs, to be always pulling and pushing, prompting and coaxing and tickling the youthful mind into condescending to be interested. You know the effects: the adolescent whose mind seems to drop when taken out of the school shafts, or at least to look round, utterly at a loss, with a plaintive appeal for a suggestion of something to do, some excitement to come, something to make it worth while to be alive on this dull earth. We saw the effects in our hapless brain work in the war.

===Chinese===
正当的教育，倘若我们寻得着它，本可把这种欢愉的创造能力，加工发展为知识、智慧与高贵的一切枝繁叶茂的可能。它是三者的开端、条件，或曰原料。而在今天，平庸教师的目标，几乎恰恰是把它从任何有幸拥有它的学生身上坚定地拿走。我们都认得那种公学（public school）的教师：他的做派透着与男孩们爽朗的同志情谊，合力共同面对那些公认乏味至极、却终究无可逃免的功课！还有那项假定：校门之外的生活太沉闷了，若无无限繁复的运动游戏之助，便无从面对！还有那些女子学校：似乎唯恐在任何一段自由时间里，若没有一场比赛、一次舞会、一场音乐会或一场带幻灯的讲课居间搭救，把女孩们从仅凭青春与健康便想当然会有的沉闷里解救出来，邪恶便必乘虚而入！到处都是这样的假定：简单的事物已经失灵；任何强健的心智生活、任何自己放眼寻觅兴味的做法，都注定落得糟糕下场；唯一的指望，是把足量的药物一直灌下去，永远连拉带推、又提示又哄劝又挠痒地撩动年轻的心智，求它屈尊俯就地对什么发生兴趣。效果如何，你们是知道的：那样养出来的青少年，一旦卸出学校的辕杆，心智便仿佛一落千丈，至少也会四下环顾、全然无措，哀声恳求有人提示一件可做的事、一场将至的兴奋、一样能让他在这颗沉闷的地球上活着还觉得值的东西。战争期间我们那些不幸的脑力劳作，已经让我们领教过这些效果了。

===Original===
But if we were to wait to save England till thousands of men and women brought up in this way see what they have lost and insist on a better fate for their children we might as well write England off as one with Tyre and Sidon already. Her case is too pressing. She cannot wait for big, slowly telling improvements in big institutions, although improvements must come. She has to be saved by a change in the individual temper. We each have to fall back, with a will, on the only way of life in which the sane simplicity of joy in plain things and in common rightness of action can be generated. Health of mind or body comes of doing wholesome things﻿—perhaps for a long time without joy in doing them, as the sick man lies chafing, eating the slops that are all he is fit for, or as the dipsomaniac drinks in weariness and depression the insipid water that is to save him. Then, on some great day, self-control may cease to be merely the sum of many dreary acts of abstention; it may take life again as an inspiriting force, both a warmth and a light, such as makes nations great.

===Chinese===
但是，倘若要等到成千上万这样教养出来的男女看清自己失去了什么、坚持要给子女一个较好的命运，才去拯救英格兰，那我们大可干脆把英格兰一笔勾销，与推罗（Tyre）和西顿（Sidon）一同销账了事。她的病情太紧迫了。她等不起庞大机构里那些收效迟缓的庞大改良，尽管改良终须到来。她的得救，只能靠个人心性的改变。我们每个人都得发愿退回那唯一的生活方式上去——唯有在其中，方能再生出对平凡事物、对行为之寻常正当的那种神志清明而单纯的欢愉。心或身的健康，来自做有益健康之事——也许有很长一段时间做得并无欢愉，正如病人一面烦躁地卧着，一面吃着仅堪其受用的稀糊；又如嗜酒狂在疲惫与抑郁中，喝下那终将救他的寡淡清水。然后，在某个盛大的日子，自我节制或许会不再只是许多可厌的克己行为的总和；它或许会重新活转过来，成为一种鼓舞人心的力量，既是暖又是光——使民族伟大的，正是这样的东西。

## III At Agincourt and Ypres / 三 阿金库尔与伊普尔

### I / 一

===Original===
Shakespeare seems to have known what there is to be known about our Great War of 1914–18. And he was not censored. So he put into his *Henry IV* and *Henry V* a lot of little things that our press had to leave out at the time for the good of the country. If you look closely you can see them lying about all over the plays. There is the ugly affair of the pyx, at Corbie, on the Somme; there are the little irregularities in recruiting; there are the small patches of baddish morale on the coast and even in Picardy; there is the painful case of the oldish lieutenant who drank and had cold feet, after talking bigger than anyone else. One almost expects to find something in *Henry V* about the mutiny at Étaples, or the predilection of the Australians for chickens. Anyhow, there is a more understanding account than any war correspondent has given of English troops about to go into battle.

Timing it for the morning of Agincourt, Shakespeare shows us three standard types of the privates who were to win the Great War. One of them, Court, says little; he just looks out for the dawn. We all know Court; he has won many battles. Bates, the second man, gives tongue pretty freely. Bates is not ruled by funk, but he professes it.

===Chinese===
莎士比亚似乎早已洞悉我们这场 1914–18 年大战中值得知晓的一切，而且他没有受到新闻检查。于是他在《亨利四世》与《亨利五世》里放进了一大堆小事——那些我国的报界当年为了国家利益不得不略去的东西。你细看之下，会发现它们散落在剧中各处：索姆河（Somme）畔科比（Corbie）那桩圣体盒（pyx）丑闻；征兵中的小小流弊；海岸一带、甚至皮卡第（Picardy）某些角落里颇为败坏的士气；还有那位上了年纪的中尉，酒不离口、嗓门比谁都大，临阵却脚底发凉的可悲案例。你几乎要指望在《亨利五世》里读到关于埃塔普勒（Étaples）兵变、或者澳大利亚兵嗜好偷鸡的段落了。无论如何，比起任何一位随军记者，他笔下的英军临战图景都要更见理解之深。

莎士比亚把场景定在阿金库尔（Agincourt）的清晨，向我们展示了日后赢得这场大战的三种标准列兵。其一科特（Court）话不多，只是望着天等破晓。我们都认识科特；他打赢过许多仗。第二个人贝茨（Bates）说得就比较放任了。贝茨并非真受怯懦支配，他只是公然自认怯懦。

===Original===
> “He” (the King) “may show what outward courage he will, but I believe, as cold a night as ’tis, he could wish himself in the Thames up to the neck, and so I would he were, and I by him, at all adventures, so we were quit here.”

===Chinese===
> 「他」（指国王）「爱摆出多少外表上的勇气，随他去摆；可是我相信，夜这么冷，他心里未尝不愿意自己泡进泰晤士河里，淹到脖子；我不但愿意他就在那儿，还愿意我陪在他身边，豁出一切干系，只要能脱身离开此地。」

===Original===
Bates, being dead, yet liveth, like Court. In 1915, as in 1415, he was prosecuting his conquests in France, and his unaltered soul was fortifying itself with chants like

===Chinese===
贝茨虽死犹生，一如科特。正如 1415 年，在 1915 年，他仍在法兰西开疆拓土，而他那毫未更易的灵魂，正用这样的歌谣给自己壮胆：

===Original===
> Far, far away would I be,
 

 Where the Alleyman cannot catch me,

and

> Oh my! I don’t want to die,
 

 *I* want to go home,

===Chinese===
> 但愿远远地离开，
 

 到「德国佬」（Alleyman）抓不到我的地方，

以及

> 哦天哪！我不想死，
 

 *我*想回家，

===Original===
sung to dourly wailful tunes, at the seasons of stress when Scotsmen and Irishmen screwed themselves up to the sticking-point with their Tyrtaean anti-English ballads, when Frenchmen would soulfully hymn Glory and Love, and when Germans, if the ear did not deceive, were calling out the whole *Landwehr* and *Landsturm* of the straight patriotic lyre. Williams, the third of the Agincourt privates, lives too. He lives with a vengeance. You will remember that he was an anti-ranter, anti-canter and anti-gusher, like Bates. But he ran a special line of his own. He was not simply “fed up”—as he would say now—with tall talk about the just cause and brothers-in-arms and the moral beauty of dying in battle. He was suspicious, besides. He darkly fancied that those who emitted the stuff must have some crooked game on. “That’s more than we know” was his stopper for all stock heroics. He would take none of his betters on trust, neither High Command nor Government nor Church—only one company officer whom he knew for himself—“a good old commander and a most kind gentleman.” This one small plot of dry ground was reclaimed from the broad sea of Williams’ scepticism.

===Chinese===
这些歌用阴郁哀婉的调子唱出来，正值那些吃紧的时节——苏格兰人和爱尔兰人用他们提尔泰俄斯式（Tyrtaean）的反英战歌把自己拧到豁出去的临界点，法国人动情地讴歌着荣耀与爱情，而德国人，倘若耳朵没有骗人，正把那架一本正经的爱国里拉琴所能奏出的整支 *Landwehr* 与 *Landsturm* 都召唤出来。阿金库尔三名列兵中的第三个，威廉斯（Williams），也还活着。他活得可有劲了。你会记得，他和贝茨一样，反高谈、反滥调、反抒情喷涌。但他自有一路独有的做派。他不只是——用如今的说法——「受够了」（fed up）那些关于正义之师、袍泽之义、战死之德性美的豪言。他还多疑。他阴沉地疑心，散布这套货色的人背后必有猫腻。「这咱们可不知道」是他用来堵死一切现成豪言的话。他不轻信任何地位高于他的人——最高统帅部也罢，政府也罢，教会也罢——只认一个他亲自摸透了的连级军官——「一位好老长官，一位极其和善的绅士」。威廉斯那片怀疑论的汪洋大海里，便围垦出这么一小块干地。

### II / 二

===Original===
If this Doubting Thomas abounded at Agincourt how could he not abound at, say, the third Battle of Ypres? At Agincourt our whole army was just small enough to have comradeship all the way through it—not the figure-of-speech used by the orators, but the thing that soldiers know. Comradeship in a battalion will come of itself; it may be grown, with some effort, in a brigade; in good divisions it has flickered into life for a while during a war; army corps know it not, though their headquarters staffs may dine together at times. At Agincourt the whole of our force was an infantry brigade and a half. It all lay handy in one bivouac. Generals led advancing troops as second-lieutenants do now. The commander-in-chief could go round the lines of a night and talk to the men; if he should speak to them about “we few, we happy few, band of brothers,” he would not be projecting gas.

But now—? It is nobody’s fault, but all of that has been lost, as utterly lost as the old comradeship of master and journeyman worker is lost in a mill where half the thousand hands may never have seen the employer who sits in a faraway office, perhaps in a faraway town. Two million men can never be a happy few; nor yet a band of brothers—you have to know a brother first. A man could serve six months in France and never see the general commanding his division. He could be there for four years and not know what a corps or an army commander looked like. How can you help it? Many generals did what they could—more, you might say, than they should. They left their desks and maps to visit their men in the line; they made excuses to get under fire; two or three were killed doing so; one corps commander smuggled himself into the front line of an attack by his corps. But these were escapades, strictly. The higher commands have no right to get hit. Modern war has pushed the right place for them farther and farther away from the fighting, away from the men, whom some of the higher commanders, as well as the lower, do really love with a love passing the love of women—“the dear men” of whom I have heard an officer, tied to the staff and the base by the results of head wounds, speak with an almost wailing ache of desire, as horses whinny for a friend—“Would I were with him, wheresoe’er he is, either in heaven or in hell.” But how were the men to know that?

===Chinese===
既然这位多疑的多马（Doubting Thomas）在阿金库尔比比皆是，那么到了譬如第三次伊普尔（Ypres）战役，他又怎么会不多多益善？在阿金库尔，我们全军恰好小到能让袍泽之义（comradeship）一以贯之——不是演说家口中那种修辞，而是士兵们认得的那种实物。营里的袍泽之义自然生成；旅里费些力气也能培育；好的师里，它在战争期间曾闪现过一阵生命；军一级就无从识得它了，尽管军的司令部参谋们偶尔同桌共餐。在阿金库尔，我们全部兵力是一个半步兵旅，全数扎营在一处露营地里。将军们像如今的少尉那样亲率进攻部队。总司令能在夜里巡视各条战线、与士兵们交谈；倘若他对他们说起「我们这些少数人，我们这些幸运的少数人，我们这支兄弟的队伍」，那可算不上是在施放毒气。

可是如今呢？——这不能怪谁，但那一切已然失落，失落得就像师傅与帮工之间的旧日情谊失落于一座千人工厂之中——厂里半数工人可能从未见过那位坐在遥远办公室、或许还身在遥远城镇里的雇主。两百万人永远当不成幸运的少数，也永远当不成一支兄弟的队伍——你总得先认识一位兄弟才行。一个人可以在法国服役六个月，却从未见过指挥他那个师的将军。他可以在那里待满四年，也不知道军长或集团军司令长什么模样。这有什么办法呢？许多将军已经尽力而为了——甚至可以说，超出了他们应尽的本分。他们离开书桌与地图，到战壕里看望部下；他们编造借口去挨炮火；有两三位因此殒命；有一位军长把自己偷运进了本军进攻的第一线。但严格说来，这些都是逞能的越轨。高级司令部没有挨打的权利。现代战争已经把他们应居的位置推得离战斗越来越远，离士兵越来越远——而对这些士兵，某些高级指挥官同低级军官一样，确实怀着一种胜过爱女人的爱。我就听过一位军官——因头部战伤的后遗症而被拴在参谋部与后方基地——谈起「那些亲爱的人」时，声调里带着近乎呜咽的渴望之痛，像马儿为同伴嘶鸣——「但愿我与他同在，无论他身在天堂还是地狱。」可士兵们又如何能知道这些呢？

===Original===
Everything helped to indispose them to know it; everything went to point the contrast between their own fate and that of its distant and unknown controllers. The evolution of the war was now calling on all ranks of troops in the actual line to put up with a much diminished chance of survival, only the barest off-chance if they stayed there year after year. While they lived it was inflicting upon them in trenches a life squalid beyond precedent. And that same evolution had pressed back the chief seats of command into places where life was said to contrast itself in wonderful ways with that life of mud and stench and underground gloom.

===Chinese===
种种情况都在使他们无心知晓这一点；一切都在彰显他们自身的命运与那些遥远而陌生的掌控者们的命运之间的反差。战局的演变此刻正要求实际战线上的各级官兵忍受大幅缩水的生还机会——倘若年复一年地留在那里，便只剩最微末的侥幸。而在他们活着的时候，这演变还让他们在战壕里过一种龌龊得史无前例的生活。也正是这同一种演变，把主要的指挥席位向后挤进了这样一些地方——据说那里的生活，与泥泞、恶臭和地穴幽暗的生活恰成奇妙的对照。

===Original===
It was quite truly said. Of the separation and contrast you got a full sense if fate took you straight from trench life in the stiff Flanders slime or the dreary wet chalk of the disembowelled Loos plain to one of the seats of authority far in the rear. G.H.Q., the most regal seat of them all, was divinely niched, during most of the war, at Montreuil, and Montreuil was a place to bring tears to the eyes of an artist, like Castelfranco, St. Andrews, or Windsor; the tiny walled town on a hill had that poignant fullness of loveliness, making the sense ache at it, like still summer evenings in England. It was a storied antique, unscathed and still living and warm, weathered mellow with centuries of sunshine and tranquillity, all its own old wars long laid aside and the racket of this new one very far from it. Walking among its walled gardens, where roses hung over the walls, or sitting upon the edge of the rampart, your feet dangling over among the top boughs of embosoming trees, you were not merely out of the war; you were out of all war! you entered into that beatitude of super-peace which fills your mind as you look at a Roman camp on a sunned Sussex down, where the gentle convexities of the turf seem to turn war into an old tale for children.

===Chinese===
这话说得一点不假。倘若命运把你直接从佛兰德（Flanders）板结的泥淖、或被开膛破肚的卢斯（Loos）平原那阴湿的白垩地里的战壕生活，送到远在后方的某一处权力席位上，你便会对这种分隔与反差获得完整的感受。其中最具王者气象的一席——总司令部（G.H.Q.）——大战的大部分岁月里，都被神圣地嵌在蒙特勒伊（Montreuil）的壁龛中；而蒙特勒伊是一个能让艺术家热泪盈眶的地方，堪与卡斯特尔弗兰科、圣安德鲁斯或温莎相提并论：山上那座小小的围城，有着令人心头发酸的丰盈之美，一如英格兰静谧的夏日黄昏。它是一件满载传说的古物，未受损伤，仍然活着、温着，被几个世纪的阳光与安宁熏染得醇熟；自家古老的战事早已搁下，这场新战争的喧嚣又离它极远。漫步在它围墙环抱的花园里——玫瑰探出墙头——或是坐在壁垒边缘，双脚垂荡在环拥全城的树冠顶梢之间，你不只是置身这场战争之外，你是置身一切战争之外！你进入了那种超级和平的至福——正如你望着苏塞克斯（Sussex）一座洒满阳光的白垩丘上的罗马营垒时充盈于心的感受，那里草皮柔缓的隆起，仿佛把战争变成了一则讲给孩子们听的古老故事。

===Original===
Such gardens of enchantment were not known by sight to most of our fighting troops, but they were rumoured. The mind of Williams, in the front line, worked with a surly zest on the contrast between the two hemispheres of an army—the hemisphere of combatancy, of present torment, of scant reward, of probable extinction, and the hemisphere of non-combatancy, of comfort, of safety, of more profuse decoration, the second hemisphere ruling over the former and decimating it sometimes by feats like the Staff work of 1915. Among the straw in billets and the chalk clods in dugouts, in the reeking hot twilight of parlours in French village inns, in the confidential darkness after Lights Out in hospital wards from Bethune to Versailles and Rouen, the vinegar tongue of Williams let itself go.

Of course, he went wrong. And yet his error, like the facts which begat it, could not be helped. If all that you know of an alleged brother of yours is that he is having the best of the deal while you are getting the worst you have to be a saint of the prime to take it on trust that it really did please God, or any godlike human authority, to call him to a station in a dry hut with a stove, among the fleshpots of an agreeable coast, and you to a station in a wet burrow full of rats and lice and yellow or white mud and ugly liabilities. And Williams was not a saint, although when he enlisted he was profusely told that he was by people who were to call him a sinner later, when as a Dundee rioter or “Bolshevik” miner, or as a Sinn Feiner or a Black-and-Tan, he transgressed some eternal law. Williams was and is only a quiet simple substance exhibiting certain normal reactions under certain chemical tests.

===Chinese===
这般迷人的花园，我们的作战部队大多数人未曾亲眼见过，但风声是有的。前线上威廉斯的头脑，正怀着愠怒的热忱，琢磨着一支军队的两个半球——作战的半球：眼前的苦刑、微薄的报偿、大概率的不归；非作战的半球：舒适、安全、更为丰沛的勋章——后一个半球统治着前一个，有时还以 1915 年参谋作业（Staff work）一类的杰作，对它成批地屠戮。在宿营地的稻草间、掩蔽部的白垩土块间，在法兰西乡村客栈小客厅闷热难当的薄暮里，在从贝蒂讷（Bethune）到凡尔赛与鲁昂的病房里、熄灯令（Lights Out）之后可以推心置腹的黑暗中，威廉斯那条醋味十足的舌头恣意驰骋。

当然，他错了。然而他的错误，一如催生这错误的种种事实，是无可如何的事。倘若你对一位所谓兄长的全部所知，只是他占尽了交易的甜头而你受尽了苦头，那你必须是上古的圣徒，才能笃信不疑：上帝，或任何一位神一般的人间权威，当真乐于把他召到一个有火炉的干爽小屋的岗位上，置身宜人海岸的膏粱之乡，而把你召到一个湿漉漉的地洞的岗位上，满地老鼠、虱子、黄泥白泥和种种不堪的负累。而威廉斯并不是圣徒，尽管他入伍时人们曾慷慨地告诉他他是——这些人后来却又管他叫罪人：在他作为邓迪的暴徒、「布尔什维克」矿工，或作为新芬党人（Sinn Feiner）、「黑棕队」（Black-and-Tan）成员，触犯了某条永恒法律的时候。威廉斯过去是、现在仍然只是一种安静的单纯物质，在某些化学试剂的作用下呈现某些正常的反应。

### III / 三

===Original===
There may be laid up in Heaven a pattern of some front line by which the Staff in its rear would be really loved. But such love is not in the nature of man. If the skin on Mr. Dempsey’s knuckles could speak, and were perfectly frank, it would not say that it loved the unexposed and unabraded tissues of Mr. Dempsey’s directive brain. Hotspur, in deathless words, has aired the eternal grudge of the combatant soldier against the Brass Hat—

===Chinese===
天上或许存放着某种前线样板，凭它，后方的参谋部（Staff）真能受人爱戴。但这种爱不合乎人的天性。倘若登普西先生（Mr. Dempsey）指节上的皮肤会说话，而且说得全然坦白，它也不会说它爱登普西先生那颗发号施令的头脑里那些从未暴露、从未磨损的组织。霍茨波（Hotspur）以不朽的言辞，道出了作战士兵对铜帽军官（Brass Hat）的千古宿怨——

===Original===
> I remember, when the fight was done,
 

 When I was dry with rage and extreme toil,
 

 Breathless and faint, leaning upon my sword,
 

 Came there a certain lord, neat and trimly dressed,
 

 Fresh as a bridegroom.

===Chinese===
> 我记得，仗打完之后，
 

 当我怒极力竭、口干舌燥，
 

 气喘吁吁、软弱无力地拄着剑，
 

 便来了那么一位爵爷，衣冠楚楚、修饰齐整，
 

 像新郎一样光鲜。

===Original===
So the jaundiced narrative flows on and on, doing the fullest justice on record to some of the main heads of the front line’s immemorial distaste for the Staff—for its too Olympian line of comment upon the vulgar minutae of combat, its offensively manifest facilities for getting a good shave, its fertility in gratuitous advice of an imperfectly practical kind, and its occasional lapses from grace in speaking of the men, the beloved men, the objects of every good combatant officer’s jealous and wrathful affection.

Or, again, you might say that a Staff is a trouser-button, which there are few to praise while it goes on with its work, and very few to abstain from cursing when it comes off. When a Staff’s work is done well the front line only feels as if Nature were marching, without actual molestation, along some beneficent course of her own. But when someone slips up, and half a brigade is left to itself in a cold, cold world encircled by Germans, the piercing eye of the front line perceives in a moment how pitifully ill the Brass Hats deserve of their country. If you are an infantryman the Brass Hats above you are, in your sight, a kind of ex officio children of perdition, like your own gunners. As long as your own gunners go on achieving the masterpiece of mathematics that is required to confine the incidence of their shells to the enemy you feel that, just for the moment, a gunner’s rich natural endowment of original sin is not telling for all it is worth. But some day the frailty of man or of metal causes a short one to drop once again among you and your friends; and then you are mightily refreshed and confirmed in the stern Calvinistic faith of the infantry that there are chosen vessels of grace and also chosen vessels of homicidal mania.

===Chinese===
这份黄胆色的叙事便这样滔滔不绝，把前线对参谋部亘古反感的主要罪状发挥得淋漓尽致，堪称有案可稽之最——诸如：参谋部对战斗的俚俗细节发表评论时那副过分奥林波斯神式的居高临下；他们刮得好一脸胡子那种显眼得招人恨的便利；他们出产不甚切实的免费建议时那等丰饶；还有他们偶尔失态，说起「那些人」——那些亲爱的人，每一位优秀作战军官怀着妒意与怒意挚爱的对象——时口出的秽语。

或者，你也可以说，参谋部是一粒裤钮：它好好干活的时候，没人夸它；它崩落的时候，没人忍得住不咒它。参谋部的活计干好了，前线只觉得大自然正不受骚扰地循着她自家某条仁慈的路线行进。可一旦有人失手，半个旅被孤零零撂在一个冷而又冷、四面皆敌的世界里，前线那只锐利的眼睛霎时便看穿：铜帽军官们多么可悲地辜负了他们的国家。如果你是步兵，你头顶上的铜帽军官们在你眼里便是一类当然的沉沦之子，同你自己的炮兵一样。只要你的炮兵还在不断完成那桩数学杰作——把他们炮弹的着点限定在敌人身上——你就觉得，至少此刻，一名炮兵天赋的那份厚重的原罪还没有物尽其用地发作。可总有一天，人或金属的脆弱使一颗近弹再次落进你和你的伙伴中间；于是你便大大地提神并坚定起来，笃守步兵那种严峻的加尔文宗信仰：世上有蒙拣选的恩典器皿，也有蒙拣选的杀人狂器皿。

===Original===
If man, in all his wars, is predestined never to love and trust his Brass Hats, least of all can he struggle against this disability when he is warring in trenches. Why? Because trench life is very domestic, highly atomic. Its atom, or unit, like that of slum life, is the jealously close, exclusive, contriving life of a family housed in an urban cellar. During the years of trench war a man seldom saw the whole of his company at a time. Our total host might be two millions strong, or ten millions; whatever its size a man’s world was that of his section—at most, his platoon; all that mattered much to him was the one little boatload of castaways with whom he was marooned on a desert island and making shift to keep off the weather and any sudden attack of wild beasts. Absorbed in the primitive job of keeping alive on an earth naked except in the matter of food, they became, like other primitive men, family separatists. Any odd chattel that each trench household acquired served as an extra dab of cement for the household’s internal affections, as well as a possible casus belli against the unblessed outsiders who dared to cast upon it the eye of desire. A brazier with three equal legs would be coveted by a whole company. Once a platoon acquired a broken, but just practicable, armchair; not exactly a stronghold of luxury; rather a freakish wave of her banner; and this symbol of lost joys was borne, at great inconvenience, from sector to sector of the front, amidst the affected derision of other platoons—veiling what was well understood to be envy. It was like the grim, ineffusive spiritual cohesion of a Scottish family soldered together to keep out the world.

===Chinese===
倘若人在他的一切战争里都注定永不能爱与信任他的铜帽军官，那么当他在战壕里打仗时，便最挣不脱这重残缺。为什么？因为战壕生活极其居家，高度原子化。它的原子，或曰单元，一如贫民窟生活，是一家人蜗居在城市地下室里那种戒备地亲密、排他、精打细算的生活。战壕战的那些年头，一个人难得见到他全连的聚合。我们的总兵力也许有两百万，也许一千万；无论多大，一个人的世界就是他那个班的世界——至多加上他的排；对他真正要紧的，只是同他一道被困在荒岛上、凑合着遮蔽风雨、抵挡野兽突袭的那一小船落难者。他们全神贯注于在那片除食物外赤裸一无所有的大地上活命的原始活计，于是同其他原始人一样，成了家庭分离主义者。每个战壕人家弄到的任何一件零星家什，都给这户人家的内部感情再抹一道水泥，同时也可能构成对那些胆敢向它投来觊觎之眼的不幸外人的开战理由（casus belli）。一只三腿齐匀的小火炉，足以让全连垂涎。有一回，某个排弄到了一把破烂但尚堪一用的扶手椅；算不上什么奢侈的堡垒；倒更像奢侈女神的旗帜一次任性的挥舞；这件逝去欢乐的象征，便在前线各个防区之间极费周章地辗转搬运，在其他各排佯装的讥笑声中——那讥笑遮掩着人人心照不宣的艳羡。这就像苏格兰人家那种冷峻而不外露的精神团结，熔接成一块，把世界关在门外。

===Original===
Constantly jammed up against one another, every man in each of these isolated knots of adventurers came to be seen by the rest for what he was worth, with the drastic clearness of open-eyed husbands and wives of long standing. They had domesticated the Day of Judgment. Many old valuations had to go by the board; some great home reputations wilted surprisingly; stones that the builders of public opinion on Salisbury Plain had confidently rejected found their way up to the heads of corners. Officers, watched almost as closely, were sorted out by the minds of the men into themes for contemptuous silence, objects of the love that doeth and beareth all things, and cases of Not Proven Yet. The cutting equity of this family council was bracing. It got the best out of everybody in whom there was anything. Imagine a similar overhauling of public life here! And the size of the scrapheap! But to the outer world, which it did not half know, the tribunal was harsh, and harshest of all to the outer and upper world of army principalities and powers.

These were, to it, the untested, unsifted, “the crowd that was never put through it.” There were presumptions against them, besides. They were akin, in the combatant’s sight, to the elfish gods that had ruled and bedevilled his training at home. They were of the breed of the wasters, the misorganizers, the beauties who sent his battalion out from the Wiltshire downs to Bruay along a course of gigantic zigzags, like a yacht beating up in the teeth of a wind, first running far south to Havre, then north to near the German Ocean, and then going about and opening out again upon the southward tack until Bruay was struck; for it was, indeed, along a trajectory somewhat like that of an actual flash of lightning in some quaint engraving that Britain hurled at the enemy many of her new thunderbolts of war. Also, they stood in the shoes of the men who in French’s day had sent platoon commanders to take woods and quarries not marked on their maps. And they were the men who, when troops had been marching twelve miles in full kit on the high-cambered, heavily greased Flanders setts in the rain, would appear on the roadside turf round a blind corner, sitting chubby and sleek on fresh horses, and say that the marching was damned bad and troops must go back tomorrow and do it again. But the chief count was the first—that they had not all gone through the mill; that they lived in a world in which all the respectable old bubbles, pricked elsewhere, were still fat and shining, where all the old bluffs were uncalled and still going strong, and the wangler could still inherit the earth and eyewash reign happy and glorious.

===Chinese===
由于时刻挤作一团，这些与世隔绝的冒险者小圈子里，每个人都按其真实斤两被其余人看得分明，分明得像长年睁着眼睛过日子的夫妻。他们把末日审判驯成了家养。许多旧的估值只得付诸东流；某些在国内大名鼎鼎的人物出人意料地蔫了；而索尔兹伯里平原（Salisbury Plain）上舆论的匠人们自信满满弃而不用的石头，反倒一路垒上了墙角的顶石。军官们也受到几乎同样贴身的注视，被士兵们的心智分拣成几类：只配报以轻蔑沉默的；配得上那种「凡事包容、凡事忍耐」之爱的；以及「尚未证实」（Not Proven Yet）一类的存疑案件。这个家庭议事会锋利如刀的公道令人振奋。它把每个肚里有货的人最好的那一面都逼了出来。想象一下此地的公共生活也来这么一场彻查！还有那座废料山该有多么大！但对它不甚了了的外部世界而言，这法庭是严苛的；而对军队里那个在外又居上层的「执政的、掌权的」世界，它严苛尤甚。

在它眼里，这些人是未经检验、未经筛分的，「那群从来没被碾过一遍的人」。此外，还有种种推定于他们不利。在作战者眼里，他们类似于那些在故乡统治并祸害他训练的小妖神。他们属于那路废物、那路瞎组织者、那路美人儿：把他那个营从威尔特郡丘陵送出去，到布吕阿（Bruay），路线是巨大的之字形，像一艘顶风抢行的游艇——先远远南下至勒阿弗尔（Havre），再北折到德意志海（German Ocean，北海旧称）附近，然后换舷转向，重新展开南行的航路，直到撞上布吕阿；因为不列颠投向敌人的许多新式战争霹雳，走的确实就是这样一条弹道，有些像古怪版画里一道真闪电的轨迹。再者，他们与弗伦奇（French）当权时那些人穿的是同一双鞋：当年正是那些人，派排长们去攻取地图上根本未曾标注的树林与采石场。他们还是那样一批人：部队冒雨全副武装、在拱度高企、油污厚重的佛兰德砌石路面上行军十二英里之后，他们会在盲弯外那截路边草皮上现身，养得白白胖胖、油光水滑地骑在新马上，说这行军烂得该死，部队明天必须回去再走一遍。但最重的罪状仍是头一条——他们没有全体经历过这道碾磨；他们活在一个世界里，那边所有体面的旧气泡，尽管在别处已被戳破，在这里却依然饱满闪光；那边一切陈年的虚声恫吓，尽管在别处已被叫破，在这里却仍无人叫破、依旧气焰熏天；钻营者仍可以承受地土，花架子（eyewash）仍能幸福而光荣地主宰一切。

===Original===
Not a judgment wholly just. But not one contemptible either; for, wherever it ended, it set out from the right idea of judging a man only by what he was worth and what he could do. And, just or not, it was real; it influenced men’s acts, not to the extent of losing us the war, but to that of helping to send the winners home possessed with that contemptuous impatience of authority which has already thrown out of gear so much of the prewar machinery for regulating the joint action of mankind.

===Chinese===
这判断并不全然公正。但也绝非可鄙；因为无论它终局落于何处，它的出发点是正的：只按一个人的真实斤两与实际本事来评判他。而且，无论公正与否，它是真实存在过的；它影响了人们的行动——没有影响到使我们输掉战争的地步，倒是影响到了这样的地步：它帮助把打胜仗的人送回了家，人人怀着对权威的那份轻蔑的不耐烦——正是这份不耐烦，已经让战前那套调节人类共同行动的机器出了那么多故障。

### IV / 四

===Original===
There was yet another special check during the war upon love and respect for the higher commands. There were so many things of moment which they were the last to find out. Time after time the great ones of this world were seen to be walking in darkness long after the lowly had seen a great light. While the appointed brains of our army were still swearing hard by the rifle, and nothing but it, as the infantry’s friend, a more saving truth had entered in at the lowly door of the infantry’s mind. Ignoring all that at Aldershot they had learnt to be sacred, they contumaciously saw that so long as you stand in a hole deeper than you are tall you never will hit with a rifle-bullet another man standing in just such another hole twenty yards off. But also—divine idea!—that you can throw a tin can from your hole into his.

In England the mighty had taken a great deal of pains to teach the New Army always to parry the thrust of its enemy’s bayonet first, and only then to get in its own. A fine, stately procedure it was when taught by an exemplary Regular Army instructor fully resolved that, whatever Shelley may say, no part of any movement must mingle in any other part’s being. In France, and no doubt on other fronts too, it abruptly dawned on those whose style this formalist had moulded, more or less, that a second German or Turk was apt to cut in before the appointed ritual of debate with the first could be carried to a happy end. Illicit abridgements followed, attended by contumacious reflections.

===Chinese===
战争期间，对高级司令部的爱与敬意，还受着另一重特殊的抑制。有那么许多要紧的事，他们总是最后知晓。一次又一次，世上的大人物们仍在黑暗中行走时，卑微者早已看见大光。当我们军队里钦定的智囊们还在死死赌咒步枪——而且唯有步枪——是步兵之友时，一个更能救命的真理，已经从步兵心智那扇低微的门走了进来。他们把奥尔德肖特（Aldershot）教给他们的神圣信条一概丢开，桀骜地看出：只要你站在一个深过你身高的坑里，你就永远别想用一发步枪子弹，打中二十码外另一个站在同样坑里的人。但是——神来之想！——你却可以从你的坑里，把一只铁皮罐扔进他的坑里。

在英国，权贵们曾煞费苦心地教导新军（New Army）：务必先格开敌人刺刀的突刺，然后再送进自己的。由一位模范的正规军（Regular Army）教官示范起来，这确是一套漂亮庄严的程序——这位教官打定了主意：任凭雪莱（Shelley）怎么说，任何一个动作的任何部分，都不得掺和进另一部分的存在里去。到了法国——无疑也在别的战线上——那些或多或少被这位形式主义者铸了模的人猛然醒悟：与第一个德国人或土耳其人之间那套规定的辩论仪式，还没来得及送进圆满的结局，第二个德国人或土耳其人往往就已经插进来了。于是随之而来的，是种种非法的删节，伴着桀骜不驯的感想。

===Original===
Whatever, again, was august in Canadian life and affairs was bent in 1914 upon arming Canadian troops with what was indeed, by a long chalk, the pick of all match-shooting rifles. It was the last word of man in his struggle against the caprices of barometric and thermometric pressures on ranges. And it was to show a purblind Europe, among other things, that Sam Hughes was the man and that wisdom would die with him. Yet hardly had its use, in wrath, begun when there broke upon the untutored Canadian foot-soldier a revelation withheld from the Hugheses of this world. He perceived that the enemy, in his perversity, did not intend to stand up on a skyline a thousand yards off to be shot with all the refinements of science; point-blank was going to be the only range, except for a few specialists; rapidity of fire would matter more than precision; and all the super-subtle appliances tending to triumphs at Bisley would here be no better than aids to picking mud from trench walls as the slung rifle joggled against them.

===Chinese===
再说，1914 年，加拿大朝野一切显赫的力量都专注于一件事：用一种确实遥遥领先、堪称射靶步枪之冠的枪械武装加拿大部队。那是人类对抗靶场上气压与气温之无常的登峰造极之作。它还要让近视的欧洲开开眼——要开的诸事之一便是：萨姆·休斯（Sam Hughes）实乃天命之人，智慧将与他偕亡。然而，此枪挟怒投入使用为时不久，一条对世上休斯们秘而不宣的天启，便降临到未受调教的加拿大步兵头上。他看出来了：敌人执拗得很，并不打算站到一千码外的天际线上，凭科学的全部精妙挨枪子儿；除了少数专家，平射将是唯一的射程；射速将比精度更要紧；而所有那些志在比斯利（Bisley）夺魁的超精妙附件，到了这里，充其量不过是步枪背在身上磕磕碰碰时，从壕壁上剔泥的家什。

===Original===
The great did not turn these truths of mean origin right away from the door. They would quite often take a discovery in. Only there was no running to greet it.

> There was no hurry in their hands,
 

 No hurry in their feet.

===Chinese===
大人物们并没有把这些出身寒微的真理即刻拒之门外。他们常常还是会把某项发现收留进来的。只是绝不会跑上前去迎接它。

> 他们的手上不见仓促，
 

 脚下也不见奔忙。

===Original===
Like smells that originate in the kitchen and work their way up by degrees to the best bedroom the new revelations of war ascended slowly from floor to floor of the hierarchy. They did arrive in the end. The Canadians got, in the end, a rifle not too great and good for business. By the third year of the war the infantry schools at the base were teaching drafts from home to use the bayonet as troops in the line had taught themselves to use it in the second. The frowning down of the tanks can hardly have lasted a year. The Stokes gun was not blackballed for good. It was not for all time, but only for what seemed to them like an age, that our troops had to keep off the well-found enemy bomber with bombs that they made of old jam tins, wire, a little guncotton, a little time fuse, and some bits of sharp stone, old iron, or anything hard that was lying about, with earth to fill in; the higher powers did the thing well in the end; they came down handsomely at last; in the next life the Mills bomb alone should be good for at least a night out once a year on an iceberg to some War Office brave who would not see it killed in the cradle.

===Chinese===
战争的新启示，就像始于厨房的气味，一级一级慢慢渗到最好的卧室里去，沿着等级体系的楼层缓缓上升。它们终究是抵达了的。加拿大人最终领到了一支不至于高贵得不屑干粗活的步枪。到大战第三年，后方基地的步兵学校已经在教国内开来的补充兵（drafts）按前线部队第二年自我教会的路数使用刺刀了。对坦克怒目而视的抵制，估计没能撑满一年。斯托克斯炮（Stokes gun）也没有被一票否决到底。我们的部队不得不拿自制的炸弹去抵挡装备齐全的敌军投弹手——旧果酱罐、铁丝、一点强棉药、一点导火索，再拾些尖石、废铁或手边任何坚硬之物装进罐里，填上土——这样的日子并非永久，只是在他们看来漫如一世；上头的权柄最终把事情办好了；他们最后还是大方地落了地；单凭米尔斯手榴弹（Mills bomb）这一桩，来世里便足够让某位当年不许它夭折于摇篮的陆军部（War Office）好汉，每年受赏一夜冰山夜游。

===Original===
And yet authority wore, in the eyes of its troops in the field, an inexpert air—sublime, benevolent, but somehow inexpert. They had begun to notice it even before leaving England. Imagine the headquarters Staff of a district command watching a test for battalion bombing officers and sergeants at the close of a divisional bombing course in 1915: the instructor in charge a quick-witted Regular N.C.O. who has shone at Loos and is now decorated, commissioned, slightly shell-shocked, and sent home to teach, full of the new craft and subtlety of trench war; the pupils all picked for the job and devouringly keen, half of them old cricketers, all able-bodied, and all now able, after hard practice during the course, to drop a bomb on to any desired square yard within thirty-five yards of their stance; and then the Staff, tropically dazzling in their red and gold, august beyond words, but genial, benign, encouraging, only too ready to praise things that they would see to be easy if only they knew more about them and were not like middle-aged mothers watching their offspring at football—so a profane bombing sergeant describes them that night to his mess.

===Chinese===
然而，在战场上它的部队眼里，权威却带着一副外行的气象——崇高、仁善，可不知怎么就是外行。他们还没离开英国，就开始注意到这一点了。试想 1915 年一期师级投弹训练班结业时，一个军区司令部的参谋们检阅营级投弹军官与军士考核的场面：主考教官是个机灵的正规军军士（N.C.O.），曾在卢斯大放异彩，如今挂了勋章、受了衔、脑震荡稍留余波，奉调回国执教，满脑子战壕战的新手艺与新机微；学员全是拣选出来干这一行的，热切得如饥似渴，半数是老板球手，个个身强力壮，而且经过训练班里的苦练，如今个个都能把炸弹投到自己站位三十五码之内任何指定的一平方码上；然后是那些参谋，红底金饰、耀眼如热带，庄严得无以言表，却又和蔼、慈祥、鼓舞人心，随时乐于夸奖那些只要多懂一点便会看出何其容易的事——活像一群在场边看儿子踢球的中年母亲——当晚，一位言语不敬的投弹军士便这样向他的军士食堂描述他们。

### V / 五

===Original===
“Your Old Army’s all bloody born amatoors,” an Australian of ripe war experience remarked with some frankness in France. His immediate occasion for generalizing so rashly was somebody’s slip in passing certain grenades as good for field use. Most of our hand and rifle grenades undoubtedly were. If anything they were too fine for it, too fit to beautify drawing-rooms as well. One objet d’art, a delight to the eye, was said to cost its country one pound five as against the two francs for which France was composing an angel of death less pretty but equally virtuous. Still, ours would kill, if you had the heart to break up an object so fair. But the batch that made the Australian blaspheme, though good in design, were mismade. They were made as if the people who made them had not guessed what they were for.

===Chinese===
「你们那支旧陆军（Old Army），全是他娘生就的臭外行」——一位战争阅历圆熟的澳大利亚人在法国如此相当坦率地评道。他这般轻率地一概而论，直接的缘起是有人失了手，把某批手榴弹验收为合乎野战之用。我们的手投与枪发榴弹，大多数无疑确是如此。真要说有什么毛病，倒是太精良了，精良到足以为客厅增色。有一件艺术品（objet d'art），悦目赏心，据说花费了它的祖国一英镑五先令；而法兰西只消两法郎，便在装配一位没那么标致、杀伤却同样过硬的死亡天使。不过，只要你狠得下心打碎这么一件美物，我们的也能杀人。只是那批惹得澳大利亚人咒天骂地的榴弹，设计虽好，制作却坏了事。它们被造得仿佛造它们的人根本不曾猜到它们的用途。

===Original===
As you know, the outside of most kinds of grenade is a thick metal case serrated with deeply-cut lines that cross each other like those more shallow sunk lines on crocodile-leather, only at right angles. These lines of weakness, cut into the metal, mark out almost the whole of the case into little squares standing up in relief, sixteen or thirty-two or forty-eight or seventy-two according to type. The burst, if all goes well, attacks the lines of weakness, cuts them right through, and so disperses all the little squares of brass, cast-iron, or steel radially as flying bits of shrapnel. What led the Australian to sin was that this batch had come out to France with their lines of weakness cut not half as deep as they should be. The burst only ripped the case open without breaking it up. It had been lovely in life, and in death it was not divided. It just gave a jump, the length of a frog’s, and presented the foe with a cheap good souvenir, reassuring besides.

===Chinese===
如你所知，大多数种类的榴弹，外壳是一只厚金属壳，壳上刻着深切的槽线，纵横交错，恰似鳄鱼皮上那些较浅的压纹，只不过成了直角。这些刻入金属的脆弱线，把几乎整个壳面划分成一颗颗凸起的小方块，按型号之别，或十六，或三十二，或四十八，或七十二。爆炸一旦一切顺遂，便攻向这些脆弱线，将它们彻底切断，于是把黄铜、铸铁或钢铁的小方块呈放射状抛散开去，化作飞溅的弹片。害澳大利亚人造次的缘由在于：这一批运抵法国时，脆弱线刻得连应有深度的一半都不到。爆炸只是把外壳撕裂了口，却未能把它炸碎。它在生时何其可爱，在死时却不分离。它只蹦了一跳——青蛙那么长的一跳——便奉送了敌人一件价廉物美的纪念品，而且还令人安心。

===Original===
There must have been a good many thousands of these. They may have done good—perhaps won a good-conduct mark to some War Office hero for rushing them out in good time to the front; perhaps assisted some politician to feel that he was riding a whirlwind and directing a storm, solving munition crises and winning the war. All human happiness counts. In France, if the physical effects of their detonation were poor, the moral reverberations which followed were lively. A bombing sergeant, sent down the line for a rest and instructing new drafts in a hollow among the sand dunes at Étaples well out of authority’s hearing, would start his lecture by holding one of them up and saying: “This ’ere, men, is a damn bad grenade. But it’s all that the bloody tailors give you to work with. So just pay attention to me.” And then he would go on to pour out his cornucopia of tips, fruits of empiric research, for doing what somebody’s slackness or folly had made it so much less easy to do.

===Chinese===
这样的东西，想必总有几千枚之数。它们或许也立过功——也许替某位陆军部英雄挣得了一枚品行优良章，因为他把它们及时抢运到了前线；也许帮某位政客找着了驾驭旋风、指挥风暴的感觉，化解了军火危机，赢得了战争。人间一切幸福都作数。在法国，它们爆炸的物理效果纵然可怜，随之而来的精神回响却是热闹的。一位投弹军士被调往后方休整，在埃塔普勒（Étaples）沙丘间一处权威听觉之外的洼地里给新补充兵上课，会先举起其中一枚开讲：「这玩意儿，弟兄们，是颗烂透了的榴弹。可那帮该死的裁缝就发这个给你们使。所以都给我好好听着。」然后他便倾倒出他那丰饶角里的一串窍门——经验研究的果实——好去办成那件被某人的懈怠或愚蠢弄得难上加难的事。

### VI / 六

===Original===
Whenever you passed from east to west across the British zone during the war you would find somebody saying with fervour that somebody else, a little more to the west and a little higher in rank, had not even learnt his job well enough to keep out of the way. Subalterns, who by some odd arrangement of flukes had come through our attacks on the Somme in 1916 and in Artois and Flanders next year, would hoot at the notion—it had a vogue with part of the Staff in a tranquil far west—that the way to get on with the war was to raise a more specific thirst for blood in the private. Battalion commanders did not soon tire of telling how in the busiest days of big battles the unseen powers would pester them for instant returns of the number of shovels they had, or of the number of men who in civil life had been fitters, or had been moulders. Brigadiers would savagely wonder aloud whether it ever occurred to a higher command that to make little attack after little attack, each on a narrow, one-brigade front, was merely to ask to have each attack squashed flat in its turn by a fan-like convergence of fire from the enemy’s guns on both flanks, not to speak of supports. The day the bad turn came for us, in the two-chaptered battle of Cambrai, an officer on the Staff of one of the worst-hit divisions observed: “Our attitude is just ‘we told you so.’ ” When the good turn in the war had come the next summer there was a day, not so good as the rest, when two squadrons of horse were sent to charge, in column, up a straight, treeless rising road for half a mile and take a little wood at the top. There were many machine-guns in the wood—how could there not have been?—and the whole air sang with warnings of that. No horse or man either got to the wood or came back. They were all in a few seconds lying in the white dust, almost in the order they rode in, the officer in command a little ahead of the rest. It looked, in its formal completeness, like a thing acted, a cinema play showing a part of Sennacherib’s army on which the angel had breathed. On the road back from the place I met a corps commander—a great man at his work. When he heard his face crumpled up for a moment—he was a softhearted man. “Another of those damned cavalry follies!” he growled. His voice had the scorn that the man who is versed in today’s practice feels for the men who still move among yesterday’s theories. So it was, from east to west, all the way.

===Chinese===
战争期间，只要你自东向西横穿英军防区，总会撞见有人在热烈地断言：另外某人——位置再偏西一点、军衔再高一点的人——连本行都没学到家，家到懂得别来碍事的地步。那些凭着某种离奇的侥幸，活过了我们 1916 年索姆河攻势、次年又活过阿图瓦（Artois）与佛兰德攻势的初级军官（subaltern）们，会对一种念头嗤之以鼻——这念头在太平无事的极西后方一部分参谋中间颇为时兴——以为推进战争的妙法，是在列兵身上唤起一种更明确的嗜血之渴。营长们久说不厌的是：大会战最吃紧的那些日子里，那些看不见的权柄会如何纠缠他们，索要即时报表，问他们现有铁锹几把，或者问他们中间当过装配工的几人、当过翻砂工的几人。旅长们会凶狠地出声纳闷：高层到底想没想过，小攻击接二连三，每一次都摆在窄窄的一个旅正面上，无异于主动请求每一次攻击都轮番被敌军两侧炮火（还不算其支援火力）的扇形汇射碾成齑粉。我们遭殃的那一天，在分作上下两章的康布雷（Cambrai）之战中，一个损失最重的师的参谋部里有位军官评论道：「我们的态度就是：『我们早说过了。』」来年夏天战运转好之后，有一天——那天不如其余日子来得好——两个骑兵中队奉命成纵队沿一条笔直无树的坡路冲上半英里，夺取坡顶一座小树林。林中机枪甚多——怎么可能不多呢？——满天的空气都为此嗡嗡示警。结果无论马还是人，没有一个抵达树林，也没有一个回来。几秒钟之内，他们尽数躺倒在白色的尘土里，队形几乎仍是骑行时的队形，指挥官稍稍冲在最前。那场面以其形式上的完满，像一出演出来的戏，一部影片，演的是西拿基立（Sennacherib）大军中被天使吹过一口气的那一部分。从那地方回来的路上，我遇见一位军长——于本行是位了不起的人物。他听了，脸霎时皱缩了一瞬——他是个心软的人。「又是那种该死的骑兵蠢事！」他低吼道。他的嗓音里含着那种轻蔑：精熟今日实践之人，对仍旧活动在昨日理论中人的轻蔑。自东徂西，一路皆然。

===Original===
All the wise men were not in the east. It was the fault of the war, the outlandish, innovatory war that did not conform to the proper textbooks as it ought to have done; an unimagined war of flankless armies scratching each other’s faces across an endless thorn hedge, not dreamt of in Staff College philosophy; a war that was always putting out of date the best that had been known and thought and invented, always sending everyone to school again; unkind, above all, to us who, if well-to-do, bring up our young to have a proper respect for the past and to feel that if yesterday’s parasol will not keep out the rain of today, then it ought to, and no one can blame them for using it.

===Chinese===
聪明人并不全在东边。要怪只能怪这场战争——这场荒诞不经、标新立异的战争，没有照它本该的那样去遵从正规的教科书；一场无人想象过的战争：两支没有侧翼的大军，隔着一道无边的荆棘篱对挠彼此的脸皮，参谋学院的哲学里连梦也不曾梦到过它；一场总是把已知、已思、已发明的精华统统作废、总是把每个人重新打发回学堂的战争；尤其是对我们这种人刻薄无礼——我们这些家境宽裕的，教养子弟要对过去怀有恰如其分的敬意，要让他们觉得：倘若昨天的阳伞挡不住今天的雨，那它也理应挡得住，谁也不能怪他们打着它出门。

### VII / 七

===Original===
Yet the men in the line talked, and so did the subalterns, most of whom had been in the ranks, now that the war ran into years. Soldiers have endless occasions for talk. Being seldom alone, and having to hold their tongues sometimes, they talk all the time that they can. And most of their talk was sour and scornful. Ever since their enlistment there had been running down in them one of the springs of health in the life of a country. An unprecedented number of the most healthy, high-spirited, and nationally valuable Englishmen in the prime of life were telling one another that, among those whom they had hitherto taken more or less completely on trust as their “betters,” things were going on which must make the war harder for us to win; while they, the common people, cared with all their hearts about saving Belgium and France, those betters, so placed that they could do more to that end if they would, seemed to be caring, on the whole, less—shouting and gesticulating enough, but ready to give up less of what was pleasant and to do less of what was hard, and perhaps not able to do much at their best. Colonel Repington’s friends, with their scented baths, their prime vintages, and their mutinous chatter, were not actually seen; but there was a bad smell about; the air stank of bad work in high places.

===Chinese===
然而，前线的人们一直在讲，初级军官们也在讲——战争拖到以年计之后，他们中间大多数本来就当过列兵。士兵们有无数说话的机会。他们难得独处，又时时不得不缄口，于是能说的时候便说个不停。而他们的话，大都是酸苦而轻蔑的。自应征之日起，一个国家生命中的一股健康泉源，便在他们体内流逝耗损。数量空前的英格兰人——最健康、最昂扬、于国家最有价值、正当盛年——正在彼此相告：在那些他们此前或多或少全然信以为真的「上等人」中间，正进行着种种必使战争更难打赢的事；他们这些平民一心一意想拯救比利时与法兰西，那些上等人——地位摆在那里，只要愿意，本可以为此多所作为——总体上看反倒不那么上心：呐喊与挥拳倒尽有余，可要舍弃的安逸更少，要担当的辛苦也更少，或许尽力而为也成不了什么大器。雷平顿上校（Colonel Repington）的那些朋友们，连同他们的香氛浴、陈年佳酿与犯上的清谈，倒没有人当真撞见过；可是四下里有一股坏味道；空气里臭着高层劣迹的气味。

===Original===
Most of our N.C.O.’s and men in the field had come to feel that it was left to them and to the soundest regimental officers to pull the foundered rulers of England and heads of the army through the scrape. They assumed now that while they were doing this job they must expect to be crawled upon by all the vermin bred in the dark places of a rich country vulgarly governed. They were well on their guard by this time against expressing any thoroughgoing faith in anything or anybody, or incurring any suspicion of dreaming that such a faith was likely to animate others; a man was a fool if he imagined that anyone set over him was not looking after number one; the patriotism of the press was bunkum, screening all sorts of queer games; the eloquence of patriotic orators was just a smoke barrage to cover their little manoeuvres against one another; the red tabs of the Staff were the “Red Badge of Funk”; a hospital ward full of sick men would exchange, when left to themselves, vitriolic surmises about the extravagant pay that the nurses were probably getting, and go on to suggest what vast profits the Y.M.C.A. must be making out of its huts. Wherever the contrary had not been proved to their own senses, the slacking, self-seeking and shirking that had muddled and spoilt their own training for war until they were put, half-trained, in the hottest of the fire must be assumed to be in authority everywhere.

===Chinese===
我们在战场上的大多数军士（N.C.O.）与士兵，已经渐渐觉得：把英格兰这些搁了浅的统治者与陆军首脑们拖出泥潭的活计，就落在他们和最扎实的团级军官身上了。他们如今认定，在干这件活计的同时，还得料到会被一个治理得俗不可耐的富国在暗处孳生的一切虫虱爬上身来。这时的他们已经严加提防，绝不对任何事物、任何人表达全盘的信仰，也绝不惹人疑心，仿佛梦想这种信仰会感召旁人；谁要是以为他上头的哪一位不是先顾自己（number one），谁就是傻子；报界的爱国主义是空话（bunkum），遮掩着种种古怪勾当；爱国演说家们的雄辩，不过是一道烟幕，掩护他们彼此之间的小小运作；参谋们的红领章是「怯懦红徽章」（Red Badge of Funk）；满病房的病号一旦无人旁听，便会交换刻毒的揣测，猜护士们多半领着离谱的高薪，进而推想基督教青年会（Y.M.C.A.）靠那些小屋该赚了多大的利。凡是他们亲身的感官未曾证明相反的地方，那种曾经搅乱并败坏他们战前训练、害得他们半生不熟就被投进最烈火线的懈怠、营私与逃责，就必须推定为在各处权位上照旧存在。

===Original===
Long ago, perhaps, the commons of England may, on the whole, have accepted the view that while they were the fists of her army there was a strong brain somewhere behind, as good at its job as the fists were at theirs; that above them, using them for the best, mind was enthroned, mind the deviser, adapter, foreseer, the finder of ever new means to new ends, mind which knew better than fists, and from which, in any time of trial, all good counsels and provident works were sure to proceed. If so, the faith of the general mass of the English common people in any such division of functions was now pretty near its last kick. The lions felt they had found out the asses. They would not try to throw off the lead of the asses just then: you cannot reorganize a fire-brigade in the midst of a fire. That had to wait. They worked grimly on at the job of the moment, resigned for the present to seeing all the things go ill which the great ones of their world ought to have caused to go well. For themselves, in each of their units, they saw what was coming. Some day soon they would be put into an attack and would come out with half their numbers or, perhaps, two-thirds, and nothing gained for England, perhaps because some old Regular in his youth had preferred playing polo to learning his job. The rest would be brought up to strength with half-trained drafts and then put in again, and the process would go on over and over again until our commanders learnt war, and then perhaps we might win, if any of us were left.

===Chinese===
很久以前，英格兰的平民或许大体上接受过这样的见解：他们是英格兰军队的拳头，而在这只拳头后面的什么地方，有一个强有力的头脑，于其本行之善，一如拳头于它们本行之善；在他们之上、善用着他们的，是端坐着的心智——心智这谋划者、变通者、先见者，这永远能为新目标找到新手段的发现者，这比拳头懂得更多的心智，而且在任何考验临头之时，一切良谋与远虑之举必定自它而出。倘若果真如此，那么英格兰平民大众对这类职能分工的信仰，如今已差不多只剩最后一蹬腿了。狮子们觉得，他们算是把驴子们瞧透了。只是他们当时还不会试图甩脱驴子的引导：火灾正烧之际，你没法重组消防队。那只好等待。他们沉着脸干着眼下的活计，暂且认了命，眼看着他们的世界里那些大人物本该办好、却偏偏办糟的种种。至于他们自己，在各自的部队里，他们看得到来的是什么。不久的某一天，他们会被投进一场攻击，出来时人数只剩一半，或者三分之二吧，而英格兰一无所得——也许就因为某位老正规军当年年轻时宁可打马球，不肯学本行。余下的人会被半生不熟的补充兵补足建制，然后再投进去，如此周而复始，直到我们的指挥官们学会战争，那时节没准我们能赢——如果我们中间还有人剩下的话。

===Original===
While so many things were shaken one thing that held fast was the men’s will to win. It may have changed from the first lyric-hearted enthusiasm. But it was a dour and inveterate will. At the worst most of the men fully meant to go down killing for all they were worth. And there was just a hope that in Germany, too, such default as they saw on our side was the rule; it was, perhaps, a disease of all armies and countries, not of ours alone; there might thus be a chance for us still. On that chance they still worked away with a sullen ardour that no muddling or sloth in high places could wholly damp down. Many of them were like children clinging with a cross crankiness to a hobby of learning to read in a school where some of the teachers were good, but some could not read themselves, and others could read but preferred other occupations to teaching.

All were so deeply absorbed in winning that no practical upshot of all their new thoughts about England’s diseases was yet, as far as I could perceive, taking shape in their minds. On that side their mood was merely one of postponement, somewhat menacing in its form, but still postponement. “We’ve *got* to win first. Then—? But we’ve got to win first.” They were almost exactly the words in which most German prisoners, till 1918, expressed their own feeling about the old rulers of Germany.

===Chinese===
尽管这么多东西都动摇了，有一样东西却岿然不动：士兵们的求胜意志。它或许已不复当初那副抒情诗心肠的热忱。但它是一种顽硬而根深蒂固的意志。就算在最坏的时候，大多数士兵也抱定主意：倒也要倒着杀敌，尽力杀个够。而且他们也还存着一线希望：在德国，他们在我们这一侧见到的那种失职，兴许也是常态；这或许是所有军队、所有国家的通病，不止我们一家；如此说来，我们或许还有一线生机。凭着这一线生机，他们仍旧怀着阴郁的热忱苦干下去——高层再怎么昏聩怠惰，也没法把它整个浇熄。他们中间的许多人，就像一些闹着别扭、执拗不堪的孩子，在一所学校里死抱着识字的爱好不放：那学校里有些老师是好的，可有些自己都不识字，还有些识得字，却宁可去干别的营生，不肯教书。

人人都一门心思扑在打赢上，因此就我所察觉，关于英格兰种种病症的这些新念头，还没有任何实际的结局在他们心里成形。在这一面，他们的心绪不过是一种延期——形态上颇带威胁，但终究是延期。「我们*非*先打赢不可。然后呢——？可我们非先打赢不可。」直到 1918 年，大多数德国战俘谈到他们对德国旧统治者的感受时，用的几乎正是这一套字眼。

## IX Autumn Comes / 九 秋来了

### I / 一

===Original===
In the autumn of 1917 the war entered into an autumn, or late middle-age, of its own. “Your young men,” we are told, “shall see visions, and your old men shall dream dreams.” The same with whole armies. But middle-aged armies or men may not have the mists of either morning or evening to charm them. So they may feel like Corot, when he had painted away, in a trance of delight, till the last vapour of dawn was dried up by the sun; then he said, “You can see everything now. Nothing is left,” and knocked off work for the day. There was no knocking off for the army. But that feeling had come. A high time was over, a great light was out; our eyes had lost the use of something, either an odd penetration that they had had for a while, or else an odd web that had been woven across them, shutting only ugliness out.
The feeling was apt to come on pretty strong if you lived at the time on the top of the little hill of Cassel, west of Ypres. The Second Army’s Headquarters were there. You might, as some Staff duty blew you about the war zone, be watching at daybreak one of that autumn’s many dour bouts of attrition under the Passchendale Ridge, in the mud, and come back, the same afternoon, to sit in an ancient garden hung on the slope of the hill, where a great many pears were yellowing on the wall and sunflowers gazing fixedly into the sun that was now failing them. All the corn of French Flanders lay cut on the brown plain under your eyes, from Dunkirk, with its shimmering dunes and the glare on the sea, to the forested hills north of Arras. Everywhere lustre, reverie, stillness; the sinking hum of old bees, successful in life and now rather tired; the many windmills fallen motionless, the aureate light musing over the aureate harvest; out in the east the broken white stalks of Poperinghe’s towers pensive in haze; and, behind and about you, the tiny hill city, itself in its distant youth the name-giver and prize of three mighty battles that do not matter much now. All these images or seats of outlived ardour, mellowed now with the acquiescence of time in the slowing down of some passionate stir in the sap of a plant or the spirit of insects or men, joined to work on you quietly. There, where the earth and the year were taking so calmly the end of all the grand racket that they had made in their prime, why not come off the high horse that we, too, in that ingenuous season, had ridden so hard? It was not now as it had been of yore. And why pretend that it was?

===Chinese===
1917 年秋，战争本身也走入了它自己的秋天，或者说迟来的中年。「你们的少年人」——有人这样告诉我们——「要见异象，你们的老年人要做异梦。」整支整支的军队也是一样。只是人到中年的军队或人，未必再有清晨或黄昏的薄雾来蛊惑他们。于是他们便可能生出柯罗（Corot）那样的感觉：他在欢愉的出神之中一路画下去，直到黎明的最后一缕水汽被太阳晒干；这时他说：「现在什么都看得见了。什么也没有剩下。」于是当天收工。军队却是无从收工的。但那种感觉已经来了。一段意气昂扬的时光结束了，一盏巨灯熄灭了；我们的眼睛失去了某样东西的功用——要么是它们一度拥有过一阵的一种奇异的透视力，要么是一层一度织在眼前的奇异的网，而那网，原是只把丑陋挡在外面的。
那时候你若住在伊普尔（Ypres）以西卡塞尔（Cassel）小山的山顶上，这种感觉很容易来得相当强烈。第二集团军（Second Army）的司令部就设在那里。你也许因为某项参谋勤务而在战区里被吹得四处打转，拂晓时分还在帕斯尚尔（Passchendaele）岭下的泥泞中，观看那年秋天许多场顽强的消耗战中的一场；当天下午却又回到悬在山坡上的一座古园里坐一坐，园里墙上一串串梨子正在转黄，向日葵定定地凝望着那如今已暖不了它们的太阳。法属佛兰德（French Flanders）的全部庄稼都收割完毕，平铺在你眼前的褐色原野上，从敦刻尔克（Dunkirk）——那边沙丘微光闪烁、海面上白光耀眼——一直铺到阿拉斯（Arras）以北的林木丘陵。到处是光泽、遐想与静谧；老蜜蜂的嗡声渐渐低沉，它们一生顺遂，如今颇有倦意；许多风车一动不动，金色的光在金色的收获上冥想；东面远处，波珀林厄（Poperinghe）塔楼残断的白影在薄霭中黯然出神；而在你身后与四周，是这座小小的山城——它在遥远的青年时代，曾是三场大战役的命名者与战利品，而那三场战役如今已无足轻重。所有这些热情已逝的意象或旧址，如今因时间默许了植物汁液或昆虫与人的精神里某种炽热骚动的缓缓平息，而变得温醇，它们合起来悄悄地在你身上发挥作用。在那里，大地与时令正如此平静地领受它们盛年时那场盛大喧闹的终结；我们自己又何妨走下那匹在天真的季节里曾被我们狠命骑过的高头大马呢？如今已不复当年了。又何必装作仍是当年呢？

### II / 二

===Original===
One leaf that had gone pretty yellow by now was the hope of perfect victory﻿—swift, unsoured, unruinous, knightly: St. George’s over the dragon, David’s over Goliath. Some people at home seem to be still clinging hard to that first pretty vision of us as a gifted, lithe, wise little Jack fighting down an unwieldy, dastardly giant. But troops in the field become realists. Ours had seen their side visibly swelling for more than two years, till Jack had become a heavier weight than the giant and yet could not finish him off. We knew that our allies and we outnumbered the Germans and theirs. We knew we were just as well armed. We had seen Germans advancing under our fire and made no mistake about what they were worth. Our first vision of victory had gone the way of its frail sister dream of a perfect Allied comradeship. French soldiers sneered at British now, and British at French. Both had the same derisive note in the voice when they named the “Brav’ Belges.” Canadians and Australians had almost ceased to take the pains to break it to us gently that they were the “storm troops,” the men who had to be sent for to do the tough jobs; that, out of all us sorry home troops, only the Guards Division, two kilted divisions and three English ones could be said to know how to fight. “The English let us down again”; “The Tommies gave us a bad flank, as usual”﻿—these were the stirring things you would hear if you called upon an Australian division a few hours after a battle in which the lion had fought by the side of his whelps. Chilly, autumnal things; while you listened, the war was apparelled no longer in the celestial light of its spring.

===Chinese===
到这时已经黄得相当厉害的一片叶子，是对完满胜利的希望——迅速、不带苦涩、不致毁伤、骑士风度的胜利：圣乔治（St. George）之于恶龙，大卫（David）之于歌利亚（Goliath）。国内有些人似乎仍死死抱着最初那个漂亮的幻象：把我们看作一班有天分、矫捷、聪明的小杰克，正在斗垮一个臃肿而卑怯的巨人。但战场上的部队会变成现实主义者。我们的部队两年来眼看着自己一方明显地膨胀起来，直到杰克已比巨人更重，却仍旧了结不了他。我们知道，我们的盟邦加上我们，在人数上压过德国人及其盟友。我们知道我们武装得同样精良。我们见过德国人在我们的炮火之下推进，对他们有多大分量从不错判。我们最初对胜利的想象，已经走上了它那孱弱的姊妹梦——完美的盟军袍泽之义——的同一条下坡路。如今法国兵讥笑英国兵，英国兵也讥笑法国兵。双方提起「勇敢的比利时人」（“Brav’ Belges”）时，嗓音里都带着同一种嘲弄的调子。加拿大人和澳大利亚人则几乎不再费心把话委婉地讲给我们听：他们是「突击部队」（storm troops），遇到硬仗就得派人去请他们出马；在我们这些不成器的英伦本土部队里，只有近卫师（Guards Division）、两个着苏格兰裙的师和三个英格兰师，勉强称得上会打仗。「英国人又坑了我们一回」；「汤米们照例又把我们的一翼给坑了」——一场狮子刚与幼狮们并肩打过的战斗过后几个小时，你若去拜访一个澳大利亚师，听到的便是这类提气的话。都是冷冷的、秋意深浓的话；你听着听着，战争便不再披着它春天的那层天光了。

===Original===
An old Regular colonel, a man who had done all his work upon the Staff, said, at the time, that “the war was settling down to peace conditions.” He meant no bitter epigram. He was indeed unfeignedly glad. The war was ceasing to be, like a fire or shipwreck, a leveller of ranks which, he felt, ought not to be levelled. Those whom God had put asunder it was less recklessly joining together. The first wild generosities were cooling off. Not many peers and heirs-apparent to great wealth were becoming hospital orderlies now. Since the first earthquake and tidal wave the disturbed social waters had pretty well found their old seemly levels again; under conscription the sons of the poor were now making privates; the sons of the well-to-do were making officers; sanity was returning. The Regular had faced and disarmed the invading hordes of 1914. No small feat of audacity, either. Think what the shock must have been﻿—what it would be for any profession, just at the golden prime of rich opportunity and searching test, to be overrun of a sudden by hosts of keen amateurs, many of them quick-witted, possibly critical, some of them the best brains of the country, most of them vulgarly void of the old professional habits of mind, almost indecently ready to use new and outlandish means to the new ends of today.
But now the stir and the peril were over. The Old Army had won. It had scarcely surrendered a single strong point or good billet; Territorials and New Army toiled at the coolie jobs of its household. It had not even been forced, like kings in times of revolution, to make apparent concessions, to water down the pure milk of the word. It had become only the more intensely itself; never in any war had commands been retained so triumphantly in the hands of the cavalry and the Guards, the leaders and symbols of the Old Army resistance to every inroad of mere professional ardour and knowledge and strong, eager brains. When Sir Francis Lloyd relinquished the London District Command a highly composite mess in France discussed possible successors. “Of course,” said a Guards colonel gravely﻿—and he was a guest in the Mess﻿—“the first point is﻿—he *must* be a Guardsman.” Peace conditions returning, you see; the peace frame of mind; the higher commands restored to their ancient status as property, “livings,” perquisites, the bread of the children, not to be given to dogs. At home, too, peace conditions were taking heart to return. The scattered coveys of profiteers and job-hunters, almost alarmed by the first shots of the war, had long since met in security; “depredations as usual” was the word; and the mutual scalping and knifing of politicians had ceased to be shamefaced; who could fairly expect an old Regular Army to practise a more austere virtue than merchant princes and statesmen?

===Chinese===
一位老正规军（Regular）上校——此公的全部职务生涯都在参谋系统里度过——当时说：「战争正在安定到和平条件上去。」他并不是要说什么刻薄的警句。他是真心欢喜。战争正在不再像一场大火或一场海难那样，充当等级的碾平器——而那些等级，依他看来，本是不该被碾平的。那些上帝使之分离的人，它如今不那么鲁莽地撮合了。最初那种狂热的慷慨正在冷却。如今已没有多少勋贵与豪富的继承人在做医院勤务员了。经过第一场地震与海啸，被搅乱的社会之水已相当妥帖地找回了旧的体面水位；在征兵制之下，穷人的儿子如今当列兵，富家子弟当军官；理智正在回归。正规军曾迎面顶住 1914 年入侵的汹汹人潮，并缴了他们的械。这也绝非一桩小小的胆略之功。想想那冲击必定何等巨大——对任何一个行当来说，正当机遇丰盈、考验严苛的黄金盛年，忽然被大队热切的业余爱好者所淹没；他们许多人才思敏捷、可能还挑剔多讥，有些是全国最好的头脑，大多数则粗陋地全无旧式行当的心智习惯，而且几乎不知避忌地随时准备用新奇古怪的手段，去达成今日的新目标。
但如今骚动与危险都过去了。旧陆军（Old Army）打赢了。它几乎没有放弃过一处坚固据点或一份肥缺；地方军（Territorials）和新军（New Army）在它的家宅里做着苦力杂务。它甚至不曾像革命年代的国王那样，被迫做出表面的让步，给纯正教义之奶掺水。它只是变得愈发纯粹地成了它自己；在任何一场战争里，指挥权都不曾如此凯旋地留在骑兵与近卫军手中——他们是旧陆军抵抗一切单纯的专业热忱、专业知识和强健急切头脑之入侵的领袖与象征。弗朗西斯·劳埃德爵士（Sir Francis Lloyd）卸任伦敦军区司令时，法国这边一个成分极为驳杂的军官食堂（Mess）议论起了可能的继任者。「当然，」一位近卫军上校郑重其事地说——而他还是这个食堂的客人——「头一条就是：他*必须*是近卫军人。」你瞧，和平条件回来了；和平的心态回来了；高级指挥权恢复了它作为财产、「圣俸」（livings）、恩俸的古旧身份——那是儿女的饼，不可丢给狗吃。在国内，和平条件也在壮起胆子回归。四散成小群的发战争财者与钻营官职者——开战最初的几炮几乎把他们吓坏——早已安然会合；通行的话是「劫掠如常」；政客们互相剥头皮、捅刀子也不再脸红；谁还能公道地指望一支老正规军，奉行比商界巨子与政治家更清苦的德行呢？

### III / 三

===Original===
Even in trenches and near them, where most of the health was, time had begun to embrown the verdant soul of the army. “Kitchener’s Army” was changing. Like every volunteer army, his had sifted itself, at its birth, with the only sieve that will riddle out, even roughly, the best men to be near in a fight. Till the first of the pressed men arrived at our front, a sergeant there, when he posted a sentry and left him alone in the dark, could feel about as complete a moral certitude as there is on the earth that the post would not be let down. For, whatever might happen, nothing inside the man could start whispering to him “You never asked to be here! if you do fail, it isn’t your doing.”
Nine out of ten of the conscripts were equally sound. For they would have been volunteers if they could. The tenth was the problem; the more so because there was nothing to tell you which was the tenth and which were the nine. For all that you knew, any man who came out on a draft, from then on, might be the exception, the literal-minded Christian who thought it wicked to kill in a war; or an anti-nationalist zealot who thought us all equally fools, the Germans and us, to be out there pasturing lice, instead of busy at home taking the hide off the bourgeois; or one of those drift wisps of loveless critical mind, attached to no place or people more than another, and just as likely as not to think that the war was our fault and that we ought to be beaten. *Riant avenir!* as a French sergeant said when, in an hour of ease, we were talking over the nature of man, and he told me, in illustration of its diversity, how a section of his had just been enriched with a draft of neurasthenic burglars.

===Chinese===
甚至在战壕里和战壕近旁——军队的元气大半所在——时间也已开始把这支军队青绿的灵魂染作褐色。「基奇纳的新军」（Kitchener's Army）正在改变。像每一支志愿军队一样，它诞生时便用世上唯一那张筛子把自己筛过一遍：这张筛子哪怕只是粗粗地，也能把战斗中最值得托身其侧的好汉筛出来。直到第一批被强征的人抵达我们的前线为止，那边的一个中士，每当他布设一个哨兵、把那人独自留在黑暗中时，都能怀着世上差不多最完全的道德确信，相信那个哨位不会被辜负。因为无论出什么事，那人心里没有任何东西会开始向他窃窃私语：「你又没有自己要求到这儿来！你若是失了职，那也不是你的过错。」
应征兵十个里有九个同样可靠。因为他们若是能够，本来也会去当志愿兵。第十个才是问题；更麻烦的是，毫无办法告诉你哪一个是要紧的第十个、哪些是无碍的九个。从那时起，凡是随一批补充兵来到前线的任何一个人，就你所知都可能是那个例外：也许是个拘泥字面的基督徒，认为在战争中杀人就是作恶；也许是个反民族主义的狂热分子，认为我们大家——德国人和我们——同样都是傻瓜，跑到这里来放养虱子，而不在家里忙着剥资产阶级的皮；也许是那些无爱的批评性头脑的飘絮之一，对哪个地方、哪一国人都不会更亲一层，而且十有八九会认为战争是我们的过错、我们理应被打败。*Riant avenir!*（「好一个光明前程！」）——一位法国中士这样说。那是在一段闲暇里，我们正纵谈人的天性，他为了说明天性之多样，告诉我他的一个班新近得了补充：一整批患神经衰弱的窃贼。

===Original===
