These vulgar considerations of military expediency never seemed to cross the outer rim of the consciousness of many worthies who were engaged at home in shooing the reluctant into the army. If a recalcitrant seemed to be lazy, spiritless, nerveless, if there was every sign of his making a specially worthless and troublesome consumer of rations in a trench, then a burning zeal to inflict this nuisance and danger on some unoffending platoon in France seemed to invade the ordinary military tribunal. Report said that the satisfaction of this impulse was called, by the possessed persons, “giving Haig the men,” and sometimes, with a more pungent irony, “supporting our fellows in the trenches.” *Non tali auxilio nec defensoribus istis.* Australia’s fellows in the trenches were suffered to vote themselves out of the risk of getting any support of the kind. Australia is a democracy. Ours were not asked whether they wanted to see their trenches employed as a penal settlement to which middle-aged moralists in England might deport, among other persons, those whom they felt to be morally the least beautiful of their juniors. So nothing impeded the pious practice of “larning toads to be toads.” For the shirker, the “kicker,” the “lawyer,” for all the types of undesirables that contribute most liberally to the wrinkled appearance of sergeants, those pious men had the nose of collectors. Wherever there was a spare fifty yards of British front to be held, they, if anyone, could find a man likely to go to sleep there on guard, or, in some cyclonic disturbance of spirit, to throw down his rifle and light out for the coast, across country.
Such episodes were reasonably few. The inveterate mercy that guards drunken sailors preserved from the worst disaster the cranks who had made a virtue of giving their country every bad soldier they could. And the abounding mercy of most courts-martial rendered few of the episodes fatal to individual conscripts. Nor, indeed, was the growth in their frequency after conscription wholly due to the more fantastic tricks played before high Heaven by some of the Falstaffs who dealt with the Mouldies, Shadows and Bull-calves. Conscription, in any case, must be dilution. You may get your water more quickly by throwing the filter away, but don’t hope to keep the quality what it was. And the finer a New Army unit had been, to begin with, the swifter the autumnal change. Every first-rate battalion fighting in France or Belgium lost its whole original numbers over and over again. First, because in action it spared itself less than the poor ones; secondly, because the best divisions rightly got the hard jobs. Going out in the late autumn of 1915, a good battalion with normal luck might have nearly half its original volunteer strength left after the Battle of the Somme. Drafts of conscripts would fill up the gap, each draft with a listless or enigmatic one-tenth that volunteering had formerly kept at a distance. The Battle of Arras next spring might leave only twenty percent of the first volunteers, and the autumn battles in Flanders would pretty well finish their business. Seasons returned, but not to that battalion returned the spirit of delight in which it had first learnt to soldier together and set foot together in France and first marched through darkness and ruined villages towards the flaring fairground of the front. While a New Army battalion was still very young, and fully convinced that no crowd of men so good to be with had ever been brought together before, it used to be always saying how it would keep things up after the war. No such genial reunions had ever been held as these were to be. But now the few odd men that are left only write to each other at long intervals, feeling almost as if they were raising their voices in an empty church. One of them asks another has he any idea what the battalion was like after Oppy, or Bourlon Wood, or wherever their own knockout came. Like any other battalion, no doubt﻿—a mere G.C.M. of all conscript battalions; conscription filed down all special features and characters.

===Chinese===
这类俗气的军事权宜的考虑，似乎从不曾越过国内许多体面人物的意识外缘——那些人当时正忙着把不情愿的人轰进军队。倘若某个顽抗者显得懒惰、委靡、没骨气，倘若种种迹象都表明他到了战壕里会成为一个格外无用、格外添乱的口粮消耗者，那么，一股要把这份祸害与危险强加给法国某个无辜的排的热望，似乎就会攫住普通的军事法庭。据传，被这股热望附了身的人，把它的满足唤作「给黑格凑人」（giving Haig the men），有时还带着更辛辣的讽刺，唤作「支援我们战壕里的弟兄们」（supporting our fellows in the trenches）。*Non tali auxilio nec defensoribus istis*（拉丁文：「要这样的援助、这样的卫士何用」）。澳大利亚战壕里的弟兄们获准投票，把自己摘出了接受任何此类支援的风险。澳大利亚是个民主国家。我们的弟兄们却无人问过，是否愿意看到自己的战壕被用作一处流放地，好让英格兰的中年道德家们把那些——除旁人之外——他们觉得在道德上最不体面的后辈放逐过去。于是，再没有什么能妨碍「教蛤蟆学做蛤蟆」（larning toads to be toads）这门虔诚的功课。对于逃避兵役者、「抗命鬼」（kicker）、「咬文嚼字者」（lawyer），对于一切最慷慨地贡献了中士们满脸皱纹的不良分子，那些虔诚之士有着收藏家般的鼻子。只要英军前线还有一段五十码的空当需要人守，他们——而且唯有他们——准能找出这样一个家伙：他很可能在站岗时睡过去，或者，在某场龙卷风般的精神动荡里，扔下步枪、横越乡野直奔海岸而去。
这类事件倒还算相当少。那种素来照拂醉汉与水手的根深蒂固的慈悲，使那些把「把国内所能找到的每一个坏兵都送给国家」奉为美德的人免于最坏的灾难。而大多数军事法庭充溢的宽仁，也使这类事件很少对个别应征兵成为致命之事。而且，说实在的，征兵制之后这类事件频率的增长，也不能全归咎于某些与「霉子」「影子」「牛犊」们（Mouldies, Shadows and Bull-calves）打交道的福斯塔夫们（Falstaffs）在皇天面前耍出的更离奇的花招。无论如何，征兵制必然是掺水。把滤水器扔掉，你可以更快得到水，但别指望水质还保持原样。而一个新军部队当初越是精良，这秋天的变化就来得越快。每一支在法国或比利时作战的一流营，都一遍又一遍折损掉它的全部原班人马。其一，因为作战时它比差的部队更不惜己；其二，因为最好的师理所当然得到最硬的差事。1915 年深秋开拔的部队里，一个运气平常的好营，打过索姆河（Somme）之战后或许还剩下将近一半最初的志愿兵力。一批批应征的补充兵会填上缺口，每一批都带着十分之一的无精打采或莫测高深者，而志愿制从前是把这些人物远远挡开的。来年春天的阿拉斯（Arras）之战可能只剩最初志愿兵的百分之二十，而秋天的佛兰德各役就会差不多办完他们的事了。季节周而复始，但不再回到那个营身上去的，是那股欢欣的精神——想当初，它正是在这股精神里学会了并肩当兵、并肩踏上法国的土地，第一次在黑暗与残破的村庄之间，朝着前线那火光熊熊的游乐场开拔。新军的营还很年轻、还深信从未有过这么好相处的一群人聚在一起的时候，总爱念叨战后要如何把这份情分维持下去；将来的这些重聚，将是空前亲切的盛会。但如今剩下的几个零落旧人，只是隔许久才通一封信，那感觉几乎像是在一座空教堂里提高嗓门说话。其中一个问另一个：你可知道咱们营在奥皮（Oppy）之后、在布尔隆森林（Bourlon Wood）之后、在他们自己挨了那一记重击的地方之后，是什么光景？跟别的任何一个营都差不多，毫无疑问——不过是全体应征营的最大公约数（G.C.M.）罢了；征兵制把一切特色与个性都锉平了。

===Original===
Quick waste and renewal are said to be good for the body; the faster you burn up old tissues, by good sweaty work, the better your health; fresh and superior tissue is added unto you all the more merrily. Capital, too, the economists say, must be swiftly used up and reborn, over and over again, to do the most good that it can. And then there is the case of the phoenix﻿—in fact, of all the birds and all the beasts too, for all evolution would seem to be just the dying of something worse, as fast as it can, in order that something better may live in its place. No need for delay in turning your anthropoid apes into Shakespeares and Newtons.
But what if you found, after all your hard work, that not all the deceased cells of your flesh were replaced by new cells of the sort you would like? If some of your good golden pounds should have perished only that inconvertible paper might live? If out of your phoenix’s ashes only a commonplace rooster should spring? If evolution were guyed and bedevilled into retrovolution, a process by which the fittest must more and more dwindle away and the less fit survive them, and species be not multiplied but made fewer? Something, perhaps, of the sort may go on in the body in its old age, or in roses in autumn. It must go on in a volunteer army when it is becoming an army of conscripts during a war that is highly lethal.

===Chinese===
据说快速的损耗与更新对身体有益；靠畅快的汗水劳作把旧组织烧掉得越快，你的健康就越好；更新鲜、更优良的组织就越是欢欢喜喜地补到你身上。经济学家们也说，资本必须被迅速用尽、再度生成，一遍又一遍，才能尽其最大的功用。然后还有凤凰的事例——其实，一切飞禽走兽的事例莫不如此，因为全部进化看来不过是某种较坏的东西尽其可能快地死去，好让某种较好的东西在原处活下去。把你的类人猿变成莎士比亚们和牛顿们，无须迟延。
可要是你辛辛苦苦一场之后发现，你血肉里死去的细胞并非全都被你喜欢的那一类新细胞替换了呢？要是你那些成色良好的金镑之所以死掉，只是为了不可兑换的纸币得以存活呢？要是你的凤凰的灰烬里只跳出一只平平无奇的公鸡呢？要是进化被人耍弄、蛊惑成了「逆进化」（retrovolution）——一个适者必得日渐凋零、较不适者反而把他们熬死、物种不是增多反而变少的过程呢？诸如此类的事情，或许会在衰老的身体里，或在秋天里的玫瑰身上进行着。而在一支志愿军队于一场高度致命的战争期间变成一支征召军队时，这种事情必定在进行着。

### IV / 四

===Original===
The fall of the leaf had brought, too, a sad shortage of heroes﻿—of highly-placed ones, for, of course, every company had its own, authenticated beyond any proof that crosses or medals could give. A few very old Regular privates would say, “Ah! if we had Buller here!” Sir Redvers Buller has always remained, in lofty disregard of conclusive disproof, the Caesar or Hannibal of the old Regular private, who sets little store by such heroes of Whitehall and Fleet Street as Roberts and Kitchener. But the chiefs of today left men cold, at the best. The name of at least one was a byword. Haig was a name and no more, though a name immune in a mysterious degree from the general scoffing surmise about the demerits of higher commands. Few subalterns or men had seen him. No one knew what he was doing or leaving undone. But some power, not ourselves, making for charity, seemed to recommend him to mercy in everyone’s judgement; as if, from wherever he was, nameless waves of some sort rippled out through an uncharted ether, conveying some virtue exhaled by that winning incarnation of honour, courage, and kindness who, seen and heard in the flesh, made you wish to find in him all other excellent qualities too. The front line gave him all the benefit of every doubt. God only knew, it said, whether he or somebody else would have to answer for Bullecourt and Serre. It might not be he who had left the door lying open, unentered, for two nights and days, when the lions had won the battle of Arras that spring, and the asses had let the victory slip till the Germans crept back in the dark to the fields east of Vimy from which they had fled in despair. But slowness to judge can hardly be called hero-worship: at most, a somewhat sere October phase of that vernal religion.

===Chinese===
落叶还带来了英雄的可悲匮乏——身居高位者的匮乏；因为，当然，每个连队都有自己的英雄，其真实可信，远非十字勋章或奖章所能给出的任何证明可比。少数极老的正规军列兵会说：「啊！要是布勒在这儿就好了！」雷德弗斯·布勒爵士（Sir Redvers Buller）——任凭反证何等确凿，都始终高傲地置之不理——在老正规军列兵心中，至今仍是他们的恺撒或汉尼拔；至于白厅（Whitehall）与舰队街（Fleet Street）捧出来的罗伯茨（Roberts）、基奇纳（Kitchener）之流的英雄，老列兵是颇不放在心上的。但今日的统帅们充其量也只能令人无动于衷。至少有一个人的名字成了笑柄。黑格（Haig）只是个名字而已，尽管这个名字在一种神秘的程度上，豁免于世人对高级指挥之缺失的普遍的哂笑猜度。初级军官和士兵见过他的很少。没有人知道他在做着什么、又搁着什么没做。但某种不属于我们自身、致力于慈善的力量，似乎在每个人的评判里都替他说情；仿佛无论他身在何处，都有某种无名的波纹从未经测绘的以太里荡漾而出，传递着某种德性——那是荣誉、勇气与仁慈的一个动人化身所呼出的气息；而这样一个化身，你亲眼见到、亲耳听到时，会不由得希望在他身上也找到一切别的优秀品质。前线把每一个疑点的利益都给了他。前线说，只有上帝知道，布勒库尔（Bullecourt）和塞尔（Serre）将来要由他还是由别的什么人来负责。那年春天，狮子们打赢了阿拉斯之战，驴子们却任凭胜利溜走，直到德国人趁黑爬回他们曾绝望弃守的维米（Vimy）以东的田野——让一扇门那样敞着、两天两夜无人踏进，未必就是他干的事。可是，判断上的迟缓很难称为英雄崇拜：至多不过是那种新春宗教的一个相当枯槁的十月阶段罢了。

===Original===
One of the heavenly things on which the New Army had almost counted, in its green faith, was that our higher commands would have genius. Of course, we had no right to do it. No X has any right to ask of Y that Y shall be Alexander the Great or Bach or Rembrandt or Garrick, or any kind of demonic first-rater. As reasonably send precepts to the Leviathan to come ashore. Yet we had indulged that insane expectation, just as we had taken it for granted that this time the nation would be as one man, and nobody “out to do a bit for himself on the quiet.” And now behold the falling leaf and no Leviathan coming ashore in response to our May Day desires.
Certainly other things, highly respectable, came. The Second Army Staff’s direction of that autumn’s almost continuous battles was of a competence passing all British precedents. Leapfrogging waves of assault, box barrages, creeping barrages, actions, interactions, and counteractions were timed and concerted as no Staff of ours had done it before. The intricate dance which has to go on behind a crowded battle front, so that columns moving east and west and columns moving north and south shall not coincide at cross roads, was danced with the circumstantial precision of the best ballets. An officer cast away somewhere in charge of a wayside smithy for patching up chipped guns felt that there was a power perched on the top of the hill at Cassel which smelt out a bit of good work, or of bad, wherever anyone did it. Sense, keenness, sympathy, resolution, exactness﻿—all the good things abode in that eyrie which have to be in attendance before genius can bring off its marvels; every chamber swept and garnished, and yet﻿—.

===Chinese===
新军在它青涩的信仰里几乎笃信的天赐之事之一，便是我们的高级指挥将会拥有天才。当然，我们并无权利这样指望。任何X都无权要求Y成为亚历山大大帝（Alexander the Great）、巴赫（Bach）、伦勃朗（Rembrandt）或加里克（Garrick），或任何一种鬼才型的第一流人物。这同给利维坦（Leviathan）下训谕、叫它上岸来一样合乎情理。然而我们纵容了那疯狂的期待，正如我们曾想当然地以为，这一回全国会万众一心，没有一个人「打算悄悄为自己捞一把」。而如今你看，叶子落了，却没有利维坦响应我们五朔节（May Day）的愿望上岸来。
当然，别的东西也来了，非常可敬的东西。那年秋天几乎连续不断的各场战斗，其指挥出于第二集团军参谋部之手，胜任之高超过了英国的一切先例。逐波跃进的攻击波、箱形弹幕（box barrage）、徐进弹幕（creeping barrage），种种行动、互动与反制，其时刻的排定与相互的协同，是我们的参谋部从未做到过的。在拥挤的战斗正面之后必须不停跳着的那支繁复的舞——好让向东向西开进的纵队与向北向南开进的纵队不在十字路口撞在一起——跳得像最好的芭蕾一样细节精确。一位被丢在某处、看管一座修补破损火炮的路边锻场的军官感觉到，卡塞尔山顶上栖着一种力量，无论谁在什么地方干出一件好活或一件坏活，它都嗅得出来。见识、敏锐、同情、决断、精确——天才创造奇迹之前必须到场侍奉的一切好东西，全都栖居在那个高巢里；每一个房间都打扫过、装点过，然而——。

===Original===
Foch tells us what he thinks Napoleon might have said to the Allied commands if he could have risen in our black times from the dead. “What cards you people have!” he would have said, “and how little you do with them! Look!” And then, Foch thinks, within a month or two he “would have rearranged everything, gone about it all in some new way, thrown out the enemy’s plans and quite crushed him.” That “some new way” was not fated to come. The spark refused to fall, the divine accident would not happen. How could it? you ask with some reason. Had not trench warfare reached an impasse? Yes; there is always an impasse before genius shows a way through. Music on keyboards had reached an impasse before a person of genius thought of using his thumb as well as his fingers. Well, that was an obvious dodge, you may say, but in Flanders what way through could there have been? The dodge found by genius is always an obvious dodge, afterwards. Till it is found it can as little be stated by us common people as can the words of the poems that Keats might have written if he had lived longer. You would have to become a Keats to do that, and a Napoleon to say how Napoleon would have got through to Bruges in the autumn that seemed so autumnal to us. All that the army knew, as it decreased in the mud, was that no such uncovenanted mercy came to transmute its casualties into the swiftly and richly fruitful ones of a Napoleon, the incidental expenses of some miraculous draught of victory.
Nothing to grouse at in that. The winds of inspiration have to blow the best way they can. Prospero himself could not raise them; how could the likes of us hope to? And yet there had been that illogical hope, almost reliance﻿—part of the high unreason of faith that could move mountains in 1914 and seems to be scarcely able to shift an anthill today.

===Chinese===
福煦（Foch）告诉我们，倘若拿破仑能在我们那些黑暗的年代里从死中复起，对盟军统帅们说几句什么，他会说什么。「你们这些人手里是副多么好的牌啊！」他会这样说，「而你们用这副牌做成的事又多么少啊！瞧着！」然后，福煦认为，不出一两个月，他就会「把一切都重新安排，用某种全新的办法来干，打乱敌人的计划，把他彻底压碎」。那「某种全新的办法」命里注定没有到来。火花不肯落下，神圣的意外不肯发生。这怎么可能呢？你这样问也不无道理。堑壕战不是已经陷入绝境了吗？是的；在天才指出穿过去的路之前，总有一处绝境。键盘上的音乐也曾陷入绝境，直到有一位天才想起，大拇指也可以与手指并用。唔，那是个明摆着的窍门，你会说；可在佛兰德，又能有什么穿过去的路呢？天才找到的窍门，事后看总是明摆着的窍门。在它被找到之前，我们普通人无法把它陈述出来，正如无法说出济慈（Keats）倘若活得长久些、可能写出的那些诗篇的词句。要做到前一件，你得变成一个济慈；要说清拿破仑会怎样在那个在我们看来如此萧瑟的秋天打到布鲁日（Bruges），你得变成一个拿破仑。这支军队在泥泞中日渐减员，它所知道的一切只是：并没有那样的非约之恩（uncovenanted mercy）降临，把它的伤亡变作拿破仑式那种迅速而丰饶多产的伤亡——某一场奇迹般的胜利大收网的区区杂费。
这没有什么可抱怨的。灵感之风只能尽力去吹。连普洛斯彼罗（Prospero）自己也唤不起这风来；我们这号人还能指望什么呢？然而，那种不合逻辑的希望、近乎依赖的确曾有过——它是信仰那崇高的无理性的一部分；那信仰，在 1914 年还能移山，如今似乎连一座蚁冢也难得挪动了。

## X Autumn Tints in Chivalry / 十 骑士精神的秋色

### I / 一

===Original===
In either of two opposite tempers you may carry on war. In one of the two you will want to rate your enemy, all round, as high as you can. You may pursue him down a trench, or he you; but in neither case do you care to have him described by somebody far, far away as a fat little shortsighted scrub. Better let him pass for a paladin. This may at bottom be vanity, sentimentality, all sorts of contemptible things. Let him who knows the heart of man be dogmatic about it. Anyhow, this temper comes, as they would say in Ireland, of decent people. It spoke in Porsena of Clusium’s whimsical prayer that Horatius might swim the Tiber safely; it animates Velasquez’ knightly *Surrender of Breda*; it prompted Lord Roberts’s first words to Cronje when Paardeberg fell﻿—“Sir, you have made a very gallant defence”; it is avowed in a popular descant of Newboldt’s﻿—

> To honour, while you strike him down,
 

 The foe who comes with eager eyes.

===Chinese===
打一场战争，可以怀着两种截然相反的心境。怀着其中之一，你会愿意把你的敌人里里外外估得尽可能地高。你可以沿着战壕追杀他，他也可以追杀你；但无论哪种情形，你都不愿看到他被某个遥远、又遥远的地方的人，描绘成一个矮胖近视的獐头鼠目之徒。不如让他被当作一位游侠骑士看待。这骨子里可能是虚荣，是感伤癖，是种种可鄙的东西。谁自信参得透人心，谁就尽管来对此下断语。反正，用爱尔兰人的说法，这种心境是体面人家出身。它流露在克卢西乌姆的波尔塞纳（Porsena of Clusium）那句异想天开的祷告里——祈愿贺雷修斯（Horatius）能安然游过台伯河；它灌注在委拉斯开兹（Velasquez）那幅骑士风的《布雷达的投降》（*Surrender of Breda*）里；帕德伯格（Paardeberg）城破之时，它化作罗伯茨（Roberts）勋爵对克罗内（Cronje）说的第一句话——「爵士，你打出了一场极其英勇的防御」；它还在纽博尔特（Newboldt）一首广为流传的歌咏里公然自陈——

> 一面将他击倒，一面仍要礼敬
> 这位目光灼灼、欣然来战的敌人。

===Original===
The other temper has its niche in letters, too. There was the man that “wore his dagger in his mouth.” And there was Little Flanigan, the bailiff’s man in Goldsmith’s play. During one of our old wars with France he was always “damning the French, the parley-voos, and all that belonged to them.” “What,” he would ask the company, “makes the bread rising? The parley-voos that devour us. What makes the mutton fivepence a pound? The parley-voos that eat it up. What makes the beer threepence-halfpenny a pot?”

Well, your first aim in war is to hit your enemy hard, and the question may well be quite open﻿—in which of these tempers can he be hit hardest? If, as we hear, a man’s strength be “as the strength of ten because his heart is pure,” possibly it may add a few footpounds to his momentum in an attack if he has kept a clean tongue in his head. And yet the production of heavy woollens in the West Riding, for War Office use, may, for all that we know, have been accelerated by yarns about crucified Canadians and naked bodies of women found in German trenches. There is always so much, so bewilderingly much, to be said on both sides. All I can tell is that during the war the Newbolt spirit seemed, on the whole, to have its chief seat in and near our front line, and thence to die down westward all the way to London. There Little Flanigan was enthroned, and, like Montrose, would bear no rival near his throne, so that a man on leave from our trench system stood in some danger of being regarded as little better than one of the wicked. Anyhow, he was a kind of provincial. Not his will, but that of Flanigan, had to be done. For Flanigan was at the centre of things; he had leisure, or else volubility was his trade; and he had got hold of the megaphones.

===Chinese===
另一种心境，在文学里也有它的一席之地。有那种「嘴里衔着匕首」说话的人。还有小弗拉尼根（Little Flanigan），哥尔德斯密斯（Goldsmith）一出戏里法警的跟班。在我们与法国打的某一场老战争期间，他开口闭口总是「咒骂法国人，咒骂『帕利巫』（parley-voos），咒骂他们的一切」。「诸位，」他会问在场的人，「面包为什么涨价？是帕利巫吞吃我们。羊肉为什么卖五便士一磅？是帕利巫把它吃光。啤酒为什么一罐要三个半便士？」

咳，战争里的头号目标，就是把敌人狠狠打痛；至于怀着这两种心境中的哪一种，能把敌人打得更痛，这问题大可悬而不决。倘若真如我们所闻，一个人的力量「因其心地纯洁而抵得上十个人」，那么，只要他嘴里不曾积攒过脏话，攻击时那股冲劲没准还能多添上几英尺磅的动量。然而，供陆军部（War Office）使用的西雷丁（West Riding）厚重毛织品的生产，就我们所知，也未尝不曾被那些传闻加速过——什么加拿大人被钉上十字架啦，什么德国战壕里发现赤裸女尸啦。两边永远都有那么多话可说，多得令人无所适从。我只能说：战争期间，纽博尔特精神的大本营，总体上看，似乎扎在我们前线之内及近旁，并从那里一路向西衰减，直抵伦敦。在伦敦，小弗拉尼根端坐宝座，而且像蒙特罗斯（Montrose）一样，宝座之侧容不得任何对手，以致一个从我们的战壕体系回国休假的人，颇有些被看得比恶徒好不了多少的危险。总而言之，他算是外省人。在人间得以奉行的，不是他的旨意，而是弗拉尼根的旨意。因为弗拉尼根稳坐在万事的中枢；他有的是闲暇，再不然，滔滔不绝本就是他的行当；而且扩音喇叭全都攥在他手里。

### II / 二

===Original===
In the first months of the war there was any amount of good sportsmanship going; most, of course, among men who had seen already the whites of enemy eyes. I remember the potent emetic effect of Flaniganism upon a little blond Regular subaltern maimed at the first battle of Ypres. “Pretty measly sample of the sin against the Holy Ghost!” the one-legged child grunted savagely, showing a London paper’s comic sketch of a corpulent German running away. The first words I ever heard uttered in palliation of German misdoings in Belgium came from a Regular N.C.O., a Dragoon Guards sergeant, holding forth to a sergeants’ mess behind our line. “We’d have done every damn thing they did,” he averred, “if it had been we.” I thought him rather extravagant, then. Later on, when the long row of hut hospitals, jammed between the Calais-Paris Railway at Étaples and the great reinforcement camp on the sand-hills above it, was badly bombed from the air, even the wrath of the R.A.M.C. against those who had wedged in its wounded and nurses between two staple targets scarcely exceeded that of our Royal Air Force against war correspondents who said the enemy must have done it on purpose.

Airmen, no doubt, or some of them, went to much greater lengths in the chivalrous line than the rest of us. Many things helped them to do it. Combatant flying was still new enough to be almost wholly an officer’s job; the knight took the knocks, and the squire stayed behind and looked after his gear. Air-fighting came to be pretty well the old duel, or else the medieval melee between little picked teams. The clean element, too, may have counted﻿—it always looked a clean job from below, where your airy notions got mixed with trench mud, while the airman seemed like Sylvia in the song, who so excelled “each mortal thing upon the dull earth dwelling.” Whatever the cause, he excelled in his bearing towards enemies, dead or alive. The funeral that he gave to Richthofen in France was one of the few handsome gestures exchanged in the war. And whenever Little Flanigan at home began squealing aloud that we ought to take some of our airmen off fighting and make them bomb German women and children instead, our airmen’s scorn for these ethics of the dirt helped to keep up the flickering hope that the postwar world might not be ignoble.

===Chinese===
战争的头几个月，光明正大的运动家风度所在多有；当然，最多的还是出自那些已经看清过敌人眼白的人。我记得弗拉尼根主义对一名小个子金发的正规军（Regular）初级军官（subaltern）产生的那种烈性催吐作用——他在第一次伊普尔（Ypres）战役中落下了残疾。「好一件寒碜的亵渎圣灵罪标本！」这位独腿的孩子凶声咕哝着，扬起一份伦敦报纸的滑稽画，画上是个抱头鼠窜的肥胖德国人。我平生听到的第一句替德国人在比利时的劣行开脱的话，出自一名正规军军士（N.C.O.）——近卫龙骑兵团（Dragoon Guards）的一位中士——他在我方战线后方的军士食堂（sergeants' mess）里高谈阔论。「他们干的那些屁事，」他断言，「换作是我们，一件不落全会干。」我当时只当他言过其实。后来，当埃塔普勒（Étaples）那一长排棚屋医院——被夹在加来—巴黎铁路与沙丘上方那座庞大的补充兵（drafts）营地之间——遭到空袭重创时，皇家陆军医疗队（R.A.M.C.）对那些把它的伤员和护士塞在两大现成靶标之间的人固然怒火中烧，我们皇家空军（Royal Air Force）对另一班人的怒火却几乎还要更旺——那些随军记者竟说，这番轰炸必定是敌人蓄意为之。

飞行员们——至少他们中的一部分——无疑在骑士精神（chivalry）这条道上，比我们其余的人走得远得多。许多条件帮他们做到了这一点。作战飞行在当时还足够新颖，新到几乎全然是一桩军官的差事；挨打的是骑士，侍从留在后方替他打理甲仗。空战到后来简直成了旧日的决斗，再不然就是两小队精选人马之间的中世纪混战。那洁净的元素，或许也起了作用——从下面望上去，那永远像一桩干净的活计；在下面，你那些轻灵飘逸的念头会跟战壕的烂泥搅作一处，而飞行员却像歌中的西尔维娅（Sylvia），远远胜过「栖居于沉闷大地上的每一凡俗之物」。无论缘由为何，他对待敌人——无论死者生者——的气度都出类拔萃。他在法国为里希特霍芬（Richthofen）举行的葬礼，是整场战争里交换过的寥寥几次漂亮姿态之一。而每当国内的小弗拉尼根开始高声尖叫，主张我们该撤下一些飞行员，不让他们作战，改让他们去轰炸德国的妇孺，我们飞行员对这种龌龊伦理的鄙夷，便有助于维系那一星摇曳不定的希望：战后的世界，或许不至于堕落得卑污。

===Original===
Even on the dull earth it takes time and pains to get a clean-run boy or young man into a mean frame of mind. A fine N.C.O. of the Grenadier Guards was killed near Laventie﻿—no one knows how﻿—while going over to shake hands with the Germans on Christmas morning. “What! not shake on Christmas Day?” He would have thought it poor, sulky fighting. Near Armentières at the Christmas of 1914 an incident happened which seemed quite the natural thing to most soldiers then. On Christmas Eve the Germans lit up their front line with Chinese lanterns. Two British officers thereupon walked some way across No Man’s Land, hailed the enemy’s sentries, and asked for an officer. The German sentries said, “Go back, or we shall have to shoot.” The Englishmen said “Not likely!” advanced to the German wire, and asked again for an officer. The sentries held their fire and sent for an officer. With him the Englishmen made a one-day truce, and on Christmas Day the two sides exchanged cigarettes and played football together. The English intended the truce to end with the day, as agreed, but decided not to shoot next day till the enemy did. Next morning the Germans were still to be seen washing and breakfasting outside their wire; so our men, too, got out of the trench and sat about in the open. One of them, cleaning his rifle, loosed a shot by accident, and an English subaltern went to tell the Germans it had not been fired to kill. The ones he spoke to understood, but as he was walking back a German somewhere wide on a flank fired and hit him in the knee, and he has walked lame ever since. Our men took it that some German sentry had misunderstood our fluke shot. They did not impute dishonour. The air in such places was strangely clean in those distant days. During one of the very few months of open warfare a cavalry private of ours brought in a captive, a gorgeous specimen of the terrific Prussian Uhlan of tradition. “But why didn’t you put your sword through him?” an officer asked, who belonged to the school of Froissart less obviously than the private. “Well, sir,” the captor replied, “the gentleman wasn’t looking.”

===Chinese===
即便在这沉闷的大地上，要把一个身心清白的少年或青年收拾进一副卑劣的心肠，也得费时费力。掷弹兵近卫团（Grenadier Guards）的一名优秀军士在拉旺蒂（Laventie）附近阵亡了——谁也不知道是怎么阵亡的——当时他正要过去同德国人握手，在圣诞节清晨。「什么！圣诞节还不握手吗？」他准会把不握手看成寒酸的、赌气的打法。1914 年圣诞节，在阿尔芒蒂耶尔（Armentières）附近出了一件事，在当时多数士兵看来全然顺理成章。圣诞前夜，德国人用中国灯笼照亮了他们的前沿。于是两名英国军官走过一段无人地带（No Man's Land），向敌方的哨兵呼喊，求见一位军官。德国哨兵说：「回去，不然我们只好开枪。」英国人说：「才不呢！」径直进到德国人的铁丝网前，再次求见军官。哨兵们没有开枪，派人请来一位军官。英国人与他订立了为期一天的休战；圣诞节当天，双方互换香烟，还一道踢了足球。英方本打算照约定当日休战、当日即止，但又决定次日敌人不开枪、我们也不开枪。次日清晨，德国人照旧在铁丝网外洗漱、用早餐；于是我们的人也爬出战壕，三三两两坐在露天里。其中一个擦枪时走了火，一名英国初级军官便过去告诉德国人，那一枪不是存心杀人。他搭话的那些德国人表示明白；可就在他往回走时，侧翼远处不知哪个德国人开了一枪，打中他的膝盖，从此他便瘸着腿走路了。我们的人认定，是哪个德国哨兵误会了我们那记走火的枪。他们并没有把这算作对方背信。在那些已然遥远的岁月里，这种地方的空气洁净得出奇。在寥寥可数的几个运动战月份里，有一回，我们的一个骑兵列兵押来一名俘虏——传统中那凶神恶煞的普鲁士枪骑兵（Uhlan）的一个华丽标本。「可你怎么不拿马刀捅了他？」一名军官问道；论弗鲁瓦萨尔（Froissart）一派的气息，这位军官可远不如那名列兵浓郁。「呃，长官，」押俘的答道，「那位绅士当时没朝这边看。」

### III / 三

===Original===
At no seat of war will you find it quite easy to live up to Flanigan’s standards of hatred towards an enemy. Reaching a front, you find that all you want is just to win the war. Soon you are so taken up with the pursuit of this aim that you are always forgetting to burn with the gem-like flame of pure fury that fires the lionhearted publicist at home.

A soldier might have had the Athanasian ecstasy all right till he reached the firing line. Every individual German had sunk the *Lusitania*; there was none righteous, none. And yet at a front the holy passion began to ooze out at the ends of his fingers. The bottom trouble is that you cannot fight a man in the physical way without somehow touching him. The relation of actual combatants is a personal one﻿—no doubt, a rude, primitive one, but still quite advanced as compared with that between a learned man at Berlin who keeps on saying *Delenda est Britannia!* at the top of his voice and a learned man in London who keeps on saying that every German must have a black heart because Caesar did not conquer Germany as he did Gaul and Britain. Just let the round head of a German appear for a passing second, at long intervals, above a hummock of clay in the middle distance. Before you had made half a dozen sincere efforts to shoot him the fatal germ of human relationship had begun to find a nidus again: he had acquired in your mind the rudiments of a personal individuality. You would go on trying to shoot him with zest﻿—indeed, with a diminished likelihood of missing, for mere hatred is a flustering emotion. And yet the hatred business had started crumbling. There had begun the insidious change that was to send you home, on your first leave, talking unguardedly of “old Fritz” or of “the good old Boche” to the pain of your friends, as if he were a stout dog fox or a real stag of a hare.

===Chinese===
无论在哪个战场，你都会发现，要活得合于弗拉尼根那份对敌人的仇恨标准，实在不是易事。一到前线，你便发现你满心要的只是打赢这场战争。用不了多久，你就被这一目标的追求占满了，以致总是忘记去点燃那宝石般纯粹的怒焰——在本土，把那些雄心狮胆的政论家烧得炽亮的，正是这团火。

一名士兵在抵达射击线之前，尽可以安然怀着阿塔纳修信经（Athanasian Creed）式的狂喜：每一个德国人，个个都击沉过「卢西塔尼亚号」（*Lusitania*）；没有一个义人，一个也没有。然而一到了前线，那份神圣的激情便开始从他的指尖往外渗漏。麻烦的根子在于：你无法以肉搏的方式与一个人交手，却不在某种程度上触到他。实际交战者之间的关系是个人性质的关系——诚然是一种粗粝的、原始的关系，但比起柏林某学者与伦敦某学者之间的关系来，仍算十分先进的了：前者一个劲儿扯着嗓子念叨 *Delenda est Britannia!*（必须摧毁不列颠），后者一个劲儿扯着嗓子断言，每个德国人必定长着一颗黑心，因为凯撒不曾像征服高卢和不列颠那样征服过德国。且让一颗德国人的圆脑袋，隔着长长的间隔，在中景处的一个土墩上浮现那么一瞬。不等你诚意十足地朝他试射过六回，人类关系的致命病菌就已开始重新找到一处巢穴：他已在你心里获得了一份个人独立性的雏形。你仍会兴致勃勃地继续朝他开枪——而且脱靶的概率反倒更低了，因为纯粹的仇恨是一种叫人手忙脚乱的情绪。然而仇恨这门营生已经开始崩解。那场悄然潜行的变化已经启动，它注定要在你头一回休假回国时，让你口无遮拦地谈起「老弗里茨」（old Fritz）、「老好博什」（the good old Boche），惹得你的朋友们痛心，仿佛他说的是一条壮实的公狐狸，或一只真正够分量的雄野兔。

===Original===
The deadliest solvent of your exalted hatreds is laughter. And you can never wholly suppress laughter between two crowds of millions of men standing within earshot of each other along a line of hundreds of miles. There was, in the Loos salient in 1916, a German who, after his meals, would halloo across to an English unit taunts about certain accidents of its birth. None of his British hearers could help laughing at his mistakes, his knowledge, and his English. Nor could the least humorous priest of ill-will have kept his countenance at a relief when the enemy shouted: “We know you are relieving,” “No good hiding it,” “Goodbye, Ox and Bucks,” “Who’s coming in?” and some humorist in the obscure English battalion relieving shouted back, with a terrific assumption of accent, “Furrst Black Watch!” or “Th’ Oirish Gyards!” and a hush fell at the sound of these great names. Comedy, expelled with a fork by the dignified figure of Quenchless Hate, had begun to steal back of herself.

At home that tragedy queen might do very well; she did not have these tenpenny nails scattered about on her road to puncture the nobly inflated tyres of her chariot. The heroes who spoke up for shooing all the old German governesses into the barbed wire compounds were not exposed to the moral danger of actually hustling, *propria persona*, these formidable ancients. But while Hamilcar at home was swearing Hannibal and all the other little Hamilcars to undying hatred of the foe, an enemy dog might be trotting across to the British front line to sample its rats, and its owner be losing in some British company’s eyes his proper quality as an incarnation of all the Satanism of Potsdam and becoming simply “him that lost the dog.”

If you took his trench it might be no better; perhaps Incarnate Evil had left its bit of food half-cooked, and the muddy straw, where it lay last, was pressed into a hollow by Incarnate Evil’s back as by a cat’s. Incarnate Evil should not do these things that other people in trenches do. It ought to be more strange and beastly and keep on making beaux gestes with its talons and tail, like the proper dragon slain by St. George. Perhaps Incarnate Evil was extinct and you went over its pockets. They never contained the right things﻿—no poison to put in our wells, no practical hints for crucifying Canadians; only the usual stuffing of all soldiers’ pockets﻿—photographs and tobacco and bits of string and the wife’s letters, all about how tramps were always stealing potatoes out of the garden, and how the baby was worse, and was his leave never coming! No good to look at such things.

===Chinese===
对你那些崇高的仇恨，最致命的溶剂是笑。而在相隔数百英里的战线上、彼此嗓门可闻的两大群数以百万计的人之间，你永远休想把笑彻底压下去。1916 年，在卢斯（Loos）突出部，有一个德国人，每顿饭之后总要隔着战线朝一个英国部队高声喊叫，嘲骂该部队出身上的某些意外事故。听他喊话的英国人没有一个绷得住不笑——笑他的口误，笑他的见识，也笑他的英语。换防的时候，敌人喊：「知道你们在换防」「瞒也没用」「再见吧，牛津白金汉团（Ox and Bucks）」「接防的是谁？」接防的那个默默无闻的英军营里，某个促狭鬼拿腔拿调地喊回去：「弗斯特黑卫团！」（Furrst Black Watch）或者「爱尔兰近卫团！」（Th' Oirish Gyards），这等响当当的名号一出，四下里顿时一片肃静。喜剧，方才被「不灭之恨」那尊威仪的身影用草叉轰出门外，这会儿已经开始自己悄悄溜回来了。

在本土，那位悲剧女王原本可以一路顺遂；她的路上没有散落这些十便士的大钉子，来扎破她那高贵鼓胀的车胎。那些高声主张把德国老家庭女教师统统轰进铁丝网圈禁营的英雄们，也不必亲冒道德的风险，亲身（*propria persona*）去推搡这些了不得的先辈。可是，当本土的哈米尔卡（Hamilcar）正领着汉尼拔（Hannibal）和其余一切小哈米尔卡们立誓永世仇恨敌人时，一条敌方的狗，没准正颠儿颠儿地跑过无人地带，来英国前线上尝尝这里的老鼠；而它的主人也正在某个英国连队的眼里，渐渐失去他作为波茨坦（Potsdam）全部撒旦主义之化身的应有成色，变成简简单单的「丢狗的那位」。

就算你占了他的战壕，情形也未必更好；没准「恶之化身」留下的一口吃食才做了一半，它最后躺过的那片泥乎乎的麦草，被「恶之化身」的脊背压出一个凹窝，像猫压出来的那样。「恶之化身」不该做这些战壕里旁人人人都会做的事。它本该更怪异些、更兽性些，该没完没了地拿利爪和尾巴比划出种种漂亮的姿态（beaux gestes），像被圣乔治（St. George）斩杀的那条如假包换的龙。说不定「恶之化身」已经绝种了，于是你翻检它的口袋。口袋里从来装不对东西——没有往我们水井里投的毒药，没有钉加拿大人十字架的实用须知；只有一切士兵口袋里那种寻常的填充——照片、烟丝、几截细绳，还有妻子的来信，信里翻来覆去无非是：流浪汉总从园子里偷土豆；娃娃的病又重了；他的假到底还来不来啊！这种东西，是看不得的。

### IV / 四

===Original===
With this guilty weakness gaining upon them our troops drove the Germans from Albert to Mons. There were scandalous scenes on the way. Imagine two hundred German prisoners grinning inside a wire cage while a little Cockney corporal chaffs them in half the dialects of Germany! His father, he says, was a slop tailor in Whitechapel; most of his journeymen came from somewhere or other in Germany﻿—“Ah! and my dad sweated ’em proper,” he says proudly; so the boy learnt all their kinds of talk. He convulses Bavarians now with his flow of Silesian. He fraternizes grossly and jubilantly. Other British soldiers laugh when one of the Germans sings, in return for favours received, the British ballad “Knocked ’em in the Ol’ Kent Road.” By the time our men had marched to the Rhine there was little hatred left in them. How can you hate the small boy who stands at the farm door visibly torn between dread of the invader and deep delight in all soldiers, as soldiers? How shall a man not offer a drink to the first disbanded German soldier who sits next to him in a public house at Cologne, and try to find out if he was ever in the line at the Brick-stacks or near the Big Crater? Why, that might have been his dog!

The billeted soldier’s immemorial claim on “a place by the fire” carried on the fell work. It is hopelessly bad for your grand Byronic hates if you sit through whole winter evenings in the abhorred foe’s kitchen and the abhorred foe grants you the uncovenanted mercy of hot coffee and discusses without rancour the relative daily yields of the British and the German milch cow. And then comes into play the British soldier’s incorrigible propensity, wherever he be, to form virtuous attachments. “Love, unfoiled in the war,” as Sophocles says. The broad road has a terribly easy gradient. When all the great and wise at Paris were making peace, as somebody said, with a vengeance, our command on the Rhine had to send a wire to say that unless something was done to feed the Germans starving in the slums it could not answer for discipline in its army; the men were giving their rations away, and no orders would stop them. Rank “Pro-Germanism,” you see﻿—the heresy of Edith Cavell; “Patriotism is not enough; I must have no hatred or bitterness in my heart.” While these men fought on, year after year, they had mostly been growing more void of mere spite all the time, feeling always more and more sure that the average German was just a decent poor devil like everyone else. One trembles to think what the really first-class haters at home would have said of our army if they had known at the time.

===Chinese===
我们的部队就是带着这桩日益加重的、有罪的软肋，把德国人从阿尔贝（Albert）一路逐到了蒙斯（Mons）。沿途尽是丢人的场面。想象一下：两百名德国战俘关在铁丝笼里咧着嘴笑，而一个伦敦佬（Cockney）下士正用德国半数的方言拿他们打趣！他说，他父亲当年是白教堂（Whitechapel）的成衣裁缝，帮工多半来自德国的这样那样地方——「哈！俺爹把他们榨得可结实啦，」他骄傲地说；于是这小子把他们各家的话都学了个遍。如今他一口西里西亚话，把巴伐利亚人逗得前仰后合。他与敌人放肆而欢畅地称兄道弟。有个德国人为了报答收到的恩惠，唱起英国歌谣《在老肯特路上揍了他们》（Knocked 'em in the Ol' Kent Road），其余的英国士兵听了直乐。等我军行进到莱茵河（Rhine）时，他们心里已经剩不下多少仇恨了。那个站在农舍门口的小男孩，一眼看得出正被两种心情撕扯着——一边是对入侵者的恐惧，一边是对一切士兵之为士兵的深深的欢喜——这样的人你怎么恨得起来？在科隆（Cologne）的酒馆里，头一个挨着他坐下的复员德国兵，一个男人怎能不请他喝上一杯，再设法打听打听：他当年是不是在「砖垛」（Brick-stacks）一线待过，或在「大弹坑」（Big Crater）附近待过？嘿，那没准就是他的狗！

宿营士兵对「炉边一个位置」的那份亘古不易的权利，把这桩害人的营生接着做了下去。倘若你整个整个冬夜坐在可憎仇敌的厨房里，而可憎的仇敌赐予你非约之恩（uncovenanted mercy）——热腾腾的咖啡——还不带半点怨毒地同你讨论英国奶牛与德国奶牛日产奶量的高下，那你那些堂皇的拜伦式（Byronic）仇恨，就彻底没救了。接下来开始发作的，是英国士兵那桩积习难改的禀性：无论身在何处，总要结下些正经的情谊。「爱，在战争中不曾被挫败，」如索福克勒斯（Sophocles）所言。这条宽路，坡度真是平缓得吓人。当巴黎的全体伟人与贤达，正如某人所说、以复仇之势缔造和平时，我们驻莱茵河的统帅部不得不拍出一封电报：除非设法救济贫民窟里挨饿的德国人，它无法再为本军的军纪负责；士兵们正把自己的口粮往外送，什么命令也拦不住。十足的「亲德行径」，你瞧——伊迪丝·卡维尔（Edith Cavell）式的异端：「光有爱国主义是不够的；我心中必须没有仇恨，没有怨毒。」这些人一年又一年打下去，心中的纯粹怨毒却多半一直在消退，越来越确信：寻常的德国人，不过是个和别人一样体面的倒霉蛋。真叫人不寒而栗：当年本土那些真正第一流的仇恨家们若是知道了我军的实情，会说出些什么话来。

### V / 五

===Original===
Even at places less distant than home the survival of old English standards of fighting had given some scandal. In that autumn of the war when our generalship seemed to have explored all its own talents and found only the means to stage in an orderly way the greatest possible number of combats of pure attrition, the crying up of unknightliness became a kind of fashion among a good many Staff Officers of the higher grades. “I fancy our fellows were not taking many prisoners this morning,” a Corps Commander would say with a complacent grin, on the evening after a battle. Jocose stories of comic things said by privates when getting rid of undesired captives became current in messes far in the rear. The other day I saw in a history of one of the most gallant of all British divisions an illustration given by the officer who wrote it of what he believed to be the true martial spirit. It was the case of a wounded Highlander who had received with a bomb a German Red Cross orderly who was coming to help him. A General of some consequence during part of the war gave a lecture, towards its end, to a body of officers and others on what he called “the fighting spirit.” He told with enthusiasm an anecdote of a captured trench in which some of our men had been killing off German appellants for quarter. Another German appearing and putting his hands up, one of our men﻿—so the story went﻿—called out, “ ’Ere! Where’s ’Arry? ’E ain’t ’ad one yet.” Probably someone had pulled the good general’s leg, and the thing never happened. But he believed it, and deeply approved the “blooding” of ’Arry. That, he explained, was the “fighting spirit.” Men more versed than he in the actual hand-to-hand business of fighting this war knew that he was mistaken, and that the spirit of trial by combat and that of pork-butchery are distinct. But that is of course. The notable thing was that such things should be said by anyone wearing our uniform. Twenty years before, if it had been rumoured, you would, without waiting, have called the rumour a lie invented by some detractor of England or of her army. Now it passed quite unhissed. It was the latter-day wisdom. Scrofulous minds at home had long been itching, publicly and in print, to bomb German women and children from aeroplanes, and to “take it out of” German prisoners of war. Now the disease had even affected some parts of the noncombatant Staff of our army.

===Chinese===
即便在比本土近些的地方，旧日英格兰式作战标准的存续，也已招来些许非议。在战争的那个秋天——其时我们的将才似乎已把自家的诸般才能勘探殆尽，只寻得了以井然有序的方式排出尽可能多的纯消耗战的手段——吹捧无骑士风度，在相当一批高级参谋（Staff）军官中间成了一种风尚。「我看今早我们的小伙子们没抓多少俘虏，」一位军长会在战斗结束的当晚，面带得意的嘻笑这样说。关于列兵们打发掉不想要的俘虏时说过的俏皮话，种种谐趣段子在远居后方的各处军官食堂（mess）里流传。前些天，我在一部战史里看到一则例证——那是英军最英勇的师之一的历史，由其军官执笔，他拿它来说明自己所信的真正的尚武精神。说的是一名负伤的苏格兰高地兵，用手榴弹「接待」了一位正赶来救护他的德国红十字救护兵。战争某阶段颇有些分量的一位将军，在接近尾声时向一群军官及其他人等作了一场讲演，讲他所谓的「战斗精神」。他眉飞色舞地讲了一桩轶事：我军攻克一条战壕后，有些人正在把前来乞求饶命的德国人一个个打发掉。这时又出现一个德国人，举起了双手，我们的一个人——故事是这么讲的——喊道：「哎！『阿利』呢？他还没轮上一个呢！」多半是有人捉弄了这位好将军，这事压根儿就没发生过。可他信了，而且对「阿利」的「初见血」（blooding）深表赞许。他解释说，那才是「战斗精神」。在这场战争的真实肉搏行当里比他老练得多的人们知道他错了：比武断讼的精神与屠猪的营生，本是两码事。不过，这原在意料之中。值得注目的只是：这样的话，竟然出自行我们军装之人的口。若在二十年前，这流言一冒头，你便会不假思索地断定，它是哪个英格兰或英军的污蔑者编造的谎话。如今它全无嘘声便通行过去了。这是当今的时新智慧。本土那些瘰疬质的心智，早已公开地、白纸黑字地巴望着用飞机去轰炸德国的妇孺，巴望着从德国战俘身上「讨回点什么」。如今这病甚至已经染指我军非战斗参谋班子的某些部分。

### VI / 六

===Original===
You know the most often quoted of all passages of Burke. Indeed, it is only through quotations of it that most of us know Burke at all﻿—

> But the age of chivalry is gone﻿ ﻿… the unbought grace of life, the cheap defence of nations, the nurse of manly sentiment and heroic enterprise is gone! It is gone, that sensibility of principle, that chastity of honour, which felt a stain like a wound, which inspired courage whilst it mitigated ferocity, which ennobled whatever it touched, and under which vice itself lost half its evil by losing all its grossness.

===Chinese===
伯克（Burke）被征引最多的那段文字，你是知道的。的确，我们大多数人之所以还知道伯克，靠的也不过是对它的种种摘引——

> 但骑士的时代已经一去不返……生命里那份不待金钱购买的优雅，各国那道廉价的屏障，男子汉情感与英雄事业的乳母，都一去不返了！一去不返了，那对原则的敏感，那荣誉的贞洁——它感到污点便如感到创伤，它激发勇气而又平抑凶残，它使所触及的一切归于高贵；在它的荫庇之下，罪恶本身也因褪尽全部粗鄙而失去了一半的邪恶。

===Original===
Burke would never say a thing by halves. And as truth goes by halves, and declines to be sweeping like rhetoric, Burke made sure of being wrong to the tune of some fifty percent. The French Revolution did not, as his beautiful language implies, confine mankind for the rest of its days to the procreation of curs. And yet his words do give you, in their own lush, Corinthian way, a notion of something that probably did happen, a certain limited shifting of the centre of gravity of West European morals or manners.

One would be talking like Burke﻿—talking, perhaps you might say, through Burke’s hat﻿—if one were to say that the war found chivalry alive and left it dead. Chivalry is about as likely to perish as brown eyes or the moon. Yet something did happen, during the war, to which these wild words would have some sort of relation. We were not all Bayards in 1914; even then a great part of our Press could not tell indignation from spite, nor uphold the best cause in the world without turpitude. Nor were we all, after the Armistice, rods of the houses of Thersites and Cleon; Haig was still alive, and so were Gough and Hamilton and thousands of Arthurian subalterns and privates and of like-minded civilians, though it is harder for a civilian not to lose generosity during a war. But something had happened; the chivalrous temper had had a setback; it was no longer the mode; the latest wear was a fine robust shabbiness. All through the war there had been a bear movement in Newbolts and Burkes, and, corresponding to this, a bull movement in stocks of the Little Flanigan group.

===Chinese===
伯克说话，从来不肯只说一半。而真理素来只说一半，又不肯像修辞那样大而化之地一网打尽，于是伯克稳稳当当地错了足足五成。法国革命并没有像他那套漂亮辞藻所暗示的那样，把人类从此后的世世代代都锁死在专事繁殖恶犬的本分上。然而，他的话确实以其自己那套丰茂的、科林斯式的笔致，向你透露了一件多半确曾发生的事：西欧道德或风尚的重心，发生了某种有限度的移位。

谁要是宣称，这场战争来时骑士精神尚存、去时便已把它杀死，谁就是在像伯克那样说话——或者，不妨说，在透过伯克的帽子信口开河。骑士精神会消亡的可能，与棕色的眼睛或月亮会消亡的可能不相上下。然而战争中确实发生了某件事，与上面那些狂言有着几分关系。1914 年，我们并非人人都是巴亚尔（Bayard）；即便在那时，我国报界（Press）的一大部分也已分不清义愤与怨毒，也撑不起世界上最好的事业，除非掺上几分卑劣。停战之后，我们也不是人人都成了忒尔西忒斯（Thersites）与克里昂（Cleon）两家的苗裔；黑格（Haig）还在人世，高夫（Gough）与汉密尔顿（Hamilton）也在，还有成千上万位亚瑟王式的初级军官与列兵，以及心怀同调的平民——尽管平民要在一场战争里保住慷慨，比军人更难。但毕竟，有什么事情发生了；骑士的性情遭到了一次挫折；它不再是时尚；最新的时款，是一种气势结实的寒酸。整个战争期间，纽博尔特股与伯克股一路走熊；与此相应，小弗拉尼根集团的股票则一路走牛。

## XII Belated Boons / 十二 迟来的恩赐

### I / 一

===Original===
There is no one day of which you can say: “My youth ended then. On the Monday the ball of my vision had eagles that flew unabashed to the sun. On the Tuesday it hadn’t.” The season of rapture goes out like a tide that has turned; a time has come when the mud flats are bare; but, long after the ebb has set in, any wave that has taken a special strength of its own from some combination of flukes out at sea may cover them up for a moment—may even throw itself far up the beach, making as if to recapture the lost high-water mark. So the youth of our war had its feints at renewal, hours of Indian summer when there was wine again in the air; in the “bare, ruined choirs” a lated golden auriole would strike up once more for a while, before leaving.

Because hope does spring eternal the evening before a great battle must always make fires leap up in the mind. The calm before Thermopylae, the rival camps on the night before Agincourt, the ball before Waterloo—not without reason have writers of genius, searching for glimpses of life in its most fugitive acme of bloom, the poised and just breaking crest of the wave, gone to places and times of the kind. For there the wits and the heart may be really astir and at gaze, and the common man may have, for the hour, the artist’s vision of life as an adventure and challenge, lovely, harsh, fleeting, and strange. The great throw, the new age’s impending nativity, Fate with her fingers approaching the veil, about to lift—a sense of these things is a drug as strong as strychnine to quicken the failing pulse of the most heart-weary of moribund raptures.

===Chinese===
没有哪一天，你能指着它说：「我的青春就是在那一天结束的。星期一，我那只幻象之球里，鹰还毫不羞怯地直飞向太阳；到星期二，便没有了。」狂喜的季节退去，像一股已经掉头的潮水；泥滩裸露的时辰已然到来；但在退潮确立之后许久，任何一道浪，只要曾在海上某处凭着种种侥幸的凑合蓄起了自己特有的一股蛮力，仍可能把它们一时漫过——甚至可能把自己远远抛上滩头，摆出一副要收复那失落的高潮线的架势。我们这场战争的青春，便这样有过几次佯装的复苏，有过几个小阳春（Indian summer）的时辰，那时空气里又有了酒意；在「荒芜的唱诗班席位」（bare, ruined choirs）间，一只迟来的金黄鹂（auriole）还会再啼啭一阵，然后离去。

正因为希望的确永远在人胸中萌生，一场大战的前夜，总不免使心中的火苗蹿起。温泉关（Thermopylae）前夜的静穆，阿金库尔（Agincourt）前夜相峙的两座营盘，滑铁卢（Waterloo）前夜的舞会——天才的作家们要捕捉人生在那最易逝的绽放极点、在那道蓄势悬定、正待迸碎的浪峰上的一瞥，纷纷去到这类时地，并非无因。因为惟有在那里，心智与心灵才真正既在躁动又在凝望；寻常人也能有那么几个小时，怀着艺术家的眼光，把人生看作一场历险与挑战——可爱，严酷，倏忽，而奇异。那一场孤注一掷的大豪掷，新时代迫近的降生，命运女神的手指正探向帷幕、行将掀起——对这一切的预感，是一味与马钱子碱同样猛烈的药，足以为最心力交疲的垂死狂喜，提振它衰微下去的脉搏。

===Original===
We all had the dope in our wine on the night of August 7, 1918. At daybreak our troops to the east of Amiens would second the first blow of Foch at the German salient towards Paris, the giant arm that was now left sticking out into the air to be hit; its own smashing blow had been struck without killing; its first strength was spent; the spirit behind it was cracking; now, in its moment of check, of lost momentum, of risky extension, now to have at it and smash it. The bull had rushed right on to gore us and missed; we had his flank to stab now.

Someone who dined at the mess had just motored from Paris, through white dust and sunshine and, everywhere, quickly turned heads and eager faces. He had been in the streets all the night of the enemy’s last mighty lunge at the city. He spoke of the silent crowds blackening the boulevards through the few hours of midsummer darkness; other crowds on the skyline of roofs, all black and immobile, the whole city hushed to hear the bombardment, and staring, staring fixedly east at the flame that incessantly winked in the sky above Château-Thierry—history come to life, still enigmatic, but audible, visible, galloping through the night. Poor old France, tormented and stoical, what could not the world forgive her? Then he had seen the news come the next day to these that had thus watched as the noncombatants watched from the high walls of Troy; and how an American had broken down uncontrollably on hearing how his country’s Third Division had bundled the Germans back into the Marne: “We *are* all right! By God, we *are* all right!” he had cried, a whole new nation’s secret self-distrust before a supercilious ancient world changing into a younger boy’s ecstasy of relief in the thought that now he has jolly well given his proofs and the older boys will not sneer at him now, and he never need bluff any more. Good fellows really, the Yanks; most simple and human as soon as you knew them. One seemed to know everyone then, for that evening.

===Chinese===
1918 年 8 月 7 日的夜里，我们每人的酒里都下了这味药。破晓时分，我们在亚眠（Amiens）以东的部队，将配合福煦（Foch）打出的第一击，直取德军伸向巴黎的那个突出部——那条此刻孤零零支在半空、专等挨打的巨臂；它自己那毁灭性的一击已经打出，却未能致命；它的头一股气力已经用尽；支撑它的那股精神正在开裂；此刻，正当它受挫、动能丧失、冒进外伸险象环生之际——此刻正该扑上去，把它砸个粉碎。公牛一路猛冲过来要抵死我们，扑了个空；如今，轮到我们来刺它的侧腹了。

那天在军官食堂（mess）搭伙的一位，刚从巴黎坐汽车过来，一路白尘烈日，沿途处处是霎时转过来的头与热切的脸。敌人向那座城发起最后一次猛扑的整个夜晚，他都在街上。他讲起那些沉默的人群，如何在仲夏短短几个钟头的黑暗里，把条条林荫大道铺成一片黑；屋顶的天际线上又是一群群的人，浑身黢黑，纹丝不动，全城屏息听着隆隆炮声，直勾勾地、直勾勾地盯着东面那道在蒂耶里堡（Château-Thierry）上空不住眨动的火光——历史活了过来，仍是个谜，却听得见、看得着，正彻夜奔腾。可怜的老法兰西啊，受尽折磨而默然坚忍，这世上还有什么不能饶恕她的呢？随后他又讲起，第二天，消息如何送到这些如同当年特洛伊（Troy）高墙上的观战者那样的非战斗人员耳中；一位美国人如何一听到他的国家第三师把德国人撵回马恩河（Marne）里去，便情不自禁地失了态：「我们*是*行的！老天作证，我们*是*行的！」他喊道——一个整个崭新的民族，在傲慢的老世界面前秘藏的自我疑惧，霎时间化作一个小弟弟如释重负的狂喜：他想到，如今他总算结结实实地交出了凭据，年长的孩子们再不会讥笑他了，他从此永远不必再虚声唬人了。美国佬（Yanks）实在是些好小伙子；一旦相识，便知道他们再单纯、再通人情不过。那天晚上，人仿佛把谁都认识了。

### II / 二

===Original===
Night came on cloudless and windless and braced with autumn’s first astringent tang of coolness. Above, as I lay on my back in the meadow, the whole dome had a stir of life in its shimmering fresco, stars flashing and winking with that eager air of having great things to impart—they have it on frosty nights in the Alps, over a high bivouac. We were all worked up, you see. Could it be coming at last, I thought as I went to sleep—the battle unlike other battles? How many I had seen outlive their little youth of groundless hope, from the approach along darkened roads through summer nights, the eastern sky pulsating with its crimson flush, the wild glow always leaping up and always drawing in, and the waiting cavalry’s lances upright, black and multitudinous in roadside fields, impaling the blenching sky just above the horizon; and then, in the bald dawn, the backward trickles of wastage swelling into great streams or rather endless friezes seen in silhouette across the fields, the trailing processions of wounded, English and German, on foot and on stretchers, dripping so much blood that some of the tracks were flamboyantly marked for miles across country; and then the evening’s reports, with their anxious efforts to show that we had gained something worth having. Was it to be only Loos and the Somme and Arras and Flanders and Cambrai, all over again?

===Chinese===
夜来了，无云，无风，带着秋天头一口涩冽的凉意，令人精神一振。我仰卧在草场上，头顶那整个穹顶，在它流光闪烁的湿壁画里有一阵生命的悸动；星子们闪着、眨着，一副有大事要相告的热切神情——在阿尔卑斯山的霜夜里，在高处的露营地之上，它们就是这副神情。你要明白，我们全都亢奋起来了。睡意蒙眬中我想：它终于要来了么——那场不同于一切战斗的战斗？我见过多少场战斗，眼看它们一个一个熬死了自己那段怀有无端希望的小小青春：先是夏夜里沿熄了灯的行军路开进，东天涨红，随脉一明一灭，那野性的红光总在腾起，又总在敛回；待命的骑兵长矛直竖在路旁田野里，黑压压、密丛丛，把地平线之上那片发白的天空挑在矛尖上；然后，在光秃秃的黎明，向后流去的伤亡细流涨成大川——不如说，涨成一道道望不到头的横饰带，成剪影横贯田野：逶迤不绝的伤兵行伍，英国的、德国的，步行的、担架上的，滴下的血竟那样多，有几道车辙被血染得鲜亮，在原野上标出好几英里；然后是晚间的战报，焦灼万状地竭力表明我们总算挣得了一点值得要的东西。难道又要原样再来一遍卢斯（Loos）、索姆河（Somme）、阿拉斯（Arras）、佛兰德（Flanders）和康布雷（Cambrai）么？

===Original===
Thought must have passed into dream when I was awakened by some bird that may have had a dream too and had fallen right off its perch in a bush near my head, with a disconcerted squeak and a scuffling sound among dry leaves. Opening my eyes, I found that a thickish veil was drawn over the stars. When I sat up the veil was gone; my eyes were above it; a quilt of white mist, about a foot thick, had spread itself over the meadow. Good! Let it thicken away and be shoes of silence and armour of darkness at dawn for our men. Soon night’s habitual sounds brought on sleep again. An owl in the wood by the little chalk stream would hoot, patiently wait for the answering call that should come, and then hoot again, and listen again. The low, dry, continuous buzz of an aeroplane engine, more evenly humming than any of ours, droned itself into hearing and softly ascended the scale of audibility; overhead, as the enemy passed, was slowly drawn across the sky from east to west a line of momentarily obscured stars, each coming back into sight as the next one was deleted. In the east the low, slow grumbling sound of a few guns from fifty miles of front seemed, in its approach to quietude, like the audible breath of a sleeper. The war was taking its rest.

Some sort of musing half-dream about summer heaths, buzzing with bees, was jarred by the big blunted sound, distant and dull, of wooden boxes tumbling down wooden stairs, “off,” as they do in a farce. Of course—that night-bomber unloading on St. Omer, Abbeville, Étaples, some one of the usual marks. But now there was something to wake for. Not a star to be seen. I jumped up and found the mist thick to my armpits, and rising. Oh, good, good! Our men would walk safe as the attacking Germans had walked in the mist of that lovely and fatal morning in March. I slept hard till two o’clock came—time to get up for work. The mist was doing its best; it seemed to fill the whole wide vessel of the universe.

===Chinese===
思绪想必已经化入梦境，这时我被一只鸟弄醒——它或许也做了个梦，从近旁一丛灌木的栖枝上直跌下来，一声狼狈的吱喳，在枯叶间扑腾了一阵。睁开眼，我发现星子上蒙了一层不薄的纱。坐起身来，纱便不见了；原来我的眼睛已在纱之上；一床白雾的棉被，约莫一英尺厚，铺满了整片草场。好！让它尽管浓去，天亮时好给我们的人当无声的鞋、黑暗的甲。不多时，夜晚惯常的种种声响又催人入睡。白垩小溪旁林子里的一只猫头鹰，呜地叫一声，耐心等着那声理应到来的应答，然后再叫一声，再听一阵。一阵飞机引擎低而干、连绵不断的嗡鸣——比我们哪一架的嗡鸣都匀净——自己哼进了人的听觉，又轻手轻脚地爬上可闻度的音阶；头顶上，当那敌人掠过，一线霎时隐没的星子自东向西缓缓划过天空，每一颗都在下一颗被抹去时重新显形。东面，五十英里战线传来的稀疏几门炮低缓的隆隆声，在那渐近的安静里，像一个沉睡者依稀可闻的鼻息。战争正在歇它的觉。

某种关于夏日石楠荒原的冥想半梦——原上蜂声嗡嗡——被一声钝大的巨响震破：又远又闷，像几口木箱顺着木楼梯一路滚到头、「哐当」落地，一如闹剧里的动静。当然是——哪架夜航轰炸机正在朝圣奥默（St. Omer）、阿布维尔（Abbeville）、埃塔普勒（Étaples）卸货，反正总是那几个惯常的目标。可如今，有值得为之醒来的东西了。一颗星也看不见。我一跃而起，发现雾已没到我的腋窝，还在上涨。哦，好，好！我们的人将走得安然，一如当年进攻的德国人在三月那个可爱而致命的清晨的雾中所走的那样。我一觉沉睡，直到两点钟来临——该起身干活了。雾正各尽其能；它仿佛注满了宇宙这整个宽大的容器。

### III / 三

===Original===
Ten miles to the east of Amiens a steep-sided ridge divides the converging rivers of Ancre and Somme. They meet where it sinks, at its western end, into the plain. From the ridge there was, in prewar days, a beautiful view. On the south the ground fell from your feet abruptly, a kind of earth cliff, to the north bank of the Somme, about a hundred feet below. Southwards, beyond the river, stretched, as far as eye could see, the expanse of the level Santerre, one of France’s best cornlands. Southeastward you looked up the Somme valley, mile after mile, towards Bray and Péronne—a shining valley of poplars and stream and linked ponds and red-roofed villages among the poplars. But now the Santerre lay untilled, gone back to heath of a faded fawn-grey. The red roofs had been shelled; the Germans possessed them; the Germans held the blasted heath, across the river; other Germans held most of the ridge on this side to a mile or so east of the point to which I was posted that morning. English troops were to carry the eastern end of the ridge and the tricky low ground between it and the Somme. Australian and Canadian troops were to attack on a broad front, out on the level Santerre, across the river and under our eyes.

But there was no seeing. The mist, in billowy, bolster-like masses, wallowed and rolled about at the touch of light airs; at one moment a figure some thirty yards off could be seen and then a thickened whiteness would rub it out; down the earth cliff we looked into a cauldron of that seething milky opaqueness. Of what might go on in that pit of enigma the eye could tell nothing; the mind hung on what news might come through the ear. We knew that there was to be no prior bombardment; the men would start with the barrage and go for five miles across the Santerre if they could, pushing the enemy off it. The stage was set, the play of plays was about to begin on the broad stage below; only, between our eyes and the boards there was hung a white curtain.

===Chinese===
亚眠以东十英里，一道陡壁的山梁，分开昂克尔河（Ancre）与索姆河（Somme）这两条渐渐相汇的河。两河相会之处，便是山梁在西端没入平原之处。战前年代，从这道山梁上望出去，是一片美景。南面，大地从你脚边陡然跌落，成一道土崖，落到约一百英尺下的索姆河北岸。往南，过了河，目力所及，铺展着平坦的桑泰尔（Santerre）原野——法兰西最好的麦田产区之一。朝东南，你溯索姆河谷望去，一英里又一英里，望向布雷（Bray）与佩罗讷（Péronne）——一道明亮的河谷：白杨、溪流、串珠相连的池塘，还有白杨丛中红顶的村庄。可如今，桑泰尔荒着无人耕种，退成了一片褪色的黄褐灰石楠荒地。红屋顶挨过了炮击；德国人占着它们；河对岸那片饱经荼毒的荒原，也在德国人手里；山梁这一侧，另一些德国人则占着大半，一直占到那天早晨我所在岗位以东约一英里处。英国部队将攻取山梁东端，以及它与索姆河之间那片刁钻的低地。澳大利亚部队与加拿大部队将在宽阔的正面上发起攻击，就在平坦的桑泰尔原野上——在河对岸，在我们眼皮底下。

可就是什么也看不见。雾结成一团团褥垫似的厚重堆积，些微的风一触，便翻滚涌动；这一刻，三十来码外还看得见一个人影；下一刻，一团加浓的白便把它擦掉了。顺着土崖望下去，我们望进一口大锅，一锅翻腾的乳白不透明体。在那口谜的大坑里正进行着什么，眼睛一点也说不上来；心只能悬在耳朵或许送来的消息上。我们知道不会有先行的炮火准备；士兵们将踏着弹幕出发，能的话便一气走完五英里的桑泰尔，把敌人从上面推下去。戏台已经布好，那出戏中之戏就要在下方宽阔的舞台上开演；只是在我们眼睛与台板之间，挂了一道白幕。

===Original===
Up the cliff, fumbling and muted, came the first burst of the barrage, suggesting, as barrages usually do, a race between sounds, a piece bangingly played against time on a keyboard. Now the men would be rising full length above earth and walking out with smoking breath and bejewelled eyebrows into the infested mist. Then our guns, for an interval, fell almost silent—first lift of the barrage—a chance for hungry ears to assess the weight of the enemy’s answering gunfire. Surely, surely it had not all the volume it had had at Arras and Ypres last year. And then down came our barrage again, like one rifle-bolt banging home, and all thought was again with the friends before whose faces the wall of splashing metal, earth, and flame had just risen and moved on ahead like the pillars of fire and cloud.

Hours passed, bringing the usual changes of sounds in battles. The piece that had started so rapidly on the piano slowed down; the notes spaced themselves out; the first continuous barking of many guns slackened off irregularly into isolated barks and groups of barks—just what you hear from a dog whose temper is subsiding, with occasional returns. That, in itself, told nothing. Troops might only have gained a few hundred yards in the old Flanders way, and then flopped down to dig and be murdered. Or—but one kept a tight hand on hope. One had hoped too often since Loos. And then the mist lifted. It rolled right up into the sky in one piece, like a theatre curtain, almost suddenly taking its white quilted thickness away from between our eyes and the vision so much longed for during four years. Beyond the river a miracle—*the* miracle—had begun. It was going on fast. Remember that all previous advances had gained us little more than freedom to skulk up communication trenches a mile or two further eastward, if that. But now! Across the level Santerre, which the sun was beginning to fill with a mist-filtered lustre, two endless columns of British guns, wagons, and troops were marching steadily east, unshelled, over the ground that the Germans had held until dawn.

===Chinese===
顺着崖壁，爬上来弹幕（barrage）的第一阵爆发，含混而滞闷——弹幕向来如此，听来像一场声音之间的赛跑，一段在键盘上抢着拍子、乒乒乓乓砸出来的曲子。此刻，士兵们该正从地里直起全身，呼吸吐着白汽，眉毛上缀着珠子，走进那片敌影充斥的雾里去了。随后，我们的炮有片刻几乎沉寂——弹幕第一次延伸——饿极了的耳朵得了个空当，去掂量敌军还击炮火的分量。的确，的确，它已经没有去年阿拉斯（Arras）与伊普尔（Ypres）两役时那副声量了。然后我们的弹幕又砸了下来，像一杆枪的枪机「哐」地合上了膛，全部心思又回到那些朋友身上——一堵飞溅的金属、泥土与火焰之墙，刚刚在他们面前升起，像火柱与云柱一般，向前推进。

几个小时过去了，带来战斗中惯常的种种声响变迁。那支在钢琴上急促开场的曲子慢了下来；音符彼此拉开了间距；起先许多门炮连绵不断的吠叫，不规则地松懈成一声声孤吠、一阵阵群吠——恰似一条怒气正在消退的狗，偶尔还要回咬一两声。这本身什么也说明不了。部队也许只按老一套的佛兰德路数推进了几百码，随即一屁股趴下挖壕，任人屠戮。要么就是——可谁也不敢放开希望。自从卢斯一役之后，人希望的次数太多了。然后，雾掀起来了。它整个儿一气卷上了天，像一道剧场的幕布，几乎霎时间便把它那床绗絮的白厚，从我们的眼睛与那幅四年来朝思暮想的景象之间撤走。河对岸，一个奇迹——那个奇迹——已经开始了。它进展得飞快。要知道，此前历次推进为我们挣得的，充其量不过是得以顺着交通壕再往东溜一两英里的自由——倘若连这也算数的话。可现在呢！平坦的桑泰尔原野上，太阳正开始注满一种滤过雾气的光泽；两列望不到尽头的英国火炮、辎重车与部队，正稳步东行，一路无炮火搅扰，走在德国人直到天亮还占据着的土地上。

===Original===
Nothing like it had ever been seen in the war. Above, on our cliff, we turned and stared at each other. We must have looked rather like Cortes’ men agape on their peak. The marvel seemed real; the road lay open and dry across the Red Sea. Far off, six thousand yards off in the shining southeast, tanks and cavalry were at work, shifting and gleaming and looking huge on the skyline of some little rumpled fold of the Santerre plateau. Nearer, the glass could make out an enemy battery, captured complete, caught with the leather caps still on the muzzles of guns. The British dead on the plain, horses and men, lay scattered thinly over wide spaces; scarcely a foundered tank could be seen; the ground had turf on it still; it was only speckled with shell-holes, not disembowelled or flayed. The war had put on a sort of benignity, coming out gallantly on the top of the earth and moving about in the air and the sun; the warm heath, with so few dead upon it, looked almost clement and kind, almost gay after the scabrous mud wastes and the stink of the captured dugouts of the Salient, piled up to ground-level with corpses, some feet uppermost, some heads, like fish in a basket, making you think what wonderful numbers there are of mankind. For a moment, the object of all dream and desire seemed to have come; the flaming sword was gone, and the gate of the garden open.

===Chinese===
这场战争里还从未见过这样的景象。上面，在我们的崖顶，我们彼此转过身来，面面相觑。我们那时的样子，想必颇像科尔特斯（Cortes）的人马立在他们的山峰上瞠目结舌。奇观像是真的；道路敞开着，干燥地横过红海。远处，六千码外闪亮的东南方，坦克与骑兵正在效力，辗转腾挪，闪着微光，在桑泰尔高原某道小小褶皱的天际线上显得硕大无朋。近一点，望远镜能辨认出一队被完整缴获的敌军炮兵，俘获时火炮的炮口上还套着皮炮口帽。平原上的英国阵亡者，马与人，稀稀落落散布在宽阔的地面上；几乎看不见一辆趴窝的坦克；地面还披着草皮；只是星星点点缀着弹坑，不曾被开膛，也不曾被剥皮。战争披上了一层近乎仁善的外衣，堂而皇之地来到地面上，在空气与阳光里走动；那片温热的荒原，上面死者如此之少，看上去几乎温良而慈祥，几乎称得上明媚——尤其是在凸角（the Salient，指伊普尔突出部）那些结痂的泥泞荒地之后，在那些被攻克的地洞的恶臭之后：洞里的尸首堆得齐了地面，有的脚朝天，有的头朝上，像篓子里的鱼，叫你想到人的数目原来这般惊人。有一刹那，一切梦想与欲求的目标仿佛当真来了；那柄火焰之剑不见了，乐园的门敞开着。

===Original===
Too late, as you know. We awoke from delight, and remembered. Four years ago, three years ago, even two years ago, a lasting repose of beatitude might have come with that regaining of paradise! Now! The control of our armies, jealously hugged for so long and used, on the whole, to so little purpose, had passed from us, thrown up in a moment of failure, dissension and dread. While still outnumbered by the enemy we had not won; while on even terms with him we had not won; only under a foreign Commander-in-Chief, and with America’s inexhaustible numbers crowding behind to hold up our old arms, had our just cause begun to prevail. And now the marred triumph would leave us jaded and disillusioned, divided, half bankrupt; sneerers at lofty endeavour, and yet not the men for the plodding of busy and orderly peace; bilious with faiths and enthusiasms gone sour in the stomach. That very night I was to hear the old Australian sneer again. The British corps on their left, at work in the twisty valley and knucklesome banks of the Somme, had failed to get on quite as fast as they and the Canadian troops on their right. “The Canadians were all right of course, but the Tommies! Well, we might have known!” They had got rid, they chuckingly said, of their own last “Tommy officers” now; they wanted to have it quite clear that in England’s war record they were not involved except as our saviours from our sorry selves.

===Chinese===
太迟了，如你所知。我们从欢欣中醒转，想起了这一切。倘若在四年前，三年前，哪怕两年前，随着那场失而复得的乐园，一场持久的至福安宁本可以一并降临！可如今呢！对我们各集团军的指挥权——被我们小气地搂了这么久、总的算来又用得这般没名堂的指挥权——已经离开了我们，在失败、龃龉与惶恐的一刻里被抛了出去。尚居劣势于敌时，我们没有赢；与他势均力敌时，我们也没有赢；唯有在一位外国总司令（Commander-in-Chief）节制之下，又有美利坚那不竭的人潮蜂拥在后，撑住我们这两条老胳膊，我们那正义的事业才开始占得上风。而如今，这场有了瑕疵的凯旋留给我们的，将是疲惫与幻灭，四分五裂，半个破产；一面讥诮一切崇高的奋励，一面自己又不是那种能在繁忙而井然的和平里埋头跋涉的人；满腹郁结的胆汁——信仰与热忱全在胃里发了酸。就在当天夜里，我又听见了澳大利亚人那老一套的讥诮。在他们左面作业的那个英国军，在索姆河曲折的河谷与坑洼嶙峋的岸间，没能推进得像他们自己和右面的加拿大部队那样快。「加拿大人当然是行的，可那些汤米（Tommies）！唉，我们早该如何料到！」他们还咯咯笑着说，如今他们把自己最后一批「汤米军官」也打发掉了；他们巴不得讲个清楚：在英格兰的战争记录里，他们是不相干的局外人——除了作为把我们从我们这不争气的自己手里搭救出来的恩人。

### IV / 四

===Original===
There were other days, during the following months of worm-eaten success, when some mirage of the greater joys which we had forfeited hung for a few moments over the sand. It must be always a strange delight to an infantryman to explore at his ease, in security, ground that to him has been almost as unimaginable as events after death. There is no describing the vesture of enigmatic remoteness enfolding a long-watched enemy line. Tolstoy has tried, but even he does not come up to it. Virgil alone has expressed one sensation of the British overflow over Lille and Cambria, Menin (even the Menin Road had an end) and Bruges and Ostend, Le Câteau and Landrecies, Liège and Namur—

> Desertosque videre locos, litusque relictum.
 

 Classibus hic locus, hic acie certare solebant,
 

 Hic Dolopum manus, hic saevus tendebat Achilles.

===Chinese===
在随后那几个月被虫蛀空了的胜利里，还有别的日子：那些被我们错失了的更大欢愉，某道海市蜃楼，会在沙地上空悬停片刻。对一名步兵来说，得以从容地、安然地踏勘一片在他心中几乎如死后光景一般无从想象的地面，想必永远是一桩奇异的欢愉。一条被人久久凝望的敌军防线所裹着的那身谜样的邈远之衣，是无法描摹的。托尔斯泰（Tolstoy）试过，可连他也够不着。唯有维吉尔（Virgil）说出了英军漫过里尔（Lille）与康布雷（Cambrai，原文误拼作 Cambria）、梅宁（Menin，连梅宁路也是有尽头的）、布鲁日（Bruges）与奥斯坦德（Ostend）、勒卡托（Le Câteau）与朗德勒西（Landrecies）、列日（Liège）与那慕尔（Namur）时的某一种感受——

> 便看见人去营空的营地，与被舍弃的海滨：
 

 这里曾是舰船争逐之地，也是行阵相持之地；
 

 这里屯过多罗普斯的兵马，这里残暴的阿喀琉斯曾支起营帐。

===Original===
And then, wherever you went, till the frontier was reached, everyone was your host and your friend; all the relations of strangers to one another had been transfigured into the sum of all kindness and courtesy. In one mining village in Flanders, quitted that day by the Germans, a woman rushed out of a house to give me a lump of bread, thinking that we must all be as hungry as she and her neighbours. Late one night in Brussels, just after the Germans had gone, I was walking with another officer down the chief street of the city, then densely crowded with radiant citizens. My friend had a wooden stump leg and could not walk very well; and this figure of a khaki-clad man, maimed in the discharge of an Allied obligation to Belgium, seemed suddenly and almost simultaneously to be seen by the whole of that great crowd in all its symbolic value, so that the crowd fell silent and opened out spontaneously along the whole length of the street and my friend had to hobble down the middle of a long avenue of bareheaded men and bowing women.

===Chinese===
还有，此后无论你走到哪里，直到抵达国境，人人都是你的东道、你的朋友；陌生人彼此之间的一切关系，都化作了全部善意与谦敬的总和。在佛兰德一座当天才被德国人放弃的矿村，一位妇人从屋里冲出来，塞给我一块面包，心想我们必定人人饿得同她和她的邻居们一样。德军走后不久，一天深夜，在布鲁塞尔（Brussels），我与另一位军官沿着城中最主要的大街走，街上这时正挤得水泄不通，满是容光焕发的市民。我那位朋友装着一条木桩假腿，走不很稳；而这个人——一个卡其布的身影，为履行盟国对比利时的义务而残了肢体——似乎在几乎同一个瞬间，被那一大群人全体看见了，并且看出了他全部的象征意味；于是人群静了下来，沿着整条长街自发地分开，我的朋友只得蹒跚着走过一条长长的甬道，甬道两侧，是脱了帽的男人与躬身行礼的女人。

===Original===
Finally—last happy thrill of the war—the first stroke of eleven o’clock, on the morning of Armistice Day, on the town clock of Mons, only captured that morning; Belgian civilians and British soldiers crowding together into the square, shaking each other’s hands and singing each other’s national anthems; a little toy-like peal of bells in the church contriving to tinkle out “Tipperary” for our welcome, while our airmen, released from their labours, tumbled and romped overhead like boys turning cartwheels with ecstasy.

===Chinese===
最后——这场战争最后一次快乐的震颤——停战日（Armistice Day）早晨十一点的第一响，敲响在蒙斯（Mons）的市镇大钟上；那座城，当天早晨刚刚攻克。比利时平民与英国士兵蜂拥着同挤进广场，彼此握手，互唱对方的国歌；教堂里一串玩具似的小钟，居然叮叮咚咚地敲出《蒂珀雷里》（Tipperary）来迎我们；我们的飞行员卸下了劳作，在头顶翻滚嬉闹，像快活得直翻筋斗的男孩。

===Original===
What a victory it might have been—the real, the Winged Victory, chivalric, whole and unstained! The bride that our feckless wooing had sought and not won in the generous youth of the war had come to us now: an old woman, or dead, she no longer refused us. We had arrived, like the prince in the poem—

> Too late for love, too late for joy,
 

 Too late, too late!
 

 You loitered on the road too long,
 

 You trifled at the gate:
 

 The enchanted dove upon her branch
 

 Died without a mate;
 

 The enchanted princess in her tower
 

 Slept, died, behind the grate:
 

 Her heart was starving all this while
 

 You made it wait.

===Chinese===
那本可能是一场怎样的胜利啊——真正的、生翼的胜利（the Winged Victory），骑士风的、完整而无玷的胜利！战争那慷慨的青春岁月里，我们那场无能的追求所寻而未得的新娘，如今到我们手里来了：成了老妪，或者死了，总之不再拒绝我们。我们终于赶到了，像诗里的那位王子——

> 爱已太迟，欢愉也已太迟，
 

 太迟了，太迟了！
 

 你在路上流连得太久，
 

 你在门前虚掷了光阴：
 

 枝头那中了魔法的鸽子
 

 无偶而死去；
 

 塔中那中了魔法的公主
 

 在栅栏后睡去、死去：
 

 她的心这整段时日都在挨饿，
 

 而是你让她等待。

## VII “Can’t Believe a Word” / 七 「一个字也不能信」

### I / 一

===Original===
If you cannot hit or kick during a fight, at any rate you can spit. But, to be happy in this arm of the service, you have to feel sure that the adversary is signally fit to be spat upon. Hence, on each side in every war, the civilian will-to-believe that the other side are a set of ogres, every man of them. What a capital fiend the Boer, the man like Botha or Smuts, was made out to be during the last Boer War! He abused the white flag, he sawed a woman in two, he advanced behind screens of niggers; O, he was a great fellow! In 1870 French civilians laid freely to their souls the flattering unction that the Prussians murdered their prisoners. Strong in what was at bottom the same joyous faith, German civilians told you that French officers usually broke their parole. A few choice spirits will even carry this fond observance into the milder climate of sport. A boy of this kidney, while looking on at a vital house match, will give his mind ease by telling a friend what “a lot of stinkers” the other house are. A follower of Cambridge cricket, a man of fifty, in whom you might expect the choler of youth to have cooled, has been found musing darkly over a large photograph of an Oxford eleven. They seemed to me, as is the way of these heroes, to lack nothing of outward charm except the light of intellect in the eye. But “Look at them!” he observed with conviction. “The hangdog expressions! The narrow, ill-set Mongol eyes! The thin, cruel lips! Prejudice apart, would you like to meet that gang in a quiet place on a dark night?” From these sombre reflections he seemed to derive a sort of pasture.

Little doubt, then, as to what had to come when five of the greatest nations on earth were suddenly rolling over and under each other in the dust. While their armies saw to the biting, the snarling was done with a will by the press of Berlin and Vienna, Petrograd, Paris, and London. That we were all fighting foul, every man, was the burden of the strain. Phone and anti-phone, the choric hymn of detraction swelled; if this had been an age of simpler faith there might have been serious fear lest the music should reach the ear of some Jove sitting at his nectar; what if he should say in a rage that those nasty little beasts were at it again, and throw such a comet down on the earth as would settle the hash of us all? But no such fears troubled Europe. And then policy, viewing these operations of instinct, was moved to cut in. Official propaganda began, and one of its stock lines was to help in stoking these fires in the noncombatant heart.

===Chinese===
打架的时候若是不能挥拳抬腿，至少总还能啐上一口。不过，要想在这个兵种里当得快活，你得确信对手格外配得上被啐。于是，在每一场战争的双方，都有平民百姓那种「信之意志」（will-to-believe）：对面那一伙全是妖魔，一个不落。上一次布尔战争（Boer War）期间，布尔人——像博塔（Botha）或史末资（Smuts）那样的人——被渲染成了何等一流的恶魔！他滥用白旗，他把一个女人锯成两半，他在一排排黑人的屏护后面推进；啊，他真是个了不起的家伙！1870 年，法国平民往各自的灵魂上慷慨涂抹一味奉承的膏油：普鲁士人屠杀他们的战俘。德国平民则凭着实质上同一种欢畅的信仰，告诉你法国军官惯于违弃假释誓言。少数雅士甚至会把这份心爱的功课带进运动这方温和些的风土。这一路数的男孩，在观战一场要紧的社际比赛时，会告诉朋友说对面那个社全是「一帮臭货」，好让心里舒坦。一位剑桥板球的拥趸，年已五十——你本以为少年的怒火在他身上总该凉了——曾被人发现正对着一幅牛津十一人队的大照片阴沉地出神。依我看——这类英雄的做派向来如此——他们外表的魅力一无所缺，只缺眼睛里那点智慧之光。可是「瞧瞧他们！」他言之凿凿地说。「那副贼眉鼠眼的鬼相！那双窄狭歪斜的蒙古种眼睛！那两片刻薄的薄唇！撇开成见不谈，黑夜里在僻静地方撞见这帮家伙，你乐意吗？」这些阴郁的想头，于他仿佛是一片可供放牧的草场。

于是，当地上五个最伟大的国家忽然在尘土里互相翻滚碾压起来的时候，注定要来的是什么，也就毫无悬念了。撕咬由它们的军队负责，嗥吠则由柏林与维也纳、彼得格勒、巴黎和伦敦的报界（Press）卖力承揽。「我们全都打得下作，人人如此」，便是这支曲子的主调。一领众和、应答轮唱，那首诋毁的合唱颂歌愈发洪亮；倘若这是个信仰更单纯的时代，说不定真会有人忧心，唯恐这乐声传进某位坐啜琼浆的朱庇特（Jove）耳中：万一他勃然大怒，说那些龌龊的小畜生又闹起来了，朝地上掷下一颗足以把我们大家一并了账的彗星，那可怎么好？但欧洲并没有被这类恐惧搅扰。随后，政策冷眼旁观了本能的这一番运作，便插手进来。官方宣传（propaganda）开始了，它的保留节目之一，便是帮着往非战斗人员心里这些火头添柴。

===Original===
Some of the fuel to hand was fine. The German command fed the best of it all into our bunkers, gratis. It owned that its “frightfulness” plan was no slip, no “indiscretion of a subordinate,” but a policy weighed and picked out﻿—worse than that, an embodied ethical doctrine. A Frenchman, when he is cross with our English virtue, will say that none of us can steal a goose without saying he does it for the public good. But the fey rulers of Germany could not even be content to say it was an act of moral beauty to sink the *Lusitania* or to burn Louvain. They must go on to boast that these scrubby actions were pieces of sound, hard thinking, the only tenable conclusions to impregnable syllogisms. Besides man’s natural aversion to cruel acts, they thus incurred his still more universal distaste for pedants. They delivered themselves into our hand. They were beautiful butts, ready made, like the learned elderly lady in *Roderick Random*, whose bookish philosophy made her desire to “drag the parent by the hoary hair,” and to “toss the sprawling infant on her spear.”

===Chinese===
手头有几种柴火，质地上乘。其中最好的一份，是德军统帅部免费投进我们燃料舱里来的。它承认，它的「恐怖政策」（frightfulness）不是失手，不是「部下的轻率失检」，而是权衡再三、择定不移的一项方针——比这更糟，还是一条已经化为血肉的伦理教义。法国人恼起我们英格兰的德性来，会说：我们这些人偷一只鹅，也非得说自己是出于公众的好处。可德国那班气数将尽的统治者，竟连把击沉卢西塔尼亚号（Lusitania）或焚毁鲁汶（Louvain）说成一桩道德之美事都不满足。他们还必须进而夸口：这些下作的行径，是扎实而严密的思考的产物，是无懈可击的三段论唯一站得住的结论。这样一来，除了人对残忍行径的天生憎恶，他们又招来了人对迂夫子更为普遍的嫌恶。他们把自己送进了我们掌心。他们是现成铸就的绝妙靶子，好比《蓝登传》（Roderick Random）里那位博学的老妇人：她那一脑袋书本哲学，使她渴望「揪住白发苍苍的家长拖行」，渴望「把四肢乱蹬的婴儿挑上矛尖」。


### II / 二

===Original===
But man, rash man, must always be trying to go one better than the best. With this thing of beauty there for our use, crying out to be used, some of our propagandists must needs go beyond it and try to make out that the average German soldier, the docile blond with yellow hair, long skull, and blue, woolgathersome eyes, who swarmed in our corps cages during the last two years of the war, craving for someone, anyone, to give him an order, was one of the monsters who hang about the gates of Virgil’s Hell. If you had to make out a good hanging case against Germany could you, as Hamlet asks his injudicious mother, on that fair mountain cease to feed and batten on this moor? And yet some of us did. The authentic scarecrow, the school of thought that ruled the old German State, was not used for half of what it was worth. But the word went forth that any redeeming traits in the individual German conscript were better hushed up. When he showed extreme courage in an attack, not much must be made of it. When he behaved well to a wounded Englishman, it must be hidden. A war correspondent who mentioned some chivalrous act that a German had done to an Englishman during an action received a rebuking wire from his employer, “Don’t want to hear about any nice, good Germans.”

===Chinese===
然而人，鲁莽的人，总不肯止于至善，非要更上一层不可。有这样一件美的东西摆在手边供我们驱遣、嚷着要被驱遣，我们有些宣传家却偏要越出它去，力图论证：那个寻常的德国士兵——黄头发、长颅、一双心不在焉的蓝眼睛的驯顺金发青年，战争最后两年成群挤在我们各军的俘虏营里，巴望着有谁、随便什么人，给他下一道命令——竟是维吉尔（Virgil）地狱大门边徘徊出没的怪物之一。倘若你不得不给德国编构一桩够上绞刑的案子，你能——像哈姆雷特（Hamlet）质问他那位欠思量的母后那样——放着那座秀美的山峦不取食，偏跑到这片荒沼上饕餮吗？我们当中偏偏就有人这么干了。那具货真价实的稻草人——统治着旧德意志帝国的那一派思想——竟连其价值的一半都不曾被用足。可是话已传下：德国应征兵个人身上任何可取的品质，最好一律按下不表。他在进攻中表现出超凡的勇气时，不宜多着笔墨。他善待一个受伤的英国人时，必须隐去不提。一位战地记者提到某个德国人在战斗中对英国人做过一件侠义之举，竟收到雇主打来的一封训斥电报：「不想听什么好心乖巧的德国人。」

===Original===
Even in the very temple of humourless shabbiness comedy may contrive to keep up a little shrine of her own, and on this forlorn altar the dread of “crying up anything German” laid, now and then, an undesigned offering. One worthy field censor was suddenly taken aback by a dangerous flaw in a war correspondent’s exultant account of a swiftly successful British attack. “Within ten minutes from zero,” I think the correspondent had written, “our men were sitting at ease on what had been the enemy’s parapet, smoking good German cigars.” “Hullo!” said the censor, “this won’t do. ‘Good’ German cigars. *Good* German cigars! No! ‘Good’ must come out.” And come out it did. Like the morale of his troops, like the generalship of his chiefs, the foeman’s tobacco had to be bad. It was the time when some of our patriotic pundits found out that Mommsen’s Roman history was all wrong, and that Poppo did not half know his Thucydides.

===Chinese===
即便在这座了无幽默的寒碜大庙里，喜剧女神也总能设法保住一座自己的小神龛；在这座冷落的祭坛上，对「吹捧任何德国东西」的忌惮，时不时还供上一份非出本意的祭品。某位可敬的战地审查官，就被一篇战地记者欢腾报道里的危险瑕疵吓了一跳——那报道写的是英军一场迅捷得手的攻击。「零时过后十分钟之内」，记者写的似乎是这样，「我们的人已安坐在不久前还是敌人胸墙的地方，抽着上好的德国雪茄。」「嚯！」审查官说，「这可不行。『上好的』德国雪茄。*上好的*德国雪茄！不行！『上好的』必须删去。」于是就删去了。一如其部队的士气，一如其首领的将道，敌方的烟草也必须低劣。那阵子，我们有些爱国的博学之士，正好发现蒙森（Mommsen）的罗马史全错了，又发现波波（Poppo）对修昔底德（Thucydides）简直全然不通。

===Original===
Of all this kind of swordsmanship the most dashing feat was the circulation of the “corpse factory” story. German troops, it was written in part of our Press, had got, in certain places near their front, a proper plant for boiling down the fat of their own dead. It was not said whether the product was to be used as a food, or as a lubricant or illuminant only. Chance brought me into one of the reputed seats of this refinement of frugality. It was on ground that our troops had just taken, in 1918. At Bellicourt the St. Quentin Canal goes into a long tunnel. Some little way in from its mouth you could find, with a flash-lamp, a small doorway cut in the tunnel’s brick wall, on the towpath side of the canal. The doorway led to the foot of a narrow staircase that wound up through the earth till it came to an end in a room about twenty feet long. It, too, was subterranean, but now its darkness was pierced by one sharp-edged shaft of sunlight let in through a neat round hole cut in the five or six feet of earth above. Loaves, bits of meat, and articles of German equipment lay scattered about, and two big dixies or cauldrons, like those in which we stewed our tea, hung over two heaps of cold charcoal. Eight or ten bodies, lying pell-mell, nearly covered half of the floor. They showed the usual effects of shellfire. Another body, disembowelled and blown almost to rags, lay across one of the dixies and mixed with the puddle of coffee that it contained. A quite simple case. Shells had gone into cookhouses of ours, long before then, and had messed up the cooks with the stew.

===Chinese===
这一路剑法里最潇洒的一击，是「尸体工厂」（corpse factory）故事的散播。我国一部分报界写道：德军在其前线附近的某些地方，建起了正经的工厂，专门熬炼自家死者的脂肪。至于产品是打算充作食物，还是仅作润滑剂或照明剂之用，则未交代。机缘把我带进了这门节俭妙艺的一处传闻据点。那是 1918 年，在我们部队刚刚夺占的地面上。在贝利库尔（Bellicourt），圣康坦运河（St. Quentin Canal）钻进一条长长的隧道。离洞口不远，你打着手电，能在运河纤道一侧的隧道砖壁上找到一扇小门。门后是一道窄梯的起点，楼梯在泥土中盘旋而上，尽头是一间约二十英尺长的屋子。它也在地下，但此刻，一束轮廓锋利的日光从顶上五六英尺土层中开出的一个齐整圆孔斜插进来，刺破了屋里的黑暗。面包、碎肉和德军装具散落四处，两口大行军锅（dixies）——就跟我们炖茶用的那种一样——架在两堆冷炭上。八具或十具尸体横七竖八，几乎盖住半间地面，显现着炮火杀伤的惯常景象。另有一具，破了膛，几乎被炸成碎布条，横搭在一口行军锅上，同锅里的咖啡积淖混作一处。案情再简单不过。早在那以前许久，炮弹就打进过我们的炊事房，把炊事兵和炖菜搅到一起过。


### III / 三

===Original===
An Australian sergeant, off duty and poking about, like a good Australian, for something to see, had come up the stairs, too. He had heard the great fat-boiling yarn, and how this was the latest seat of the industry. Sadly he surveyed the disappointing scene. Ruefully he noted the hopelessly normal nature of all the proceedings that had produced it. Then he broke the silence in which we had made our several inspections. “Can’t believe a word you read, sir, can you?” he said with some bitterness. Life had failed to yield one of its advertised marvels. The Press had lied again. The propagandist myth about Germans had cracked up once more. “Can’t believe a word you read” had long been becoming a kind of catchphrase in the army. And now another good man had been duly confirmed in the faith, ordained as a minister of the faith, that whatever your pastors and masters tell you had best be assumed to be just a bellyful of east wind.

===Chinese===
一位澳大利亚中士下了班，正像个地道的澳大利亚人那样东扒西寻，想找点东西看看，也上了楼来。他听说过熬尸油的大故事，也听说过这里正是这门行当最新的据点。他怏怏地打量着这令人失望的现场，懊恼地记下：造就这一切的全部经过，寻常得无可救药。随后，他打破了我们各自察看时的一片沉默。「报上写的东西一个字也不能信，长官，是不是？」他说，带着几分怨气。生活没能交出它广告吹嘘的一桩奇迹。报界又撒了一回谎。关于德国人的宣传神话又崩了一回。「报上的话一个字也不能信」，久已在军中变成一句口头禅。而如今，又一位好人如期受了坚信礼，被按立为这一信仰的执事：凡你的牧师与师长告诉你的一切，最好都假定不过是一肚子东风。

===Original===
Partly it came of the nature﻿—which could not be helped by that time﻿—of war correspondence. In the first months of the war our General Staff, being what we had made it, treated British war correspondents as pariah dogs. They might escape arrest so long as they kept out of sight; that was about the sum of their privileges. Long before the end of the war the Chiefs of Staff of our several armies received them regularly on the eve of every battle, explained to them the whole of our plans and hopes, gave them copies of our most secret objective and barrage maps; every perilous secret we had was put into their keeping. A little later still an Army Commander would murmur, with very little indistinctness, if he thought the war correspondents had not been writing enough about his army of late. After the Armistice Sir Douglas Haig made them a speech of thanks and praise on the great bridge over the Rhine at Cologne, and at the Peace all the regular pariah dogs were offered knighthoods.

===Chinese===
这一半也出自战地报道这行当的天性——到那时已经无可救药的天性。开战头几个月，我们的总参谋部（General Staff）——它是我们自己一手造就的那副样子——把英国战地记者当作贱民野狗对待。只要不露面，他们就可以免于被捕；他们的特权，加起来大致便是这些。而远在战争结束之前，我们各集团军的参谋长们已每逢大战前夜定期接见他们，向他们讲解我们全部的计划与希望，把最机密的目标图和弹幕图的副本交给他们；我们手中每一件性命攸关的机密，都托付到了他们手里。再往后，一位集团军司令若觉得战地记者近来为自己麾下的部队写得不够，还会意思毫不含糊地咕哝上两句。停战之后，道格拉斯·黑格爵士（Sir Douglas Haig）在科隆（Cologne）横跨莱茵河（Rhine）的大桥上，向他们致了一篇感谢与褒扬的演说；到了和会，那群正规编制的野狗，个个获授爵士之衔。


### IV / 四

===Original===
The Regular Army had set out by taking a war correspondent to be, ex officio, a low fellow paid to extract kitchen literature from such private concerns of the military profession as wars. It harboured the curious notion that it would be possible in this century to feed the nation at home on communiqués from G.H.Q. alone or eked out with “Eyewitness” stuff﻿—official “word-painting” by some Regular Officer with a tincture of letters. With that power of learning things, only just not too late, which distinguishes our Regular Army from the Bourbons, it presently saw that this plan had broken down. About the same time the Regular Army began to recognise in the abhorred war correspondent a man whom it had known at school, and who had gone to the university about the time when it, the Army, was going into the Army Class. That was enough. Foul as was his profession, still he might be a decent fellow; he might not want to injure his country.

===Chinese===
正规军起初认定，战地记者依其职分便是个下等人，拿钱专从战争这一类军界私务里榨取「厨房文学」。它还怀着一个古怪的念头：在本世纪，单靠总司令部（G.H.Q.）发出的公报，或再掺上些「目击者」栏的文字——由某位沾过几分文墨的正规军军官执笔的官方「文字写生」——就足以把国内的国民喂饱。凭着那副学东西的功夫——学得恰好不算太迟，正是这功夫把我们的正规军同波旁王朝（Bourbons）区别开来——它不久便看出，此计已告失败。大约同一时候，正规军又渐渐认出，那个被它深恶痛绝的战地记者，原来是它上学时就认识的人，而且此人进大学的年月，恰逢它自己进陆军班（Army Class）深造。这就够了。行当虽贱，人倒未必不体面；他未必想坑害自己的国家。

===Original===
When these reflections were dawning slowly over the Regular Army mind it happened﻿—Sir Douglas Haig having a mind himself﻿—that his Chief of Intelligence was a fully educated man with a good fifty percent more of brains, imagination, decision, and initiative than the average of his fellow-Regulars on the Staff. He knew something of the Press at first hand. Being a Scotsman, he regarded writers and well-read people with interest and not with alarm.
