Under his command the policy of helping the Press rose to its maximum. War correspondents were given the “status,” almost the rank, of officers. Actual officers were detailed to see to their comfort, to pilot them about the front, to secure their friendly treatment by all ranks and at all headquarters. Never were war correspondents so helped, shielded and petted before. And, almost without an exception, they were good men. Only one or two black sheep of the trade would try to make a reader believe that they had seen things which they had not. The general level of personal and professional honour, of courage, public spirit, and serious enterprise, was high. No average Staff Officer could talk with the average British correspondent without feeling that this was a sound human being and had a better mind than his own﻿—that he knew more, had seen more, and had been less deadened by the coolie work of a professional routine. When once known, the war correspondents were trusted and liked﻿—by the Staff.

===Chinese===
当这些想法在正规军的头脑里缓缓破晓时，恰好——道格拉斯·黑格爵士自己也有个头脑——他的情报处长是位受过完整教育的人，比起参谋部同僚正规军人的平均水准，脑力、想象力、决断与主动精神足足多出五成。他对报界有第一手的了解。身为苏格兰人，他看待作家与饱学之士，怀着兴味而非惊惶。在他治下，扶助报界的政策攀升到了顶点。战地记者获得了「身份」（status），几乎就是军官的军衔。正式军官被指派去照料他们的起居，领着他们周游前线，并确保各级官兵、各处司令部都善待他们。战地记者从未受过这样的扶助、庇护与娇宠。而且，他们几乎无一例外都是好人。全行当里只有一两匹害群之马，会试图让读者相信自己见过并未见过的东西。个人与职业的荣誉、勇气、公益精神与严肃进取的总体水准是高的。随便哪位寻常的参谋军官，同随便哪位寻常的英国战地记者谈过话，都不能不感到：这是个靠得住的人，心智胜过自己——他懂得更多，见得更多，受职业性苦役的磨钝也更少。一经相识，战地记者便得到信任与喜爱——来自参谋部。

===Original===
There lay the trouble. They lived in the Staff world, its joys and its sorrows, not in the combatant world. The Staff was both their friend and their censor. How could they show it up when it failed? One of the first rules of field censorship was that from war correspondents “there must be no criticism of authority or command”; how could they disobey that? They would visit the front now and then, as many Staff Officers did, but it could be only as afternoon callers from one of the many mansions of G.H.Q., that heaven of security and comfort. When autumn twilight came down on the haggard trench world of which they had caught a quiet noonday glimpse they would be speeding west in Vauxhall cars to lighted châteaux gleaming white among scatheless woods. Their staple emotions before a battle were of necessity akin to those of the Staff, the racehorse-owner or trainer exalted with brilliant hopes, thrilled by the glorious uncertainty of the game, the fascinating nicety of every preparation, and feeling the presence of horrible fatigues and the nearness of multitudinous deaths chiefly as a dim, sombre background that added importance to the rousing scene, and not as things that need seriously cloud the spirit or qualify delight in a plan.

===Chinese===
麻烦恰在这里。他们生活在参谋部的世界里，与它同甘共苦，而不在战斗人员的世界里。参谋部既是他们的朋友，又是他们的审查官。它失手的时候，他们怎么能揭它的短呢？战地检查的第一批规则里便有一条：战地记者「不得对当局或指挥有任何批评」；他们怎么可能违抗？他们也时不时造访前线，同许多参谋军官一样，但那只能是来自总司令部众多宅第之一——那座安稳与舒适的天堂——的午后访客。当秋日的暮色降落到那片形容枯槁的战壕世界上——他们不过曾在正午的安静里对它投过一瞥——他们正坐着沃克斯豪尔（Vauxhall）汽车向西疾驰，奔向灯火通明、在无损的树林间莹莹泛白的庄园古堡。大战之前，他们惯常的情绪必然近于参谋部的情绪，近于赛马马主或驯马师的情绪：为灿烂的希望而飞扬，为这场游戏光荣的悬念、为每项筹备迷人的精微而心颤，而把可怕的困瘁之在场与累累死亡之迫近，主要当作一层昏昧、阴沉的背景——只是给这激昂的场面添分量，而不是什么当真会蔽暗心神、折损一番计划之乐的东西。


### V / 五

===Original===
“Our casualties will be enormous,” a General at G.H.Q. said with the utmost serenity on the eve of one of our great attacks in 1917. The average war correspondent﻿—there were golden exceptions﻿—insensibly acquired the same cheerfulness in face of vicarious torment and danger. In his work it came out at times in a certain jauntiness of tone that roused the fighting troops to fury against the writer. Through his despatches there ran a brisk implication that regimental officers and men enjoyed nothing better than “going over the top”; that a battle was just a rough, jovial picnic; that a fight never went on long enough for the men; that their only fear was lest the war should end on this side of the Rhine. This, the men reflected in helpless anger, was what people at home were offered as faithful accounts of what their friends in the field were thinking and suffering.

===Chinese===
「我们的伤亡将会极其巨大。」1917 年我们一次大战役的前夜，总司令部里一位将军以极度的安详说道。寻常的战地记者——金子般的例外自然是有——面对这种隔了一层的苦痛与危险，不知不觉也习得了同样的开朗。这在他的笔下，时时流为一种轻快活泛的调子，惹得作战部队对执笔者怒不可遏。他的通讯里贯穿着一股脆生生的言外之意：团级军官与士兵最享受的莫过于「跃出壕顶」（going over the top）；一场战斗不过是一趟粗放而欢快的野餐；仗总打得不够久，官兵们意犹未尽；他们唯一的畏惧，是怕战争在莱茵河这一边就收了场。官兵们在无可如何的愤怒里想道：这就是端给国内人们的东西，竟号称是他们前线亲友所思所受的忠实报道。

===Original===
Most of the men had, all their lives, been accepting “what it says ’ere in the paper” as being presumptively true. They had taken the Press at its word without checking. Bets had been settled by reference to a paper. Now, in the biggest event of their lives, hundreds of thousands of men were able to check for themselves the truth of that workaday Bible. They fought in a battle or raid, and two days after they read, with jeers on their lips, the account of “the show” in the papers. They felt they had found the Press out. The most bloody defeat in the history of Britain, a very world’s wonder of valour frustrated by feckless misuse, of regimental glory and Staff shame, might occur on the Ancre on July 1, 1916, and our Press come out bland and copious and graphic, with nothing to show that we had not had quite a good day﻿—a victory really. Men who had lived through the massacre read the stuff open-mouthed. Anything, then, could figure as anything else in the Press﻿—as its own opposite even. Black was only an aspect of white. With a grin at the way he must have been taken in up to now, the fighting soldier gave the Press up. So it comes that each of several million ex-soldiers now reads every solemn appeal of a Government, each beautiful speech of a Premier or earnest assurance of a body of employers with that maxim on guard in his mind﻿—“You can’t believe a word you read.”

===Chinese===
这些官兵一辈子都在把「报纸上这儿写着的事儿」当作推定无误的实情接受下来。他们向来不假核对，对报界言听计从。打赌起了争执，也是翻报纸了断。如今，在一生最重大的事件里，几十万人得以亲自核验这部日常圣经的真实性。他们打了一场仗或一次袭击，两天之后，便嘴角挂着讥诮，读报上关于「那场好戏」的报道。他们觉得，自己总算把报界揪住了尾巴。英国历史上最血腥的败绩——勇气遭无能的浪掷而受挫，堪称一桩真正的世界奇观；团队的光荣与参谋部的耻辱——尽可以在 1916 年 7 月 1 日发生在昂克尔河（Ancre）上，而我国的报界照旧和颜悦色、长篇累牍、有声有色地出了报，看不出我们这一天过得不算好——简直是一场胜利。从那场屠杀里活下来的人，张着嘴读这些东西。这样说来，在报界笔下，任何东西都可以充作任何别的东西——甚至充作它自己的反面。黑，不过是白的一个侧影。作战的士兵咧嘴一笑——笑自己此前不知被瞒了多少——就此把报界撒手放下了。于是就有了今天这样的局面：几百万退役军人当中的每一个，如今读到政府的每一篇庄重呼吁、首相的每一篇漂亮演说、一伙雇主的每一番恳切保证，心头都守着那句格言——「报上的话，一个字也不能信。」

===Original===
*To*

Aubrey Montague

Of Lautoka, Fiji
“We twa hae paidlet i’ the burn.”

===Chinese===
*献给*

奥布里·蒙塔古

斐济劳托卡人
「我们俩曾一起蹚过那溪流。」

## XVI Fair Warning / 十六 郑重的警告

### I / 一

===Original===

To give the cure a chance we must have a long quiet time. And we must secure it now.

For the moment, no doubt, war has gone out of fashion; it pines in the shade, like the old horsehair covers for sofas, or antimacassars of lace. Hardly a day can pass, even now, but someone finds out, with a start and a look of displeasure, that war has been given its chance and has not done quite so well as it ought to have done. One man will write to the Press, in dismay, that the meals in the Simplon express are not what they were in 1910. Another, outward bound by Calais to Cannes, has found that the hot-water plant in his sleeping-compartment struck work﻿—and that in a specially cold sector down by the Alps. Thus does war in the end, knock at the doors of us all: like the roll of the earth upon its axis, it brings us, if not death or destitution or some ashy taste in the mouth, at any rate a sense of a fallen temperature in our bunks. However nonporous our minds, there does slowly filter into us the thought that when a million of a country’s men of working age have just been killed there may be a plaguey dearth of the manpower needed to keep in pleasant order the lavatories of its *trains de luxe*. Sad to think how many tender minds, formed in those Elysian years﻿—Elysian for anyone who was not poor﻿—before the war, will have to suffer, probably for many years, these little shocks of realization.

Surely there never was any time in the life of the world when it was so good, in the way of obvious material comfort, to be alive and fairly well-to-do as it was before the war. Think of the speed and comfort and relative cheapness of the Orient Express; of the way you could wander, unruined, through long aesthetic holidays in Italy and semi-aesthetic, semi-athletic holidays in the Alps; of the weekend accessibility of London from Northern England; of the accessibility of public schools for the sons of the average parson or doctor; of the penny post, crown of our civilization﻿—torn from us while the abhorred halfpenny post for circulars was yet left; of the Income Tax just large enough to give us a pleasant sense of grievance patriotically borne, but not to prostrate us, winter and summer, with two “elbow jolts” or “Mary Ann punches” like those of the perfected modern prizefighter.

Many sanguine well-to-do people dreamt, in the August of 1914, that the war, besides attaining its primary purpose of beating the enemy, would disarrange none of these blessings; that it would even have as a byproduct a kind of “old-time Merrie England,” with the working classes cured of the thirst for wages and deeply convinced that everyone who was not one of themselves was a natural ruler over them. For any little expense to which the war might put us the Germans would pay, and our troops would return home to dismiss all trade-union officials and to regard the upper and middle classes thenceforth as a race of heaven-sent colonels﻿—men to be followed, feared, and loved. Ah, happy vision, beautiful dream!﻿—like Thackeray’s reverie about having a very old and rich aunt. The dreamer awakes among the snows of the Mont Cenis with a horrid smell in the corridor and the hot-water pipes out of order. And so war has gone out of fashion, even among cheery well-to-do-people.

===Chinese===

要让这剂药有机会奏效，我们必须有一段长久而安静的时日。而且我们必须现在就把它稳稳拿到手。

眼下，战争无疑已经过时；它在背阴处憔悴着，像旧日沙发的马鬃椅套，或花边织的椅背防污巾。即便是现在，几乎没有一天不有人猛然一惊、面带愠色地发现：战争得到过它的机会，却干得远不如它本该干的那样好。有人会惶惶然投书报界（Press），说辛普伦快车（Simplon express）上的餐饭已不复 1910 年的光景。另一个人取道加来（Calais）前往戛纳（Cannes），发现卧铺车厢里的热水锅炉罢了工——而且是在阿尔卑斯山（Alps）脚下一段格外寒冷的地段。战争到头来就这样叩响我们每个人的家门：像大地绕轴自转一样，它带给我们的，纵然不是死亡、赤贫，或嘴里某种灰烬似的味道，至少也是铺位里一阵下降的凉意。我们的心智无论多么不透水，那个念头总会慢慢渗进来：当一个国家一百万名劳动年龄的男子刚刚丧命，要把这个国家豪华列车（trains de luxe）的盥洗室保持得舒适体面，人手怕是要缺得恼人。想起来不免难过：多少娇嫩的心智——战前那些极乐年代（对不穷的人而言确是极乐年代）里养成的心智——恐怕还要在往后许多年里，承受这一记记恍悟的小小震击。

就触手可及的物质安逸而论，在世界的一生中，从来不曾有一个时代，做个活人、而且家境宽裕的活人，能像战前那样好过。想想东方快车（Orient Express）的速度、舒适与相对低廉；想想你可以一路漫游而不致破产，在意大利度过漫长的审美假期，在阿尔卑斯山度过半审美半运动的假期；想想从英格兰北部去伦敦度周末何等便捷；想想寻常牧师或医生的儿子也进得起公学（public school）；想想一便士邮政——我们文明的冠冕——它被从我们手里夺走了，那可恨的专寄传单的半便士邮资却还留着；再想想所得税——它恰好大到让我们怀着愉快的感觉，爱国地忍受一种冤屈，却不至于像炉火纯青的现代职业拳师那样，冬夏都补上两记「肘击」或「玛丽·安式重拳」，把我们打趴在地。

1914 年 8 月，许多乐天的富足人士梦想着：这场战争，除了达成打败敌人的首要目的之外，一丝一毫也不会搅乱这些福气；它甚至还会附带产出一种「昔日的快活英格兰」（Merrie England）——工人阶级被治好对工资的渴求，并深深确信凡不是他们一伙的，个个都是天生的统治者。战争可能让我们破费的那一点小钱，自然由德国人来付；我们的部队将凯旋还乡，遣散所有的工会官员，从此把上等阶级与中等阶级奉为一族天降的上校——可追随、可敬畏、也可爱戴的人。啊，幸福的憧憬，美妙的梦！——正像萨克雷（Thackeray）那篇梦想自己有一位极老极富的姑母的遐想。做梦人在塞尼山（Mont Cenis）的雪野间醒来，走廊里一股可怕的气味，热水管也出了毛病。于是战争过时了——连在快乐的有钱人当中也过时了。

### II / 二

===Original===

But may it not come into fashion again? Do not all the great fashions move in cycles, like stars? When our wars with Napoleon were just over, and all the bills still to be paid, and the number of visibly one-legged men at its provisional maximum, must not many simple minds have thought that surely man would never idealize any business so beastly and costly again? And then see what happened. We were all tranquilly feeding, good as gold, in the deep and pleasant meadows of the long Victorian peace when from some of the frailest animals in the pasture there rose a plaintive bleat for war. It was the very lambs that began it. “Shall we never have carnage?” Stevenson, the consumptive, sighed to a friend. Henley, the cripple, wrote a longing “Song of the Sword.” Out of the weak came forth violence. Bookish men began to hug the belief that they had lost their way in life; they felt that they were Neys or Nelsons manqués, or cavalry leaders lost to the world. “If I had been born a corsair or a pirate,” thought Mr. Tappertit, musing among the ninepins, “I should have been all right.” Fragile dons became connoisseurs, faute de mieux, of prizefighting; they talked, nineteen to the dozen, about the still, strong man and “straight-flung words and few,” adored “naked force,” averred they were not cotton-spinners all, and deplored the cankers of a quiet world and a long peace. Some of them entered quite hotly, if not always expertly, into the joys and sorrows of what they called “Tommies,” and chafed at the many rumoured refusals of British innkeepers to serve them, little knowing that only by these great acts of renunciation on the part of licensees has many a gallant private been saved from falling into that morgue an “officer house,” and having his beer congealed in the glass by the refrigerative company of colonels.

The father and mother of this virilistic movement among the well-read were Mr. Andrew Lang, the most donnish of wits, and one of the wittiest. Lang would review a new book in a great many places at once. So, when he blessed, his blessing would carry as far as the more wholly literal myrrh and frankincense wafted abroad by the hundred hands of Messrs. Boot. The fame of Mr. Rider Haggard was one of Lang’s major products. Mr. Haggard was really a man of some mettle. By persons fitted to judge he was believed to have at his fingers’ ends all the best of what is known and thought by mankind about turnips and other crops with which they may honourably and usefully rotate. But it was for turning his back upon these humdrum sustainers of life and writing, in a rich Corinthian style, accounts of fancy “slaughters grim and great,” that his flame lived and spread aloft, as Milton says, in the pure eyes and perfect witness of Lang. Another nursling of Lang’s was the wittier Kipling, then a studious youth exuding Border ballads and Bret Harte from every pore, but certified to carry about him, on paper, the proper smell of blood and tobacco.

Deep answered unto deep. In Germany, too, the pibrochs of the professors were rending the skies, and poets of C4 medical grade were tearing the mask from the hideous face of peace. The din throughout the bookish parts of Central and Western Europe suggested to an irreverent mind a stage with a quaint figure of some shortsighted pedagogue of tradition coming upon it, round-shouldered, curly-toed, print-fed, physically inept, to play the part of the warhorse in Job, swallowing the ground with fierceness and rage, and “saying among the trumpets ‘Ha, ha!’ ” You may see it all as a joke. Or as something rather more than a joke, in its effects. Mr. Yeats suggested that an all-seeing eye might perceive the Trojan War to have come because of a tune that a boy had once piped in Thessaly. What if all our millions of men had to be killed because some academic Struwwelpeter, fifty years since, took on himself to pipe up “Take the nasty peace away!” and kick the shins of Concord, his most kindly nurse?

===Chinese===

可是，它难道不会重新时兴起来吗？一切大的时髦，难道不都像星辰一样循环运转吗？我们同拿破仑（Napoleon）的诸般战争刚刚打完，账单都还没有付清，明眼可见的独腿男子的数目还停在暂时的最高点上，那时候，多少头脑简单的人必定想过：人类总该再也不会把一门如此下作又如此赔钱的营生加以理想化了吧？然后呢，且看后来如何。我们正金子般乖乖地，在维多利亚长期和平那深沉而惬意的牧场上吃草，草场里几头最羸弱的牲口却发出恳切的咩叫，讨要起战争来。挑头的偏偏是羊羔。「难道我们永远不会有屠戮了吗？」患痨病的史蒂文森（Stevenson）向一位朋友叹道。跛足的亨利（Henley）写下一首满怀渴慕的《剑之歌》（Song of the Sword）。「从软弱中生出了强暴。」书本子里的人开始死死抱定一个信念：他们在人生路上走岔了道；他们觉得自己是蹉跎了的内伊（Ney）或纳尔逊（Nelson），是被这个世界错失了的骑兵统帅。「我要是生下来就是个私掠客或海盗，」塔珀蒂特先生（Mr. Tappertit）在九柱戏的木柱间寻思，「我就不愁没出息了。」纤弱的学究们退而求其次，做起了拳击的鉴赏家；他们口若悬河，大谈沉静的强者和「直截而稀少的言辞」，崇拜「赤裸的武力」，断言自己并非个个都是纺纱匠，并且悲叹安静的世界与长期的和平养出的毒疮。他们当中有些人还十分起劲地——虽说未必内行——体验起他们所谓「汤米」（Tommies）们的甘苦来，并且对传闻中英国的酒馆主人屡屡拒不招待汤米的行状愤愤不平；殊不知，全亏卖酒执照人这些伟大的克己之举，多少英勇的列兵才免于跌进「军官之家」那座太平间，免于在上校们冷气的陪伴下，眼看杯中的啤酒冻结起来。

这班读书人中间那场尚武运动（virilistic movement）的生身父母，是安德鲁·兰先生（Mr. Andrew Lang）——才士中最具学究气的一位，又是最具才气的一位。兰惯于同时在许多地方评论同一本新书。因此，当他赐福的时候，他的福泽传得同布茨公司（Messrs. Boot）上百只手撒向四方的、更其字面意义上的没药与乳香一样远。哈格德先生（Mr. Rider Haggard）的名声，便是兰的主要产品之一。哈格德先生倒真是个有些气概的人。够格的评断者相信，关于芜菁，以及可以同芜菁体面而有益地轮作的其他作物，人类所知所想的精华，他无一不烂熟于指端。可是他的火焰之所以能在高处存活、蔓延——如弥尔顿（Milton）所说，活在兰的纯净眼眸与完美的见证里——却是因为他背转身去，抛开这些养活世界的平淡作物，反倒用一种富丽的科林斯派文笔，去铺陈一场场凭空虚构的「狰狞而伟大的屠杀」。

「深渊便与深渊响应。」在德国，教授们的风笛曲也同样撕裂着天空，体检 C4 等级的诗人们正从和平那张狰狞的脸上撕下面具。中欧与西欧所有书香地界的这一片喧嚣，在不大虔敬的心里唤起这样一幅舞台景象：传统里那种近视的教书先生——圆着肩膀、内八字脚、吃印刷品长大、四肢百无一用——蹊跷地登了场，来扮演约伯记（Job）里的战马，「以猛烈和怒气吞吃大地」，还「在号筒中说：『呵，哈！』」你可以把这一切看成一个玩笑。或者，就其后果而论，看成比玩笑严重得多的东西。叶芝先生（Mr. Yeats）提示过：一只全视之眼或许会看出，特洛伊战争（Trojan War）之降临，只因一个男孩当年在色萨利（Thessaly）吹过一支曲子。那么，倘若我们这几百万男子之所以不得不死，只是因为五十年前某位学界的「蓬头彼得」（Struwwelpeter）擅自主张，吹起一声口哨——「把讨厌的和平拿走！」——还朝他最慈爱的乳母、和平女神（Concord）的胫骨踢了一脚呢？

### III / 三

===Original===

If he did, it was natural. All Struwwelpeters are natural. All heirs-apparent are said to take the opposite side to their fathers still on the throne. And those learned men were heirs to the age of the Crystal Palace, the age of the first “Locksley Hall,” with its “parliament of man” and “federation of the world,” the age that laid a railway line along the city moat of Amiens and opened capacious Hôtels de la Paix throughout Latin Europe, the age when passports withered and Baedeker was more and more, the age that in one of its supreme moments of ecstasy founded the London International College, an English public school (now naturally dead) in which the boys were to pass some of their terms among the heathen in Germany or France.

The cause of peace, like all triumphant causes, good as they may be, had made many second-rate friends. It had become safe, and even sound, for the worldly to follow. The dullards, the people who live by phrases alone, the scribes who write by rote and not with authority﻿—most of these had drifted into its service. It had become a provocation, a challenge, vexing those “discoursing wits” who “count it,” Bacon says, “a bondage to fix a belief.” A rebound had to come. And those arch-rebounders were men of the teaching and writing trades, wherein the newest fashions in thought are most eagerly canvassed, and any inveterate acquiescence in mere common sense afflicts many bosoms with the fear of lagging yards and yards behind the foremost files of time; perhaps﻿—that keenest agony﻿—of having nothing piquant or startling to say, no little bombs handy for conversational purposes. “I sat down,” the deserving young author says in *The Vicar of Wakefield*, “and, finding that the best things remained to be said on the wrong side, I resolved to write a book that should be wholly new. I therefore dressed up three paradoxes with some ingenuity. They were false, indeed, but they were new. The jewels of truth have been so often imported by others that nothing was left for me to import but some splendid things that, at a distance, looked every bit as well.” “Peace on earth, goodwill towards men,” “Blessed are the peacemakers”﻿—these and the like might be jewels; but they were demoded; they were old tags; they were clichés of bourgeois morality; they were vieux jeu, like the garnets with which, in *She Stoops to Conquer*, the young woman of fashion declined to be pacified when her heart cried out for the diamonds.

===Chinese===

他若真这么干了，那是天性使然。一切「蓬头彼得」都是天性使然。据说凡储君，总要站到还坐在宝座上的父王的对立面去。而那些饱学之士，正是水晶宫（Crystal Palace）时代的一代储君——那是《洛克斯利堂》（Locksley Hall）初版的时代，带着它的「人类的议会」与「世界的联邦」；那时代沿着亚眠（Amiens）城墙下的壕沟铺起铁路，在整个拉丁欧洲开设起宽敞的「和平大饭店」（Hôtels de la Paix）；那时代护照日渐凋零，贝德克尔指南（Baedeker）却一版多过一版；那时代还在一次极乐的巅峰时刻创办了伦敦国际学院——一所英国的公学（如今自然已经死了），学生们要在其中把某些学期放到德国或法兰西的异教徒中间去度过。

和平的事业，同一切胜利在望的事业一样——无论那些事业本身多么好——结交了许多二流的朋友。它已经变得安全，甚至变得稳妥，可供世故人放心追随。蠢人们，单凭套话过活的人们，照本宣科而胸无灼见的文丐们——这些人大半漂进了它的麾下效力。于是它成了一种冒犯、一种挑衅，惹恼了那些「清谈的才智之士」——培根（Bacon）说，他们「把确立一种信仰看作一种束缚」。反弹遂在必然之中。而反弹的健将们，又恰恰出自教书与卖文这两种行当；在这两种行当里，思想界最新的时髦受到最热烈的聚会论衡，而对区区常识积习难改的默许，则使许多胸中惴惴，唯恐被时代最前列的队伍甩下几码又几码；或许——那最钻心的苦楚——唯恐说不出一句泼辣或骇人的话，拿不出几枚备于席间投掷的小炸弹。《威克菲尔德的牧师》（The Vicar of Wakefield）里那位可嘉的青年作者说：「我坐下来，发现最好的话还都留在错的一边没人说，便决意写一本全书崭新的书。于是我略施巧思，乔装了三个悖论。它们确实是假的，但它们是新的。真理的宝石被人进口得这样勤，轮到我，除了进口一些华彩的东西——远远望去成色毫不逊色的东西——再没有什么可进口的了。」「在地上平安归与他所喜悦的人」，「使人和睦的人有福了」——这些以及诸如此类的话，本来也许是宝石；可是它们过时了；它们是老掉了牙的套语；是小市民道德的陈词滥调；是老掉牙的旧把戏（vieux jeu），一如《屈身求爱》（She Stoops to Conquer）里那几粒石榴石——那位时兴的小姐心声声喊着要钻石，拿石榴石是怎么也哄不好的。

### IV / 四

===Original===

Then the Church itself must needs take a hand﻿—or that part of the Church which ever cocks an eye at the latest fashions in public opinion, the “blessed fellows,” like Poins, that “think as every man thinks” and help to swell every passing shout into a roar. I find among old papers a letter written in Queen Victoria’s reign by an unfashionable curmudgeon whose thought would not keep to the roadway like theirs. “I see,” this rude ironist writes, “that ‘the Church’s duty in regard to war’ is to be discussed at the Church Congress. That is right. For a year the heads of our Church have been telling us what war is and does﻿—that it is a school of character, that it sobers men, cleans them, strengthens them, knits their hearts, makes them brave, patient, humble, tender, prone to self-sacrifice. Watered by ‘war’s red rain,’ one bishop tells us, virtue grows; a cannonade, he points out, is an ‘oratorio’﻿—almost a form of worship. True; and to the Church men look for help to save their souls from starving for lack of this good school, this kindly rain, this sacred music. Congresses are apt to lose themselves in wastes of words. This one must not﻿—surely cannot﻿—so straight is the way to the goal. It has simply to draft and submit a new Collect for ‘war in our time,’ and to call for the reverent but firm emendation, in the spirit of the best modern thought, of those passages in Bible and Prayerbook by which even the truest of Christians and the best of men have at times been blinded to the duty of seeking war and ensuing it.

“Still, man’s moral nature cannot, I admit, live by war alone. Nor do I say, with some, that peace is wholly bad. Even amid the horrors of peace you will find little shoots of character fed by the gentle and timely rains of plague and famine, tempest and fire; simple lessons of patience and courage conned in the schools of typhus, gout, and stone; not oratorios, perhaps, but homely anthems and rude hymns played on knife and gun, in the long winter nights. Far from me to ‘sin our mercies’ or to call mere twilight dark. Yet dark it may become. For remember that even these poor makeshift schools of character, these second-bests, these halting substitutes for war﻿—remember that the efficiency of every one of them, be it hunger, accident, ignorance, sickness or pain, is menaced by the intolerable strain of its struggle with secular doctors, plumbers, inventors, schoolmasters, and policemen. Every year thousands who would in nobler days have been braced and steeled by manly tussles with smallpox or diphtheria are robbed of that blessing by the great changes made in our drains. Every year thousands of women and children must go their way bereft of the rich spiritual experience of the widow and the orphan. I try not to despond, but when I think of all that Latimer owed to the fire, Regulus to a spiked barrel, Socrates to prison, and Job to destitution and disease﻿—when I think of these things and then think of how many of my poor fellow creatures in our modern world are robbed daily of the priceless discipline of danger, want, and torture, then I ask myself﻿—I cannot help asking myself﻿—whether we are not walking into a very slough of moral and spiritual squalor.

“Once more, I am no alarmist. As long as we have wars to stay our souls upon, the moral evil will not be grave; and, to do the Ministry justice, I see no risk of their drifting into any long or serious peace. But weak or vicious men may come after them, and it is now, in the time of our strength, of quickened insight and deepened devotion, that we must take thought for the leaner years when there may be no killing of multitudes of Englishmen, no breaking up of English homes, no chastening blows to English trade, no making, by thousands, of English widows, orphans, and cripples﻿—when the school may be shut and the rain a drought and the oratorio dumb.”

But what did a few unfashionable curmudgeons count for, against so many gifted divines?

===Chinese===

接着，连教会自己也非要插一手了——或者说，教会里那一翼非要插一手不可：那一翼永远瞟着舆论界的最新时髦，是「有福的家伙们」，像波因斯（Poins）那样「人人怎么想，我就怎么想」，专帮着把每一阵过路的呐喊煽成一片咆哮。我在旧文件堆里找到一封信，写于维多利亚女王（Queen Victoria）治下，写信的是个不入时的坏脾气老家伙，他的思想不肯像他们的思想那样规规矩矩走在马路正中。「我看到，」这位粗暴的讽刺家写道，「『教会对战争负有何种义务』将在教会大会（Church Congress）上得到讨论。这就对了。一年来，我们教会的首脑们一直在告诉我们，战争是什么、战争做些什么——说它是一座锻炼品格的学校，说它使人清醒，使人洁净，使人坚强，使人心心相连，使人勇敢、坚忍、谦卑、温存，动辄舍己。一位主教告诉我们，德性靠『战争的红雨』浇灌而生长；他又指出，一场炮击是一部『清唱剧』——几乎是一种敬拜的形式。不错；而世人正是指望教会伸出援手，搭救他们的灵魂，免得灵魂们因缺少这所良校、这场慈雨、这部圣乐而忍饥挨饿。大会这一类聚会，动辄迷失在言语的荒原里。这一届决不可以——想必也决不会——迷失，因为通往目标的道路再直捷没有了。它只消起草并呈上一篇为『我们时代的战争』用的新的总祷文（Collect），并号召大家本着最优秀的现代思想的精神，对圣经与公祷书（Prayerbook）中的某些段落加以虔敬而坚决的修订——正是那些段落，使连最真诚的基督徒、人间最好的人，也时时看不清寻求战争并进而追击战争的义务。

「话虽如此，我承认，人的道德天性并不能单靠战争过活。我也不同某些人一起，说和平全然是坏东西。即便在和平的种种恐怖中间，你也找得到一株株小小的品格嫩芽，由瘟疫与饥馑、风暴与火灾那温柔而应时的雨水浇灌；找得到一堂堂简单的功课，教人坚忍与勇气，是在斑疹伤寒、痛风与结石的学校里诵读习熟的；找得到的或许不是清唱剧，而是家常的颂歌与粗朴的圣诗，在漫长的冬夜里用刀与枪奏出。我万万不敢『亏负我们的恩典』（sin our mercies），也不敢把区区薄暮就叫作黑夜。然而它毕竟可能变成黑夜。因为请记住：就连这些可怜的、权宜的品格学校，这些次优之选，这些战争的一瘸一拐的替身——请记住，它们中间的每一个，无论是饥饿、意外、愚昧、疾病还是疼痛——其效能都正受着威胁，威胁来自它们同世俗的医生、管道工、发明家、中小学教师和警察进行的那场不堪重负的搏斗。每一年，都有成千上万的人——在更高贵的年代，他们本可以同天花或白喉来上几场男子汉的角力，从而振奋淬炼、百炼成钢——被我们排水系统的大改良剥夺了这份福气。每一年，都有成千上万的妇女儿童照常赶他们的路，被剥夺了做寡妇与做孤儿的丰厚灵性阅历。我竭力不灰心；可是一想到拉蒂默（Latimer）受惠于火焰的有多少，雷古勒斯（Regulus）受惠于钉满尖刺的木桶的有多少，苏格拉底（Socrates）受惠于监狱的有多少，约伯（Job）受惠于赤贫与疾病的有多少——一想到这些，再想到在我们这个现代世界里，我多少可怜的同类每天都在被剥夺危险、匮乏与酷刑这无价的操练——我便问自己——我禁不住问自己——我们是不是正在走进一片道德与灵性上污秽不堪的大泥沼。」

「再说一遍，我不是危言耸听之辈。只要我们还有战争可供灵魂倚靠，道德的邪恶便不会深重；而且，替本届政府说句公道话，我并不见它有滑入任何长期而认真的和平的风险。但在他们之后，也许会上来软弱的人或邪恶的人；所以正当此时——趁我们力量尚在、眼光正锐、虔敬正深——我们必须为那些歉年预先筹谋：那些年头，可能再没有成批的英国人可杀，没有英格兰的家庭可拆散，没有英格兰的贸易可受惩戒的打击，也没有英格兰的寡妇、孤儿与残废可成千上万地制造——那些年头，学校可能关门，慈雨可能成旱，清唱剧可能喑哑。」

可是，区区几个不入时的坏脾气老家伙，在这许多天才的神职人员面前，又算得了什么呢？

### V / 五

===Original===

And yet all mortal things are subject to decay, even reactions, even decay itself, and there comes a time when the dead Ophelia may justly be said to be not decomposing, but recomposing successfully as violets and so forth. Heirs-apparent grow up into kings and have little heirs of their own who, hearkening to nature’s benevolent law, become stout counter-reactionists in their turn. So now the prewar virilists, the literary braves who felt that they had supped too full of peace, have died in their beds, or lost voice, like the cuckoos in June, and a different breed find voice and pipe up. These are the kind, the numerous kind, whose youth has supped quite full enough of war. For them Bellona has not the mystical charm, as of grapes out of reach, that she had for the Henleys and Stevensons. All the veiled-mistress business is off. Battles have no aureoles now in the sight of young men as they had for the British prelate who wrote that old poem about the “red rain.” The men of the counter-reaction have gone to the school and sat the oratorio out and taken a course of the waters, after the worthy prelate’s prescription. They have seen trenches full of gassed men, and the queue of their friends at the brothel-door in Bethune. At the heart of the magical rose was seated an earwig.

Presently all the complaisant part of our Press may jump to the fact that the game of idealizing war is now, in its turn, a back number. Then we may hear such a thudding or patter of feet as Carlyle describes when Louis XV was seen to be dead and the Court bolted off, ventre à terre, along the corridors of Versailles, to kiss the hand of Louis XVI. And then will come the season of danger. Woe unto Peace, or anyone else, when all men speak well of her, even the base. When Lord Robert Cecil and Mr. Clynes and Sir Hubert Gough stand up for the peace which ex-soldiers desire, it is all right. But what if Tadpoe and Taper stood up for it? What if all the vendors of supposedly popular stuff, all the timid gregarious repeaters of current banalities, all the largest circulations in the solar system were on the side of peace, as well as her old bodyguard of game disregarders of fashion and whimsical stickers-up for Christianity, chivalry, or sportsmanship?

We must remember that, in the course of nature, the proportion of former combatants among us must steadily decline. And war hath no fury like a noncombatant. Can you not already forehear, in the far distance, beyond the peace period now likely to come, the still, small voice of some Henley or Lang of later days beginning to pipe up again with Ancient Pistol’s ancient suggestion: “What? Shall we have incision? Shall we imbrue?” And then a sudden furore, a war-dance, a beating of tom-toms. And so the whole cycle revolving again. “Seest thou not, I say, what a deformed thief this fashion is? How giddily a’ turns about all the hot bloods between fourteen and five-and-thirty? Sometimes fashioning them like Pharaoh’s soldiers in the reechy painting, sometimes like god Bel’s priests in the old church window; sometimes like the shaven Hercules in the smirched worm-eaten tapestry?” Anything to be in the fashion.

There is only one thing for it. There must still be five or six million ex-soldiers. They are the most determined peace party that ever existed in Britain. Let them clap the only darbies they have﻿—the Covenant of the League of Nations﻿—on to the wrists of all future poets, romancers, and sages. The future is said to be only the past entered by another door. We must beware in good time of those boys, and fiery elderly men, piping in Thessaly.

===Chinese===

然而凡有死之物皆不免于衰朽，反弹也不例外，衰朽自身也不例外；总会有那么一天，人们尽可以公道地说，死去的奥菲利娅（Ophelia）并非在腐解，而是在成功地重新化合为紫罗兰之类。储君们长成国王，又生下自己的小小储君；这些储君遵照自然仁慈的法则，轮到自己也做起坚定卓绝的反反弹派来。于是如今，战前那班尚武文人（virilists）——自觉和平吃得过饱的文坛健儿们——或者已寿终正寝，或者已失了嗓子，像六月里的杜鹃；换了一族人来开嗓弄笛。正是这一类人，人数众多的一类：他们的青春，战争已经给得十足十足的了。在他们眼里，贝洛娜（Bellona）再没有那种神秘的魅力——那种恰似够不着的葡萄的魅力——她在亨利们与史蒂文森们眼里却是有的。蒙着面纱的情妇，那整套把戏都收场了。如今在青年男子的眼里，战役再没有光轮，不像在那位写过咏「红雨」旧诗的英国主教眼里。反反弹的这班人，上过了那所学校，坐听完了那部清唱剧，又遵那位可敬主教的处方，服过一个疗程的泉水。他们见过塞满中毒气者的战壕，见过自己的朋友在贝蒂讷（Bethune）妓院门口排起的长队。魔法似的玫瑰，花心里坐着一只蠼螋。

要不了多久，我国报界里那班殷勤趋时的角色，或许就会猛然醒悟：把战争理想化的把戏，如今轮到它自己成了过期号。到那时，我们或许会听见一阵卡莱尔（Carlyle）描写过的那种脚步的奔踏与噼啪——路易十五（Louis XV）眼看断了气，凡尔赛（Versailles）宫廷上下便腹贴着地（ventre à terre）、顺着走廊亡命疾奔，抢着去吻路易十六（Louis XVI）的手。而那便是危险季节的到来。和平有祸了——任何人有祸了——当众人，连卑劣之徒也在内，都说她好的时候。当罗伯特·塞西尔勋爵（Lord Robert Cecil）、克莱因斯先生（Mr. Clynes）和休伯特·高夫爵士（Sir Hubert Gough）站起来维护退役军人所渴望的和平时，一切都好。可要是塔德波（Tadpoe）与泰珀（Taper）也站起来维护它呢？要是所有兜售「想必畅销」货色的小贩、所有怯懦而群居的时下滥调的复读机、全太阳系发行量最大的各家报纸，都站到和平一边来——连同她那班老卫队：那班不把时髦放在眼里的猛士，和那班为基督教、骑士精神或运动家精神任性地硬撑到底的怪人——那可怎么办呢？

我们必须记住：顺乎自然的进程，我们中间昔日战斗人员所占的比例，必然稳步下降。而战争的暴怒，再没有甚过一个非战斗人员的。你难道还不能预先听见吗——在遥远的远处，在眼下多半要来的和平时期的那一头——某个后世的亨利或兰，正用那微小的声音，开始重新吹起旗官毕斯托（Ancient Pistol）的那条老建议：「什么？要不要我们动刀见血？要不要我们杀个痛快？」随即是一场骤起的狂热，一场战争舞，一阵咚咚的手鼓。整个循环于是又转动起来。「你看不出吗，我说，时髦是怎样一个畸形的贼？它把十四岁到三十五岁之间一切血气方刚的人，转弄得多么晕头转向？一时把他们打扮得像烟熏火燎的画里法老的兵士，一时像古老教堂窗上彼勒神（Bel）的祭司，一时又像污渍斑斑、虫蛀朽坏的挂毯上剃净了须发的海格立斯（Hercules）？」——只要赶得上时髦，什么都行。

此事只有一个办法。退役军人如今总还有五六百万。他们是英国有史以来最坚决的和平党。让他们把他们仅有的一副手铐——国际联盟盟约（Covenant of the League of Nations）——铐到未来一切诗人、小说家与圣哲的手腕上。据说未来，不过是从另一扇门走进来的过去。我们必须趁早提防那些男孩，还有那些火性子的老头子——提防他们在色萨利吹笛。

## II Misgiving / 二 疑虑

### I / 一

===Original===

What could the New Army not have done if all the time of its training had been fully used! A few, at least, of its units had a physique above that of the Guards; many did more actual hours of work, before going abroad, than Guardsmen in peacetime do in two years; all were at first as keen as boys, collectors, or spaniels﻿—whichever are keenest; when the official rations of warlike instruction fell short they would go about hungrily trying to scratch crumbs of that provender out of the earth like fowls in a run.

But there was an imp of frustration about. He pervaded, like Ariel, all the labouring ship of our State. I had seen him in Lancashire once, on one of the early days of the war, when fifty young miners marched in from one pit, with their colliery band, to enlist at an advertised place and time of enlistment. The futilitarian elf took care that the shutters were up and nobody there, so that the men should kick their heels all the day in the street and walk back at night with their tails between their legs, and the band not playing, to tell their mates that the whole thing was a mug’s game, a ramp, got up by the hot-air merchants and crooks in control. The imp must have grinned, not quite as all of us have grinned since, on the wrong side of our mouths, at the want of faith that miners have in the great and wise who rule over them. Another practical joke of his was to slip into the War Office or Admiralty and tear up any letters he found from people offering gifts of motorcars, motorboats, steam-yachts, training grounds, etc., lest they be answered and the writers and other friends of their country encouraged. Perhaps his brightest triumph of all was to dress himself up as England and send away with a flea in her ear the Ireland whom the wonder-working Redmond had induced to offer to fight at our side. Those were a few of his masterpieces. Between times he would keep his hand in by putting it into the Old Army’s head to take the keenness out of the New.

===Chinese===

新军（New Army）若是把训练的时日尽数用足，还有什么做不成的！它的部队至少有几支，体格在近卫师（Guards Division）之上；许多人在开赴国外之前实际出力的钟点，比近卫军人（Guardsmen）和平时期两年干的还多；起初，人人都热切得像男孩子、收藏家，或者西班牙猎犬——三者之中哪样最热切，就像哪样；一旦尚武课目的官定口粮不敷分配，他们便饿着肚子四处刨掘，像圈栏里的母鸡，想从泥土里刨出几粒那种饲料的碎屑。

可是四下里却游荡着一个专事挫败的小鬼。他像爱丽儿（Ariel）一般，弥漫渗透着我们国家这艘劳碌的船。战争最初的日子里，我曾在兰开夏（Lancashire）见过他一回：五十名年轻的矿工从同一个矿井列队而来，带着矿场的乐队，按张榜公布的时间地点前去应征。那个败事主义（futilitarian）的小精灵特意打点好一切：铺面上着门板，连个人影也没有；于是那些人只好在街上一整天踢着脚跟干等，入夜时夹着尾巴往回走，乐队也不再吹奏，好去告诉他们的伙伴：这整个儿是一场冤大头的营生、一场骗局，是当权的空话贩子和骗子们捣鼓出来的。那小鬼想必咧开嘴笑了——却又不全像我们后来人人那样，笑得嘴角发苦——笑矿工们对统治他们的伟人贤达竟然如此缺乏信心。他的又一出恶作剧，是溜进陆军部（War Office）或海军部（Admiralty），把搜到的来信统统撕掉——那些信，是人们表示愿意捐献汽车、汽艇、蒸汽游艇、训练场地等等的——唯恐它们得到答复，让写信人和祖国的其他朋友们受到鼓舞。而他种种凯旋里最亮的一场，或许莫过于把自己装扮成英格兰（England），把爱尔兰（Ireland）抢白一顿、让她耳中嗡嗡着悻悻而归——那位有回天妙术的雷德蒙（Redmond），本来已经说动爱尔兰主动提出与我们并肩作战。以上不过是他几件代表作。空当里，他也练手不辍——索性把手伸进旧陆军（Old Army）的脑袋，从那里把新军的锐气掏出去。

===Original===

Dearest of all the New Army’s infant illusions was the Old Army﻿—still at that time the demigod host of an unshattered legend of Mons. To the new recruits any old Regular sergeant was more﻿—if the world can hold more﻿—than a county cricketer is to a small boy at school. He had the talisman; he was a vessel full of the grace by which everything was to be saved; like a king, he could “touch for” the malady of unsoldierliness. How could he err, how could he shirk, now that the fate of a world hung upon him?

There was something in that. No doubt there always is in illusions. They are not delusions. The pick of the old N.C.O.’s of the Regular Army were packed as tight as bits of radium with virtues and powers. A man of fifty-five who came back to the army from spending ten years in a farcical uniform whistling for taxis outside a flash music-hall would teach every rank in a battalion its duties for 4s. 8d. a day﻿—coaching the dugout colonel in the new infantry drill, the field officers in court-martial procedure, the chaplain in details of drumhead worship, the medical officer in the order of sick parades, the subalterns and N.C.O.’s in camp economy, field hygiene, and whatnot, and always holding the attention of a man or a mess or a battalion fixed fast by the magic of his own oaken character, his simple, vivid mind, his passion for getting things right, and his humorous, patient knowledge of mankind. Even such minor masterpieces as average Guards ex-sergeant-majors were rather godlike on parade. In drill, at any rate, they had the circumstantial vision and communicable fire of the prophets. Early in 1915 a little famished London cab-tout, a recruit, still rectilinear as a starved cat even after a month of army rations, was to be heard praying softly at night in his cot that he might be made like unto one of these, whom he named.

===Chinese===

新军种种乳臭未干的幻象里，最亲爱的一桩，便是旧陆军——其时它尚是一支半神的大军，蒙斯（Mons）的传奇未破，光轮犹在。在新兵眼里，随便哪一位正规军（Regular）的老中士，比郡级板球手之于学校里的小男孩还要了不起——倘若世上还容得下更了不起的东西的话。他身怀护符；他是一口盛满恩宠的器皿，万事都要凭那恩宠得救；他又像国王，能以「御手一触」医治缺乏军人气概之症。如今一个世界的命运就悬在他身上，他怎会出错，怎会偷懒？

这话自有几分道理。幻象里无疑总有几分道理。它们终究不是妄想。正规军的老军士（N.C.O.）中的尖子，浑身密密实实，填满了美德与才具，如同一小粒一小粒的镭。一个五十五岁的人，从一桩滑稽制服的营生里回到军队——先前十年，他穿着那身制服，在一家阔气的杂耍剧场门外吹哨替人叫出租车——如今以一天 4 先令 8 便士的身价，把一营人上上下下的职责教个遍：给复役的老上校（dugout colonel）开小灶，讲授新的步兵操典；教高级军官军法审判的程序；给随军牧师指点架鼓为坛的战地礼拜的细节；给军医官排定病号检阅的章程；教初级军官（subaltern）和军士们营地内务、战地卫生等等名目；而无论面对一个人、一间食堂（mess）还是一营人马，他总能凭自己橡木般品格的魔力、单纯而鲜活的心智、把事情做对的痴迷，以及幽默而耐烦的阅世之识，把他们的注意力牢牢钉住。就连近卫军（Guards）寻常退役军人士长（ex-sergeant-major）这样二流的杰作，在阅兵场上也颇有几分神明气象。至少在操练上，他们有众先知那种洞悉详情的目光与可以传递的火。1915 年初，一个小个子饿瘪了的伦敦出租车揽客仔——一个新兵，吃了整整一个月的军队口粮，身条仍像饿猫一般嶙峋笔挺——夜里躺在板铺上，听得见他轻声祷告，求上天把他变得像他指名点到的其中一位那样。

### II / 二

===Original===

Where, then, did the first shiver of disillusion begin? Perhaps with some trivial incident. Say a newborn company, quartered in a great town, was sent out for a long afternoon’s marching. Only through long, steady grinds can the perfect rhythm of marching, like that of rowing, be generated at last. The men, youthfully eager to kiss all possible rods and endure any obtainable hardness, march forth in a high state of delight﻿—they are going to learn how to march to Berlin! No officer being present﻿—and scarcely any existing as yet﻿—a sergeant-major is in command. He is a very old hand. For twenty minutes he leads his 250 adorers into the thick of a populous quarter. Then he orders them to fall out. A public-house resembling Buckingham Palace, but smaller, is near. Most of the men, in their ardour, stand about on the kerb, ready to leap back to their places as soon as the whistle shall sound. A few thirsty souls jostle hurriedly into the bars, where they find that arrangements for serving a multitude are surprisingly complete. Soon they are further reassured by descrying the sergeant-major’s handsome form, like Tam o’ Shanter’s, “planted unco’ right” in a chair in an inner holy of holies along with the landlord. This esoteric session has an air of permanence; the sergeant-major is evidently *au mieux* with the management. The thirsty souls settle down to their beer.

Five minutes, twenty, half an hour pass fairly fast for them, less fast for the keener warriors pawing the kerbstone without. At the end of an hour fifty percent of the kerbstone zealots have been successfully frozen into the bars. The rest stare at each other with a wild surmise. Rumour shakes her wings and begins to fly round. The sergeant-major, she says, is holding a species of court in the depths of the pub; some privates with money upon them, children of this world, are pressing in, she says, even now, into that heart of the rose, and with a few manly words are standing the great man the extremely expensive combination of nectars that he prefers. “Were it not better done as others use?”﻿—the Spartan residuum on the kerb is diminishing. Another hour goes; only an inconsiderable remnant of Spartans is left; these are exchanging profane remarks about patriotism and other virtues. One of them quotes a famous Conservative statesman whose footman he was before he enlisted: “I believe we shall win, in spite of the Regular Army.” When just enough time is left to march back to quarters the whistle is blown, the men slouch into their places and stump unrhythmically home, revolving many things according to their several natures. A child who has rashly taken its parent on trust, and yet more rashly taken the parent’s all-round perfection as some sort of sample and proof of a creditable government of the world, must have a good deal of mental rearrangement to do the first time the parent comes home full of liquor and sells the furniture to get some more.

===Chinese===

那么，幻灭（disenchantment）的第一阵寒噤是从哪里开始的？也许始于某件芝麻小事。比如说，一个新组建的连队驻在大城市里，被拉出去做整个下午的长途行军。唯有经久不断的苦磨，行军那完美的节奏——一如划船的节奏——方能最终生成。士兵们少年心气，巴不得凡世上有的戒尺都亲上一亲，凡弄得到的苦头都吃上一吃，兴高采烈地开步出发——他们要去学会怎么一路开进柏林！军官一个不在场——军官当时也几乎还没征出来——便由一位军士长（sergeant-major）带队。他是位老于此道的行家。有二十分钟，他领着他那 250 名崇拜者，一头扎进人烟稠密的街区深处。然后他下令解散。近旁有一家酒馆，形似白金汉宫（Buckingham Palace），只是小一号。大多数人出于热忱，在路边石上四处站着，只等哨声一响便立刻跳回位置。少数口渴的灵魂急匆匆挤进酒吧，却发现那里接待大群酒客的安排周全得出奇。很快他们又吃下第二颗定心丸：他们望见军士长那堂堂的身影，像《汤姆·奥桑特》（Tam o' Shanter）里的主儿，「端端正正、稳稳当当」地植在里间一座至圣所的椅子里，与店主同席。这场秘会俨然有长治久安之象；军士长与店里掌事的关系，分明再融洽不过（au mieux）。口渴的灵魂们便安顿下来，慢慢受用他们的啤酒。

五分钟、二十分钟、半个钟头，对他们过得相当快；对外头刨着路边石的更热切的战士们，便慢了些。一个钟头下来，路边石上的热忱分子，百分之五十已成功冻进了酒吧。余下的面面相觑，满心茫然的惊疑。谣言抖开翅膀，开始四处盘旋。她说，军士长正在酒馆深处升帐问事，俨然一个小朝廷；她说，几个身上带钱的列兵——今世之子——此刻还在往那朵玫瑰的花心里挤，几句有丈夫气概的豪言，便请那位大人物受用了所好的那味昂贵得出奇的琼浆配剂。「何不照旁人的样子行事？」——路边石上的斯巴达残余渐渐稀薄。又一个钟头过去；只剩一小撮不成气候的斯巴达人；这几位正在就爱国主义及其他种种美德交换渎神的评语。其中一位引用一位著名保守党政治家的话——入伍前他当过此公的听差：「我相信我们终会打赢，尽管有正规军（Regular Army）从中作梗。」等到回营时间刚够，哨声一响，士兵们没精打采各归各位，踢踢踏踏、毫无节奏地开回营盘，各自按各自的天性，心里盘旋着许多事。一个孩子，先是轻率地把父亲信了个十足，又更轻率地拿父亲的全副完美，当作这世界治理得尚属可信的某种样品与凭据；那么当父亲头一回满身酒气回家、变卖家具再去换酒时，这孩子心里便有许多东西，得重新归置了。

### III / 三

===Original===

Perhaps, in another company or another battalion, some private of relative wealth has felt, in the strength of his youth and the heat of his zeal, that he wants more to do. He longs to get on with the job. So he guilelessly goes to his own sergeant-major and asks him if there is a chance of getting some lessons in bayonet-fighting anywhere in the town. The sergeant-major sizes him up with a stare. “You’re a fine likely man,” he says, “for a stripe.” He stares harder. “Or three,” he subjoins.

The gilded youth is confounded. He an N.C.O.! He would as soon have thought of being a primate. “I’ll give you,” the Old Army continues, “the lessons myself. It’ll be twelve quid﻿—*for the lot*.” To reproduce the emphasis upon the last three words is beyond the resources of typography.

The gilded youth may feel a slight pricking in his thumbs. Still, there is no overt crook in the deal. The teaching is sure to be good. And he has the cash and an inexact sense of values. So he agrees. The senior man-at-arms expresses a preference for ready money. Agreed, too. After one lesson the tutor is frankly bored by his tutorial function. “Hang it,” he says, “what’s the sense of you and me sweating our ’oly guts out? You’ve paid, and you’ll find I won’t bilk you.” Youth is mystified; feels it is getting somewhat short weight. But what are acolytes against high priests? Youth leaves it at that.

===Chinese===

也许，在另外一个连队、另外一个营里，某个家道稍殷实的列兵，仗着青春的气力、热忱的高热，觉得还想多做些事。他巴不得这活计早日上手。于是他毫无心机地去找本连的军士长，问城里可有地方上得到白刃战的课。军士长拿眼一扫，把他从头到脚量了一遍。「你是块好料，」他说，「佩一道杠正合适。」他又盯紧了几分。「或者三道。」他补上一句。

这位纨绔子弟懵住了。他，当军士（N.C.O.）！他还不如想想去当大主教。「这课，」旧陆军接着说，「由我来给你上。十二镑——*全套包圆*。」要把落在最后三个词上的那股着重劲儿原样印出来，实非印刷术之所能。

纨绔子弟也许觉得大拇指有一阵轻微的刺痒。不过这桩买卖面上并没有明摆着的拐。教学料想是好。他又现钱在手，对价值的分寸感又素来不精。于是他应承下来。这位年长的武士表示，偏好现款交易。这一条也照办了。上过一堂课，这位师傅对自己执教的职分已经腻烦得全不加掩饰。「见鬼，」他说，「你我两个何苦把肚肠都熬出汗来？你钱付过了，你自会看到，我绝不赖你的账。」少年如坠五里雾中；只觉得拿到的分量有些不足秤。可是侍僧如何拗得过大祭司？少年便听其自然。

===Original===

In two or three weeks the frustrated pupil is sent for by his frustrater. A man is wanted for Post Corporal, or even for Battalion Provost Sergeant. What would the gilded youth say to the job? On his saying nothing at first the sergeant-major, with swiftly rising contempt for such friarly hesitancy, recites the beauties of this piece of preferment. “Cushiest job in the ’ole outfit! Long as you’re sober enough to stand up at the staff parade of a night, that’s all there is to it. Where’d the crime be among you ’oly Christians?” (The almost fanatical abstention of the New Army from ordinary military crimes often gave some scandal to experts drawn from the Old. They regarded it with perplexity and suspicion. The phenomenon was really simple, the men being in panic-fear of getting left behind in England if their unit should suddenly be sent abroad.) While the gilded youth tries to explain, without a lapse from tact, that the ranks are good enough for himself he feels a regal scorn beat down on him like a vertical sun. A fulmination follows. “Then what the ’ell did you ever come to me for? ’Op off! Out of it!”

The youth retires feeling that he has somehow strayed into a black list. He talks it over with a friend. The friend, he finds, has heard something like it from somebody else. Ribald jibes are soon flying about﻿—“Four pound a stripe!” “Stripes are ris’ today!” “Corporals, three for a tenner!” The story goes that a little “Scotch draper,” the worst drill in a section, has felt that in this newly revealed world his professional credit for tactful effrontery is at stake; he has bet a fiver that he will offer the bare market price of a recommendation for “lance-Jack” and bring the thing off; the enterprise has prospered and the architect of his own fortunes is wearing the stripe, spending his pound balance on the transaction, commanding his brethren, and enjoying his new dispensation from fatigues. The band of brothers begin to look at each other with some circumspection. They wonder. How far does the dirty work go? Who may not try it on next? And did not somebody say he had seen the stud pass between the contractor who emptied the swill-tubs and the sergeant-cook who filled them with half-legs of mutton? What was that shorter creed to which the sergeants’ mess waiters said that the Regular sergeants always recurred in their cups﻿—“Stick together, boys,” and “Anything can be wangled in the army”?

===Chinese===

过了两三个星期，那位受挫的学徒被他的挫败者派人唤了去。营里缺一名邮务下士（Post Corporal），甚至缺一名营纠察中士（Battalion Provost Sergeant）。纨绔子弟觉得这桩美差如何？见他半晌不作声，军士长对这种修士式的迟疑迅速升起了鄙夷，便开始历数这桩美缺的种种好处。「全营里顶舒坦的差事！夜里营中点名（staff parade）时你站得起来，便万事齐了。在你们这些『圣』洁的基督徒中间，哪还谈得上罪过？」（新军对寻常军中过犯近乎狂热的戒绝，常叫从旧陆军抽调来的行家们看不顺眼。他们又困惑、又起疑地看待这一现象。其实这现象简单得很：士兵们个个怕得要死，生怕部队突然开赴国外时，自己被撂在英国。）纨绔子弟一面竭力不失体面地解释，说当个大兵于愿已足，一面只觉得一道帝王的轻蔑，像当顶的烈日般直压下来。跟着便是一声炸雷。「那你他妈当初找我做什么？滚！滚一边去！」

少年退下来，只觉得自己不知怎么误上了一本黑名单。他把这事同一个朋友商量。一打听，那朋友原来也从旁人那里听说过类似的事。下流的嘲弄很快便四处乱飞——「一道杠四镑！」「杠子今天看涨！」「下士，十镑卖仨！」传闻说，有个小个子「苏格兰布商」，全班列队动作数他最糟，却觉得在这个新近揭开的世界里，他凭圆滑的厚脸皮挣下的职业信誉面临考验；他打了五镑的赌，说要按光溜溜的市场价，出一笔「代理下士」（lance-Jack）的荐举费，把这事办成；这番事业果然兴旺，这位自我命运的缔造者如今佩上了那道杠，把手里一镑的结余花在了这桩交易上，指挥起自己的弟兄，享受起豁免杂役（fatigues）的新颁恩典。这一群兄弟开始带着几分提防彼此打量。他们犯嘀咕。这肮脏营生到底有多深？下一个下手试身的会是谁？还有，不是有人说，亲眼见一枚领扣（stud）在掏泔水桶的承包商与往桶里填半条羊腿的炊事中士之间易过手吗？还有军士食堂（sergeants' mess）的侍者们说，正规军的军士们三杯下肚总要回到的那篇较短的信经，又是哪一篇——「弟兄们，抱紧团」，还有「军队里的事，没有做手脚办不成的」？

### IV / 四

===Original===

What about officers, too? The men wonder again. That new company commander who started in as a captain, but never could give the simplest command on parade without his sergeant-major to give him the words like a parson doing a marriage? What about little Y., who suddenly got a commission when he was doing a fortnight’s C.B. for coming on parade with a dirty neck? And the major’s lecture on musketry? And the colonel’s on field operations?

Part of the scheme of training is that all the senior officers should lecture to the men on something or other﻿—marching, map-reading, field hygiene, and whatnot. An excellent plan, but terribly hard on an old Regular Army not exactly officered by the brightest wits of public schools. The major’s musketry lecture has made the men think. He has told them first that, just to let them know that he was not talking through his hat, he might say he had been, in his time, the champion shot of the Army in India. The men had known that already﻿—had doted, in fact, on anything known to the glory of any of their commanders. Fair enough, too, they had felt, that a man should buck a bit about what he had done. Anyone would. And so they had not even smiled. But then the major had amplified. He had recited his moderate, but not bad, earlier scores in competitions: he had given statistics of his rapid rise; he had painted the astonishment of all who saw him shoot in those days﻿—above all, the delight of the men of his old regiment; for, the major had said, “I may have faults, but this at least I can say, that wherever I went the men simply worshipped the ground that I trod on.”

All this had filled the first half of the lecturer’s hour. The men had begun to look at each other cautiously, marvelling. When would the major begin? Could this be a Regular Army custom? But then the major had warmed to his subject. With rising zest he had described the dramatic tension pervading the butts as the crisis of each of his greater triumphs approached. And then the climax had come﻿—“the one time that I failed.” In sombre tones the major had told how five shots had to be fired at one out of several targets arranged in a row. “I fired my first four shots. A bull each time. I fired again, and the marker signalled a miss! Everyone present was thunderstruck. I knew what had happened. I said to the butt officer, ‘Do you mind, sir, enquiring if there is any shot on the target to the right of mine?’ He did so. ‘Yes,’ was signalled back. ‘What is it?’ I asked, though I knew. ‘A bull.’ ‘That was my last shot,’ said I. I had made the mistake of my life. I had fired at the wrong target. Fall out.”

===Chinese===

那么军官们又怎样呢？士兵们又纳起闷来。那位新上任的连长，一出场便是上尉，可是在阅兵场上，若没有军士长像牧师主持婚礼那样给他递词，他连最简单的一句口令也喊不出口——这位怎么说？还有小 Y，因阅兵时脖颈不净正关着十四天禁闭（C.B.），忽然便领到了军官委任状——这位又怎么说？还有少校的步枪射击课？还有上校的野战课？

训练计划里有一项，是全体高级军官都该给士兵们讲上一课——行军、识图、战地卫生等等名目。计划本身极好，只是苦了那支旧日的正规军：它的军官席次，本就不是由公学（public school）里最聪明的头脑坐满的。少校的射击课让士兵们动起了脑筋。他一上来先告诉他们，为了让大伙知道他不是在瞎吹，不妨一说：当年在印度，他做过全军冠军射手。这事士兵们早已知道——事实上，凡有助于任何一位长官之光荣的已知事迹，他们一概爱得要命。他们当时也觉得，这很公道：一个人对自己干成的事夸上几句，谁人不会。所以他们连笑都没有笑。可是接着，少校铺展开了。他历数自己早年在竞赛里尚属平平、却也不赖的成绩；给出自己青云直上的统计数字；描画当年看他射击的人人无不惊愕——尤其他老团队弟兄们的欢腾；因为少校说过：「我也许有种种毛病，但至少这一层我敢说：无论我走到哪里，弟兄们简直把我脚下踩过的土都当神明敬拜。」

这一切填满了这堂课的前半个钟头。士兵们已开始小心翼翼地面面相觑，惊叹不已。少校几时才讲到正题？这难道也是正规军的规矩？可是接着，少校的谈兴上来了。他以愈发高昂的兴致，描绘每一次较大的凯旋临近关头时，弥漫于靶壕（butts）全场的戏剧性紧张。然后高潮来了——「我唯一失手的那一回。」少校用低沉的语调讲道：一列排开的几个靶子中，须对其中一个连发五枪。「我打完头四枪。枪枪正中靶心。再打一枪，报靶员打出脱靶信号！在场人等无不惊得目瞪口呆。我明白出了什么事。我对靶壕军官说：『敢烦长官，问一声我右边那个靶上可有弹着？』他问了。信号回过来：『有。』『是什么？』我问，虽然心里已有数。『正中靶心。』『那是我最后一枪。』我说。我犯了一生的大错。我打错了靶子。解散。」

===Original===

On this tragic climax the lecture had ended, the men had streamed out, some silent, bewildered, some dropping words of amazement. “Lecture! W’y, it’s the man’s pers’nal ’istory!”

And then the C.O. has lectured on training in field operations﻿—the old, cold colonel, upright, dutiful, unintelligent, waxen, drawn away by a genuine patriotism from his roses and croquet to help joylessly in the queer labour of trying to teach this uncouth New Army a few of the higher qualities of the old. Too honest a man to pretend that he was not taking all that he said in his lecture out of the Army’s official manual, *Infantry Training, 1914*, he has held the little red book in his hand, read out frankly a sentence at a time from that terse and luminous masterpiece of instruction, and then has tried to “explain” it while the men gaped at the strange contrast between the thing clearly said in the book and the same thing plunged into obscurity by the poor colonel’s woolly and faltering verbiage. Half the men had bought the little book themselves and devoured it as hungrily as boys consume a manual of rude boat-building or of camping-out. And here was the colonel bringing his laboured jets of darkness to show the way through sunlight; elucidating plainness itself with the tangled clues of his own mind’s confusion, like Bardolph: “ ‘Accommodated’; that is, when a man is, as they say, accommodated; or when a man is, being, whereby a’ may be thought to be accommodated.”

===Chinese===

讲演便在这悲怆的高潮上收场，士兵们涌出门去，有的沉默，有的惶惑，有的漏出几句惊叹。「讲演？嗐，这分明是这位老哥本人的生平嘛！」

接着是团长（C.O.）讲野战训练课——那位又老又冷的上校，端正，尽职，不聪明，一张蜡塑的脸，被一片真诚的爱国心从他的玫瑰与槌球边上拽了回来，闷闷不乐地帮着干那桩古怪的活计：设法教这支粗野的新军几分旧军队的高级素质。他这人太诚实，不肯假装自己讲演里说的东西并非照搬陆军的官方教范《1914 年步兵训练教范》（*Infantry Training, 1914*）；于是他把那本小红书攥在手里，从那部言简意赅、明澈如水的教学杰作里，坦坦荡荡一次念一句，然后试着来「讲解」——而士兵们张着嘴，望着那桩奇异的对照：书里说得明明白白的东西，一到可怜的上校那一团含混磕绊的废话里，便一头栽进黑暗。半数士兵自己买了那本小书，狼吞虎咽地读，像男孩子吞食一本粗浅的造船或露营手册那样饥渴。而这里，上校竟搬出他辛苦挤出的一股股黑暗，来照阳光底下的路；用他自己心智迷乱绞出的一团乱线索，来阐明「明白」本身，活像巴道夫（Bardolph）：「『设备周全』；就是说，一个人，像人家所说的，设备周全了；或者说，一个人，既是——从而，他就可以被认为设备周全了。」

### V / 五

===Original===

A favourite trick with the disillusioning imp was to get hold of authority’s wisely drafted timetable of work for a new division in training and mix up all the subjects and times. The effect must have often diverted the author of this piece of humour. Some day a company, say, would begin to learn bayonet-fighting. This would at once revive in the men the fading ecstasies of their first simple faith. Whenever instructors said﻿—“Now then, men, I want to see a bit more murder in them eyes” pleasant little thrills of chartered pugnacity would inspirit them. This, they would feel, was the real thing; this was what they were there for. Then just as, perhaps, they approached the engaging and manifestly serviceable “short jab” Puck’s little witticism would suddenly tell; bayonet-fighting would abruptly stop; an urgent order would come from on high to “get on with night operations” or “get on with outpost work,” and one of these bodies of knowledge would, in its turn, be partly imbibed by the infant mind and then as suddenly withdrawn from its thirsty lips for something else to be started instead﻿—perhaps a thing that had already been once started and dropped. In the working out of this fantastic pattern of smatterings a company might begin to learn bayonet-fighting three or four times and each time be switched off it before getting halfway, and go to France in the end with the A.B.C. of each of several alphabets learnt to boredom and the X.Y.Z. of none of them touched, the men being left to improvise the short jab and other far-on letters by the light of nature, in intimate contact, perhaps, with less humorously instructed Germans.

All this was not universal. Still, it could and did happen. And then the men stared and marvelled. Authority, it is true, had, at the worst, some gusts of passion for perfection. But even these might fortify, in their way, the new occupant of the seat of the scorners. A sudden order might come for a brigade or other inspection, and then authority might in a brief hour become like medieval man when he fell suddenly ill and the pains of hell gat hold of his mind and he felt that God must be squared without conduct because it might take more time to conduct himself than he had got. In this pious frenzy all attention to measures for incommoding the Germans would yield to the primary duty of whiting the sepulchre; energies that would carry a Hohenzollern Redoubt would be put into the evolution of sections which, through somebody’s slackness, did not exist, or the hiding of men who, through someone’s mismanagement, were not fit to be seen on parade; old N.C.O.’s would present the men with the tip for making a seemingly full valise look nicely rectangular by the judicious insertion of timber, and other homely recipes for cleaning the outsides of cups and platters. “Eyewash?” these children of light would say, as they taught. “Of course, it’s all eyewash. What ain’t eyewash in this old world?”

===Chinese===

那个专司幻灭的小鬼最爱玩的一手，是逮住当局为某个训练中的新师苦心拟就的课程表，把科目和时间统统搅乱。这出幽默的效果，想必常把这桩玩笑的作者自己也逗乐了。比如说，某一天某个连队开始学白刃战。这立刻便唤回士兵们初信时代那正在消褪的种种狂喜。每当教官们说——「喂，弟兄们，我要在那双双眼睛里多看见点儿杀气」——一阵阵领了执照的斗性，便给他们小小地通了电。他们感到，这才是真家伙；这才是他们到此地来的目的。然后，正当他们大约要学那招惹人喜爱又显然实用的「短刺」时，帕克（Puck）的小小俏皮话突然应验；白刃战戛然而止；一道十万火急的命令从天而降，要「接着搞夜战」，或者「接着搞前哨勤务」；于是这两门学问之一又轮到那颗幼稚的心啜上几口，随即又突然从它焦渴的唇边抽走，换上别的什么重新开张——没准还是一件早已开张过又被丢下的事。在这幅东鳞西爪的奇幻图样的织造过程中，一个连队可能三四次开始学白刃战，每次不等学到一半便被岔开，末了开到法国去，怀里揣着好几套字母表，每套的 A、B、C 都学到发腻，任何一套的 X、Y、Z 却都不曾沾唇；士兵们只好凭天赋之光，去即兴自创那记短刺和其他更靠后的字母——兴许还是在同那些所受训练不如我们这般诙谐的德国人的贴身接触里。

这一切并非无处不在。但它可能发生，也确实发生过。于是士兵们瞪眼惊叹。诚然，当局最不济的时候，也还有几阵追求完美的狂热。可就连这几阵，也会以其自己的方式，坐实讥诮者座位上的那位新客。一道命令可能突然下来，说要举行旅级或其他检阅，于是在短短一个钟头里，当局便可能变成中世纪的人：那人骤然病倒，地狱的苦痛攫住了他的心，他只觉得必须不靠修身就把上帝打点妥帖——因为把品行端正起来，所要的时间恐怕比他手里有的还多。在这场虔诚的癫狂里，一切使德国人不痛快的措施，都得让位给头等要务：粉饰坟墓；那本来攻得下霍亨索伦突出部（Hohenzollern Redoubt）的干劲，全数投进并不存在的班的队形操演——因为某人疏懒，班压根没有编成——或者投进把一些人藏起来的工程——因为某人失于调教，他们上不得检阅的台面；老军士们会向士兵们传授诀窍：往背囊里恰到好处地塞进木条，叫那看似装满的背囊显得方方正正、有棱有角，另有诸如此类洗净杯盘外面的家常偏方。「花架子（eyewash）？」这些光明之子一面传授，一面说。「当然是花架子，全是花架子。这老世界上，什么不是花架子？」

===Original===

It was a question much asked at the time by those whose postwar inclinations to answer “Nothing, among the lot who run England now” are whitening the hair of statesmen. They were then only asking “How far does it go? How much of the timber is rotten”? Enough to bring down the whole house? Here, there, everywhere the men’s new suspicion peered about in the dark and the half-light. Most of the men were the almost boundless reservoirs of patience, humility, and good humour that common Englishmen are. They would take long to run dry. But the waters were steadily falling. Most of them had come from civil employments in which the curse of Adam still holds and a man must either work or get out, mind his p’s and q’s, or go short of his victuals. They knew that in civil life a foreman who thieved like some of the Regular N.C.O.’s would soon be in the street or in gaol. They knew that in civil life a manager who could not get down to the point any better than the colonel or the major would soon have the business piled up on the rocks. Here was an eye-opening find﻿—a world in which any old rule of that kind could be dodged if you got the right tip. It became the dominant topic for talk, more dominant even than food, the staple theme of the conversation of soldiers. How far did the rottenness go? Would they ever get to the other side of this bog through which poor old England was wading? If you bored deeper and deeper still into this amazing old Regular Army would there ever come a point at which you would strike the good firm stone of English decency and sense again? And was it open to hope that in Germany, too, such failures abounded﻿—that these diseases of ours were rife in all armies and not in the British alone, so that there might be a chance for us still, as there is for one toothless dog fighting another?

Whatever else might lack in our training-camps throughout England during the spring and summer of 1915, good fresh food for suspicion always abounded. Runlets of news and rumour came trickling from France; wounded soldiers talked and could not be censored; they talked of the failure of French; of the sneer on the face of France; of Staff work that hung up whole platoons of our men, like old washing or scarecrows, to rot on uncut German wire; of little, splendid bands of company officers and men who did take bits of enemy trench, in spite of it all, and then were bombed to death by the Germans at leisure, no support coming, no bombs to throw back﻿—and here, at home, old Regular colonels were saying to hollow squares of their men: “I hear that in France there’s a certain amount of throwing of some sort of ginger-beer bottles about, but the old Lee-Metford’s good enough for me.”

===Chinese===

当年这个问题被问得很多，问的人正是那样一批人——他们战后动辄回答：「如今治理英格兰的这一拨人里，没有一样不是花架子」——正是这类倾向，眼下正叫政治家们愁白了头。那时他们问的还只是：「这毛病蔓延多深？木料朽了多少？」朽得够不够把整座房子压塌？这里、那里、四处，士兵们新生的疑心，在黑暗与半明里窥探。士兵们大多是寻常英国人（Englishmen）那种几乎无边无际的耐心、谦卑与好性气的蓄水池。要抽干，得费很久的工夫。可水位在稳稳地落。他们大多来自那样的民间职业：亚当所受的诅咒在那里依然有效，人要么干活，要么走人；要么谨言慎行，要么挨饿。他们知道，民间生活里，一个手脚不干净如某些正规军军士的工头，很快便会上街讨饭或进班房。他们知道，民间生活里，一个论切中要害还不如那位上校或少校的经理，很快便会让整桩生意触礁搁浅。而这里却是一个开眼的发现——一个世界，在那里，这一类老规矩，只要摸着正确的门道，随便哪一条都躲得过去。于是它成了压倒一切的谈资，比伙食还压倒一切——须知伙食本是士兵谈话的主粮。朽坏蔓延到多远了？他们到底能不能走到这片泥沼的对岸——可怜的老英格兰（England）此刻正在其中跋涉？倘若你往这支令人惊异的正规军老营里一钻再钻、愈钻愈深，会不会钻到一个处所，重新触到英国人的体面与见识那块坚实的好石头？还有没有指望，德国那边也尽是这样的败绩——我们的这些病，各国军队里都在流行，不独英国军队如此——因而我们或许还有一线生机，正如一条没了牙的狗跟另一条没了牙的狗打架，总还有一线生机一样？

1915 年春夏，遍布英格兰的各个训练营里，别的无论缺什么，喂养疑心的好新鲜饲料从来不缺。一股股消息与传闻从法国涓涓渗来；负伤的士兵们要说话，又无法审查；他们讲到弗伦奇（French）的败绩；讲到法兰西脸上的讥诮；讲到参谋（Staff）工作的妙笔：把我们整排整排的人，像旧衣裳或稻草人似的，挂上未曾剪断的德国铁丝网，任其腐烂；讲到一小股一小股光彩夺目的连排军官与士兵，不顾一切，硬是夺下几段敌军战壕，随后德国人不慌不忙，把他们一个个炸死——援兵不来，也没有手榴弹可回掷——而在本土这边，正规军的老上校们正对围成空心方阵的部下训话：「听说在法国，有那么点子扔姜汁汽水瓶子的营生；可要我说，老李-梅特福（Lee-Metford）步枪就尽够我用了。」

===Original===

No need, indeed, to look as far away as France. London, to any open eye, was grotesque with a kind of fancy-dress ball of noncombatant khaki: it seemed as if no well-to-do person could be an abstainer from warfare too total to go about disguised as a soldier. He might be anything﻿—a lord lieutenant, an honorary colonel, a dealer in horses, a valuer of cloth, an accountant, an actor in full work, a recruiter of other men for the battles that he avoided himself, a “soldier politician” of swiftly and strangely acquired field rank and the “swashing and martial outside” of a Rosalind, and a Rosalind’s record of active service. No doubt this latter carnival was not to be at its height till most of the New Army of 1914 was well out of the way. Conscription had not yet been vouchsafed to the prayers of healthy young publicists who then begged themselves off before tribunals. The ultimate farce of the mobbing of the relatively straight “conscientious objector” by these, his less conscientious brother-objectors, had still to be staged. But already the comedy, like Mercutio’s wound, was enough; it served. Colonel Repington’s confessional diary had not been published, but the underworld which it reveals was pretty correctly guessed by the New Army’s rising suspicion. And rumour said that all the chief tribes of posturers, shirkers, “have-a-good-timers,” and jobbers were banding themselves together against the one man in high place whom the New Army believed, with the assurance of absolute faith, to be straight and “a tryer.” It was said that Kitchener was to be set upon soon by a league of all the sloths whom he had put to work, the “stunt” journalists whom he had kept at a distance, the social principalities and powers whose jobs he would not do. All the slugs of the commonwealth were to combine against the commonwealth unpleasantly dutiful gardener﻿—down with his lantern and can of caustic solution!

===Chinese===

其实，何须望到法国那么远。伦敦，在任何一只睁开的眼睛看来，正被一场非战斗卡其服的化装舞会装点得怪模怪样：似乎凡是家境优渥的人，都无法把战争戒得太彻底，以致不肯乔装成士兵四处走动。他可以是什么都行——郡尉、名誉上校、马贩子、呢料估价员、会计师、戏约不断的演员、专替别人报名去打他自己避之不及的仗的招募员、一位以又快又蹊跷的方式挣得校官军衔的「军人政客」，有着罗瑟琳（Rosalind）那副「咋呼威武的武人外表」，外加罗瑟琳那份戎马生涯的赫赫战功。毫无疑问，这场压轴的狂欢，要等到 1914 年新军（New Army）的大部被打发得远远的，方能臻于极盛。征兵制（conscription）当时还没有应那些健壮的年轻政论家的祷告而降临——这些人后来又在免役裁审庭（tribunal）面前替自己讨了免役。这些良心欠着斤两的「拒服兄弟」们，围攻相对清白的「良心拒服兵役者」（conscientious objector）——这一终极闹剧，当时还有待开演。可这场喜剧已经像茂丘西奥（Mercutio）的伤口一样，虽不深如井、阔如教堂之门，却也够了；管用。雷平顿上校（Colonel Repington）那部忏悔录式的日记当时尚未出版，但它所揭出的那个阴间世界，新军那日益高起的疑心早已猜得八九不离十。又有传闻说，装腔作势者、逃差者、「及时行乐者」、营私舞弊者的各个大头领部落，正在结成同盟，对付高阶上唯一那个人——新军以绝对信仰的确信，认定此人清正，是个「肯实干的人」。据说基奇纳（Kitchener）很快就要遭到围攻了：结盟反对他的，有被他赶去干活的全体懒汉，有被他拒之门外的「爆猛料」记者，还有那些他偏不去做的、社会上「执政的、掌权的」们的美差。全邦国的鼻涕虫们就要合起来，对付邦国那位令人不快却恪尽职守的园丁了——放翻他的提灯，打翻他那罐苛性药水！

### VI / 六

===Original===

It was, of course, an incomplete view of the case. Shall we have Henries, Fluellens, and Erpinghams at the hand of God, and no Bardolphs, Pistols, and Nyms? Our stage was not really rotten by any means; only half-rotten, like others of man’s institutions. Half the Old Army, at least, was exemplary. Even among politicians unselfishness may, with some trouble, be found. Still, this is no exposition of what the New Army ought to have said to itself as it lay on the ground after Lights Out compounding the new temper which comes out today, but only of what it did say. It was reacting. In the first weeks of the war most of the flock had too simply taken on trust all that its pastors and masters had said. Now, after believing rather too much, they were out to believe little or nothing﻿—except that in the lump pastors and masters were frauds. From any English training-camp, about that time, you almost seemed to see a light steam rising, as it does from a damp horse. This was illusion beginning to evaporate.

===Chinese===

当然，这只是对案情的一个不完全的看法。难道上帝既赐下亨利王们、弗鲁爱伦们和厄平厄姆们，便不附送几个巴道夫们、毕斯托们和尼姆们么？我们的舞台绝说不上真正朽坏；只是半朽，一如人类的其他制度。旧陆军至少有一半堪称楷模。便是在政客中间，只要肯费些气力，也寻得到无私的人。不过，这里并非要陈述新军在熄灯号（Lights Out）之后卧在地上、酝酿今日显露的这副新心境时本该对自己说些什么，只是记录它实际说了什么。它是在反弹。战争的头几个星期，这群羊大多把牧人与师长们说的话，太简单地信了个十足。如今，在相信得太多之后，他们转过身来，要少信，乃至什么都不信——只除一条：牧人与师长，一概而论，皆是骗子。大约就在那时，从英格兰的任何一座训练营，你几乎都能望见一缕轻汽袅袅升起，像湿漉漉的马身上蒸起的那种。这便是幻象开始蒸散。

## XIV Our Moderate Satanists / 十四 我们温和的撒旦主义者

### I / 一

===Original===
Satanism is one of the words that most of us simple people have heard others use; we guiltily feel that we ought to know what it means, but do not quite like to ask, lest we expose the nakedness of the land. Then comes Professor Gilbert Murray, one of the few learned men who are able to make a thing clear to people not quite like themselves, and tells us all about it in a cheap, small book, easy to read. It seems that the Satanists, or the pick of the sect, were Bohemian Protestants at the start, and quite plain, poor men from the country.

===Chinese===
撒旦主义（Satanism）是这样一种词：我们大多数头脑简单的人都听人用过；我们心虚地觉得理应知道它的意思，却又不太敢问，唯恐把这方土地的赤身露体暴露出来。于是来了吉尔伯特·默里教授（Gilbert Murray）——寥寥几位能把一件事向与自己不甚相同的人讲明白的学者之一——用一本便宜易读的小书，把这事原原本本讲给我们听。原来撒旦主义者（Satanists），或者说这个教派里的尖子，起初是波希米亚（Bohemian）的新教徒，而且是地地道道的乡下穷人。

===Original===
> “Every person in authority met them with rack and sword, cursed their religious leaders as emissaries of the Devil, and punished them for all the things which they considered holy. The earth was the Lord’s, and the Pope and Emperor were the vicegerents of God upon the earth. So they were told; and in time they accepted the statement. That was the division of the world. On the one side God, Pope and Emperor, and the army of persecutors; on the other themselves, downtrodden and poor.﻿ ﻿…”

How easy to understand! In crude works of non-imagination the wicked, *repente turpissimus*, suddenly says, some fine morning, “Evil, be thou my good.” In life the conversion is slower. It is a gradual process of coming to feel that what has passed officially as true, right, and worshipful is so implicated in work manifestly dirty, and so easily made to serve the ends of the greedy, lazy, and cruel, that faith in its authenticity has to be given up as not to be squared with the facts of the world. From feeling this it is not a long step to the further surmise that the grand traditional foe of that old moral order of the world, now so severely discredited, may be less black than so lying an artist has painted him. Does he not, anyhow, stand at the opposite pole to that which has just proved itself base? He, too, perhaps, is some helpless butt of the slings and arrows of an enthroned barbarity tormenting the world. The legend about his condign fall from heaven may only be some propagandist lie﻿—all we are suffered to hear about some early crime in the long, beastly annals of governmental misdoing. So thought trips, fairly lightly, along till your worthy Bohemian peasant, literal, serious, and straight, like the plain workingman of all countrysides, turns, with a desperate logical integrity and courage, right away from a world order which has called itself divine and shown itself diabolic. He will embrace, in its stead, the only other world order supposed to be extant: the one which the former order called diabolic; at any rate, he has not wittingly suffered any such wrong at its hand as the scourges of Popes and of Emperors. So the plain man emerges a Satanist.

===Chinese===
> 「每一位当权者都以刑架与刀剑迎接他们，咒骂他们的宗教领袖是魔鬼的使者，并因他们视为神圣的一切而惩罚他们。大地是主的，教皇与皇帝是上帝在尘世的摄理。他们听到的便是如此；久而久之，他们也接受了这个说法。世界就这样分作两半。一边是上帝、教皇与皇帝，以及迫害者的大军；另一边是他们自己，受践踏的穷人。……」

这多好懂！在粗制滥造的缺乏想象之作里，恶人会「一转眼丑到极处」（repente turpissimus），在某个晴朗的早晨忽然开口：「恶啊，你就作我的善吧。」在真实人生里，这种改宗要慢得多。它是一个渐进的过程：人渐渐感到，那些经官方认可为真、为正、为可敬的东西，同明显肮脏的勾当牵连得那样深，又那样容易被拿来充当贪婪者、懒惰者与残忍者营私的工具，以至于对它本真性的信仰不得不放弃——因为再无法与这世界的事实相合。由此再往前迈一小步，便不难生出进一步的猜想：那个旧日世界道德秩序的传统大敌——那秩序如今已颜面扫地——未必像那位撒谎的画师把他涂抹得那样黑。无论如何，他不正是刚刚自证其卑劣之物的对立极吗？他，或许，也是某个高踞宝座、荼毒世界的野蛮势力投石与流矢之下无助的靶子。关于他从天上罪有应得地坠落的那段传说，说不定只是某个宣传的谎言——是那部冗长而兽性的政府作恶编年史里，某桩早期罪行获准让我们听见的全部。思绪就这样相当轻盈地一路溜达下去，直到你那位可敬的波希米亚农夫——同一切乡野的朴实做工者一样认死理、认真、直性子——怀着一种孤注一掷的逻辑上的完整与勇气，从那个自称神圣、实已显出魔性的世界秩序上整个掉转身去。他将转而拥抱据信现存的唯一另一种世界秩序：旧秩序称之为魔性的那一种；至少，他还不曾明知故受它加于己身的这等冤屈，像教皇们与皇帝们的那些鞭笞一样。于是，朴实的人便脱胎为一名撒旦主义者。

### II / 二

===Original===
Today the convert does not insist upon bearing the new name. He does not, except in the case of a few doctrinaire bigots, repeat any Satanist creed. But in several portions of Europe the war made conversions abound. Imagine the state of mind that it must have induced in many a plain Russian peasant, literal, serious, and straight, like the Bohemian. First the Tsar, in the name of God and of Holy Russia, sent him, perhaps without so much as a rifle, to starve and be shelled in a trench. If he escaped, the Soviet chiefs, in the name of Justice, sent him to fight against those for whom the Tsar had made him fight before, while his wife and babies were starved by those whom he fought both for and against. When his fighting was done he was made, in the name of social right, an industrial conscript or wage-slave. If alive, today, he is probably overworked and starved, perhaps far from home, his family life broken up, his instinct or right of self-direction ignored or punished as treason by rulers whom he did not choose, his whole country in danger of lapsing into the abject miseries of an uncared-for fowl-run﻿—all brought about in the name of human freedom.

Consider, again, the case of some German or Austrian widow with many young children. The Kaiser’s Government, breathing the most Christian sentiments, gave the Fatherland war in her time; her husband was killed, her country is ruined, her children are growing up stunted and marred by all the years of semi-starvation; the Paris Press is crying out, in the name of moral order throughout the world, that they ought to be starved more drastically; part of the English Press complains, in the tone of an outraged spiritual director, that she has shown no adequate signs of repentance of the Kaiser’s sins, and that she and hers are living like fighting cocks; the German Agrarian Party, in the name of Patriotism, manoeuvres to keep her from getting her weekly ounce or two of butcher’s meat from abroad more cheaply than they would like to sell it to her at home.

What could you say to such people if they should break out at last in despair and defiance: “Anyhow, all these people, here and abroad, who take upon themselves to speak for God and duty and patriotism and liberty and loyalty are evil people, and do evil things. Shall not all these trees that they swear by be judged by their fruits? Away with them into the fire, God and country and social duty and justice and every old phrase that used to seem more than a phrase till the war came to show it up for what it was worth as a means to right conduct in men?” Of course you could say a great deal. But at every third word they could incommode you with some stumping case of the foulest thing done in the holiest name till you would be shamed into silence at the sight of all the crowns of thorns brought to market by keepers of what you still believe to be vineyards. So, throughout much of Europe, Satan’s most promising innings for many long years has begun.

===Chinese===
今天，改宗者并不坚持背负这个新名号。除了少数教条派的顽固者，他也不复诵什么撒旦主义信经。但在欧洲的好几个地方，战争使改宗者大为繁衍。试想这场战争必然在许多俄国农夫心里造成的心境——他们同波希米亚人一样认死理、认真、直性子。起初，沙皇（Tsar）奉上帝与神圣俄罗斯（Holy Russia）之名，把他送进战壕挨饿挨炮，兴许连一支步枪都不发给。他若逃得性命，苏维埃（Soviet）的首领们便奉正义之名，派他去打先前沙皇让他为之而战的那一方，与此同时，他的妻儿正被那些他既为之打仗、又与之作战的人饿着。仗打完了，他又奉社会权利之名，被造成一名产业应征工（industrial conscript）或工资奴隶。他若活到今天，多半劳作过度、饥饿缠身，或许还流落远方，家庭生活破碎，自作主张的本能或权利被忽视，或被那些并非由他选出的统治者当作叛国罪惩办，他的整个国家濒于沦入一座无人照看的家禽圈那种卑贱苦境——而这一切，都是以人类自由之名促成的。

再看某个拖着一群年幼子女的德国或奥地利寡妇的情形。德皇（Kaiser）的政府满口最基督徒的情操，在她的年代给了父邦一场战争；她的丈夫死了，她的国家毁了，她的孩子们在经年累月的半饥半饱中长得孱弱受损；巴黎的报界（Press）正以全世界的道德秩序之名大喊大叫，说他们本该饿得更狠一些；英国报界的一部分则摆出一副被冒犯的神师（spiritual director）的腔调，抱怨她对德皇的罪愆没有表现出足够的悔意，抱怨她和她的一家活得像斗鸡一般滋润；德国农业党（Agrarian Party）奉爱国主义之名巧为布置，不让她从国外更便宜地买到每周那一两盎司的鲜肉，好教她按他们乐意的价钱在国内买。

倘若这些人终于在绝望与抗争里发作起来，你还能对他们说什么呢——「横竖是这么回事：这些此地的、外洋的，自任为上帝、义务、爱国主义、自由与忠贞代言人的家伙，全是恶人，干的全是恶事。他们凭以起誓的这些树，难道不该凭它们的果实来审判吗？把它们统统砍倒，丢进火里去吧——上帝、国家、社会义务、正义，还有一切旧日听着不止是空话的老词，直到战争来揭它的底，显出它作为使人行为端正的手段究竟值几个钱？」你当然可以说上一大篇。可每说到第三个词，他们就能拿出一桩以至圣之名行至脏之事的压场铁案，叫你难堪，直到你在那一顶顶荆棘冠面前羞愧得哑口无言——贩卖它们的，恰恰是你至今还信为葡萄园看园人的那批人。就这样，在欧罗巴的许多地方，撒旦多年来最看好的一局击球，已然开场。

### III / 三

===Original===
In their vices as well as their virtues the English preserve a distinguished moderation. They do not utterly shrink from jobbery, for example; they do from a job that is flagrant or gross. They give judgeships as prizes for party support, but not to the utterly briefless, the dullard who knows no more law than necessity. Building contractors, when in the course of their rise they become town councillors, do not give bribes right and left: their businesses thrive without that. An Irishman running a Tammany in the States cannot thus hold himself in: the humorous side of corruption charms him too much: he wants to let the grand farce of roguery rip for all it is worth. But the English private’s pet dictum, “There’s reason in everything,” rules the jobber, the profiteer, the shirker and placeman of Albion as firmly as it controls the imagination of her Wordsworths and the political idealism of her Cromwells and Pitts. Like her native cockroaches and bugs, whose moderate stature excites the admiration and envy of human dwellers among the corresponding fauna of the tropics, the caterpillars of her commonwealth preserve the golden mean; few, indeed, are flamboyants or megalomaniacs.

So, when the war with its great opportunities came we were but temperately robbed by our own birds of prey. Makers of munitions made mighty fortunes out of our peril. Still, every British soldier did have a rifle, at any rate when he went to the front. I have watched a twelve-inch gun fire, in action, fifteen of its great bales or barrels of high explosives, fifteen running, and only three of the fifteen costly packages failed to explode duly on its arrival beyond. Vendors of soldiers’ clothes and boots acquired from us the wealth which dazzles us all in these days of our own poverty. They knew how to charge: they made hay with a will while the blessed suns of 1914﻿–﻿18 were high in the heavens. Still, nearly all the tunics made in that day of temptation did hold together; none of the boots, so far as I knew or heard tell, was made of brown paper. “He that maketh haste to be rich shall not be innocent.” Still, there is reason in everything. “Meden agan,” as the Greeks said﻿—temperance in all things, even in robbery, even in patriotism and personal honour. Our profiteers did not bid Satan get him behind them; but they did ask him to stand a little to one side.

===Chinese===
英格兰人无论在美德上还是在恶行上，都保持着一种出众的节制。比如说，他们对营私并不全然却步；对明晃晃、赤裸裸的肥缺才却步。他们把法官职位当党派支持的犒赏分发，却不至于发给全然接不到案子的律师，发给那种所懂的法律不比「必要」二字多上一分的蠢材。建筑承包商在发迹途中当上市议员后，并不四处撒贿：没有这个，他们的生意照样兴旺。一个在美国操办坦慕尼（Tammany）协会式营生的爱尔兰人就憋不住这份克制：腐败的滑稽面太迷他了：他巴不得让那出无赖大闹剧放开手脚、演足本钱。但英格兰列兵的心爱格言——「凡事都有个分寸」（There's reason in everything）——同样牢固地统治着阿尔比恩（Albion）的营私者、暴利者、逃差者与占缺食俸者，一如它统辖着她那些华兹华斯（Wordsworth）们的想象力，统辖着她那些克伦威尔（Cromwell）们与皮特（Pitt）们的政治理想主义。正像她土生的蟑螂与臭虫以适中的体型，引得栖身于热带同类虫豸之间的人类住户艳羡称奇，她邦国里的「邦国之蠹」（caterpillars of the commonwealth）也持守黄金中道；其中真正长得像凤凰木（flamboyant）那样炫夸的、或长成自大狂的，确乎寥寥。

于是，当战争携着它的大好机会到来时，我们不过被自家的猛禽有节制地劫掠了一场。军火制造商们从我们的危难里挣出了泼天的家私。不过，每一名英国士兵终究还是有一支步枪的——至少在开赴前线时是如此。我看过一门十二英寸炮在实战中打出它那大宗高爆炸药的十五大包或大桶，十五发连珠，十五个昂贵的包裹中只有三发，抵达彼端时未能在应爆之际适时炸开。军服军靴的卖主们从我们手里赚下的财富，在我们今日的自家贫穷里，晃得我们所有人眼晕。他们懂得开价：趁着 1914–18 年那些蒙福的太阳高悬中天，他们起劲地晒他们的草。不过，那个试炼之日缝制的军上装几乎全都还兜得住针脚；军靴呢，就我所知所闻，没有一双是拿棕色的纸做的。「急于发财的，必不无辜。」话虽如此，凡事都有个分寸。「凡事勿过度」（Meden agan），希腊人如是说——诸事有节，即便在劫掠之中，即便在爱国主义与个人名节之中。我们的暴利者不曾喝令撒旦「退到我后边去」；不过倒确实请他稍稍往旁边站一站。

===Original===
So, too, in the army. Some old Regular sergeant-majors would sell every stripe that they could, but they would not sell a map to the enemy. Some of our higher commanders would use their A.D.C. rooms as funk-holes to shelter the healthy young nephew or son of their good friend the earl, or their distant cousin the marquis. But there were others. Sometimes a part of our Staff would almost seem to forget the war, and give its undivided mind to major struggles﻿—its own intestine “strafes” and the more bitter war against uncomplaisant politicians at home. But presently it would remember, and work with a will. There was, again, an undeniable impulse abroad, among the “best people” of the old Army, to fall back towards G.H.Q. and its safety as soon as the first few months made it clear that this was to be none of our old gymkhana wars, but almost certainly lethal to regimental officers who stayed it out with their units. But this centripetal instinct, this “safety first” movement, though real, was moderate. Lists of headquarter formations might show an appreciable excess of names of some social distinction. But not an outrageous excess. Some peers and old baronets and their sons were still getting killed, by their own choice, along with the plebs to the very end of the war. Again, all through the war one could not deny that those who had chosen the safer part, or had it imposed upon them, absorbed a stout and peckish lion’s share of the rewards for martial valour. And yet they did not absolutely withhold these meeds from officers and men who fought. The king of beasts being duly served, these hard-bitten jackals got some share, though not perhaps, for their numbers, a copious one. Some well-placed shirkers were filled with good things, but the brave were not sent utterly empty away. Guardsmen and cavalrymen, the least richly brained soldiers we had, kept to themselves the bulk of the distinguished jobs for which brain-work was needed; and yet the poor foot-soldier was not expressly taboo; quite a good billet would fall to him sometimes﻿—Plumer commanded an army.

As with the moral virtues, so with the mental. Brilliancy, genius, scientific imagination in any higher command would have caused almost a shock; a general with the demonic insight to see that he had got the enemy stiff at Arras in 1917 and at Cambrai the same autumn, might have seemed an outré highbrow, almost unsafe. And yet the utter slacker was not countenanced, and the dunce had been known to be so dull that he was sent home as an empty by those unexacting chiefs. There was reason in everything, even in reason.

===Chinese===
军队里也是一样。有些正规军的老军士长肯把能卖的每一道杠都卖掉，却不肯把一张地图卖给敌人。我们的某些高级指挥官会把身边的副官处（A.D.C. rooms）用作怯洞，收容好友伯爵家健壮的外甥或公子，或收容他们那位身为侯爵的远房表亲。但也另有其人。有时，我们参谋部（Staff）的一部分人几乎像是要把战争忘了，把全副心思投给几场大战——自家内部的「惩戒」混战，以及针对国内不凑趣政客的那场更苦的战争。可过不多时他们便会想起正务，发奋干上一阵。再者，旧陆军的「上流人士」当中，无疑涌动着一股向总司令部（G.H.Q.）及其安全地带回撤的冲动——头几个月一过，事情已然清楚：这一仗绝非我们旧日那些竞技会式的战争，对随本部队撑到底的团级军官而言，几乎必然致命。但这股向心的本能，这场「安全第一」的运动，虽则真实，却是有节制的。各级司令部编队的名册上，或许显出社会名流的姓氏略有过量之数。但并非骇人地过量。直到战争末了，仍有一些贵族院议员、老从男爵及其子嗣出于自己的选择，与平民子弟一同战死。再者，整个战争期间无可否认：那些选中了较安全一途、或被安插在较安全一途的人，把武勇犒赏中一份肥硕馋人的狮子份额吞了下去。然而他们并未把这些报偿从真正作战的军官与士兵那里绝对扣住。百兽之王既已如仪供奉，这些咬惯硬骨头的豺狼也分得了一份——尽管论人头，未必算丰。几个占得善位的逃差者被塞足了好处，但勇士们也不至于被全然两手空空地打发。近卫军人与骑兵——我们这里脑子最不富裕的兵——把那些需要动脑的体面差事的大头留给了自己；然而可怜的步卒也未被明令列为禁忌；相当像样的一份好差事有时也会落到他头上——普卢默（Plumer）就统率过一个集团军。

道德上的诸德如此，心智上的诸德亦然。任何一级高层司令部里，才华、天才、科学的想象力都几乎会引起一场惊吓；一位将军若具魔性的洞察，看得出 1917 年在阿拉斯（Arras）、同年秋天在康布雷（Cambrai）他已把敌人打得瘫软，那他多半要显得是个过火的高眉，几乎不太保险。然而彻头彻尾的懒虫也没人容让，低能儿也有过出名的先例：蠢到被那些不苛求的长官当作「返程空舱」（empty）送回了国内。凡事都有个分寸，连理性之中也有分寸。

### IV / 四

===Original===
All this relative mildness in the irritants administered to the common Englishman as soldier had its counterpart in the men’s ingrained moderateness of reaction. At Bray-sur-Somme during the battle of 1916 I saw a French soldier go so mad with rage at what he considered to be the deficiencies of his leaders that he brought out each article of his kit and equipment in succession to the door of his billet and threw it into the deep central mud of the road with a separate curse, at each cast, on war, patriotism, civilization, and the Commander-in-Chief. This Athanasian service of commination endured for a full quarter of an hour. But from an English private who witnessed the rite it only drew the phlegmatic diagnosis: “He’ll ’ave ’ad a drop o’ sugar-water an’ got excited.” Firewater itself could not excite the English soldier to so rounded an eloquence or to so sweeping a series of judgements. He never thought of throwing his messing-tin and his paybook into the mud; still less of forming a Council of Soldiers and Workmen. Either step would have been of the abhorred nature of a “scene.”

Unaggressive, unoriginal, anti-extreme, contemptuous of all “hot air” and windy ideas, he too was braked by the same internal negations that helped to keep his irredeemably middling commanders equidistant from genius and from arrant failure. Confronted now with the frustration of so many too-high hopes, the discrediting of so many persons or institutions hitherto taken on trust, he did not say, as the humbler sort of Bolshevist seems to have said in his heart: “What order, or disorder, could ever be worse than this which has failed? Why not anything, any wild-seeming nihilism or fantasy of savage rudeness, rather than sit quiet under this old contemptible rule?” Instead of contracting a violent new sort of heat he simply went cold, and has remained so.
