Where a Slav or a Latin might have become a hundred percent. Satanist he became about a thirty percenter. The disbelief, the suspicion, the vacuous space in the disendowed heart, the spiritual rubbish-heap of draggled banners and burst drums﻿—all that blank, unlighting and unwarming part of Satanism was his, without any other: a Lucifer cold as a moon prompted him listlessly, not to passionate efforts of crime, but to self-regarding and indolent apathy.

===Chinese===
施加给当兵的普通英格兰人的这些刺激物既相对温和，士兵们骨子里反应的温和便与之相对称。1916 年战事期间，在索姆河畔布雷（Bray-sur-Somme），我见过一个法国士兵因认定其长官们种种不济而怒到发狂，把随身装备的一件件行头依次拿到宿营地门口，掷进路中央的深泥里，每掷一件，便分别把战争、爱国主义、文明与总司令（Commander-in-Chief）各咒一遍。这场阿塔纳修信经式的宣罪大礼足足持续了一刻钟。而目击此典礼的一名英格兰列兵，只给出了一副黏液质的诊断：「他准是灌了一口糖水，这才来劲的。」就是火水本身，也激发不出英格兰士兵这般圆熟的辞令，或这般横扫一切的一连串判词。他从不曾想把饭盒和薪饷簿扔进泥里；更不曾想去组织一个士兵与工人委员会（Council of Soldiers and Workmen）。无论哪一步，都属于他所深恶的那类「当众闹一场」（scene）的性质。

不好斗，不喜独创，反极端，蔑视一切「空话」（hot air）与虚浮的理念，他同样被那几道内在的否定刹着车——正是它们，把他那些无可救药的中游长官们拴在与天才等距、与彻头彻尾的失败也等距之处。如今面对这么多期望过高的希望的落空、这么多先前凭信而取的人物或机构的信誉扫地，他没有像出身较寒微的那类布尔什维克（Bolshevik）似乎在心底说过的那样说：「既然失败的是这个，还有什么秩序、什么无秩序能比它更糟？何不随便来点什么——哪种看似狂野的虚无主义、哪种蛮荒粗鄙的狂想都行——总好过在这套老旧可鄙的治道下安坐？」他没有染上一种新式的暴热，只径直冷了下来，而且一直冷着。斯拉夫人或拉丁人也许会变成百分之百的撒旦主义者，他则只成了个大约百分之三十的。那份不信，那份疑心，那颗被褫夺净尽的心里的空落空间，那堆拖着泥的破旗与胀裂的鼓堆成的精神垃圾——撒旦主义中所有那片不发光、不供暖的空白都是他的，此外再无别的份额：一位冷得像月亮的路西法（Lucifer）懒懒地点拨着他——不是点拨他去干热血沸腾的罪恶，而是点拨他堕入只顾自家、怠惰成性的冷漠。

===Original===
From the day he went into the army till now he has been learning to take many things less seriously than he did. First what Burke calls the pomps and plausibilities of the world. He has tumbled many kings into the dust and proved the strongest emperor assailable. I remember a little private, who seemed to know Dickens by heart, applying to William the Second in 1915 the words used by the Game Chicken about Mr. Dombey﻿—“as stiff a cove as ever he see, but within the resources of science to double him up with one blow in the waistcoat.” This he proved, too, he and his like, casting down the proud from their seats with little help from all that was highly placed and reverently regarded in his own country. Our ruling class had, on the whole, failed, and had to be pulled through by him and the French and Americans; that feeling, in one form or another, is clear in the common man’s mind. He may not know in detail the record of French as commander-in-chief, nor the exact state of the Admiralty which let the *Goeben* and the *Breslau* go free, nor the inner side of the diplomacy which added Turkey, and even Bulgaria, to our enemies, nor yet the wellborn underworld of wartime luxury, disloyalty, and intrigue which notorious memoirs have since revealed. But some horse instinct or some pricking in his thumbs told him correctly that in every public service manned mainly by our upper classes the wartime achievement was relatively low. There is very little natural inclination to class jealousy among plain Englishmen. Equalitarian theory does not interest them much. Their general relish for a gamble makes them rather like a lucky-bag or bran-tub society in which anyone may pick up, with luck, a huge unearned prize. By cheerfully helping to keep up the big gaming-hell, by giving Barnatos and Joels pretty full value for their win, the prewar governing class gained a kind of strength which a prouder and more fastidious aristocracy would have forgone. It stands in little physical danger now. But it lives, since the war, in a kind of contempt. The one good word that the average private had for bestowal among his unseen “betters” during the latter years of the war was for the King. “*He* did give up his beer,” was said a thousand times by men whom that symbolic act of willing comradeship with the dry throat on the march and the war-pinched household at home had touched and astonished.

===Chinese===
从入伍那天起到今天，他一直在学着把许多事情看得不像从前那样郑重。头一样，便是伯克（Burke）所谓尘世的浮华与巧伪。他把好些国王掀进了尘土，还证明了最强大的皇帝也挨得着打。我记得一个小个子列兵——他似乎能把狄更斯（Dickens）倒背如流——在 1915 年把「斗鸡」（the Game Chicken）评董贝先生（Mr. Dombey）的那句话用到了威廉二世（William the Second）身上：「是他这辈子见过的顶硬挺的一个家伙，可凭科学的能耐，朝马甲上一记，就能把他撂成对折。」这话他和他一类人也当真证明了——把骄傲者从座位上掀了下来，而他自己国内那些位居高处、备受敬畏的一切，几乎没帮上什么忙。我们的统治阶级总的来说败了阵，全亏他、法国人和美国人合力才拽过了关；这种感觉，以这样那样的形貌，清清楚楚地摆在普通人心里。他未必知道弗伦奇（French）任总司令期间的成绩细账，未必知道放走「戈本」号（Goeben）与「布雷斯劳」号（Breslau）的那班海军部（Admiralty）当时的底细，未必知道把土耳其、连保加利亚都添到我们敌人一边的那场外交的里子，也未必知道战时奢侈、不忠与密谋的那个出身高贵的阴间世界——如今已被几部恶名昭彰的回忆录揭了出来。但某种马匹式的直觉，或大拇指里的一阵刺痒，正确地告诉他：凡主要由我们上流阶级充任人员的公共部门，战时成绩都相对低下。普通英格兰人对阶级嫉恨的天然癖好极小。平等主义的理论引不起他们多大兴致。他们对博彩的普遍嗜好，倒使他们相当中意一种福袋或糠桶摸彩式的社会——人人凭手气都可能摸到一大份不劳而获的重奖。战前的统治阶级靠兴冲冲地帮衬着维持这座大赌窟，靠让巴纳托们（Barnatos）与乔尔们（Joels）把赢的钱花得十足够本，挣得了一种更骄傲、更讲究的贵族宁可弃掉的力量。它如今几乎没有什么人身的危险。但自开战以来，它一直活在一种轻蔑里。战争后几年，普通列兵在那些从未谋面的「上等人」中间唯一肯赏出的一句好话，是给国王的。「他倒是真把啤酒戒了」——这句话被说过上千遍，说话的人都曾被那个象征性的举动打动过、震住过：那是自愿结成的袍泽之义，与行军途中干渴的喉咙、与国内被战争掐紧的一户户人家同其甘苦。

===Original===
Other institutions, too, had been weighed in the balance. The War Office was only the commonest of many bywords. The Houses of Parliament, in which too many men of military age had demanded the forced enlistment of others, wore an air of insincerity, apart from the loss of prestige inevitable in a war; for armies always take the colour out of deliberative assemblies. To moderate this effect a large number of members who did not go to the war found means to wear khaki in London instead of black, but this well-conceived precaution only succeeded in further curling the lip of derision among actual soldiers. The churches, as we have seen, got their chance, made little or nothing of it, and came out of the war quite good secular friends with the men, but almost null and void in their eyes as ghostly counsellors, and stripped of the vague consequence with which many men had hitherto credited them on account of any divine mission they might be found to have upon closer acquaintance. Respect for the truthfulness of the Press was clean gone. The contrast between the daily events that men saw and the daily accounts that were printed was final. What the Press said thenceforth was not evidence. But still it had sent out plum puddings at Christmas.

Neither was anything evidence now that was said by a politician. A great many plain men had really drawn a distinction, all their lives, between the solemn public assurances of statesmen and the solemn public assurances of men who draw teeth outside dock-gates and take off their caps and call upon God to blast the health of their own darling children if a certain pill they have for sale does not cure colds, measles, ringworm, and the gripes within twenty-four hours of taking. A Swift might say there never was any difference, but the plain man had always firmly believed that there was. Now, after the war, he is shaken. Every disease which victory was to cure he sees raging worse than before: more poverty, less liberty, more likelihood of other wars, more spite between master and man, less national comradeship. And then the crucial test case, the solemn vow of the statesmen, all with their hands on their sleek bosoms, that if only the common man would save them just that once they would turn to and think of nothing else, do nothing else, but build him a house, assure him of work, settle him on land, make all England a paradise for him﻿—a “land fit for heroes to live in.” And then the sequel: the cold fit; the feint at house-building and its abandonment; all the bankruptcy of promise; the ultimate bilking, done by way of reluctant surrender to “anti-waste” stunts got up by the same cheap-jacks of the Press who in the first year of the war would have had the statesmen promise yet more wildly than they did. Colds, measles, ringworm, and gripes all flourishing, much more than twenty-four hours after, and new ailments added unto them.

===Chinese===
别的机构也一一被放上了天平。陆军部（War Office）不过是众多笑柄里最寻常的一个。议会两院——里面役龄男子太多了，他们曾大声要求强迫他人入伍——如今透着一股不真诚的气，这还不算战争必不可免的威望剥落；因为军队总会把议事机关的颜色吸褪。为缓和这种效果，一大批没有去参战的议员设法在伦敦穿卡其服、不穿黑衣；可这一番设想周到的防范，只成功地让真正打过仗的士兵嘲弄的嘴角又翘高了几分。诸教会呢，如我们所见，得了机会，却几乎无所建树，战争结束时与士兵们成了相当好的世俗朋友，但在他们眼里作为灵性顾问几乎归于无有无效，而且被剥去了那层虚悬的分量——先前许多人因他们兴许身负什么神圣使命而一直记在他们名下的分量，及至朝夕相处细看一番，也就看清了。对报界（Press）诚实性的敬意则荡然无存。人们每日所见之事与每日所印之言之间的反差，已成定谳。从此报界说的话，不再算是证据。不过，圣诞节往外寄李子布丁的事，它倒确实做过。

如今政治家说的任何话，也都算不得证据了。许多普通人一生中确实作过一个区分：政治家们庄严的公开保证，与另一种人的庄严公开保证之间——那种人在船坞门外拔牙，脱下帽子吁请上帝降祸于自己心肝儿女的健康，声称他叫卖的某种药丸若在服后二十四小时内治不好伤风、麻疹、癣疥与腹绞痛，便甘受天诛——是有分别的。换一位斯威夫特（Swift）也许会说，这两者从来就没有分别；可普通人向来坚信有分别。如今，战争过后，他动摇了。胜利本该治愈的每一种病，他都看见闹得比先前更凶：贫穷更多，自由更少，再打仗的可能更大，主仆之间的怨毒更深，举国的袍泽之义（comradeship）更少。接着是那桩决定性的试案：政治家们手按油亮的胸脯，个个庄严立誓——只要普通人肯救他们这一回，他们必将专心致志，别的什么都不想、什么都不做，只给他造屋，保他工作，安置他于土地，把整个英格兰给他造成乐园——一片「配得上英雄居住的国土」（a land fit for heroes to live in）。随后便是续集：那阵寒热病的冷发作；造屋的虚晃一招与撒手不管；许诺的全盘破产；到最后的赖账——以半推半就向「反浪费」（anti-waste）运动缴械的方式完成，而那场运动正是报界同一批江湖贩子鼓捣起来的；战争头一年，唯恐政治家们许诺得还不够疯狂、还想撺掇他们再疯狂些的，也是这批人。药服下去早已不止二十四小时、又不知多少个二十四小时，伤风、麻疹、癣疥与腹绞痛依然一派兴旺，新的病痛还添加其上。

===Original===
No relief, either, by running from one medicine-man to the next. Few of our disenchanted men doubt that the lightning cure of the Communist is only just another version of the lightning cure of the Tory, the authoritarian, the peremptory regimentalist. “Give *me* a free hand and all will be well with you.” Both say exactly the same thing in the end. One of them may call it the rule of the fittest, the other the rule of the proletariat; each means exactly the same thing﻿—the rule of himself, the enforcement on everyone else of his own darling theory of what is best for them, whether they know it or not. Small choice in rotten apples; one bellyful of east wind is a diet as poor as another. Not in the yells and counter-yells of this and that vendor of patent hot-air is the heart of the average ex-soldier engaged. Rather “Away with all gas-projectors alike” is his present feeling towards eloquent men, Left or Right. For the moment he knows them too well, and is tired of hearing of plans which might work if he were either a babe in arms or a Michael of super-angelic wisdom and power.

===Chinese===
指望从这个巫医跑到下个巫医去讨宽解，同样没有门。我们这些幻灭（disenchanted）了的人，很少有几个怀疑：共产党人的闪电疗法，不过是托利党人、威权主义者、那号颐指气使的纪律主义者的闪电疗法的又一个版本而已。「给我放开手脚，你们就万事皆好。」到头来，两边说的一字不差。一边或许名之曰适者当政，另一边或许名之曰无产者当政；各自所指分毫不差——他自己当政，把他自己那套「什么对他们最好」的心爱理论强加于其余所有人，不管人家识破不识破。烂苹果里没什么可挑的；饱喝一顿东风，跟饱喝另一顿东风一样，都是穷乏的饭食。普通退役军人（ex-soldier）的心，并不投在这个或那个专卖空话的贩子的叫喊与反叫喊里。面对口若悬河之辈，无论左右，他眼下的心情毋宁是：「把毒气投射器一律撤掉吧。」眼下他把这班人看得太透，也听腻了那些计划——那些唯有在他要么是怀中婴孩、要么是具有超天使级智慧与权能的米迦勒（Michael）时，才可能行得通的计划。

### V / 五

===Original===
You may be disillusioned about the value of things, or about their security, either coming to feel that your house is a poor place to live in or that, pleasant or not, it is likely enough to come down on your head. Of these two forms of discomfort our friend experiences both. Much that he took to be fairly noble now looks pretty mean; and much that seemed reassuringly stable is seen to be shaky. Civilization itself, the at any rate habitable dwelling which was to be shored up by the war, wears a strange new air of precariousness.

Even before the war a series of melancholy public misadventures had gone some way to awake the disquieting notion that civilization, the whole ordered, fruitful joint action of a nation, a continent, or the whole world, was only a bluff. When the world is at peace and fares well, the party of order and decency, justice and mercy and self-control, is really bluffing a much larger party of egoism and greed that would bully and grab if it dared. The deep antisocial offence of the “suffragettes,” with their hatchets and hunger-strikes, was that they gave away, in some measure, the bluff by which non-criminal people had hitherto kept some control over reluctant assentors to the rule of mutual protection and forbearance. They helped the baser sort to see that the bluff of civilization is at the mercy of anyone ready to run a little bodily risk in calling it. Sir Edward Carson took up the work. He “called” the bluff of the Pax Britannica, the presumption that armed treason to the law and order of the British Empire must lead to the discomfiture of the traitor, whoever he was; he presented Sinn Fein and every other would-be insurgent with proof that treason may securely do much more than peep at what it would; British subjects, he showed, might quite well conspire for armed revolt against the King’s peace and not be any losers, in their own persons, by doing it.

===Chinese===
你可以对事物的价值幻灭，也可以对事物的牢固幻灭：前者是渐渐觉得你的房子是个不宜居住的寒碜地方，后者是觉得它无论住得惬意与否，都很可能塌到你头上来。这两种不适，我们的朋友两样都在经受。许多他原以为相当高贵的东西，如今看着颇为卑琐；许多先前看似稳当可靠的东西，也显出了摇晃。文明本身——那座好歹尚可栖身、本该由这场战争来撑固的居所——也罩上了一层陌生的新危殆气。

甚至早在战前，一连串阴郁的公共灾厄便已多少唤醒了那个令人不安的念头：文明——一个民族、一片大陆或整个世界那整套有秩序、有收成的联合行动——只不过是一副唬人的空牌。当世界太平、光景顺遂时，秩序与体面、正义与仁慈与自制这一党，其实是在唬着人多得多的自私与贪婪那一党——那一党若是敢，是要欺人要抢夺的。抡斧头、搞绝食的「妇女参政论者」（suffragettes）们，其深重的反社会罪过在于：她们在某种程度上泄露了那副唬牌——非罪犯的人们先前正是靠着它，对「互保互容」之治的那些不情愿的默认者维持着几分节制。她们帮着较下等的一路人看清：文明的唬局，任凭任何一个准备冒几分皮肉之险来叫牌的人拿捏。爱德华·卡森爵士（Sir Edward Carson）接过了这桩工作。他「叫破」了「不列颠治世」（Pax Britannica）的唬局——那套假定说：武力叛乱不列颠帝国的法律与秩序，无论叛者是谁，终必落得叛贼的败亡；他向新芬党（Sinn Fein）和一切有意作乱者奉上证明：叛逆大可稳稳当当地远不止于窥探窥探它想干什么；他还表明：英王的臣民尽可以密谋武装作乱、对抗国王的太平，而作乱者本人竟毫不吃亏。

===Original===
The greatest of all bluffs, the general peace of the world and the joint civilization of Europe, remained uncalled for a year or two more. It was a high moral bluff. People were everywhere saying that world-war was too appalling, too frantically wicked a thing for any government to invite or procure. Peace, they argued, held a hand irresistibly strong. Had she not, among her cards, every acknowledged precept of Christianity and of morality, even of wisdom for a man’s self or a nation’s? Potsdam called the world’s bluff, and the world’s hand was found to be empty. Potsdam lost the game in the end, but it had not called wholly in vain. To a Europe exhausted, divided, and degraded by five years of return to the morals of the Stone Age it had suggested how many things are as they are, how many things are owned as they are, how many lives are safely continued, merely because our birds of prey have not yet had the wit to see what would come of a sudden snatch made with a will and with assurance. The total number of policemen on a racecourse is always a minute percentage of the total number of its thieves and roughs. The bad men are not held down by force; they are only bluffed by the pretence of it. They have got the tip now, and the plain man is dimly aware how surprisingly little there is to keep us all from slipping back into the state we were in when a man would kill another to steal a piece of food that he had got, and when a young woman was not safe on a road out of sight of her friends.

===Chinese===
最大的一副唬牌——世界的普遍和平与欧洲的共同文明——又过了一两年才有人来叫牌。那是一副极高的道德唬牌。人们到处都说，世界大战太骇人、太疯狂地邪恶，任何政府都不会去邀致或促发它。他们论证道：和平手中握着一副强得无法抗拒的牌。她手里的牌，岂非包括基督教与道德的每一条公认诫命，甚至包括个人自处与民族自处的每一条明哲？波茨坦（Potsdam）叫了世界的牌，摊开来一看，世界手里竟是空的。波茨坦终局输了这一局，但它的叫牌并非全然徒劳。它向一个被五年「重返石器时代道德」折腾得精疲力竭、四分五裂、斯文扫地的欧洲提示了：有多少事物照旧安放着，有多少财产照旧归属着，有多少性命照旧延续着，仅仅因为我们的猛禽们还没长出那份头脑，看不出若是下了狠心、拿稳了主意、猝然一攫，将会是什么下场。赛马场上的警察总数，从来只占场内窃贼与暴徒总数的一个微末百分比。坏人不是被武力压倒的；他们只是被武力的假象唬住。如今他们拿到了这个门道，而普通人也隐约意识到：要叫我们大家一齐滑回从前那种境地——人为了抢一块别人已经到口的食物而去杀人、年轻女子一离开朋友视线走上大路便不得安全——所需要的东西，竟然少得惊人。

===Original===
The plain man, so far as I know him, is neither aghast nor gleeful at this revelation. For the most part he looks somewhat listlessly on, as at a probable dogfight in which there is no dog of his. A sense of moral horror does not come easily when you have supped full of horrors on most of the days of three or four years; sacrilege has to go far, indeed, to shock men who have seen their old gods looking extremely human and blowing out, one by one, the candles before their own shrines. Some new god, or devil, of course, may enter at any time into this disfurnished soul.

Genius in some leader might either possess it with an anarchic passion to smash and delete all the old institutions that disappointed in the day of trial or fire it with a new craving to lift itself clear of the wrack and possess itself on the heights. For either a Lenin or a St. Francis there is a wide field to till, cleared, but of pretty stiff clay. Persistently sane in his disenchantment as he had been in his rapture, the common man, whose affection and trust the old order wore out in the war, is still slow to enlist out-and-out in any Satanist unit. There’s reason, he still feels, in everything. So he remains, for the time, like one of the angels whom the Renaissance poet represented as reincarnate in man; the ones who in the insurrection of Lucifer were not for Jehovah nor yet for his enemy.

===Chinese===
就我所识得的普通人而论，面对这番启示，他既不惊骇，也不称快。多半只是有些恹恹地旁观，像看一场多半要开打的斗狗，而场中并没有他的狗。在三四年里的大多数日子都饱餐过恐怖的人，道德恐怖感不会轻易袭来；渎神之事确乎得走得很远，才能震动这些见过自家老神明露出十足凡人嘴脸、又看着他们一个个亲手吹熄自家神龛前蜡烛的人。当然，某位新神、或新魔，随时可能走进这间被搬空了家具的灵魂。

某位领袖身上的天才，或者会以一种无政府的狂热攫住它，把考验之日令众人失望的一切旧制度统统砸碎、抹除；或者会以一种新的渴望点燃它，让它拔出残骸之外，在高处占有它自己。无论对一位列宁（Lenin）还是一位圣方济各（St. Francis），这里都有一片广田可耕——垦是垦过了，只是黏土相当硬。普通人——旧秩序在战争里磨光了他的爱意与信任——正如他当年在狂热中始终神志清明，如今在幻灭中也始终神志清明，仍不肯痛痛快快地应征加入任何一支撒旦主义部队。凡事都有个分寸——他至今仍这样觉得。于是，就眼前而论，他仍旧像文艺复兴诗人笔下那些托生为人的天使之一：在路西法叛乱中，他们既不站在耶和华（Jehovah）一边，也不站在他的敌人一边。

## XI Stars in Their Courses / 十一 众星循轨

### I / 一

===Original===
“Doth any man doubt,” the wise Bacon asks, “that if there were taken out of men’s minds vain opinions, flattering hopes, false valuations, imaginations as one would, and the like, but it would leave the minds of a number of men poor shrunken things, full of melancholy and indisposition and unpleasing to themselves?” One of the most sweetly flattering hopes that we had in the August of 1914 was that in view of the greatness of the occasion causes were not going to have their effects.

Nothing new, you may truthfully answer, in that. The improvement is one which man, in his cups and his dreams and other seasons of maudlin vision, has always perceived to have just come at last. Now, he exaltedly says to himself, for a clean break with my inadequately wise and brilliant past. Away with that plaguey old list of my things done which should not have been done, and of things left undone which I ought to have done. At the end of popular plays the sympathetic youth who had idled, philandered, or stolen till then would book to the Rand or the Yukon, fully assured that “in that free, outdoor life” one’s character is not one’s fate any longer; blessed, “out there,” are Europe’s slackers and wasters, for they shall inherit the earth, or its auriferous parts. Grasshoppers, too, if they drank or resorted to sentimental novels and plays, might have gallant little revolts in their hearts, and chirrup “Down with causation!” and feel cocksure that some good-natured god would give them a chance of “redeeming their pasts” quite late in autumn, and put in their way a winter provision far ampler than that which crowns the coolie labours of those sorry daughters of Martha, the bees. But, for working this benign miracle in the soul, no other strong waters can equal the early days of a war. If, with unbecoming sobriety, anyone hints, in such days, that causes may still retain some sort of control, he is easily seen to have no drop of true blood in him; base is the slave who fears we must reap as we sowed; shame upon spiritless whispers about any connection between the making of beds and the lying thereon; now they shall see what excellent hothouse grapes will be borne by the fine healthy thistles that we have been planting and watering.

Something in it too, perhaps﻿—at least some centuries ago. When a great nation’s army was only a few thousands strong the freak and the fluke had their chance. An Achilles or two, at the top of their form on the day, might upset the odds. But when armies are millions of men, and machinery counts for more than the men, the few divine accidents of exceptional valour cannot go far. With eleven a-side a Grace or an Armstrong may win a game off his own bat. He will hardly do that in a game where the sides are eleven thousand apiece. More and more, as the armies increase, must the law of averages have it its own dreary way; glorious uncertainties wither; statistical “curves” of relative national fitness to win, and to stand the strain of winning or losing, overbear everything else. What are the two armies’ and the two nations’ relative numbers? What is the mean physique on each side? And the mean intelligence? How far has each nation’s history﻿—social, political, religious, industrial﻿—tended to make its men rich in just pride, self-reliance, high spirit, devotion, and hardihood? How many percent on each side have been sapped by venereal disease? How much of their work have its officers troubled to learn? These are the questions. The more men you have in a war, and the longer it lasts, the more completely has it to lose the romance of a glorious gamble and sink﻿—or, as some would say, rise﻿—to the plane of a circumstantial, matter-of-fact liquidation of whatever relative messes the nations engaged have made of the whole of their previous lives.

===Chinese===
「可有人怀疑，」明哲的培根（Bacon）问道，「倘若把虚妄的意见、谄媚的希望、虚假的估价、随心所欲的想象，以及诸如此类，从人们心中尽数取走，那不会把许多人的心智留成一副可怜巴巴的皱缩之物——满载忧郁与倦怠，连他们自己看着都生厌么？」1914 年 8 月，我们怀着的诸般谄媚希望里最甜蜜的一条，便是：鉴于这场时机何其重大，种种原因将不会再生出它们的后果了。

你可以如实作答：这里头毫无新意。这种长进，人在酒酣之际、睡梦之中，以及其他一切易致伤感性幻视的时节里，总是一眼认定它恰好刚刚终于到来。此刻，他志高气扬地对自己说：现在，同我那不够英明、不够辉煌的过去，来一个干净的决裂吧。把我那份恼人的旧账单——记着我不该做而做了的事、该做而未做的事——统统拿开。在通俗戏的收场处，那位此前一直游手好闲、追逐风月、或行些偷窃勾当的惹人怜爱的小青年，会订下票去兰德（Rand，南非金矿区）或育空（Yukon，北美金矿地），满心笃定：在那「自由自在的户外生活」里，一个人的性格不再即是他的命运；「在那边」，欧洲的懒汉与游惰败家子是有福的，因为他们必承受地土——或者说，承受其中含金的地段。就连蚱蜢们，若是灌下了几杯，或是读惯了伤感的小说戏文，心里也会掀起小小的、意气昂扬的叛乱，唧唧嚷着「打倒因果！」，并且深信不疑：总有位好性儿的神明，肯在深秋时节给它们一个「弥补前愆」的机会，还会替它们在道上备下一份越冬的口粮，比马大（Martha）那些可怜的女儿们——蜜蜂——做牛做马挣得的那份丰盛得多。然而，论在灵魂里行出这等良善的奇迹，别的什么烈酒（strong waters）都及不上战争的初期。在这样的日子里，谁若带着不合时宜的清醒，暗示原因或许仍握着几分支配之力，人们一眼便看穿他身上没有一滴真正的血性；唯恐我们必须怎样播种便怎样收割的，是卑贱的奴隶；怯生生地嘀咕铺床与卧床之间或有关联的，该当羞耻；如今他们就要看看，我们一直栽种浇灌的这些健壮的好蓟，会结出何等上品的温室葡萄。

这话里或许也有些道理——至少在几百年前。当一个强国的陆军只有区区数千之众时，异军突起的奇才与纯粹的侥幸都还有机会。一两个阿喀琉斯（Achilles），当天正处在巅峰状态，或许真能搅翻众寡之势。但当军队以百万计，机器又比人更算数时，寥寥几场神异非凡的英勇意外，就成不了气候。十一人对十一人的比赛里，一位格雷斯（Grace）或阿姆斯特朗（Armstrong）能凭自己一根球棒赢下全场；若双方各出一万一千人，他就断然办不到了。军队越是庞大，平均律（law of averages）便越要按它那套沉闷的路数一意孤行；光荣的不确定性凋萎了；衡量各民族相对的取胜资质、以及承受胜负重压之能力的统计「曲线」，压倒了其余一切。两支军队、两个国家的相对兵数几何？双方的平均体格如何？平均智力又如何？每个国家的历史——社会的、政治的、宗教的、工业的——在多大程度上使其男子富于正当的自豪、自立、锐气、忠悃与坚毅？双方各有百分之几的人已被性病拖垮？双方的军官又有多少肯费心研习本行？这些才是问题。一场战争投入的人越多、持续得越久，它就越发彻底地失掉那场光荣赌博的浪漫，而沉落——或者照有些人的说法，升腾——到一个就事论事、按部就班的清算平面上：清算交战各国把各自全部既往生活搅成的相对烂摊子。

### II / 二

===Original===
Any soldier will tell you the bayonet does not win battles. It only claims, in a way that a beaten side cannot ignore, a victory won already by gunfire, rifles, gas, bombs, or some combination of these. The bayonet’s thrust is more of a gesture: a cogent appeal, like the urgent “How’s that?” from the whole of the field when a batsman is almost certainly out. But you may go much further back. That predominant fire itself is just such another appeal. Its greater volume and better direction are only the terms of an army’s or a nation’s claim to be registered as the winner of what it had really won long ago when, compared with the other nation, it minded its job and lived cleanly and sanely. All war on the new huge scale may be seen as a process, very expensive, of registration or verification. Whenever a war is declared you may say that now, in a sense, it is over at last; all the votes have been cast; the examination papers are written; the time has come for the counting of votes and adjudging of marks. Of course, we may still “do our bit,” but the possible size of our bit had its limit fixed long ago by the acts of ourselves and our fathers and rulers which made us the men that we are and no more. No use now to try to cadge favour with any ad hoc God of Battles. For this, of all gods, is the most dourly Protestant. No squaring of him on the deathbeds of people who would not work while it was yet light.

From many points in the field﻿—some of the best were in the tops of high trees on high ground﻿—you could watch through your glass the casting up of accounts. You might survey from beginning to end a British attack up a bare opposite slope, perhaps with home troops on the left and Canadian or Australasian troops on the right. You had already seen them meet on roads in the rear: battalions of colourless, stunted, half-toothless lads from hot, humid Lancashire mills; battalions of slow, staring faces, gargoyles out of the tragical-comical-historical-pastoral edifice of modern English rural life; Dominion battalions of men startlingly taller, stronger, handsomer, prouder, firmer in nerve, better schooled, more boldly interested in life, quicker to take means to an end and to parry and counter any new blow of circumstance, men who had learned already to look at our men with the half-curious, half-pitying look of a higher, happier caste at a lower. And now you saw them, all these kinds, arise in one continuous line out of the earth and walk forward to bear in the riddled flesh and wrung spirit the sins of their several fathers, pastors, and masters.

===Chinese===
随便哪个当过兵的人都会告诉你：刺刀并不打赢战斗。它只是以一种战败一方无从置喙的方式，去认领一场早已由炮火、步枪、毒气、手榴弹、或其中几样的组合赢下的胜利。刺刀的突刺更像一个手势：一记有力的申诉，好比击球员几乎必死无疑出局时，全场从四面八方齐声发出的那声急切的「怎么判？」（How’s that?）。但你还可以往前追溯得更远。那占尽上风的炮火本身，也不过是同样的一种申诉。它更大的火力、更准的射向，不过是一支军队或一个民族申请书上的条款，申请把自己登记为那场胜利的赢家——而那场胜利，它其实早已赢得，赢得于往昔：与另一国相较时，它更尽心于本业，也更洁净清明地过活。新的大规模战争，尽可以看作一道耗资浩大的登记或核验程序。每当宣战，你都可以说：从某种意义上讲，战争此刻终于打完了；选票都已投下；考卷都已答完；点票与判分的时刻到了。当然，我们仍可以「尽自己一份力」，但这一份力可能有多大，其限度早已被锁定——被我们自己、我们的父辈与统治者的种种作为锁定；正是那些作为，把我们造成了我们如今这副样子，且仅止于此。如今再想向哪一位临事抱佛脚的战神（God of Battles）乞恩讨好，也是枉然。因为在一切神祇之中，独这一位是最铁面顽固的新教徒。趁天还亮着不肯做工的人，想在死床上贿通他，断无门路。

从战场上的许多个观测点——其中几处最好的，在高地上的大树顶梢——你可以举着望远镜，观看这笔总账的结算。你可以从头到尾俯瞰英军向对面一道光秃山坡发起的一场进攻，左边也许是本土部队，右边是加拿大的或澳新（Australasian）的部队。你早已在后方的道路上见过他们会合：一营又一营面色黯淡、发育受阻、缺了半口牙的小伙子，来自兰开夏（Lancashire）闷热潮湿的纱厂；一营又一营神情迟缓、目光发直的面孔，活像从现代英格兰乡村生活那座「悲喜史田园」式巨厦上探出来的怪状兽饰；还有自治领（Dominion）的营：营里的汉子个头高得惊人，更强壮、更英俊、更骄傲，神经更坚稳，受的教育更好，对人生怀有更大胆的兴趣，更善于为达目的择取手段，更善于招架并回击时运的任何新打击；这些人早已学会用一种较高的、较快乐的种姓打量较低种姓时那种半好奇、半怜悯的眼光，来看我们的士兵。而现在你看见他们，所有这几类人，连成一条不断的横队从地里站起，向前走去，以被打成筛子的肉体与被拧绞的灵魂，去担负他们各自的父辈、牧者与师长的罪愆。

===Original===
Time after time there would come to the watching eye, to the mind still desperately hugging the hope that known causes might not bring their normal effects, the same crushing demonstration that things are as we have made them. Sometimes the line of home troops would break into gaps and bunches, lose touch and direction and common purpose, some of the knots plunging on into the back of our barrage or feasting some enemy machine-gunner on their density, others straggling back to the place whence they had started, while the Dominion troops still ambled steadily on, their line delicately waving but always continuous, closing again, as living flesh closes over a pinprick, wherever an enemy shell tore a hole.

===Chinese===
一次又一次，那双守望的眼睛，连同那颗还在拼死抱着「已知的原因未必带来惯常后果」之心的人，总要领受同一个粉碎性的演示：事物就是我们所造就的那个样子。有时，本土部队的横队会破裂成一道道缺口、一团团人堆，失掉联络、方向与共同的目标：有几股一头扎进我们弹幕（barrage）的后尾，或是拿自己的密集身躯去饱餍某个敌军机枪手；另一些则稀稀拉拉地折回他们出发的地方；而自治领部队仍从容不迫地稳步向前，他们的横线轻轻摇漾，却始终连续，哪里被敌军炮火撕开一个口子，哪里就像活肉在针孔上重新合拢那样重新合拢。

===Original===
Perhaps the undersized boys from our slums and the under-witted boys from the “agricultural, residential, and sporting estates” of our auctioneers’ advertisements would get to their goal, the spirit wrestling prodigies of valour out of the wronged flesh, hold on there for an hour or two with the shells splashing the earth up about them like puddle water when great raindrops make its surface jump, and then fall back under orders, without any need, the brain of our army failing to know how to use what its muscle had won. Then, while you saw the triumphant Australians throw back a protective flank from the left of their newly-won front to the English right, far in their rear, you knew bitterly what the Australians were saying once more: “They’ve let us down again!” “Another Tommy officer who didn’t know he’d won!” As if it were the fault, that day, of anyone there! Our men could only draw on such funds of nerve and physique, knowledge and skill, as we had put into the bank for them. Not they, but their rulers and “betters,” had lost their heads in the joy of making money fast out of steam, and so made half of our nation slum-dwellers. It was not they who had moulded English rustic life to keep up the complacency of sentimental modern imitators of feudal barons. It was not they who had made our Regular Army neither aristocratic, with the virtues of aristocracy, nor democratic, with the different virtues of democracy, nor keenly professional with the professional virtues of gusto and curiosity about the possibilities of its work. *Delicta majorum immeritus lues.* Like the syphilitic children of some jolly Victorian rake, they could only bring to this harsh examination such health and sanity as all the pleasant vices of Victorian and Edwardian England had left them.

===Chinese===
也有这样的可能：来自我国贫民窟的发育不良的男孩，和来自拍卖行广告里那种「宜农宜居宜游猎的庄园」的头脑孱弱的男孩，一路打到了他们的目标——精神从受亏待的肉体里搏出奇迹般的英勇——在那里顶住一两个钟头，炮弹把泥土在他们四周溅起，像滂沱大雨点砸得泥洼水面直跳；随后奉命退回，退得毫无必要，因为我们陆军的头脑不知道该怎样运用它的筋肉所赢得的东西。这时，你望着得胜的澳大利亚人从他们新夺下的前线的左端，朝英军的右翼远远甩回一道掩护侧翼——你便辛酸地知道澳大利亚人又在说什么了：「他们又坑了咱们一回！」「又一位打赢了自己还不知道的汤米（Tommy）军官！」仿佛那天在场的任何人有什么过错似的！我们的士兵所能支取的神经与体格、知识与技艺的储金，也只有我们替他们存进银行的那一点。不是他们，而是他们的统治者与「尊长们」，在靠蒸汽快快发财的狂喜里失了魂，才使我们民族的一半人沦为贫民窟居民。不是他们，把英格兰的乡野生活捏塑成那副模样，好供养一帮感情用事的现代男爵模仿家们的自满。也不是他们，把我们的正规军（Regular Army）造就得既不贵族——没有贵族制的诸般美德，也不民主——没有民主制的另一些美德，又不专业到家——没有那种对本行的兴致盎然、对其种种可能性怀有好奇的专业美德。*Delicta majorum immeritus lues*（拉丁语：父辈之罪，无辜者亦须偿还）。他们好比某个快活的维多利亚浪荡子生下的梅毒儿女，只能把维多利亚与爱德华时代的英格兰的一切惬意罪过给他们剩下的那点健康与清明，带进这场严酷的考试。

### III / 三

===Original===
The winter after the battle of Loos a sentry on guard at one part of our line could always see the frustrate skeletons of many English dead. They lay outside our wire, picked clean by the rats, so that the khaki fell in on them loosely﻿—little heaps of bone and cloth half hidden now by nettles and grass. If the sentry had been a year in the army he knew well enough that they had gone foredoomed into a battle lost before a shot was fired. After the Boer War, you remember, England, under the first shock of its blunders, had tried to find out why the Staff work was so bad. What it found, in the words of a famous Report, was that the fashion in sentiment in our Regular Army was to think hard work “bad form”; a subaltern was felt to be a bit of a scrub if he worried too much about discovering how to support an attack when he might be more spiritedly employed in playing polo; “The nobleness of life,” as Antony said, when he kissed Cleopatra, was to go racing or hunting, not to sit learning how to forecast the course of great battles and how to provide for answering their calls. And so the swathes of little brown bundles, with bones showing through, lay in the nettles and grass.

Consider the course of the life of the British Regular officer as you had known him in youth﻿—not the pick, the saving few, the unconquerably sound and keen, but the average, staple article made by a sleek, complacent, snobbish, safe, wealth-governed England after her own image. Think of his school; of the mystic aureole of quasi-moral beauty attached by authority there to absorption in the easy thing﻿—in play; the almost passionate adoration of all those energies and dexterities which, in this world of evolution towards the primacy of the acute, full brain, are of the least possible use as aids to survival in men and to victory in armies. Before he first left home for school he may have been a normal child who only craved to be given some bit, any odd bit, of “real work,” as an experience more thrilling than games. Like most children, he may have had a zestful command of fresh, vivid, personal speech, his choice of words expressing simply and gaily the individual working of his mind and his joy in its work. Through easy contact with gardeners, gamekeepers, and village boys he often had established a quite natural, unconscious friendliness with people of different social grades. He was probably born of the kind that pries young, that ask, when they play on sea sands, why there are tides, and what goes on in the sky that there should be rain. And then down came the shades of the prison-house. To make this large, gay book of fairy tales, the earth, dull and stale to a child importunately fingering at its covers might seem a task to daunt the strongest. But many of the teachers of our youth are indomitable men. They can make earth’s most ardent small lover learn from a book what a bore his dear earth can be, with her strings of names of towns, rivers, and lakes, her mileages *à faire mourir*, and her insufferable tale of flax and jute. With an equal firmness your early power of supple and bright-coloured speech may be taken away and a ragbag of feeble stock phrases, misfits for all your thoughts, and worn dull and dirty by everyone else, be forced upon you instead of the treasure you had. You may leave school unable to tell what stars are about you at night or to ask your way to a journey’s end in any country but your own. Between your helpless mind and most of your fellow-countrymen thick screens of division are drawn, so that when you are fifteen you do not know how to speak to them with a natural courtesy; you have a vague idea that they will steal your watch if you leave it about. Above all, you have learnt that it is still “bad form” to work; that the youth with brains and no money may well be despised by the youth with money and no brains; that the absorbed student or artist is ignoble or grotesque; that to be able to afford yourself “a good time” is a natural title to respect and regard; and that to give yourself any “good time” that you can is an action of spirit. So it went on at prep. school, public school, Sandhurst, Camberley. That was how Staff College French came to be what it was. And as it was what it was, you can guess what Staff College tactics and strategy were, and why all the little brown bundles lay where they did in the nettles and grass.

===Chinese===
卢斯（Loos）战役之后的那个冬天，在我们战线某一段站岗的哨兵，总能望见许多英军亡者的挫败骸骨。他们躺在我们铁丝网的外面，被老鼠啃剔得干干净净，卡其布松松地塌在骨架上——一小堆一小堆的骨头与布片，如今半掩在荨麻与荒草之间。这哨兵若已在陆军里待满一年，便十分清楚：他们是抱着注定的厄运走进那场战斗的——仗还没放响第一枪，就已经输定了。你总还记得：布尔战争（Boer War）之后，英格兰在初闻自身种种乖谬举措的震惊之下，曾试图查明参谋（Staff）工作何以如此糟糕。用一份著名报告（Report）里的话说，它查明的是：我们正规军中盛行的性情时尚，是把苦干看作「有失体统」；一位初级军官（subaltern）若过分操心去钻研怎样支援一场进攻——原本他大可以更神气地去打马球——人家便觉得他有点寒碜；正如安东尼（Antony）吻克莉奥佩特拉（Cleopatra）时所说，「生命的高贵」在于赛马与行猎，而不在于坐下来学习怎样预见大战的进程、怎样预先筹备应答大战的召唤。于是一排排小小的棕色包袱，透出森森白骨，躺在荨麻与荒草之间。

想一想英国正规军军官的一生轨迹吧——你青年时代认识的那种——不是那些出类拔萃、寥寥可数、救得大局的，不是那些不可征服地健全而热忱的，而是那种成批生产的普通大路货：由一个油光水滑、沾沾自得、势利眼、四平八稳、受财富支配的英格兰，照着她自己的形象铸造出来。想想他的学校；想想那里当权者给沉溺于轻松之事——玩耍游戏——沾上的那圈准道德之美的神秘光轮；想想人们对一切体力与身手那种近乎狂热的崇拜——而在这个朝着敏锐充实的头脑居于至高地位演化的世界上，这些体力与身手，作为个人求存、军队求胜的助力，用处是小得不能再小的。初次离家上学之前，他本可能是个正常的孩子，一心只盼有人给他一点——随便什么零碎的一点——「真正的活计」，当作一种比游戏更刺激的历练。同大多数孩子一样，他本可能娴熟地驾驭一种清新、鲜明、带着个人印记的语言，选词用字简简单单、欢欢喜喜，把他心智的独特运转、以及他在这运转中的欢愉，都表达出来。凭着同园丁、猎场看守和村里男孩的自在往来，他往往已经同各个社会阶层的人建立了一种全然自然、不假思索的友善。他多半生来就属于自幼爱刨根问底的那一类：在海边沙滩上玩耍时会问，为什么有潮汐；天上在进行着什么，才会落下雨来。然后，「牢狱的幽影」（the shades of the prison-house）便当头罩了下来。要把大地——这本又大又欢快的童话书——变得乏味而陈旧，变得让一个缠着大人的手急急掰弄封面的孩子也觉索然，这任务看上去足以吓退最强的人。但我们青年时代的许多教师，却是些不可征服的人。他们能让大地最热忱的小恋人从书本上学到：他亲爱的大地可以多么乏味——拖着她一串串城镇、河流与湖泊的名字，拖着她那些足以要人命（*à faire mourir*）的里程数，还有她那篇叫人无法忍受的亚麻与黄麻的故事。以同样的坚定，你早年那副柔韧而色彩鲜亮的语言能力可以被拿走，换作强塞给你的一口袋疲软的现成套语——同你的每一种思想都不合身，又被别人穿戴得又钝又脏——顶替了你原本拥有的珍宝。你离开学校时，可能说不出夜间围绕着你的是哪些星辰；可能除了本国，到了任何国家都问不出一句通向旅程终点的路。在你那无助的心智与你的大多数同胞之间，拉起了厚厚的隔断之幕；于是到你十五岁上，你不知道该怎样带着一份自然的礼数同他们讲话；你还有个模模糊糊的念头：手表若随手一放，他们便会偷走。尤其是，你已经学得了：用功干活仍旧「有失体统」；有头脑而没钱的小伙子，尽可以叫有钱而没头脑的小伙子看不起；潜心专注的学生或艺术家，不是低贱便是可笑；供得起自己「快活一场」的，自然配受人的敬重与看重；而能给自己弄到什么「快活时光」就尽管去弄，乃是有血性的行径。在预备学校，在公学（public school），在桑德赫斯特（Sandhurst），在坎伯利（Camberley），事情就这样一路下来。参谋学院（Staff College）的法语之所以是后来那副样子，缘由便在于此。而法语既然是那副样子，你便猜得出参谋学院的战术与战略是什么样子，也猜得出那些小小的棕色包袱，何以都躺在荨麻与荒草之间它们所躺的地方了。

### IV / 四

===Original===
You are more aware of the stars in war than in peace. A full moon may quite halve the cares of a sentry; the Pole Star will sometimes be all that a company has, when relieved, to guide it back across country to Paradisiac rest; sleeping often under the sky, you come to find out for yourself what nobody taught you at school﻿—how Orion is sure to be not there in summer, and Aquila always missing in March, and how the Great Bear, that was straight overhead in the April nights, is wont to hang low in the north in the autumn. Childish as it may seem to the wise, a few years’ nightly view of these and other invariable arrangements may give a simple soul a surprisingly lively twinge of what the ages of faith seem to have meant by the fear of God﻿—the awesome suspicion that there is some sort of fundamental world order or control which cannot by any means be put off or dodged or bribed to help you to break its own laws. “Anything,” the old Regular warrant-officers say, “can be wangled in the army,” but who shall push the Dragon or the Great Dog off his beat? And﻿—who knows?﻿—that may be only a part of a larger system of cause and effect, all of it as hopelessly undodgable.

===Chinese===
战争里你对星辰的知觉，比和平里更敏锐。一轮满月足能把哨兵的挂虑减去一半；换防之后，北极星有时是全连仅有的凭仗，引它越野走回乐园般的安息；常常露宿在星空底下，你会自己弄明白学校里没人教过你的那些事——猎户座到了夏天准不在那里，天鹰座到了三月份总是缺席；还有大熊座，四月夜里正悬头顶，到了秋天却惯于低低挂在北天。这在智者看来也许幼稚；可是夜复一夜地看上几年这些以及其他种种恒定不变的排列，竟可以让一个朴素的灵魂出奇鲜活地打一个寒噤，懂得信仰的诸时代说「对神的敬畏」时似乎指的是什么——那令人生畏的疑心：世上存在着某种根本的世界秩序或支配，任你如何也拖延不得、闪避不得、更贿赂不动它来帮你违犯它自己的律法。「军队里的事，」正规军的老准尉们说，「没有一样不能想法子通融」；可是谁又能把天龙座（Dragon）或大犬座（Great Dog）从它们的巡更道上推开呢？况且——谁知道呢？——那兴许只是一整套更大的因果体系的一部分，而那整套体系，全都同样地无从逃遁。

===Original===
These apprehensions were particularly apt to arise if you had spent an hour that day in seeing herds of the English “common people” ushered down narrowing corridors of barbed wire into some gap that had all the German machine guns raking its exit, the nature of Regular officers’ prewar education in England precluding the prompt evolution of any effectual means on our side to derange the working of this ingenious abattoir. We had asked for it all. We had made the directing brains of our armies the poor things that they were. Small blame to them if in this season of liquidation they failed to produce assets which we had never equipped them to earn﻿—mental nimbleness, powers of individual observation, quickness to cap with counter-strokes of invention each new device of the fertile specialists opposite. Being as we had moulded them, they had probably done pretty well in doing no worse.

> What’s *done* we partly may compute,
> But know not what’s *resisted*.

===Chinese===
这样的忧惧尤其容易在这样的日子里涌起：那一天你刚刚花了一个钟头，看着一群群英国「老百姓」被领着走下一段段渐窄的铁丝网走廊，走进某处出口被德军全部机枪来回耙扫的缺口——而英格兰正规军军官战前所受教育的性质，又使我们这一方无从迅速演变出任何有效的手段，去搅乱这座精巧屠宰场的运转。这一切都是我们自己讨来的。我们把我们各集团军的指挥头脑，造就成了后来那副可怜样子。在这清算的季节里，他们拿不出那些我们从未装备他们去赚取的资产——心智的灵快、个人观察的功力、以及以发明的新招针锋相对地还击对面那班多产专家每一件新器械的机变——这实在怪不得他们。他们既已被我们捏成那副样子，那么能不做得更坏，恐怕已经算干得漂亮了。

> 已行之事，我们尚可约略计算，
> 所抗之事，却无从知晓。

===Original===
Who shall say what efforts it may have cost some of those poor custom-ridden souls not to veto, for good and all, an engine of war so far from “smart” as the tank, or to accept any help at all from such folk as the newfangled, untraditional airmen, some of whom took no shame to go forth to the fray in pyjamas. Not they alone, but all of ourselves, with our boastful chatter about the “public school spirit,” our gallant, robust contempt for “swats” and “smugs” and all who invented new means to new ends and who trained and used their brains with a will﻿—we had arranged for these easy battues of thousands of Englishmen, who, for their part, did not fail. Tomorrow you would see it all again﻿—a few hundred square yards of ground gained by the deaths, perhaps, of twenty thousand men who would

> Go to their graves like beds, fight for a plot
> Which is not tomb enough and continent
> To hide the slain.

===Chinese===
谁说得准，那班被积习压在头上的可怜灵魂里，有些人费了多大的劲，才克制住自己，没有把坦克这种与「漂亮时新」相去甚远的战争器械一票否决、永绝后患；或者才勉强咽得下，肯从那班新奇而无传统可言的飞行员那里接受哪怕一点帮助——这些人里，有的穿着睡衣裤上阵厮杀，也丝毫不以为耻。还不止他们：还有我们全体——带着我们关于「公学精神」的那套自夸的絮叨，带着我们对「死读书的」（swats）与「假正经的乖学生」（smugs）、对一切为新目的发明新手段的人、一切肯下狠功训练和运用自己头脑的人的那份英武而茁壮的轻蔑——这些把成千上万英国人驱进枪口的轻松围猎，都是我们一手安排好的；而他们这一方，倒是从不曾误事。明天你还会把这一切再看到一遍——用也许两万条人命，换来几百平方码的土地；这些人将——

> 像上床一样走向坟墓，为争夺一方
> 既不够作坟茔、也盛不下
> 阵亡者的土地而战。

===Original===
So it would go on, week after week, sitting after sitting of the dismal court that liquidated in the Flanders mud our ruling classes’ wasted decades, until we either lost the war outright or were saved from utter disaster by clutching at aid from French brains and American numbers. Like Lucifer when he was confronted with the sky at night, you “looked and sank.”

> Around the ancient track marched, rank on rank,
> The army of unalterable law.

What had we done, when we could, that the stars in their courses should fight for us now? Or left undone, of all that could provoke this methodical universe of swinging and returning forces to shake off such dust from its constant wheels?

===Chinese===
事情就这样一周又一周地继续下去：那阴惨的法庭一庭接一庭地开庭，在佛兰德（Flanders）的泥泞里，清算我们统治阶级荒掷掉的那几十年，直到我们要么干脆把战争输个精光，要么死死抓住法国头脑与美国人数的援手，才得免于全盘的灾难。一如夜间与满天星穹照面的路西法（Lucifer），你「望了一眼，便沉落下去」。

> 沿着亘古的轨道，一列又一列，
> 行进着不可更易之法律的大军。

趁我们尚能为力的时候，我们究竟做过什么，配得众星如今循着轨道为我们而战？又漏做了什么——在一切足以触怒这个由摆荡往复之力组成的井然宇宙、使它从自己恒常的车轮上抖落这等尘埃的事端之中？

### V / 五

===Original===
“I planted a set of blind hopes in their minds,” said Prometheus, making it out to be quite a good turn that he had done to mankind. And the Dr. Relling of Ibsen, a kind of Prometheus in general practice, kept at hand a whole medicine-chest of assorted illusions to dope his patients with. “Illusion, you know,” said this sage, “is the tonic to give ’em.” It may be. But even illusions cost something. The bill, as Hotspur said of the river Trent, “comes me cranking in” presently, nature’s iron law laying it down that the more superb your state of inflation the deeper shall the dumps occasioned by a puncture be. The Promethean gift of Mr. Dunlop to our race undoubtedly lifted the pastime of cycling out of a somewhat bumpy order of prose into a lyric heaven. And yet the stoutest of all nails could plunge itself into the solid tyre of old without compelling you to walk a foundered Pegasus from the top of the Honister Pass the whole way to Keswick, enjoying en route neither the blessing of a bicycle nor that of the unhampered use of Shanks’ Mare.

So War, who keeps such a pump to blow you up with, and also such thorns for your puncturing, had to leave us the “poor shrunken things” that we are, anyhow. It is as if the average man had been passing himself off on himself, in a dream, as the youthful hero of some popular drama, and, in a rousing last act, had departed, in 1914, on excellent terms with himself and the audience, bands playing and flags flying, to start a noble and happy new life on the virgin soil of the “golden West.” And now he awakes in the “golden West” on a slobbery and a dirty farm, with all the purchase money still to pay, and tools and manures remarkably dear, and no flag visible, nor instrument of music audible, and dismal reports coming in from neighbouring farmers, and cause and effect as abominably linked one to another as ever, and all the time his mind full of a sour surmise that many sorts of less credulous men have “made a bit” of inordinate size out of the bit that he did rather than made, during the raging and tearing run of the drama now taken off and, as far as may be, forgotten.

===Chinese===
「我在他们心里种下了一整套盲目的希望，」普罗米修斯（Prometheus）说，把这件事说成是他给人类帮的一个大忙。而易卜生（Ibsen）笔下的瑞林医生（Dr. Relling）——一位普通挂牌开业行医的普罗米修斯——手边也常备着一整箱各色配好的幻觉，好给他的病人灌服。「幻觉，你知道，」这位智者说，「就是该给他们服的补药。」也许是的。可就连幻觉也是要付账的。账单不久就会像霍茨波（Hotspur）形容特伦特河（Trent）那样，「曲曲折折地淌进来」；自然的铁律早有规定：你充胀的状态越是堂皇，一记戳破所引致的消沉便越是深重。邓洛普先生（Mr. Dunlop）赠予我们种族的这件普罗米修斯式的礼物，无疑把骑车这门消遣从一种相当颠簸的散文，提升进了抒情诗的天堂。然而，任凭天下最壮实的钉子扎进旧日的实心胎，也绝不至于逼得你牵上一匹瘸了蹄的珀伽索斯（Pegasus），从霍尼斯特山口（Honister Pass）的顶上一路走到凯西克（Keswick），沿途既享不到自行车的福，也享不到无拘无束骑用「尚克斯的母马」（Shanks’ Mare，戏指自己的两条腿）的福。

于是，战争——它既备着这样一具把你吹胀的气筒，也备着这样一丛供你扎破的荆棘——到头来也只好任凭我们做回我们本来的那「可怜的皱缩之物」。这情形就好像：那个寻常人一直在一场梦里，把自己冒充给自己看，冒充成某出通俗戏里年轻的主人公；又在振奋人心的最后一幕里，于 1914 年，同自己、同观众都好聚好散地退了场，在军乐齐奏、旌旗飘扬之中登程，去「黄金西部」的处女地上开垦一种高贵而幸福的新生活。而如今，他在「黄金西部」醒来，醒在一片泥泞肮脏的农场上：购地的款项还分文未付，农具和肥料又贵得惊人；看不见一面旗帜，也听不见一件乐器；邻近的农人不断捎来愁惨的音信；原因与结果一如既往、可憎至极地环环相扣；而他心里无时不怀着一股酸溜溜的猜度：就在那出如今已然停演、且能忘多忘少就忘多少的戏吵嚷狂奔的演出期间，各色比他少些轻信的人，早已靠他「出」的那一份力——出力的是他，进账的可不是他——赚下了大得没了边的一票。

## IV Tedium / 四 沉闷

### I / 一

===Original===

A book may be bad and yet tell you much. Lately I came across such a book. It is surely one of the crossest books ever written. Its author fought in France, in the ranks, for a good many months of the war. He must have been one of the men who make sergeants grey﻿—a “proper lawyer,” as Regulars call the type which a cotton district labels as “self-acting mules.”

I seem to know that man. He was a volunteer, but he would not enlist until conscription came in, because of some precious doctrine he had about younger men without families. When he did join his first act was to ask to speak to the colonel. He was aggrieved because army doctors would not act, when he desired it, except as such. When anyone checked him he felt an ardent thirst to “explain,” and the explanation was always that he who had checked was wrong. In the field he kept a diary and sternly would he note on its recording page that tea one day﻿—nay, on more than one﻿—was served “very late indeed.” Heinous!

The continued existence of war is precarious. More than the League of Nations menaces its future. For it depends, at the last, on the infrequency of “proper lawyers.” Armies can now be made, and moved about when made, only because the plain man who keeps the world going round does not stick up for the last ounce of his rights, or stick out for the joys of having the last word, so dourly as these. Even to keep up a game with so modest an element of voluntaryism about it as penal justice you have to have some little effort of cooperation all round. If your convicts will not even eat the whole thing begins crumbling. The “suffragettes” showed us that. A pioneer still earlier, an Indian coolie, proved it in a Fijian gaol. Were every soldier like this diarist war would have to be dropped, not because men were too good, but because they were too prickly.

===Chinese===

一本书可能很坏，却仍能告诉你许多东西。近来我就碰到了这样一本书。它想必是有史以来写得最暴躁的书之一。其作者在法国当兵，在行伍中熬过了战争的好些个月份。他必是那种能把军士们熬白头发的人——正规军（Regulars）把这一类型叫作「十足的讼师」（proper lawyer），而棉业地区则给他们贴上「自动骡子」（self-acting mules）的标签。

我仿佛认得这个人。他本是志愿投效者，却迟迟不肯入伍，直到征兵制（conscription）颁行才去当兵，因为他怀有一套宝贵的教义，认为该先轮到那些没有家室的更年轻的人。他一入伍，头一个举动便是请求面见团长。他愤愤不平，因为军医们不肯在他想要的时候便听凭差遣，只肯按军医自己的身份行事。每当有人拦他，他便燃起一股热切的干渴，非要「解释」不可，而解释的结论永远是：拦他的人错了。在战地他记日记，会在那记录的页面上铁面无私地记下：某日的茶——不，不止一日的茶——送得「实在是晚」。罪大恶极！

战争之得以继续存在，本就岌岌可危。比国际联盟（League of Nations）更厉害地威胁着它前途的，另有其物。因为归根结底，战争系于「十足的讼师」们之不多见。如今之所以还组建得起军队、组建之后还调得动，只是因为那位推着世界转个不停的普通人，并不像这一类人那样，死硬地坚持自己权利的最后一盎司，也不那样死死咬住「非说最后一句话不可」的乐趣。就连刑罚司法这样一门自愿成分已颇为微薄的游戏，要维持下去，也得人人四面八方通力合作那么一小点。倘若连你的苦役犯都绝食了，整座建筑便开始崩塌。「妇女参政论者」（suffragettes）们已经向我们示范过这一点。更早的一位先驱，一个印度苦力，在斐济（Fiji）的一座监狱里证实了它。倘若每个士兵都像这位日记作者一样，战争就只好作罢——不是因为人太好，而是因为他们刺头得过了头。

### II / 二

===Original===

And yet the book told something which no other book has yet succeeded in telling you. Wordy, cantankerous, dull, repeating itself like a decimal, padded with cheap political “thoughts” gathered from old “stunts” in bad papers﻿—still, it came nearer than any other to showing you the way trench warfare struck a mind and soul quite commonplace in everything except a double dose of native sourness. Here was nothing of M. Barbusse’s doctrinaire fire to make the author pervert or exaggerate. No thrill of drastic passion, not even the passionate self-pity of Dickens describing his childhood as Copperfield’s, stirred the plodding and crabbed narrative. The writer seemed too peevish to be at the pains to beautify or exalt. And so his account of the bungled attack in which he took part is extraordinarily true to all that the commonplace man found to be left in almost any attack when once all the picturesque fluff filling the current literary pictures of it were found not to be there﻿—the touch of bathos; the supposed heroic moment only seeming a bit of a “dud,” a miscarriage; the hugger-mugger element of confusion; the baffling way that the real thing did not so often give men obvious gallant things to do as irritating puzzles to solve, muddles to liquidate at short notice; the queer flashes of revelation, in contact with individual enemies, of the bottomless falsity of the cheaper kind of current war psychology.

Advances, however, were far from being the staple of warfare. They caused the most losses, but still they did less than the years of less sensational routine to make what changes were made by the war in the minds of the men in the ranks. And here our pettish author found the congenial theme for his own acrid, accurate method. His trivial reiterations succeed, in the end, in piling up in the reader’s mind an image of that old trench life as the sum of innumerable dreary units of irksome fatigue. This was the normal life of the infantry private in France. For N.C.O.’s it was lightened by the immunity of their rank from fatigue work in the technical sense. For the officer it was much further lightened by better quarters and the servant system. For most of his time the average private was tired. Fairly often he was so tired as no man at home ever is in the common run of his work.

===Chinese===

然而这本书讲出了一样东西，那是任何别的书都还没能成功地讲给你的。冗长，乖戾，乏味，像循环小数一般自我重复，塞满了从蹩脚报纸的旧「噱头」里捡来的廉价政治「思想」——即便如此，它仍比任何别的书都更接近于让你看清：堑壕战是怎样打击了一副头脑与灵魂——这副头脑与灵魂在一切方面都平庸无奇，唯独天生的酸劲比常人多上一倍。书中没有巴比塞先生（M. Barbusse）那种教条主义的火气，足以诱使作者歪曲或夸大。没有凌厉激情的震颤，甚至也没有狄更斯借科波菲尔（Copperfield）之笔描写自己童年时那种火热的自怜，来搅动这部步履滞重、脾气乖张的记叙。这位作者似乎火气太盛，懒得劳神去美化或拔高。于是，他对他亲身经历的那场打糟了的进攻的记述，格外真切地呈现了平凡人在几乎任何一场进攻中终于发现剩下来的东西——前提是，当人们发现，把当时文学图景填塞得满满当当的种种如画绒絮全都不在场之后——那一下由崇高跌落的突降（bathos）；那个想当然的英雄时刻，看上去只是一枚「哑弹」，一场失手；那乱成一团的乌烟瘴气；还有那令人困惑之处：真家伙交给人的，往往不是堂堂正正的英勇事业，而是叫人恼火的谜题，是要在仓促之间料理干净的乱局；还有同个别敌兵接触时种种怪异的灵光一闪，照出时下那类廉价战争心理学的无底虚假。

然而，进攻远非战争的主食。进攻造成的损失最重，但比起那些年头里不甚耸动的日常勤务来，它们在行伍士兵心里促成的改变——战争于士兵内心造成的一切改变——反倒更少。而正是在这里，我们这位易怒的作者为他那套尖刻而精确的笔法找到了投缘的题材。他那些琐屑的重复，最终成功地在读者心里堆起一幅图景：那旧日的堑壕生活，乃是无穷多个令人生厌的烦重苦役单元之和。这便是法国土地上步兵列兵的正常生活。对军士（N.C.O.）们而言，他们的军阶豁免了严格意义上的苦役，日子轻省了一层。对军官而言，更好的住处与勤务兵制度又使之轻省得多。普通列兵的大部分时间都在疲惫之中。他还常常疲惫到那样的地步——本土上任何人在寻常工作的常规进程里，是从来不曾疲惫到那里的。

===Original===

If a company’s trench strength was low and sentry-posts abounded more than usual in its sector a man might, for eight days running, get no more than one hour off duty at any one time, day or night. If enemy guns were active many of these hours off guard duty might have to be spent on trench repair. After one of these bad times in trenches a company or platoon would sometimes come out on to the road behind the communication trench like a flock of over-driven sheep. The weakest ones would fall out and drop here and there along the road, not as a rule fainting, but in the state of a horse deadbeat, to whom any amount of thrashing seems preferable to going on. Men would come out lightheaded with fatigue, and ramble away to the men next them about some great time which they had had, or meant to have, at home. Or a man would march all right till the road fetched a bend, and then he would march straight on into the ditch in his sleep. Upon a greasy road with a heavy camber I have seen a used-up man get the illusion, on a night-march back to billets, that he was walking on a round, smooth, horizontal pole or convex plank above some fearsome sort of gulf. He would struggle hard to recover imaginary losses of footing, pant and sweat and scrape desperately sideways with his feet like a frightened young horse new to harness when it leans in against the pole, with its feet skidding outwards on the setts. Down he would go, time after time, in the mud, each time as unable to rise of himself, under the weight of his pack and equipment, as any medieval knight unhorsed and held down by the weight of his armour. Hauled up again to his feet, to be driven along like one of the spent cab-horses in Naples just strong enough to move when up, but not to rise, he would in another five minutes be agonizing again on the greasy pole of his delirium.

The querulist of the book took it hard, I remember, that more kind words did not come to the men. He saw his own lot very clearly, but not so clearly the lot of those other unfortunates who had to put the job through. A man who finds himself in charge of a spent horse at night, in a place where there may be no safe waiting till dawn, must do something. Ten to one he will flog or kick the horse into moving. He may feel that he, not the horse, is the beast; but still he will do it. So, too, will he bully and curse exhausted men into safety. That was what happened. Every decent N.C.O. and company officer﻿—and far the larger part were decent﻿—did what they could to humour and “buck” the bad cases through the pangs of endurance. Some would reach the journey’s end carrying whole faggots of rifles. Some would put by their own daily rations of rum to ginger beaten men through the last mile. But there would come times when only hard driving seemed to be left. *Bella, horrida bella!*

===Chinese===

倘若一个连队的堑壕兵力不足，而其防区里的哨位又比平常更密，那么一个人可能一连八天，无论昼夜，每次下哨都得不到多于一个钟头的休息。倘若敌炮活跃，这些离哨的钟头里有许多还得花在修补战壕上。熬过这样一段糟糕的堑壕时日之后，一个连队或一个排有时会走出交通壕（communication trench），上了后面的公路，像一群被赶过了头的羊。最虚弱的人会掉队，零零落落地倒在路边，通常并不是晕倒，而是处在一匹力竭的马的境地——对这样的马，怎么鞭打似乎都比继续前行可取。人们会带着疲惫到头昏的脑子走出来，向身旁的人语无伦次地絮叨着他们在国内曾度过的、或打算要度过的什么好时光。也有的人一路走得挺好，直到公路拐了弯，便在睡梦里照直走进路沟里去。在一条油腻泛光、路拱厚重的路上，我见过一个精疲力尽的人在夜行军回宿营地的途中生出错觉，以为自己正走在一根浑圆光滑的水平杆上、或一块凸面板上，下方是某种可怕的深渊。他会拼命挣扎，去夺回想象中失掉的立足处，喘气，流汗，两只脚绝望地朝旁边刨蹭，活像一匹初次上套、惊惶失措的年轻马——那马斜倚进辕杆，蹄子在石块路面上直向外打滑。他会一次又一次栽进泥里，每一次都在背包与全副装具的重量下，像任何一个被挑落马下、又被盔甲压住的中世纪骑士那样，自己再也起不来。被拽着重新架到脚上，像那不勒斯（Naples）那些拉客车的疲马——站起来了才挪得动、卧下去便起不来——那样被驱赶着往前走，不出五分钟，他又会在他那谵妄的油腻滑杆上苦苦挣扎起来。

我记得，书中那位满腹冤情的诉苦者对一件事耿耿于怀：温存话到士兵们这里来得太少。他把自己那份苦楚看得清清楚楚，对另外那些倒霉蛋的苦楚却看得不那么清楚——那些人必得把这趟差事押送到头。一个人若在夜里发觉自己管着一匹力竭的马，又身处一个未必能安然等到天亮的地方，就必须有所动作。十有八九，他会鞭打或脚踢那匹马，逼它挪步。他也许会觉得，是他自己、而不是那匹马，才是畜生；但他还是会干。同样，他也会连吓带咒，把精疲力尽的人们轰进安全地带。当时发生的就是如此。每一位正派的军士和连队军官——而他们绝大多数是正派的——都尽力哄着、鼓着那些垮得厉害的人，熬过忍耐的剧痛。有的人到达旅程终点时，肩上扛着一整捆步枪。有的人省下自己每日的朗姆酒配给，好在最后一里路上给被打垮的人提提气。但总有些时候，剩下的似乎只有狠命的驱赶。*Bella, horrida bella!*（战争，可怕的战争！）

### III / 三

===Original===

Suppose those first eight days in the front and support trenches to be the beginning of a divisional tour of sixteen days’ duty in the line. For four days now the weary men would be in reserve, under enemy fire, but not in trenches; probably in the cellars of ruined houses. But these were not times of rest. Each day or night every man would make one or more journeys back to the trenches that they had left carrying some load of food, water, or munitions up to the three companies in trenches, or perhaps leading a pack-mule over land to some point near the front line, under cover of night. Even to lead a laden mule in the dark over waste ground confusingly wired and trenched is work; to get him back on to his feet when fallen and wriggling, in wild consternation, among a tangle of old barbed wire may be quite hard work.

In intervals between these journeys most men would lie in the straw in the cellars or hobble weakly about the outside of the premises, looking as boys sometimes do when stiff with many hearty hacks sustained in a hard game of football, with a chill after it. They crawled in and out of their billets like late autumn bees, feebly scraping the eight days’ plating of mud off their clothes and cleaning their jackknives after meals with the languor of the elders in the Bible to whom the grasshopper was a burden. A few robust spirits, armed with craft and subtlety more fully than the rest, would strike out, whenever released, for some “just-a-minute,” or estaminet, not too far off, nor yet too near, and there lie perdus, lest the Company Orderly Sergeant warn them for some new liturgy. This defensive policy did not lighten the work of their brethren.

After four days of their labours as sumpter mules, or muleteers, the company would plod back for another four days of duty in trenches, come out yet more universally tired at their end, and drift back to rest-billets, out of ordinary shellfire, for their sixteen days or so of “divisional rest.” Here their work was really lighter, but still it was work and not rest. It did not wholly wind up in most of the men the spring that had run down while they were in the line. And then the division would go again into the line, and the old cycle be worked through once more. So most of the privates were tired the whole of the time; sometimes to the point of torment, sometimes much less, but always more or less tired.

===Chinese===

且把在前沿堑壕与支援堑壕里度过的头八天，当作一个师十六天阵地值勤轮回的开端。此后四天，疲惫的人们将充作预备队，仍在敌军炮火之下，只是不在堑壕里；多半待在倾颓房屋的地窖中。但这些并不是休憩的时光。每个昼或夜，人人都得回到他们撤离的那些堑壕跑上一趟或几趟，扛着食物、水或弹药，送上堑壕里的那三个连去；或者趁着夜色的掩护，牵一头驮骡越野走到前线附近的某个地点。黑夜里牵着满载的骡子，走过那片铁丝网与堑壕纵横交错、令人晕头转向的荒地，这本身就是活计；待它跌倒了，在一团陈年带刺铁丝中间惊得乱蹬乱扭，要把它重新弄站起来，那更是相当吃重的活计。

在这些往返的间隙，大多数人躺在地窖的稻草里，或者有气无力地在宅子外头一瘸一拐地蹭来蹭去，那模样像极了踢完一场硬仗似的足球赛、结结实实挨了许多狠铲、赛后又着了凉的男孩们，浑身僵痛。他们爬进爬出宿营地，像晚秋的蜜蜂，有气无力地刮掉衣裳上那八天镀上去的一层泥，饭后清理随身小刀时，带着圣经里那些长老们的倦怠——对那些长老，蚱蜢已成了重担。少数精壮的角色，凭着比旁人装备得更充足的狡黠与心机，一得释放便直奔某家「一分钟小店」（just-a-minute），也就是法国乡间小酒馆（estaminet），路程不远不近，到了便潜伏不出，免得连值班军士（Company Orderly Sergeant）点了名，把他们派上什么新的日课。这一防御性方针，并没有减轻他们弟兄们的活计。

当满四天驮骡、或满四天赶骡人之后，连队会再步履沉重地回去当四天堑壕值勤，期满出来时更是人人都疲，然后漂回寻常炮火之外的休整宿营地去，过他们那十六天上下的「师级休整」。在那里，活计确实轻了，但仍是活计，不是休息。它并不能把大多数人在阵地里走空了的那根发条，重新上满。然后这个师便再度开进阵地，旧的轮回再从头经历一遍。于是大多数列兵自始至终都疲着；有时疲到苦刑的地步，有时轻得多，但总是或多或少地疲着。

### IV / 四

===Original===

Many, of course, lost health and drifted “down the line,” as it was called, to the base, where work might be light, but much of the company rather more blighting than any work to the spirit. Hither, to all the divisional base depots and into the ultimate dust-hole or sink that was called “Base Details,” there gravitated most of the walking wreckage and wastage, physical and moral, of active warfare: convalescent, sick and wounded from hospital, men found too old or too young for trench work, broken-nerved men smuggled out of the way before disaster should come, and malingerers triumphant and chuckling, or only semi-successful, suspect, and tediously overacting.

There was the good man fretting and raging to get back to his friends and the fight, away from this tainted backwater in which the swelling flotillas of the unfit and the unwilling were left to rot at their moorings. There was the pallid and bent London clerk, faintly disguised in khaki but too blind to fight, now working furiously fifteen hours each day of his seven-day week in the orderly room﻿—no Sunday here, no Saturday afternoon﻿—for pure love of international right. There was the dugout, the Grenadier Guards sergeant-major of sixty, the handsome and melancholy old boy, a Victorian survivor into our little vulgar age, with a careful and dignified manner and mighty memories of a radiant past in London, when all parades, for a good-conduct-man well up in his drill, were over by half-past ten in the morning and he had a permanent midnight pass into barracks and so could act as a super at one of the theatres every night except when doing a guard, and see life and move among genius and beauty, making good money. Oh, yes, he had acted with Irving and Booth, and lived the life, and heard the chimes at midnight.

===Chinese===

当然，也有许多人垮了身体，顺流「退往下线」（down the line）——当时的说法——漂到了基地；那里的活计兴许轻省，但那地方的大半交际，对人的精神却比任何活计都更具摧残性。往这里，往各个师的基地兵站，往那个被唤作「基地杂员」（Base Details）的终极尘坑与污水坑里，汇聚起运动战绝大部分尚能行走的残骸与损耗，身体的与道德的：医院里出来的康复者、病号与伤号，被认定过老或过嫩、不堪堑壕勤务的人，趁大祸未至先被悄悄挪开的垮了神经的人，还有得意洋洋、暗自窃笑的装病者，或者只成功了一半、招人疑心、把戏演得过火而令人生厌的装病者。

有那好人，焦灼暴怒地想回到他的伙伴们和战斗那边去，离开这片污浊的死水——在那里面，不适役者与不情愿者日益庞大的船队，正系在泊位上任其朽烂。有那苍白佝偻的伦敦职员，薄薄罩了层卡其，眼睛却瞎得不能作战，如今在文书室里一周七天、一天十五个钟头发疯似地干——这里没有礼拜天，没有礼拜六下午——纯然出于对国际公道的爱。有那位复役老军官（dugout），六十岁的掷弹兵近卫团（Grenadier Guards）军士长（sergeant-major），一位英俊而忧郁的老哥，维多利亚时代的幸存者，流落到我们这个颇见庸俗的小时代里来，举止谨慎而庄重，满腹伦敦那段辉煌往昔的宏富记忆：那时候，对一个操练娴熟的优秀士兵，所有检阅到上午十点半便一律收场，他持有一张永久有效的午夜回营证，因此除站岗之外，每晚都能去某家剧院充当一名群众演员，见识人生，周旋于天才与美人之间，还大把挣钱。哦是的，他同欧文（Irving）和布斯（Booth）同台演过戏，过过那样的日子，听见过午夜的钟声。

===Original===

But also the veteran crooks, old dregs of the Regular Army, Queen Victoria’s worst bargains, N.C.O.’s who would boast that they had not been once on parade in the last twenty years, waiters and caterers for the whole of their martial careers till the liquor fairly lipped over the edge of their eyelids and bleached the blue of their eyes. You would hear one of them boast that no doctor on earth could find him out to be fit when he, the tactician, wished otherwise. Another had made pathological studies, learning up the few conjectural symptoms of maladies that show no outward trace; as science advanced to the point of recording detectively the true state of the heart he had deftly changed ground, relinquished rheumatism of that organ and done some work of research into pains in the head; much faith did he put, too, in the sciatic nerve. When a couple of these savants slept in one tent they would argue after Lights Out﻿—was sciatica safest, or shell-shock, or general debility? “Them grey hairs should be a lot of use to you, corp.,” one of them would quite feelingly say to a new man in the tent, “when you want to get swinging the lead.”

While these ignoble presences befouled the air of a base, good things, also, were there; but you seldom quite knew which was which. All very well for the King to come out with his “Go, hang yourself, brave Crillon! We fought at Arques and you were not there.” But if you, too, were not at the battle﻿—if some unlucky effect of combustion compelled you to live as a messmate of Crillon, far, far from Arques when the battle was on, you would have to use tact. Somehow the man who was undisguisedly keen to get back to the centre of things felt a slight coldness pervading the air about him. It was as if a workman, who might have so easily let well alone, had sinned against the trade-union spirit, helped to raise the standard of employers’ expectation, forced the pace of dutifulness in a world where authority could be trusted to speed things up quite enough. Even officers tended to deprecate the higher temperatures of ardour in other ranks of base establishments. “You’re out for distinction,”﻿—one honest rationalist would advise﻿—“that’s what it is. Well, trust to me﻿—up the line’s not the place where you get it. Every time a war ends you’ll find most of the decorations go to the people at G.H.Q., L. of C., and the bases. So, if you want a nice row of ribbons to show to your kiddies, stop here.” And another would put it more subtly: “Isn’t one’s duty, as a rule, just here and now?” Some were good-natured; they were not for keeping the primrose path all to themselves. Others were anxious lest the taking of steep and thorny paths, as they thought them, should come to be “the done thing.”

===Chinese===

但也有那些老牌的骗子手，正规军的陈年沉渣，维多利亚女王（Queen Victoria）做下的最亏本的买卖，一些军士——他们夸口说，过去二十年里自己连一次检阅也没上过，整个武人生涯都在当侍者与酒席承办人，直到酒液当真漫过了眼睑的边沿，把眼里的蓝色都泡得褪尽。你会听见其中一位夸口：只要他这位战术家不愿意，天底下便没有哪个医生能查出他是适役的。另一位做过些病理学研究，把那几种不显外部痕迹的病症仅有的一点猜测性症状背得烂熟；等科学进步到能用侦察手段把心脏的真实状态记录在案时，他便灵巧地转移了阵地，放弃了心脏的风湿症，改在头痛方面做了些研究工作；对坐骨神经，他也寄予颇多信仰。当这样两位大学者同睡一顶帐篷时，熄灯号（Lights Out）之后还要辩论——是坐骨神经痛最稳妥，还是弹震症（shell-shock），还是全身衰弱？「你那一头灰头发，往后该顶用了吧，下士，」其中一位会相当动情地对帐篷里的新人说，「等你想装病开小差（swing the lead）的时候。」

这类卑劣的角色熏污着基地的空气，但好东西那里也有；只是你很难分清哪个是哪个。国王尽可以脱口甩出他那句「去上吊吧，勇敢的克里永（Crillon）！我们打阿尔克（Arques）之役的时候，你不在场」。可倘若你自己也不曾赶上那场战役——倘若燃烧的某种不巧效应迫使你做了克里永的食堂同席，在战事正酣之时远而又远地离开了阿尔克——那你就得讲点手腕了。也不知怎么，那个毫不掩饰地一心想回到万事中心去的人，总会觉得围绕自己的空气里弥漫着一层薄薄的寒意。这就好比一个工人，本可以轻而易举地安于现状，却触犯了工会精神的忌讳，帮着抬高了雇主期望的标准，在一个大可信赖当局自会把一切催得够快的世界上，硬把尽职的步子催快了。连军官们也倾向于不赞成基地机构里其他军阶的人热度过高。「你是想博出挑，」——某位老实的理性主义者会这样进言——「就是这么回事。得，信我一句——前线可不是你捞着它的地方。每逢一场战争结束，你都会发现大多数勋章落到了总司令部（G.H.Q.）、交通线（L. of C.）和各基地的人手里。所以，你要是想攒一排漂亮勋表日后给孩子们看，就待在这儿吧。」另一位则说得更隐微：「一个人的本分，照例说，不就在此时此地么？」有些人是好意；他们倒不想把这条安逸花径独自占尽。另一些人则忧心忡忡，生怕去走他们心目中那些陡峭多刺的小径，竟会变成「时兴的做法」。

### V / 五

===Original===

The men who could not shirk the choice of Hercules, for other people, were the doctors. The stay of every N.C.O. or man at a base depot was on probation. Each had to go before a Medical Board soon after he came. It adjudged him either T.B. (Temporary Base) or P.B. (Permanent Base). If marked T.B. he went before the Board again once a week, and each time he might be marked T.B. again, or, if his disablement was thought graver or more likely to last, P.B.; or he might be marked A. (Active Service), and then he would join the next draft from home going up to his own battalion or another battalion of his regiment. When once a man was marked P.B. he only went before the Board once a month, and each time he, too, might be marked either P.B., T.B., or A.

Chance relegated me once for some weeks to a base and gave me the job of marching parties of crocks, total and partial, real, half-real, and sham, across the sand dunes to the place where the faculty did its endeavour to sort them. A picture remains of a hut with a long table in it: two middle-aged army doctors sitting beyond it, like dons at a Viva, and each of my party in turn taking his stand at attention, my side of the table, facing the Board, like so many Oliver Twists. The presiding officer takes a manifest pride in knowing all the guile and subtlety of soldier-men. No taking *him* in﻿—that is proclaimed in every look and tone. He has had several other parties before him today, and the lamp of his faith, never dazzling while these rites are on, has burnt low.

“Well, my man﻿—cold feet, I suppose?” he begins, to the first of my lamentable party. As some practitioners are said to begin all treatments with a prefatory purge, so would this psychologist start with a good full dose of insult and watch the patient’s reaction under the stimulus.

“No, sir, me ’eart’s thrutched up,” says the examinee. Then, while the Board perforates him from head to foot for some seconds with a basilisk stare of unbelief, he dribbles out at intervals, in a voice that bespeaks falling hope, such ineffective addenda as “Can’t get me sleep” and “Not a smile in me.”

===Chinese===

替旁人扛着「海格立斯的抉择」、想躲也躲不掉的，是军医们。每一名军士或士兵在基地兵站的居留，都属察看之身。人人一到，不久便须到医务审查委员会（Medical Board）面前走一遭。委员会裁定他或为 T.B.（临时留基，Temporary Base），或为 P.B.（永久留基，Permanent Base）。若被判为 T.B.，他每星期得再上一次委员会的堂，每次他可能再判 T.B.；或者，若他的残障被认为更重、或更可能迁延，则判 P.B.；他也可能被判为 A.（现役，Active Service），那样他便编入本土开来的下一批补充兵（draft），上行回到自己营里，或本团另外哪个营里去。而一个人一旦被判为 P.B.，便只须每月上一次堂，且每次他也可能被判为 P.B.、T.B. 或 A.。

机缘一度把我贬到某基地待了几个星期，派给我的差事，是押送一队队病号——全废的与半废的，真的、半真的与假造的——穿过沙丘，到那座学人们勉力把他们分拣开的地方去。有一幅图景留了下来：一间棚屋，屋里一张长桌；两位中年的军医坐在桌后，像口试（viva）席上的导师；我那一队人依次立正，站到桌子的我这一侧，面朝委员会，一个个都像奥利弗·退斯特（Oliver Twist）。主审官以洞悉士兵们的全部狡黠与伎俩自许，且引以为豪。想蒙*他*——没门；他的每一瞥、每一语都在宣告这一点。今天在他手下已经过了好几队人，而他那盏信仰之灯——这类仪式进行期间从不曾明亮照人——已经燃得低了。

「喂，老弟——是怯阵了吧，我猜？」他向我这支可悲队伍的第一位开了腔。据说有些开业医生动辄以一剂开路的泻药开始一切治疗，这位心理学家也如出一辙，先奉上一大瓢足量的侮辱，再观察病人在这剂刺激下的反应。

「不是，长官，俺的心口给攥得慌。」受检者说。随后，当委员会用蛇怪般的不信的盯视把他从头到脚穿刺了几秒钟，他便用一副昭示着希望正在跌落的嗓音，零星挤出一串效力不大的补充，如「俺睡不着觉」与「俺怎么也笑不起来」。

===Original===

“Very picturesque, indeed,” says the senior expert in doubting. “We’ll see to that ‘thrutched’ heart of yours. Cardiograph case. Next man.”

The suspect, duly spat upon, slinks out. The next man takes his place at the table. The president gives him the Dogberry eye that means: “Masters, it is proved already that you are little better than false knaves; and it will go near to be thought so shortly.” What he says is: “Another old hospital bird? Eh? Now, hadn’t you better get back to work before you’re in trouble?”

The target of this consputation is almost convinced by its force that he must be guilty of something, if only he knew what it was. Still, he repeats authority’s last diagnosis as well as he can: “Mine’s Arthuritic rheumatism, sir. *An’* piles.”

“Fall out and strip. Next man.” While the next is taking his stand the presiding M.O. has been making a note, and does not look up before saying “Well, what’s the matter with *you*﻿—besides rheumatism?”

“No rheumatism, sir. And nothing else.” The voice is as stiff as it dares.

The presiding M.O. seems taken aback. Why, here is a fellow not playing up to him! Making a nasty break in the long line of cases that fed his darling cynicism so well! Flat burglary as ever was committed. The second member of the Board comes to life and begins in a tone that savours of dissatisfaction: “Well, you’re the first man﻿—”

===Chinese===

「真是绘声绘色。」怀疑这门学问的资深专家说。「你那颗『攥得慌』的心，我们会来关照的。心动图病例。下一个。」

那嫌疑犯挨过了应得的一口唾沫，缩着身子出去了。下一位在桌前就了位。主任委员向他投去一道道格伯里（Dogberry）式的眼色，意思是：「众家伙计，已有凭据证明，你们比奸猾的骗子好不到哪里去；而且不久就要被人认作是这路人了。」他口头说的则是：「又是一个老病号？嗯？我说，趁还没惹上麻烦，你是不是最好先回去干活？」

这场训诫的靶子几乎被它的声势说服了，认定自己必是犯了什么过失——只要他知道那是什么。但他还是竭尽所能地复述了当局的最新诊断：「俺得的是『亚瑟里蒂克』风湿，长官。*还有*痔疮。」

「出列，脱衣裳。下一个。」趁下一位立正站定的当口，主审的军医官（M.O.）一直在记录着什么，头也不抬便说：「喂，你有什么毛病——风湿除外？」

「没有风湿，长官。别的毛病也没有。」那嗓音在不越矩的范围内绷得笔直。

主审军医官似乎吃了一惊。嚯，这家伙竟不接他的茬！在他那条喂养着他心爱的犬儒主义的长长病例队伍里，劈出了一处恼人的裂口！这简直是不折不扣的盗窃行径。委员会的第二位成员活了过来，用一种带着不满余味的调子开了口：「喂，你可是头一个——」

===Original===

“I’m an N.C.O., sir.” The young lance-sergeant’s voice is again about as stiff as is safe. Quite safe, though, this time. For the presiding M.O. is a Regular. Verbal points of military correctitude are the law and the prophets to him. He cannot be wholly sorry when junior colleagues, temporary commissioners, slip up on even the least of these shreds of orange-peel. Like Susan Nipper, he knows his place﻿—“me being a permanency”﻿—and thinks that “temporaries” ought to know theirs. So he amends the outsider’s false start to: “You’re the first N.C.O. or man who has come before us this morning and not said he had rheumatism.”

The sergeant, whom I have known for some days as a choleric body, holds his tongue, having special reasons just now not to risk a court-martial. “Well,” the president snaps as if in resentment of this self-control, “what *is* the matter with you?”

“Fit as can be, sir.”

“What are you doing down here, then, away from your unit?”

“Obeying orders of Medical Board, sir. No. 8 General Hospital, December 8.”

“Not sorry, either, I daresay,” the president mutters, wobbling back towards his first line of approach to the business. “Not very keen to go back up the line, sergeant, eh?”

===Chinese===

「我是军士，长官。」年轻的代理中士（lance-sergeant）的嗓音又一次在不越矩的边缘上绷得笔直。不过这一次，是完全安全了。因为主审军医官是正规军出身。军事措辞正确与否的细节，在他便是律法与先知。年资较浅的同僚——那些临时委任官——哪怕在最不起眼的一片橘子皮上滑了跤，他也无法全然不暗自庆幸。他像苏珊·尼珀（Susan Nipper）一样清楚自己的地位——「俺是永久编制」——并且认为「临时们」也该清楚他们自己的地位。于是他把那位外人起步时的失言更正为：「你是今天上午到我们面前来的军士或士兵里，头一个不说自己有风湿的。」

这位中士，我结识他已有几天，知道是个火爆性子，此刻却缄口不言——眼下他有不惹军法审判的特殊理由。「那么，」主任委员像是愤恨于这份克制似的，厉声说道，「你*到底*有什么毛病？」

「结实得不能再结实，长官。」

「那你跑到这儿来做什么，不在自己部队里待着？」

「奉医务审查委员会的命令来的，长官。第 8 总医院，12 月 8 日。」

「恐怕你心里也不觉得遗憾吧。」主任委员咕哝道，又晃回到他对这桩差事的第一条进路上来了。「不太急着回前线去吧，中士，嗯？」

===Original===

“It’s all I want, sir, thank you.” The sergeant puts powerful brakes on his tongue and says only that. But he has sadly disconcerted the faculty. A major with twenty years’ service has cast himself for the fine sombre part of recording angel to note all the cowardice and mendacity that he can. And here is a minor actor forgetting his part and putting everything out. From where I am keeping a wooden face near the door I see opposition arising in the heart of the outraged psychologist beyond the table.

A sound professional instinct reinforces the personal one. Whenever a soldier goes before a Medical Board it is soon clear that he wants to be thought either less fit than he is or more fit. The doctor’s first impulse, as soon as he sees which way the man’s wishes tend, is to lean towards the other. And this, in due measure, is just. We all understate or overstate symptoms to our own family doctors according to what we fear or desire. The doctor rightly tries to detect the disturbing force in the patient’s mind, and to discount for it duly﻿—just like “laying-off” for a side-wind in shooting. So now the president sees light again. The Board is now out to find the lance-sergeant a crock. “Hold out your wrist,” says the senior member. The pulse is jealously felt.

“Rotten!” the senior member says to the junior. Then, penetratingly, to the sergeant: “What’s that cicatrix you’ve got on the back of your hand? Both hands! Show me here.”

Two spongy, purplish-red pads of new flesh are inspected. “Burns, scarcely healed!” says the president wrathfully. “Skin just the strength of wet tissue-paper! Man alive, you’ve a bracelet of ulcers all round your wrists. Never wash, eh?” When liquid fire flayed a man’s hands to the sleeve, but not further, the skin was apt to break out, as he recovered, in small, deep boils about the frontier of the new skin and the old. The sergeant does not answer. He wants no capital punishment under the Army act.

“Man’s an absolute wreck,” says the major. “Debility, wounds imperfectly healed, blood-poisoning likely. Not fit for the line for two months to come. P.B.﻿—eh?” he turns to his junior.

“That’s what *I* should say, sir,” the junior concurs, in a tone of desperate independence.

===Chinese===

「那正是我全部的所求，长官，谢谢您。」中士给他的舌头猛踩了刹车，只说了这一句。但他可把学者们搅得狼狈不堪。一位服役二十年的少校，早给自己派定了记录天使这个深沉的漂亮角色，要把他能搜罗到的一切怯懦与虚诓都记录在案。而这里偏偏有个小角色忘了台词，把整台戏都搅乱了。从我在门边绷着一张木头脸的位置望过去，我看见桌那头那位被冒犯了的心理学家心里，正升起一股对抗的意志。

健全的职业本能又给个人本能加了码。每当一名士兵站到医务审查委员会面前，人们很快便看得分明：他想被当成比实际更不健康的，或者比实际更健康的。医生的第一冲动，一看出这人的愿望偏向哪头，便是朝另一头倾斜。而这在合宜的分寸之内，是公正的。我们每个人对着自家的家庭医生，也都会依照所怕所愿，把症状说轻些或说重些。医生理当尽力探出病人心里那股扰动之力，并恰当地折算剔除——正如射击时为侧风留出修正量。于是此刻主任委员又重新看见了光。委员会现在的目标，是要给这位代理中士查出个病号来。「伸出手腕。」资深的那位说。脉搏被满怀戒心地按诊着。

「糟透了！」资深的那位对资浅的说。随即，一针见血地，冲着中士：「你手背上那道瘢痕是怎么来的？两只手都有！伸到这里来给我看。」

两块海绵质、紫红色的新肉垫被查验了一番。「烧伤，刚刚才收口！」主任委员怒气冲冲地说。「皮肤的劲道只抵得上打湿的纸巾！老兄，你两只手腕上整整箍着一圈溃疡。从来不洗吧，嗯？」当液火（liquid fire）把一个人的双手燎到袖口为止、不再往上时，随着康复，那皮肤很容易在新皮与旧皮的边界上发出一颗颗又小又深的疖子。中士不作回答。他可不想触犯《陆军法》（Army Act）去领极刑。

「这人是个彻底的残骸。」少校说。「衰弱，伤口愈合不良，还很可能血液中毒。今后两个月不适于上火线。P.B.——嗯？」他转向资浅的那位。

「*我*也正是这个意见，长官。」资浅的那位附和道，语调里透着一股拼命的独立。

===Original===

“Next man,” says the major. Before the lance-sergeant has quite stalked to the door the major calls after him “Sergeant!”

“Sir?” says the sergeant, furious and red but contained.

“You’re a damned good man, but it won’t do,” says the major. “Good luck to you!” Great are the forces of decent human relentment after a hearty let-out with the temper.

The inquisition proceeds, still on that Baconian principle of finding out which is a man’s special bent and then bending the twig pretty hard in the other direction; still, too, with the dry light of reason a little suffused, as Bacon would say, with the humours of the affections, of vanity, ill-temper and impatience. Nearly everybody is morally weary. Most of the men inspected have outlived the first profuse impulse to court more of bodily risk than authority expressly orders. Most of the doctors, living here in the distant rear of the war, have outlived their first generous belief in an almost universally high morale among the men. In the training-camps in 1914 the safe working presumption about any unknown man was that he only wanted to get at the enemy as soon as he could. Now the working presumption, the starting hypothesis, is that a man wants to stay in, out of the rain, as long as you let him. Faith has fallen lame; generosity flags; there has entered into the soul as well as the body the malady known to athletes as staleness.

===Chinese===

「下一个。」少校说。代理中士还没大步跨到门口，少校又冲他背后喊了一声：「中士！」

「长官？」中士说，怒火中烧，满面通红，却克制着。

「你是个顶呱呱的好汉子，可这事不由人。」少校说。「祝你好运！」痛痛快快发过一场脾气之后，正派人性的宽解之力，实在伟大。

审讯继续进行，依旧遵循那条培根（Bacon）式原则：先探明一个人的特殊偏向何在，再把那根枝条朝相反的方向狠狠拗过去；也依旧带着那束理性的干光——如培根会说的那样——稍稍洇上了情感的体液：虚荣、坏脾气与不耐。几乎人人在道德上都已困乏。受检的士兵大多数已经活过了那段丰沛的初发冲动——曾巴望去迎受比当局明令更多的身体风险。而医生们，住在这场战争遥远的后方，也大多活过了他们最初那种慷慨的信念——曾相信士兵们几乎普遍士气高昂。1914 年，在训练营里，关于任何一个陌生人的稳妥工作假定是：他一心只想尽快扑向敌人。如今的工作假定，那条出发的假设，则是：只要你容许，一个人巴不得一直躲在雨外头。信仰瘸了腿；慷慨泄了气；那种运动员们称作「疲沓」（staleness）的病症，已经侵入了灵魂，一如侵入了躯体。

### VI / 六

===Original===

The war had more obvious disagreeables, too; you have heard all about them: the quelling coldness of frosty nights spent in soaked clothes﻿—for no blankets were brought up to the trenches; the ubiquitous dust and stench of corpses and buzzing of millions of corpse-fed flies on summer battlefields; and so on, and so on﻿—no need to go over the list. But these annoyances seemed to me to do less in the way of moulding the men’s cast of mind than that general, chronic weariness, different from all the common fatigues of peace, inasmuch as each instalment of this course of exhaustion was not sandwiched in between heavenly contrasts of utter rest before and after﻿—divine sleeps in a bed and dry clothes, and meals on a table, with a white tablecloth on it and shiny glasses. It raised some serious thoughts in professional football-players and boxers who had believed they were strong, and in navvies and tough mountaineers. You need to know this in order to understand the redoubled ardour with which that capital soldier, the Lancashire miner, has sought the off-day and ensued it since he came back from campaigning abroad.

You need, too, to know it in order to chart out the general postwar condition of mind with its symptoms of apathy, callousness, and lassitude. Something has gone to come of it if you have lain for a time in the garden of Proserpine, where the great values decline and faith and high impulse fall in like soufflés grown tepid, and fatalistic indifference comes out of long flat expanses of tiring sameness.

> I am tired of tears and laughter,
> And men that laugh and weep;
> Of what may come hereafter
> For men that sow to reap:
> I am weary of days and hours,
> Blown buds of barren flowers,
> Desires and dreams and powers
> And everything but sleep.

> From too much love of living,
> From hope and fear set free,
> We thank with brief thanksgiving,
> Whatever gods may be,
> That no life lives forever;
> That dead men rise up never;
> That even the weariest river
> Winds somewhere safe to sea.

===Chinese===

战争还有一些更明显的苦处；那些你们全听说过：霜夜穿着湿透的衣裳挨过的那种砭骨的寒冷——因为毯子从不运上堑壕；夏日战场上无处不在的尸灰与尸臭，还有以尸为食的亿万苍蝇的嗡嗡；诸如此类，诸如此类——不必把单子从头点一遍。但在我看来，这些烦扰在塑造士兵心性方面的作为，都及不上那种全面的、慢性的困乏。它与和平年代一切寻常的疲劳都不同，因为这一场场耗竭的分期偿付，前后并不夹衬着彻底休息的天堂式对照——不曾在床铺与干衣裳里睡上神赐的酣觉，不曾坐在桌前进餐，桌上铺着白桌布，摆着亮晶晶的玻璃杯。它在自以为强健的职业足球运动员和拳击手心里，在挖土工与强悍的山民心里，都曾激起过一些沉重的思索。你需要知道这一层，才能理解那位头等的士兵——兰开夏（Lancashire）矿工——自海外征战归来之后，是以何等倍增的热忱寻觅节假日、又紧追着节假日不放的。

你也需要知道这一层，才能绘出战后普遍心境的图谱，连同它的种种症状：淡漠，麻木，倦怠。倘若你曾在普洛塞庇娜的花园（the garden of Proserpine）里躺过一段时日，那便必有某种东西要从中生发出来——在那花园里，宏大的价值衰落了，信仰与崇高的冲动像凉透了的舒芙蕾一般塌陷下去，而宿命论的冷漠，则从一片片漫长平坦、令人疲倦的千篇一律里生长出来。

> 我已厌倦眼泪与欢笑，
> 厌倦那些且笑且哭的人；
> 厌倦日后将至的一切，
> 属于那些播种求收割的人；
> 我已倦于一个个白日与钟点，
> 厌于荒花吹绽又吹散的蓓蕾，
> 厌于欲望、梦想与权能，
> 以及一切——唯独除却安睡。

> 从太盛的生之爱恋，
> 从希望与恐惧中释然，
> 我们以短短的感恩，
> 谢那无论何许的神祇：
> 幸而无生命长生不老；
> 幸而死者永不起身；
> 幸而连最疲惫的河流，
> 也终得蜿蜒，安然入海。

===Original===

Heaven forbid that I should impute any melodious Swinburnian melancholy, or any other form of luxurious self-pity, to millions of good fellows still fighting the good fight against circumstance. They would hoot at the notion. But in nearly all of them hope has, at some time or other, lost her first innocence. Time and place came when the spirit, although unbroken, went numb: the dull mind came to feel as if its business with ardour and choric spheres and quests of Holy Grails, and everything but rest, had been done quite a long while ago. Well chained to an oar in the galley, closely kept to a job in the mine, men caught a touch of the recklessness of the slave﻿—if the world were so foul, let it go where it chose; they would snatch what they could, when they could; drink, and let the world go round.

It is not sense to hope to reattain at will that deflowered virginity of faith. Others who have it may come in good time to be a majority of us all. Already three yearly “classes” of men who did not suffer that immense loss of experience which came with war service have come of age since the war; the new skin grows over the wounds. But we cannot write off as mere dream, with no after effects, the time when it was a kind of trench fashion to meet the demoded oaths of a friend with the dogma that “There *is* no —﻿—﻿— God.”

===Chinese===

上天不容我把任何一种斯温伯恩（Swinburne）式的悦耳忧郁，或任何别一种奢侈的自怜，加到千百万仍在与环境打那场美好之战的好汉子头上。他们准会朝这念头喝倒彩。但在他们几乎每个人身上，希望都曾在某个时节失去过她最初的纯真。总有过那样的时间与地点，精神虽未折断，却已麻木：迟钝的心开始觉得，它同热忱、同天球的合唱、同圣杯（Holy Grail）的追寻——同一切、唯独除休息之外——的那段旧公案，仿佛早在很久以前便已结清。被锁链牢牢拴在桨帆船的桨位上，被严严实实按在矿井的活计上，人们染上了几分奴隶的无所顾忌——这世界既已如此污浊，便随它漂到哪里去；他们能捞便捞，能抢便抢；喝酒，且由这世界自转去。

指望随意复得信仰那已被玷辱的童贞，是没有道理的。另一些生来有它的人，到时候自会成为我们全体的多数。战争结束以来，已有三个年度「征级」的男子成年了，他们不曾蒙受随战役而来的那笔经验的巨损；新皮正长满伤口。但有一段时光，我们无法把它当作一场了无后效的幻梦一笔勾销——那时，用「根本就没有——他妈的——上帝」这条教义，去回应朋友那过了时的咒誓，几乎是堑壕里的一种时尚。

## VIII The Duty of Lying / 八 说谎的义务

### I / 一

===Original===
To fool the other side has always been fair in a game. Every fencer or boxer may feint. A Rugby football player “gives the dummy” without any shame. In cricket a bowler is justly valued the more for masking his action.

In war your licence to lead the other fellow astray is yet more ample. For war, though it may be good sport to some men, is not a mere sport.
