In sport you are not “out to win” except on certain terms of courtesy and handsomeness. Who would take pride in a race won by a fluke? At Henley, a long time ago, there were five or six scullers in for the Diamonds. One of them, L﻿—﻿—, was known to be far the best man in the race. In the first heat he was drawn against A﻿—﻿—, of Oxford, about the best of the others. L﻿—﻿— had one fault﻿—a blind eye; and it often made him steer a bad course. Before the two had raced for fifty yards L﻿—﻿— blundered out of his course, crashed into A﻿—﻿—, and capsized him. The rules of boat-racing are clear: L﻿—﻿— had done for himself. A﻿—﻿—, who was now swimming, had only to look up to the umpire’s launch and hold up a hand. A nod would have been the reply, and the heat would have been A﻿—﻿—’s, and the final heat, in all likelihood, too. A﻿—﻿— looked well away from the umpire and kept his hands down, got back into his boat and said to his contrite opponent, “Start again here, sir?” A﻿—﻿— was decisively beaten, and never came so near to winning the Diamonds again.

===Chinese===
在比赛中愚弄对方，从来都被视为正当。击剑手与拳击手尽可虚晃佯攻。橄榄球员「假传一手」（gives the dummy）而毫无愧色。板球场上，投球手因善于掩饰出手动作，反倒更受推重。

战争里，你引对手入歧途的特许更为宽泛。因为战争虽然在某些人看来是桩好运动，却绝非单纯的运动。在运动里，除非依着礼让与正大光明的条款，你谈不上「志在取胜」。谁会以一场侥幸得来的胜利为荣？很久以前，在亨利（Henley）赛船会上，有五六名单桨手报名竞逐「钻石杯」（Diamonds）。其中一位 L——，公认远非赛中诸人所能望其项背。第一轮预赛他抽签对上了牛津的 A——，后者约莫是其余人中之最强。L—— 有一个毛病——一只瞎眼；这毛病常害他划出糟糕的航线。两人划出不到五十码，L—— 便昏头昏脑偏出航线，撞上 A——，把他掀翻落水。赛艇规则写得明白：L—— 这是自己断送了自己。此刻正在水里游泳的 A——，只需抬头望一望裁判的汽艇，举起一只手。对方一点头便算答复，这一轮便是 A—— 的了，而且十有八九，决赛也是他的。A—— 却把目光远远避开裁判，双手按着不动，爬回自己的艇里，对那位悔恨不已的对手说：「先生，就从这儿重新开始？」A—— 一败涂地，此后再不曾那样接近过钻石杯。

===Original===
Of course he was right, the race being sport. He had “loved the game beyond the prize”; he had, like Cyrano, *emporté son panache*; he had seen that in sport the thing to strive for is prowess itself, and not its metallic symbol. But the prize of victory in war is no symbol; it is the thing itself, the real end and aim of all that you do and endure. If A﻿—﻿— had been sculling not for a piece of silversmith’s work but for the righting of a wronged nation or for the reassertion of public right throughout Europe, not only would he have been morally free to take a lucky fluke when he got it: he would not have been morally free to reject it. In war you have to “play to win”﻿—words of sinister import in sport. Pot-hunting, unhonoured in sport, is a duty in war, where the pot is, perhaps, the chance of a free life for your children.

===Chinese===
当然，他是对的，因为那场比赛是运动。他「爱竞赛甚于奖品」；他像西哈诺（Cyrano）一样，「带着羽冠扬长而去」（emporté son panache）；他看清了：在运动中值得奋力以求的是英武本身，而非英武的金属象征。但战争中胜利的奖赏不是象征；它就是事物本身，是你一切所为、一切所忍的真正目的与归宿。倘若 A—— 当日竞逐的不是一件银匠的活计，而是为一个蒙冤的民族伸冤，或为重振全欧洲的公义，那么不仅侥幸到手的好运，他尽可在道义上坦然领受：他甚至在道义上无权拒绝它。在战争中你必须「为打赢而打」——在运动里，这是些带着凶险意味的字眼。专为奖杯而猎（pot-hunting），在运动中不获敬重，在战争中却是本分，何况那奖杯兴许正是你的儿女过上自由生活的机会。

===Original===
Hence your immemorial right to fall on your enemy where he is weak, to start before he is ready, to push him out of the course, to jockey him on to the rails, to use against him all three of Bacon’s recipes for deceiving. A good spy will lie to the last, and in war a prisoner may lie like a saint and hero. With unmistakable glee the Old Testament tells us of Gideon’s excellent practical fib with the crockery and trumpets. Even the Wooden Horse of the Greeks has long ceased to raise moral questions. The pious Aeneas, certainly, called it a foul. But what did he do himself, when he got a good opening? Went, as the Irish say, beyond the beyonds and fought in an enemy uniform. Ruses of war and war lies are as ancient as war itself, and as respectable. The most innocent animals use them; they shammed dead in battle long before Falstaff.

The only new thing about deception in war is modern man’s more perfect means for its practice. The thing has become, in his hand, a trumpet more efficacious than Gideon’s own. When Sinon set out to palm off on the Trojans the false news of a Greek total withdrawal, that first of Intelligence officers made a venture like that of early man, with his flint-headed arrow, accosting a lion. Sinon’s pathetic little armament of yarns, to be slung at his proper peril, was frailer than David’s five stones from the brook. Modern man is far better off. To match the Lewis gun with which he now fires his solids, he has to his hand the newspaper Press, a weapon which fires as fast as the Lewis itself, and is almost as easy to load whenever he needs, in his wars, to let fly at the enemy’s head the thing which is not.

===Chinese===
于是便有了你那自古以来的权利：专挑敌人虚弱之处下手，趁敌人未备而发难，把对手挤出航道，诱他撞上围栏，并把培根（Bacon）所开的三帖骗人方略悉数用在他身上。一个好间谍会撒谎到底；在战争中，一个俘虏尽可以像圣徒与英雄一般撒谎。《旧约》以毫不掩饰的兴头，向我们讲述基甸（Gideon）拿瓶罐与号角做下的那场出色的实战骗局。就连希腊人的木马，也早已不再引出道德问题。虔诚的埃涅阿斯（Aeneas）固然骂过它卑劣。可他自己一旦得了好机会，又干了什么？照爱尔兰人的说法，出到「超出之超出」去了——穿上敌人的军服作战。战争的诡计与战争的谎言，与战争本身一样古老，也一样体面。最无辜的动物也用它们；早在福斯塔夫（Falstaff）之前，它们就在战场上装过死了。

战争中欺骗唯一的新东西，是现代人实践它的手段更为完善。这东西到了他手里，已变成一支比基甸自己的号角更灵验的喇叭。当年西农（Sinon）着手向特洛伊人兜售「希腊人已全军撤离」的假消息时，这位情报军官的祖师爷所冒的险，好比初民揣着一支燧石箭头去迎面招呼一头狮子。西农那点可怜的谎话军械——还得他自己担着风险抛出去——比大卫（David）从溪里捡的五块石子还要单薄。现代人的境况优渥得多。如今他用刘易斯机枪（Lewis gun）发射实弹；与之相配，他手边还有报纸、还有报界（Press）这件兵器——发射起来与刘易斯机枪一样快，装填又几乎同样便当，随时可以在他的战争里朝敌人脑袋，把那「并非如此之事」射将过去。

===Original===
He has this happiness, too: however often he fires, he can, in a sense, never miss. He knows that while he is trying to feed the enemy with whatever it may be bad for him to read the enemy will be trying just as hard to leave no word of it unread. As busily as your enemy’s telescopes will be conning your lines in the field, his Intelligence will be scrutinising whatever is said in your Press, worrying out what it means and which of the things that it seems to let out are the traps and which are the real, the luminous, priceless slips made in unwariness. What the Sphinx was to her clientele, what the sky is to mountain-climbers and sailors, your Press is to him: an endless riddle, to be interrogated and interpreted for dear life. His wits have to be at work on it always. Like a starved rat in a house where rat-poison is laid, he can afford neither to nibble a crumb that has got the virus on it, nor yet to leave uneaten any clean crumb that has fallen accidentally from a table. Do not thrilling possibilities open before you?

> What cannot you and I perform upon
 

 The unguarded Duncan? What not put upon
 

 His spongy officers?

—that is, if Duncan be really unguarded enough to “ravin down his proper bane,” like a dutiful rat, and his officers spongy enough to sop up, according to plan, the medicated stuff that you give them.

===Chinese===
他还有这样一桩福气：无论发射多少回，从某种意义上说，他决不会打空。他知道，正当他竭力喂给敌人一些读了于他有害的东西时，敌人也正同样卖力，务求其中一字一句也不漏读。正如敌人的望远镜将在战地上细细打量你的防线，他的情报部门也将细究你的报界所说的一切，苦苦抠索它的含义，抠索它看似泄露的事情里，哪些是陷阱，哪些又是真正的、发亮的、无价的不慎失漏。斯芬克斯（Sphinx）之于她的问卜者，天空之于登山者与水手，你的报界之于他，也正是如此：一道无穷无尽的谜，要拼着性命去盘问、去解读。他的心智须得时刻在它上面运转。像一座下了鼠药的宅子里的饿鼠，沾了毒液的面包屑他咬不得，可偶然从桌上落下的一粒干净屑，他也不敢留着不吃。在你们面前展开的，难道不是一片令人战栗的可能性么？

> 你我不能在那毫无防备的邓肯
> 身上做到哪一步？又有甚么不能
> 安到他那些海绵似的臣仆头上？

——那就是说，倘若邓肯（Duncan）果真毫无防备，肯像一只尽职的耗子那样「把自己应得的那份毒鸩贪婪吞下」，而他的臣仆们也果真是块够吸的海绵，肯按计划把你给他们的那份药饵蘸吸干净。

### II / 二

===Original===
It is the common habit of nations at war to ascribe to the other side all the cunning, as if the possession of a Ulysses were some sort of discredit. Happily for us our chosen Ulysses in France, at the most critical time, was of the first order. But no soldier can go far ahead of his time; he has to work in it and with it. And so the rich new mine of Intelligence work through the Press was not worked by either side, in the Great War, for all it was worth. Only a few trial borings were made; experimental shafts were sunk into the seam, and good, promising stuff was brought to the top.

Here are a couple of samples. Some readers of popular science, as it is called, may have been shocked to see in a technical journal, rather late in the war, a recklessly full description of our “listening sets”﻿—the apparatus by which an enemy telephone message is overheard in the field. “Why,” they must have thought, “this is giving away one of our subtlest devices for finding out what the enemy is about. The journal ought to be prosecuted.” The article had really come from G.H.Q. It was the last thrust in a long duel.

===Chinese===
交战诸国有个共同的习惯：把一切狡诈都记到对方头上，仿佛家里出一位尤利西斯（Ulysses），倒是件不大光彩的事。所幸我们在法国选中的那位尤利西斯，在最危急的时日里，属头一等的人物。但任何军人都无法远远跑在他时代的前头；他只能在时代之中、借时代行事。于是，通过报界开展情报工作这座富庶的新矿，在大战（the Great War）期间，交战双方谁也不曾开采到它的全部价值。只打了几个试验钻孔；往矿层里下了几口试验竖井，也确实把上好的、大有指望的矿石提到了地面上来。

这里有两件样品。某些所谓通俗科学的读者，见大战后期一家技术期刊上，对我国那些「侦听机」（listening sets）——即在战场上窃听敌方电话的器材——登了一篇无所顾忌的详尽描述，也许大吃一惊。「哎呀，」他们必定这样想，「这是把我们刺探敌情最精微的器具之一给白送出去了。这期刊应当被治罪。」这篇文章其实出自总司令部（G.H.Q.）。它是一场漫长决斗中的最后一剑。

===Original===
When the war opened the Germans had good apparatus for telephonic eavesdropping. We had, as usual, nothing to speak of. The most distinctly traceable result was the annihilation of our first attack at Ovillers, near Albert, early in July 1916. At the instant fixed for the attack our front at the spot was smothered under a bombardment which left us with no men to make it. A few days after when we took Ovillers, we found the piece of paper on which the man with the German “listening set” had put down, word for word, our orders for the first assault. Then we got to work. We drew our own telephones back, and we perfected our own “listening sets” till the enemy drew back his, further and further, giving up more and more of ease and rapidity of communication in order to be safe. At last a point was reached at which he had backed right out of hearing. All hope of pushing him back further still, by proving in practice that we could still overhear, was now gone. All that was left to do was to add the effects of a final bluff to the previous effects of the real strength of our hand. And so there slipped into a rather out-of-the-way English journal the indiscretion by which the reach of our electric ears was, to say the least of it, not understated. Few people in England might notice the article. The enemy could be trusted to do so.

===Chinese===
大战一开打，德国人就有了精良的电话窃听器材。我们呢，一如既往，拿不出什么值得一提的东西。最确凿可考的后果，便是 1916 年 7 月初我军在阿尔贝（Albert）附近奥维莱尔（Ovillers）的第一次进攻遭了歼灭。进攻指定的时刻一到，我军该处前沿便被一通弹幕压得死死的，压得我们再无兵员可以发动进攻。几天之后我们攻下奥维莱尔，找到了那张纸片——操德国「侦听机」的那个人，把我方第一次突击的命令一字不落地记在了上面。于是我们着手赶上去。我们把自家的电话往后撤，又把自家的「侦听机」日臻完善，直到敌人把他的也越撤越远，为了安全，把通讯的安逸与迅捷越弃越多。最后到了这样一步：他已经整个退出了听得见的范围。想靠实战证明我们仍能窃听、从而把他推得更远的一切希望，至此都落了空。剩下的可做之事，只有在我们手中真实力量已然奏效的诸般效果之上，再添一场终局唬人的效果。于是，一家相当偏僻的英文期刊上，悄悄溜进了一篇失慎之文，把我们电动耳朵之所及说得——往轻里讲——决无折扣。英格兰或许没有几个人会留意这篇文章。敌人却尽可放心，必定留意。

===Original===
When the Flanders battle of July 31, 1917, was about to be fought, we employed the old ruse of the Chinese attack. We modernised the trick of medieval garrisons which would make a show of getting ready to break out at one gate when a real sally was to be made from another. The enemy was invited to think that a big attack was at hand. But against Lens, and not east of Ypres. Due circumstantial evidence was provided. There were audible signs that a great concentration of British guns were cautiously registering, west of Lens. A little scuffle on that part of the front elicited from our side an amazing bombardment﻿—apparently loosed in a moment of panic. I fancy a British Staff Officer’s body﻿—to judge by his brassard and tabs﻿—may have floated down the Scarpe into the German lines. Interpreted with German thoroughness, the maps and papers upon it might easily betray the fact that Lens was the objective. And then a really inexcusable indiscretion appeared﻿—just for a moment, and then was hushed up﻿—in the London Press. To an acute German eye it must have been obvious that this composition was just the inconsequent gassing of some typically stupid English General at home on leave; he was clearly throwing his weight about, as they say, without any real understanding of anything. The stuff was of no serious value, except for one parenthetic, accidental allusion to Lens as the mark. As far as I know, this ebullition of babble was printed in only one small edition of one London paper. Authority was then seen to be nervously trying, as Uncle Toby advised, “to wipe it up and say no more about it.” Lest it should not be observed to have taken this wise precaution some fussy member of Parliament may have asked in the House of Commons how so outrageous a breach of soldierly reticence had occurred. And was there no control over the Press? It all answered. The Germans kept their guns in force at Lens, and their counter barrage east of Ypres was so much the lighter, and our losses so much the less.

===Chinese===
1917 年 7 月 31 日佛兰德（Flanders）之战将启之时，我们用了「中国式攻击」（Chinese attack）这条老计策。中世纪守军惯使的花招——装作要从此门突围，真正的出击却从彼门杀出——被我们现代化了一回。我们邀请敌人相信：一场大进攻已迫在眉睫。但是指向朗斯（Lens），而非伊普尔（Ypres）以东。相应的旁证备得妥妥帖帖。有听得见的迹象表明，大批英军火炮正在朗斯以西小心地试射标定。前线那一地段上的一场小摩擦，引得我方回敬出一通惊人的弹幕——显然是慌乱之际失手放出来的。我想，一具英军参谋军官的尸体——凭那臂章与领章判断——多半顺斯卡普河（Scarpe）漂进了德军防线。经德国式的彻底解读，尸身上的地图与文件不难供出朗斯便是进攻目标这一事实。接着，伦敦的报界上又当真冒出一桩无可原谅的失慎——只露了一小会儿，随即被捂了下去。在锐利的德国人眼里，这通文章分明只是某位典型的愚蠢英格兰将军回国休假时信口开河的胡扯；他显然是在，如人们所说，仗着身份耍威风，对任何事情都没有真正的理解。那东西本无甚正经价值，只除一处插在句缝里、偶然把朗斯点作目标的提法。就我所知，这阵饶舌的沸滚，只印在伦敦一家报纸的一小版里。随后便见当局神经紧张地照托比叔叔（Uncle Toby）的劝告行事，「把它擦掉，别再提了」。唯恐人家不曾看见它采取了这项明智的防范，兴许还有哪位好事的议员在下议院发问：如此骇人听闻地违反军人缄默，究竟是怎么发生的？难道对报界竟没有一点管制？这一切全都奏了效。德国人把成建制的火炮留在了朗斯，他们在伊普尔以东的反击弹幕便轻了那么些，我们的损失也少了那么些。

### III / 三

===Original===
If we did these things in the green leaf, what might we not do in the dry? Mobilize our whole Press, conscribe it for active service under a single control, a﻿—let us be frank﻿—a Father-General of Lies, the unshaming strategic and tactical lies of “the great wars” which “make ambition virtue,” and sometimes make mendacity a virtue too? Coach the whole multitudinous orchestra of the Press to carry out the vast conceptions of some consummate conductor, *splendide mendax*? From each instrument under his baton this artist would draw its utmost contributive aid to immense schemes of concerted delusiveness, the harping of the sirens elaborated into Wagnerian prodigies of volume and complexity.

===Chinese===
若说我们枝青叶绿之时尚且行了这些事，到了枝枯叶干之日，还有什么行不出来呢？把我们整个报界动员起来，征它服现役，置于单独一人统辖之下——恕我们直言——一位谎言之总会长（Father-General of Lies），并把「伟大战争」——那「使野心成为美德」、有时也使撒谎成为美德的战争——的那些不知羞耻的战略与战术谎言也一并动员起来？把报界这整支芸芸众生的管弦乐队教练停当，去执行某位造诣极深的指挥的宏大构想，谎得辉煌壮丽（splendide mendax）？这位艺术家将从他棒下的每一件乐器里，汲取其极致的助力，汇入协同惑人的宏图巨案——塞壬们的琴吟，被铺陈为瓦格纳式的音量与繁复的奇观。

===Original===
As you gaze from the top of a tree or a tower behind your own front, in a modern war, all the landscape beyond it looks as if man had perished from the earth, leaving his works behind him. It all looks strangely vacant and dead, the roofs of farms and the spires of churches serving only to deepen your sense of this blank deletion of man, as the Roman arches enhance the vacuous stillness of the Campagna. Your Intelligence Corps has to convert this first impression, this empty page, into a picture, built up line by line, dot by dot, of the universe of activities that are going on out there. Its first and easiest task is to mark out correctly the place where every enemy unit is, each division, each battery, each railhead, aerodrome, field hospital and dump. Next it has to mark each movement of each of these, the shiftings of the various centres of gravity, the changes in the relative density and relative quality of troops and guns at various sectors, the increase, at any sector, of field hospitals, the surest harbingers of heavy attacks. The trains on all lines must be counted, their loads calculated. Next must be known in what sort of spirits the enemy is, in the field and also at home. Do the men believe in their officers? Do the men get confident letters from their civilian friends? Do they send cheerful ones back? Is desertion rare and much abhorred? Or so common that men are no longer shot for it now? So you may go on enumerating until it strikes you that you are simply drifting into an inventory of all the details of the enemy’s wartime life, in the field and at home. And then you understand.

===Chinese===
在现代战争中，从你自家防线后方的一棵树顶或一座塔楼顶上望出去，防线那边的全部景色，看上去仿佛人类已经从大地上绝迹，只把他的作品留在了身后。一切都显得出奇地空寂死灭，农舍的屋顶与教堂的尖顶，只会加深你对「人被空白地删去」之感，一如罗马的拱门反倒加重了坎帕尼亚（Campagna）荒原上那种空茫的岑寂。你的情报部队（Intelligence Corps）必须把这第一印象、这页白纸，转化为一幅图画，一线一线、一点一点，把那边正在进行的整个活动的宇宙搭建出来。它头一件也最容易的一件差事，是准确标出敌方每一个单位的位置：每个师、每个炮队、每个铁路端点、每座机场、每所野战医院、每处补给场。接下来它得标出上述一切的每一次移动：各个重心的挪移，各扇区部队与火炮相对密度、相对素质的变化，任何一地野战医院的增加——那是最可靠的重击先声。所有线路上的火车必须清点，载重必须估算。再接下来，必须知道敌方的士气如何，在前线的与在后方的都是。士兵们信任他们的军官吗？他们收到民间友人满怀信心的来信吗？回信也写得欢畅吗？逃亡是罕见而深遭鄙夷的吗？还是已经多到如今不再枪毙逃兵了？你可以这样一路枚举下去，直到猛然察觉：你简直是在开列一份清单，把敌方战时生活的一切细节、前线的与后方的，悉数网罗在内。于是你恍然大悟。

===Original===
For what you want to know, in order to beat him, is no less than this﻿—to see him steadily and see him whole. In the past we have talked of information “of military value” as distinct from other information. But all information about either side is of military value to the other. News of the outbreak or settlement of a strike in a Welsh coalfield was of military value to Ludendorff. News of the day’s weather in Central Europe was of military value to Sir Douglas Haig. News of anything that expressed in any degree the temper of London or Berlin, of Munich or Manchester, helped to eke out that accurate vision of an enemy’s body and mind which is the basis of success in combat. A black dot, of the size of a pinhead, may seem, when looked at alone, to give no secret away. But when the same dot is seen, no longer in isolation, but as part of a pen-and-ink drawing, perhaps it may leap into vital prominence, showing now as the pupil of the eye that completes a whole portrait, gives its expression to a face and identifies a sitter.

Throughout the Great War our own Press and that of the Germans were each pouring out, for the undesigned benefit of their enemy, substantially correct descriptions of everything in the war life of their respective nations, except a few formal military and naval secrets specially reserved by the censors. Each nation fought, on the whole, with the other standing well out in the light, with no inscrutability about its countenance. If we were ever again in such risk of our national life, would we not seriously try to make ourselves an enigma? Or would we leave this, as we have left some other refinements of war, to the other side to introduce first?

===Chinese===
因为你要想击败他，所需要知道的恰恰不少于此——要定睛看他，也要通体看他。过去我们谈「有军事价值」的情报，是把它同其他情报区别开的。但关于任何一方的全部情报，对另一方都有军事价值。威尔士某煤田罢工爆发或平息的消息，对鲁登道夫（Ludendorff）有军事价值。中欧当日天气的消息，对道格拉斯·黑格爵士（Sir Douglas Haig）有军事价值。任何多少表达出伦敦或柏林、慕尼黑或曼彻斯特之情绪的消息，都有助于补足对敌人身与心两面的精确察视——那正是作战成功的基础。一粒针头大小的黑点，孤立地看，似乎不曾泄露任何秘密。可当同一个黑点不再孤立、而是作为一幅钢笔画的一部分被看见时，它或许会一跃而入攸关的显要：此刻它显形为一枚瞳仁，补全了整幅肖像，为一张面孔赋予神情，也认出了入画的那位坐者。

整个大战期间，我们自己的报界与德国人的报界，都在为对方非出本意的利益，倾泻着对各自民族战时生活一切事物大体准确的描摹，唯有审查官们特意保留的寥寥几项正式陆海军秘密除外。两个民族交起战来，大体上都是让对方好好地站在光天化日之下，眉眼间毫无莫测高深之色。倘若我们再有一次陷于民族生死存亡之危，我们难道不会认真下一番功夫，把自己变成一个谜？还是说，我们要把这一手——像我们已经把其他几样战争的精微改良那样——留给对方先来引进？

### IV / 四

===Original===
Suppose us again at war with a Power less strong at sea than ourselves. If we should want its fleet to come out and fight in the open, why not evoke, some fine morning, from every voice in our daily press, a sudden and seemingly irrepressible cry of grief and rage over the unconcealable news﻿—the Censor might be defied by the way﻿—that our Grand Fleet, while ranging the seas, had struck a whole school of drift mines and lost half its numbers? Strategic camouflage, however, would go far beyond such special means to special ends as that. It would, as a regular thing, derange the whole landscape presented to enemy eyes by our Press. There was in the war a French aerodrome across which the French camouflage painters had simply painted a great white high-road: it ran across hangars, huts, turf, everything; and everything was amazingly obliterated by it. Across our real life, as seen under the noonday rays of publicity in ordinary times, the supreme controller might draw some such enormous lines of falsification.

===Chinese===
且假设我们再度与一个海上力量逊于我们的强国交战。倘若我们想要它的舰队出来在开阔海面上打一仗，何不在某个晴好的早晨，从我们日报的每一张口中，唤起一阵骤发的、仿佛再难按捺的悲愤呼号，哭喊那条瞒不住的消息——审查官嘛，不妨顺带冲撞一下——说我们的大舰队（Grand Fleet）巡弋四海之时，撞上了整整一群漂雷（drift mines），折损了半数舰只？不过，战略伪装（strategic camouflage）会远远超出这类为一时一地之目的而设的特殊手段。作为一种常规，它会把我们的报界呈现在敌人眼前的整幅风景统统搅乱。大战期间有一座法国机场，法国的伪装画家们索性横穿它画了一条白色大道：大道穿过机库、棚屋、草皮，穿过一切；一切也被它惊人地抹去了。在我们的真实生活之上——那在平时正午公开性的光线下被看见的生活——那位至高的统制者，也可以画下几条这样巨大的伪证线条。

===Original===
Most of the fibs that we used in the war were mere nothings, and clumsy at that. When the enemy raided our trenches in the dead winter season, took fifty prisoners, and did as he liked for a while﻿—so much as he liked that a court of inquiry was afterwards held and a colonel deprived of his command﻿—we said in our official communiqué that a hostile raiding party had “entered our trenches” but was “speedily driven out, leaving a number of dead.” When civilian morale at home was going through one of its occasional depressions, we gave out that it was higher than ever. We did not officially summon from the vasty deep the myth about Russian soldiers in England. But when it arose out of nothing we did make some use of it. These were, however, little more than bare admissions of the principle that truthfulness in war is not imperative. Falsification was tried, but it was not “tried out.” Like really long-range guns, the kindred of “Bertha,” it came into use only enough to suggest what another world-war might be. *Vidimus tantum.* And then the war ended.

===Chinese===
我们在大战中用过的那些小谎，多半不过是些无足轻重的东西，而且笨手笨脚。敌人在死寂的冬季袭击了我们的战壕，掳走五十名俘虏，一时为所欲为——所欲为到那个地步，以致事后开了一场调查法庭，一位上校被褫夺了指挥权——而我们官方公报里的说法是：一支敌方袭击队「进入了我们的战壕」，但「旋即被逐出，遗尸若干」。国内民气正经历它周期性的一次低落时，我们发布说民气空前高涨。「俄国兵在英国」的神话，我们没有正式把它从幽冥深水（the vasty deep）里召唤上来。可它无中生有地冒出来之后，我们也确曾拿它派过些用场。然而这些都不过是刚刚够承认那条原则：战争之中，诚实并非当尽的义务。伪造是试用过了，却不算「试用到家」。像真正的远程巨炮、像「贝尔塔」（Bertha）们的同族，它投入使用的程度，只够让人窥见另一场世界大战可能是什么模样。我们不过见了见而已（Vidimus tantum）。然后，战争就结束了。

===Original===
Under a perfected propaganda system the whole surface presented by a country’s Press to the enemy’s Intelligence would be a kind of painted canvas. The artist would not merely be reticent about the positions, say, of our great training camps. He would create, by indirect evidence, great dummy training camps. In the field we had plenty of dummy aerodromes, with hangars complete and a few dummy machines sprawling outside, to draw enemy bomb-fire. At home we would have dummy Salisbury Plains to which a guarded allusion would peep out here and there while the new unity of command over the Press would delete the minutest clue to the realities. Episodes like that of the famous Lansdowne letter would not be left for nature to bungle. If at any time such an episode seemed likely to touch any diplomatic spring with good strategic effect, it would happen at that moment and no other. Otherwise it would not happen, so far as any trace of it in the Press could betray. By-elections, again, their course and result, may tell an enemy much of what your people are thinking. But, for military purposes, there is always some particular thing which you want him to believe them to be thinking. So you would not leave it to the capricious chances of an actual election to settle whether he should be led to believe this or not. You would see to it. Just as you camouflage your real guns and expose dummy guns, so you would obliterate from the Press all trace of your real elections and offer to view, at the times that best suited, dummy elections, ad hoc elections, complete in all their parts.

===Chinese===
在一套完善的宣传（propaganda）制度之下，一国报界呈现在敌人情报部门面前的整个表面，将是一幅画就的画布。这位画家不会仅仅对我国各大训练营地之类的位置保持缄默。他会用间接的证据，创造出巨大的假训练营地。在战场上，我们有过的是大批假机场，机库一应俱全，外面歪歪斜斜趴着几架假飞机，专引敌人的炸弹。在国内，我们将拥有几座假的索尔兹伯里平原（Salisbury Plain），关于它们的守得住分寸的隐指，会在各处露一露头，而报界的新统一指挥，会把通向真实的最细小的线索删个干净。像那封著名的兰斯多恩信函（Lansdowne letter）之类的事件，将不会被放任天性去笨手笨脚地摆布。任何时候，只要这样一桩事件看来有望以良好的战略效果触动某一根外交发条，它就会在那一刻、且只在那一刻发生。否则它便不会发生——只要报界里能泄露它行踪的任何痕迹都不存在。再说补缺选举（by-elections），它的进程与结果，能把你的国民在想些什么告诉敌人许多。但为军事计，总有那么一件特定的事，是你想要他相信他们在想的。所以你不会把「他是否该被引导着相信这一点」，交给一场真实选举那变化无常的偶然去裁决。你会亲自料理妥当。正如你伪装真炮而暴露假炮，你也会从报界上抹去你真实选举的一切痕迹，并在最合宜的时刻，端出假选举来供人观看——临时拼制的选举，各部件一应俱全。

===Original===
We have imagined a case in which it would be our interest to raise false confidence in the enemy, perhaps to draw a hurried attack on our shores at a time of our own choosing. Then, if the whole of our Press is held in our hand like a fiddle, ready to take and give out any tune, what should prevent us from letting fall, in sudden distress, a hundred doleful, forced admissions that the strain has proved too great, the smash has come, the head of the State is in hiding from his troops, the Premier in flight, naval officers hanging from modern equivalents to the yardarm, Ministers and Commanders-in-Chief shaking their fists in one another’s faces? Or take the opposite case, that you mean to attack in force, in the field. Here you would add to the preliminary bombardment of your guns such a bombardment of assertion and insinuation, not disprovable before “zero” hour, as has never yet been essayed; plausible proofs from neutral quarters that the enemy’s troops are being betrayed by their politicians behind, that typhus has broken out among the men’s homes, that their children are dying like flies, and some of the mothers, insane with famine and grief, are eating the dead in hope of nursing the living. Oh, you could say a great deal.

And you could deliver your messages, too. The enemy’s command might try to keep the contents of your Press from reaching his troops. But, thanks to the aeroplane, you can circularize the enemy’s troops almost as easily as traders can canvass custom at home. You can flood his front line with leaflets, speeches, promises, rumours, and caricatures. You can megaphone to it. Only in recent years has human ingenuity thought of converting the older and tamer form of political strife into the pandemonic “stunt” of a “whirlwind election.” Shall war not have her whirlwind canvasses no less renowned than those of peace? Some rather shamefaced passages of love there have been between us and the Rumour of Shakespeare, the person “painted full of tongues,” who “stuffs the ears of men with false reports,” to the advantage of her wooers. Why not espouse the good lady right out? Make an honest woman of her?

===Chinese===
我们已经设想过一种情形：让敌人升起虚假的信心，或许还把他一记仓促的进攻诱到我们海岸上来，时机由我们自己挑。那时，倘若我们整个报界像一把提琴似的握在手里，随时可以接进任何曲调、再把它们放送出去，还有什么能拦住我们，不在一阵骤发的哀痛中，撒下一百句凄凉的、被迫吐出的招认：撑不住了，崩溃临头了，国家元首躲开自己的军队藏起来了，首相出逃了，海军军官吊上了桅桁（yardarm）的现代等价物，各位大臣与各位总司令（Commanders-in-Chief）正互相指着对方的脸挥拳？再举相反的情形：你打算在战场上以重兵强攻。这时，除火炮的预备弹幕之外，你还要加上一场断言与影射的弹幕——在「零时」（zero hour）之前无从驳斥——规模为前所未有：来自中立方面、煞有介事的证明，说敌军正被他们后方的政客出卖，说斑疹伤寒已在士兵们家中爆发，说他们的孩子正像苍蝇一样成片死去，说有些做母亲的，被饥馑与悲伤逼疯了，正在吃死人，指望以此哺养活着的。

而且，你的信也递得出去。敌人的统帅部可以设法不让你的报界内容传到他的部队耳中。但托飞机的福，你向敌军部队散发传单，几乎同商贩在国内兜揽生意一样容易。你可以用传单、演说、许诺、谣言与讽刺漫画灌满他的前线。你可以对它开动扩音器。也只是近年来，人类的机巧才想到，把政治争斗那种较古老、较温驯的形式，改造成「旋风式选举」这般群魔乱舞的「绝招」。难道战争就不配有她的旋风式拉票，名声不亚于和平时期的那些吗？我们与莎士比亚（Shakespeare）笔下的「传闻」（Rumour）——那位「周身画满舌头」、专「用虚假的报道塞满世人的耳朵」、好叫她的追求者得利的人物——之间，本已有过几段相当害臊的情缘。何不索性把这位好女士明媒正娶？替她正一正名分？

### V / 五

===Original===
Perhaps you would shrink back. Perhaps at any rate you do so now, when for the moment this great implement is not being offered to you, to take or leave, at an instant crisis of your country’s fate. You feel that even in such a case you would stand loftily aloof in your cold purity? You would disclaim as a low, unknightly business the uttering of such base coinage as cooked news, whatever your proud chastity may cost anyone else? Or arrive, perhaps, at the same result by a different route, and make out to yourself that really it pays, in the end, to be decent; that clean chivalry is a good investment at bottom, and that a nation of Galahads and Bayards is sure to come out on top, on the canny reckoning that the body housing a pure heart has got the strength of ten? That is one possible course. And the other is to accept, with all that it implies, the doctrine that there is one morality for peace and another morality for war; that just as in war you may with the clearest conscience stab a man in the back, or kick him in the bowels, in spite of all the sportsmanship you learnt at school, so you may stainlessly carry deception to lengths which in peace would get you blackballed at a club and cut by your friends.

===Chinese===
也许你会畏缩后退。至少眼下你是畏缩的，此刻这件大器械并没有递到你手里，任你在民族命运危急存亡的一瞬取用或弃置。你觉得自己纵然身处那样的境地，也依旧会带着一身冰清的高洁，昂然独立于事外？你会申明：烹制出来的新闻（cooked news）这等劣质伪币，铸出来、发出去是一桩低贱而不合骑士风度的营生——无论你这份骄傲的贞节要让别的什么人付出代价？又或者，你会绕另一条道抵达同一个结论，暗自盘算：说到底，行正派终究是划算的；干净的骑士精神（chivalry）本质上是桩好投资，一个由加拉哈德（Galahad）们与巴亚尔（Bayard）们组成的民族，精明地盘算着「栖着一颗纯洁之心的身体，自有着以一当十的力气」，笃定要拔得头筹？这是一种可能的走法。另一种则是连它牵连的一切一并无保留地接受，接受那套教义：和平有一种道德，战争有另一种道德；正如在战争中，你可以怀着最清白的良心从背后捅人一刀，或朝人小腹踹上一脚——全不顾你在学校里学来的那一套运动家风度——你同样可以一尘不染地把欺骗推进到那样的地步：在和平时期，那会让你在俱乐部里被投黑球拒之门外，被朋友们断绝往来。

===Original===
It may be too much to hope that, whichever of these two paths we may choose, we shall tread it with a will. We have failed so much in the way of what Germany used to call “halfness,” the fault of Macbeth, the wish to hunt with the hounds while we run with the hare, that it would be strange if we did not still try to play Bayard and Ulysses as one man and succeed in combining the shortcomings of an inefficient serpent with those of a sophisticated dove. If we really went the whole serpent the first day of any new war would see a wide, opaque veil of false news drawn over the whole face of our country. Authority playing on all the keys, white and black, of the Press as upon one piano, would give the listening enemy the queerest of Ariel’s tunes to follow. All that we did, all that we thought, would be bafflingly falsified. The whole landscape of life in this island, as it reflects itself in the waters of the Press, would come out suddenly altered as far past recognition as that physical landscape amid which it is passed has been changed by a million years of sunshine, rain, and frost. The whole sky would be darkened with flights of strategic and tactical lies so dense that the enemy would fight in a veritable “fog of war” darker than London’s own November brews, and the world would feel that not only the Angel of Death was abroad, but the Angel of Delusion too, and would almost hear the beating of two pairs of wings.

===Chinese===
至于说无论拣了这两条路中的哪一条，我们都会起劲地走到底——这恐怕是奢望。我们在德国人旧称「半吊子」（halfness）的那种毛病上栽过太多跟头——那是麦克白（Macbeth）的毛病，是一面跟着猎犬追、一面又随着野兔跑的心愿——倘若我们如今还不曾想着把巴亚尔与尤利西斯一人兼演，并且当真把一条不中用的蛇与一只老于世故的鸽子的短处集于一身，那倒怪了。倘若我们真的一蛇到底，那么任何一场新战争的头一天，便会看见一幅又宽又不透光的假新闻之幕，罩上我们整个国家的面孔。当局在报界这台钢琴上，白键黑键一齐按下去，给侧耳倾听的敌人奏出爱丽儿（Ariel）曲子里最古怪难追的调子。我们的一切所行、一切所思，都将被伪造得扑朔迷离。此岛人生风景的全部倒影——那映在报界水面上的倒影——将骤然改容，改到远非所能辨认，一如生活于其间度过、映着这倒影的那片自然风景，经百万年的日晒、雨水与霜冻而改容。整个天空将被战略与战术谎言的密集群飞遮得昏黑，敌人将在一场名副其实的「战争之雾」里作战——比伦敦自家十一月酿出的浓雾还黑——而世界也会感到：出巡的不止死亡天使一位，还有迷幻天使，而且几乎听得见两对翅膀的鼓动。

### VI / 六

===Original===
Well﻿—and then? Any weapon you use in a war leaves some bill to be settled in peace, and the Propaganda arm has its cost like another. To say so is not to say, without more ado, that it should not be used. Its cost should be duly cast up, like our other accounts; that is all. We all agree﻿—with a certain demur from the Quakers﻿—that one morality has to be practised in peace and another in war; that the same bodily act may be wrong in the one and right in the other. So, to be perfect, you need to have two gears to your morals, and drive on the one gear in war and on the other in peace. While you are on the peace gear you must not even shoot a bird sitting. At the last stroke of some August midnight you clap on the war gear and thenceforth you may shoot a man sitting or sleeping or any way you can get him, provided you and he be soldiers on opposite sides.

Now, in a well-made car, in the prime of its life, there is nothing to keep you from passing straight and conclusively from one gear to another. The change once made, the new gear continues in force and does not wobble back fitfully and incalculably into the old. But in matters of conduct you cannot, somehow, drive long on one gear without letting the other become noticeably rusty, stiff, and disinclined to act. It was found in the Great War that after a long period of peace and general saturation with peace morals it took some time to release the average English youth from his indurated distaste for stabbing men in the bowels. Conversely it has been found of late, in Ireland and elsewhere, that, after some years of effort to get our youths off the no-homicide gear, they cannot all be got quickly back to it either, some of them still being prone to kill, as the French say, *paisiblement*, with a lightness of heart that embarrasses statesmen.

===Chinese===
好吧——然后呢？你在战争里动用的任何武器，都会留下一笔账，留待和平时期来清算；宣传（Propaganda）这一兵种，也同别的兵种一样，有它的代价。这样说，并不等于马上断言它不该动用。它的代价应该照实折算，同我们别的账目一样；如此而已。我们都同意——贵格会（Quakers）教友们稍作了一点保留——和平时期须践行一种道德，战争时期须践行另一种道德；同一个身体行为，在彼为错，在此为对。所以，要做到完美，你的道德就得配上两个档位，战争挂一个档开，和平挂另一个档开。挂在和平档上时，你连栖在枝头不肯飞的鸟都不许射。待到某个八月午夜的最后一响钟声，你咔哒一声挂上战争档，从此你尽可以射杀一个坐着的人、睡着的人，或以任何抓得到他的方式射杀，只要你与他是对立两军的士兵。

而在一辆制造精良、正当盛年的汽车里，没有什么妨碍你干脆利落、一锤定音地从一档换入另一档。换档一旦完成，新档便持续生效，不会一阵一阵、没个准头地晃回旧档。可在行为这档子事上，不知怎么，你在一个档上开得久了，就免不了让另一个档明显生锈、发僵、不肯咬合。大战中人们发现：经过漫长的和平时期、经过和平道德的普遍浸泡之后，要解开普通英格兰青年对捅人小腹那种根深蒂固的憎恶，得花上些时日。反过来，晚近在爱尔兰等地又发现：经过几年的努力，把我们的青年从「不杀人」的档上摘下来之后，他们也未必都能麻利地挂回去，其中有些人照旧动辄杀人——照法国人的说法，「平平静静地」（paisiblement）杀人——那份心头的轻松，很叫政治家们难堪。

===Original===
We must, to be on the conservative side, assume that the same phenomenon would attend a postwar effort to bring back to the truth gear of peace a Press that we had driven for some years on the war gear of untruthfulness. Indeed, we are not wholly left to assumption and speculation. During the war the art of Propaganda was little more than born. The various inspired articles-with-a-purpose, military or political, hardly went beyond the *vagitus*, the earliest cry of the newborn method, as yet

> An infant crying in the night,
 

 And with no language but a cry.

===Chinese===
出于从保守处着想，我们必须假定：同一现象也会跟来看战后这样一番努力——把一部我们在不诚实的战争档上开过几年的报界，重新挂回和平的诚实之档。何况我们也并非全凭假定与推想。战争期间，宣传这门艺术充其量只是呱呱坠地。各种授意而发的带任务文章，军事的也罢，政治的也罢，几乎没有超出这新生方法的初啼（vagitus）——此刻它还是

> 一个婴儿在夜里啼哭，
> 除了啼哭，没有别的语言。

===Original===
Yet for more than three years since the Armistice our rulers have continued to issue to the Press, at our cost as Blue Books and White Papers, long passages of argument and suggestion almost fantastically different from the dry and dignified official publications of the prewar days. English people used to feel a sovereign contempt for the “semiofficial” journalism of Germany and Russia. But the war has left us with a Press at any rate intermittently inspired. What would be left by a war in which Propaganda had come of age and the State had used the Press, as camouflaging material, for all it was worth?

===Chinese===
然而停战以来三年多，我们的统治者们仍在继续向报界发布——以蓝皮书（Blue Books）与白皮书（White Papers）的形式、花着我们的钱——长篇大论的论证与暗示，同战前那些干燥而庄重的官方出版物相比，几乎有着离奇的差异。英格兰人从前对德国与俄国的「半官方」新闻业，怀有一种至高无上的轻蔑。可这场战争给我们留下的，是一份至少间歇接受授意的报界。倘若打的是那样一场战争——宣传已经成年，国家已经把报界当作伪装材料、用足了它的全部价值——那又会留下什么呢？

===Original===
It used at one time to be a great joke﻿—and a source of gain sometimes﻿—among little boys to take it as a benign moral law that so long as you said a thing “over the left,” it did not matter whether it was true or not. If, to gain your private ends, or to make a fool of somebody else, you wanted to utter a fib, all that you had to do was to append to it these three incantatory words, under your breath, or indeed without any sound or move of your lips at all, but just to yourself in the session of sweet silent thought. Then you were blameless. You had cut yourself free, under the rules, from the vulgar morality. War confers on those who wage it much the same self-dispensing power. They can absolve themselves of a good many sins. Persuade yourself that you are at war with somebody else and you find your moral liberty expanding almost faster than you can use it. An Irishman in a fury with England says to himself “State of war﻿—that’s what it is,” and then finds he can go out and shoot a passing policeman from behind a hedge without the discomfort of feeling base. The policeman’s comrades say to themselves “State of war﻿—that’s what it has come to,” and go out and burn some other Irishman’s shop without a sense of doing anything wrong, either. They all do it “over the left.” They have stolen the key of the magical garden wherein you may do things that are elsewhere most wicked and yet enjoy the mental peace of the soldier which passeth all understanding.

===Chinese===
曾经有个时期，小男孩们中间流行着一桩大大的笑谈——有时还是一桩财源——他们把这样一条奉为慈祥的道德律：只要说一件事时「往左边去」（over the left），它是真是假便全不要紧。倘若为了谋你私人的目的，或者为了把别人耍弄一回，你想撒个小谎，你只消在这句话后面缀上那三个咒语般的字——压着嗓子说，或者干脆嘴唇不动、不出一点声响，只在「甜美无声的静思」里对自己默念。于是你便清白无罪了。你已照规则把自己从庸众的道德上割了下来。战争也把这大致相同的一套自我豁免权，授予那些进行战争的人。他们可以赦免自己的许许多多罪过。你只须说服自己正在同什么人交战，便发现你的道德自由膨胀起来，快得几乎叫你来不及享用。一个对英格兰狂怒的爱尔兰人对自己说：「这是战争状态——就是这么回事。」随即发现，他可以出门去，躲在一道树篱后面朝一个路过的警察开枪，而不带一点儿自觉卑鄙的不适。警察的同僚们对自己说：「这是战争状态——已经到这一步了。」随即也出门去，烧掉另一个爱尔兰人的铺子，同样不觉着做错了什么。他们全都是「往左边去」地干着的。他们偷走了那座魔法花园的钥匙——在园子里，你可以做出在别处最最邪恶的事，却仍享有士兵们那种「超乎一切理解」的心境安宁。

===Original===
To kill and to burn may be sore temptations at times, but not so besetting to most men as the temptation to lie is to public speakers and writers. Another frequent temptation of theirs is to live in a world of stale figures of speech, of flags nailed to the mast, of standing to one’s guns, of deaths in last ditches, of quarter neither asked nor given. It is their hobby to figure their own secure, squabblesome lives in images taken from war. And their little excesses, their breaches of manners, and even, sometimes, of actual law, are excused, as a rule, in terms of virile disdain for anything less drastic and stern than the morals of the real warfare which they know so little. We have to think in what state we might leave these weak brethren after a long war in which we had practised them hard in lying for the public good and also in telling themselves it was all right because of the existence of a state of war. State of war! Why, that is what every excitable politician or journalist declares to exist all the time. To the wild party man the party which he hates is always “more deadly than any foreign enemy.” All of us could mention a few politicians, at least, to whom the Great War was merely a passing incident or momentary interruption of the more burningly authentic wars of Irish Orange and Green, or of English Labour and Capital.

===Chinese===
杀戮与纵火，有时或许是揪心的诱惑，但对大多数人来说，总不像说谎之于公众演说家与作家那样缠人不放。他们的另一桩常来纠缠的诱惑，是住在一个陈腐辞藻的世界里：钉死在桅杆上的旗，死也不弃的炮位，末路壕沟里的死战，不求也不给的宽宥。用取自战争的意象，来描画他们自己安稳而好吵闹的生活，是他们的癖好。而他们那些小小的过火，那些失礼之举，有时甚至真正犯法之举，照例都得到了宽谅——宽谅的名义，是一种男子气概的轻蔑：轻蔑一切不及真正战争之道德那样酷烈严峻的东西——对那真正的战争，他们其实知道得那样少。我们不得不想一想：经过一场漫长的战争——其间我们狠狠地操练过他们，教他们为公益撒谎，也教他们告诉自己：因为存在着战争状态，一切都好——我们会把这些软弱的弟兄留在什么境地？战争状态！嗐，这可是每一位容易激动的政客或记者、时时刻刻都宣告存在的东西。在狂热的党人眼里，他所憎恨的那个党，永远「比任何外国敌人都更致命」。我们人人至少都举得出几位政客来：对他们来说，这场大战不过是爱尔兰「奥兰治与绿带」（Orange and Green）之战、或英格兰「劳与资」之战那些烧得更旺、更地道的战争里，一段路过的小插曲、一瞬间的中断罢了。

### VII / 七

===Original===
Under the new dispensation we should have to appoint on the declaration of war, if we had not done it already, a large Staff Department of Press Camouflage. Everything is done best by those who have practised it longest. The best inventors and disseminators of what was untrue in our hour of need would be those who had made its manufacture and sale their trade in our hours of ease. The most disreputable of successful journalists and “publicity experts” would naturally man the upper grades of the war staff. The reputable journalists would labour under them, trying their best to conform, as you say in drill, to the movements of the front rank. For in this new warfare the journalist untruthful from previous habit and training would have just that advantage over the journalist of character which the Regular soldier had over the New Army officer or man in the old. He would be, as Mr. Kipling sings,

> A man that’s too good to be lost you,
 

 A man that is ’andled and made,
 

 A man that will pay what ’e cost you
 

 In learnin’ the others their trade.

===Chinese===
在那新制度之下，我们一宣战就得——倘若先前还没办的话——设立一个庞大的参谋部（Staff）部门：报界伪装部（Press Camouflage）。一切事情，总是操练最久的人做得最好。危难时刻替我们编造与散播不实之词的最好手笔，自然就是承平岁月里把这门制造与贩卖当营生干的那批人。功成名就的新闻记者与「宣传专家」里最声名狼藉的那些，自然会把战时参谋部的高阶位置坐满。声名清白的记者们将在他们手下效力，尽力照操练口令所说的那样，「向前排看齐」。因为在这一新式战争里，素来习惯与训练都不诚实的记者，比起有品格的记者来，恰有着旧日正规军（Regular）士兵之于新军（New Army）军官或士兵的那种优势。他正如吉卜林先生（Mr. Kipling）所唱的——

> 一个汉子，好得叫你不忍丢弃，
> 一个汉子，由你调教、由你造就，
> 一个汉子，对得起你花下的本钱，
> 在教旁人学他那一行的时候。

===Original===
After the war was over he would return to his trade with an immense accession of credit. He would have been decorated and publicly praised and thanked. Having a readier pen than the mere combatant soldiers, he would probably write a book to explain that the country had really been saved by himself, though the fighting men were, no doubt, gallant fellows. He would, in all likelihood, have completed the disengagement of his mind from the idea that public opinion is a thing to be dealt with by argument and persuasion, appeals to reason and conscience. He would feel surer than ever that men’s and women’s minds are most strongly moved not by the leading articles of a paper but by its news, by what they may be led to accept as “the facts.” So the practice of colouring news, of ordering reporters to take care that they see only such facts as tell in one way, would leap forward. For it would have the potent support of a new moral complacency. When a man feels that his tampering with truth has saved civilization, why should he deny himself, in his private business, the benefit of such moral reflections as this feeling may suggest?

===Chinese===
战争结束之后，他将带着极大地增殖的信誉回到他的本行。他将得过勋章，受过公开的表彰与感谢。他手上那支笔比单纯作战的军人们利落，多半还会写一本书，解释说这个国家其实是他救下来的，虽然那些打仗的人无疑都是勇猛的汉子。他多半还会把心里最后一个念头也解除干净——那个「舆论是要靠论证与劝说、靠对理性与良心的吁求来对付的东西」的念头。他会比从前任何时候都更确信：男男女女的心智，最受撼动的不是一家报纸的社论，而是它的新闻，是他们可以被引导着当作「事实」接受下来的那些东西。于是，给新闻上色的做法——吩咐记者们务求只看见朝一个方向说话的事实——将一跃而前。因为它将得到一种新式道德自满的强力支撑。一个人既然觉得正是自己在真相上动的手脚拯救了文明，那么在私家生意里，他又何必拒绝享用这种感觉所能提示的那类道德省思的好处呢？

===Original===
Scott gives, in *Woodstock*, an engaging picture of the man who has “attained the pitch of believing himself above ordinances.” The independent trooper, Tomkins, finds his own favourite vices fitting delightfully into an exalted theory of moral freedom. In former days, he avows, he had been only “the most wild, malignant rakehell in Oxfordshire.” Now he is a saint, and can say to the girl whom he wants to debauch:

> Stand up, foolish maiden, and listen; and know, in one word, that sin, for which the spirit of man is punished with the vengeance of heaven, lieth not in the corporal act, but in the thought of the sinner. Believe, lovely Phoebe, that to the pure all acts are pure, and that sin is in our thought, not in our actions, even as the radiance of the day is dark to a blind man but seen and enjoyed by him whose eyes receive it. To him who is but a novice in the things of the spirit much is enjoined, much is prohibited; and he is fed with milk fit for babes﻿—for him are ordinances, prohibitions, and commands. But the saint is above all these ordinances and restraints. To him, as to the chosen child of the house, is given the passkey to open all locks which withhold him from the enjoyment of his heart’s desire. Into such pleasant paths will I guide thee, lovely Phoebe, as shall unite in joy, in innocent freedom, pleasures which, to the unprivileged, are sinful and prohibited.

===Chinese===
司各特（Scott）在《伍德斯托克》（Woodstock）里，给那种「已经爬到了相信自己高居典章之上」的人，画了一幅引人入胜的画像。独立骑兵汤姆金斯（Tomkins）发现，自己心爱的种种恶癖，恰好舒舒服服地嵌进了一套崇高的道德自由论。往日里，他自陈，他不过是「牛津郡最狂野、最恶毒的浪荡子」。如今他是圣徒了，可以对那位他要诱奸的姑娘说：

> 起来，愚蠢的姑娘，站着听；须知，一言以蔽之：使人的灵魂遭受天谴报罚的罪，不在身体的作为，而在罪人的心思。相信吧，美丽的菲比（Phoebe）：对纯洁的人，一切作为都是纯洁的；罪在我们的心思，不在我们的行动，正如白昼的光辉，于盲人是黑暗，于那睁眼承受它的眼睛，却被看见、被受用。对那在灵性之事上不过是个新手的人，许多事受吩咐，许多事被禁止；他吃的是婴儿当吃的奶——典章、禁令与诫命，是给他定的。但圣徒超越这一切典章与约束。如同家中特选的孩子，一把总钥匙交到了他手里，去开一切拦阻他享受心头之欲的锁。我要引你走进的，正是这样几条宜人的幽径，美丽的菲比：它们将在欢乐里、在天真的自由里，把那些对无特权者而言有罪而遭禁的欢愉，合为一体。

===Original===
So when a journalist with no strong original predisposition to swear to his own hurt shall have gained high public distinction by his fertility in falsehoods for consumption by an enemy in the field, the fishes that tipple in the deep may well “know no such liberty” as this expert in fiction will allow himself when restored to his own more intoxicating element.

The general addition of prestige to the controversial device of giving false impressions and raising false issues would naturally be immense. To argue any case merely on its merits and on the facts would seem to the admirers of the new way a kind of virtuous imbecility. In what great industrial dispute or political campaign, in what struggle between great financial interests, would both sides, or either, forego the use of munitions so formidable? Such conflicts might almost wholly cease to be competitions in serious argument at all; they might become merely trials of skill in fantastic false pretences, and of expertness in the morbid psychology of credulity.

===Chinese===
这样，当一位原本并无多少「宁损己身亦守其誓」之癖的记者，靠着他向战场上的敌人倾销谎言的丰产、挣得了崇高的公众勋名之后，深海里啜饮的鱼儿们，恐怕也「不识这样的自由」——不识这位虚构行当的行家，重新回到他自家那更醉人的元素里时，将要给自己放任的那种自由。

制造假象、挑起假议题这套论战器械，其威望的普遍增殖，自然会是巨大的。在新路子的仰慕者们看来，只就事论事、只凭事实去为任何主张辩护，简直成了一种美德型的低能。哪一场重大的产业纠纷或政治竞选里，哪一场大金融利益集团之间的角力里，双方或任何一方，肯放弃动用这样威猛的军械？这类冲突几乎可以完全不再是认真说理的较量；它们可能沦为单纯的技艺比试：比谁更擅长异想天开的巧诈，比谁更精于轻信那门病态的心理学。

===Original===
So men argued, surmised and predicted, talking and talking away in the endless hours that war gives for talking things out. When first they began to ask each other why so many lies were about, the common hypothesis, based on prior experience, was that they must be meant to save some “dud,” up above, from losing his job. Then they came to admit there was something more in it than that. Lies had a good enough use for fooling the Germans. A beastly expedient, no doubt; acquiescence in lying does not come quite so easily to a workman of good character as it does to men of a class in which more numerous formal fibs are kept in use as social conveniences. Still, the men were not cranks enough to object. “They love not poison that do poison need.” The men had hated, and still continued to hate, the use of poison gas, too. It was a scrub’s trick, like vitriol-throwing. But who could have done without it, when once the Germans began? And now who could object to the use of this printed gas either? Could they, in this new warfare of propaganda, expect their country to go into action armed in a white robe of candour, and nothing besides, like a maskless man going forth to war against a host assisted by phosgene and all her foul sisters?

===Chinese===
士兵们就这样争论、揣测与预言，在战争赐下的、够把事情谈个透的无尽钟点里谈啊谈啊。当初他们刚开始彼此打听，为什么四处尽是谎言时，根据先前经验得出的通行假设是：这些谎言，必定是想保住上头哪位「哑弹」（dud）先生的差事。后来他们渐渐承认，里头还有比这更多的名堂。谎言拿去愚弄德国人，是个够好的用场。一条可憎的权宜，无疑；对撒谎的默许，在一个品格端方的工人那里，来得不像在另一阶级的男人们那里那样轻易——那一阶级把数量更多的客套小谎，当作社交的便利经常备用着。尽管如此，士兵们还没有怪僻到出面反对的地步。「需要毒药的人，并不爱毒药。」对毒气，士兵们也曾憎恨过，而且继续憎恨着。那是下三滥的伎俩，跟泼镪水是一路。可德国人一旦开了头，谁还离得了它？如今，又有谁能反对这种印刷毒气的动用呢？在这一新的宣传战争里，难道他们能指望自己的国家，披一袭坦白的白袍、此外寸缕不挂地上阵——像一个不戴面具的人，迎着一支有光气（phosgene）和她全部肮脏姐妹们助阵的大军出征？

===Original===
It was a clear enough case: decency had to go under. But it was hard luck not to be able to know where you were. Where were they? If all the news they could check was mixed with lies, what about all the rest, which they were unable to check? Was it likely to be any truer? Why, we might be losing the war all the time, everywhere! Who could believe now what was said about our catching the submarines? Or about India’s being all right? And how far would you have to go to get outside the lie belt? Could our case for going to war with the Germans be partly lies too? Beastly idea!

How would it be, again, when we came to play these major tricks which the men were already discussing as likely to come into use? Suppose it became part of our game to publish, for some good strategical reason, news of a naval or military disaster to ourselves, the same not having happened? To take in the enemy this lie would have to take in our own people too; the ruse would be given away if the Government tried to tip so much as a wink to the British reader of the British Press. So men’s friends at home would have the agonies of false alarms added to their normal wartime miseries, and wives might be widowed twice and mothers of one son made childless more than once before the truth finally overshadowed their lives.

===Chinese===
这是一桩够清楚明白的案子：体面不得不沉没。可是，连自己身在何处都无从知道，实在是倒霉。那么，他们究竟身处何地？倘若他们所能查证的全部新闻都掺了谎，那么其余那些他们无法查证的，又当如何？会更能是真的么？哎呀，说不定我们从头到尾、处处都在吃败仗呢！如今谁还能相信那些关于我们正在捕获潜艇的说法？关于印度太平无事的那些说法？还要走多远，才走得出这条谎带？我们对德国人开战的理由，难道就不会也有一部分是谎话？可憎的念头！

再有，等我们当真玩起这些大戏法来——士兵们当时已经在议论，说它们多半会投入使用——光景又将如何？假设出于某种高明的战略理由，发布一条我方海陆军惨祸的新闻，而这惨祸并不曾发生，成了我们棋局的一部分？要骗过敌人，这条谎就必须连我们自家人一并骗过；政府哪怕只想对英国报界的英国读者递一个眼色，整个计策便会败露。于是，后方士兵们的亲友，便要在他们战时的寻常苦难之上，再添虚惊的煎熬；做妻子的可能在真相最终笼罩她们的生活之前，两度做了寡妇；独子的母亲，可能不止一次地痛失子嗣。

===Original===
And then, your war won, there would be that new lie-infested and infected world of peace. In one of his great passages Thucydides tells us what happened to Greece after some years of war and of the necessary war morality. He says that, as far as veracity, public and private, goes, the peace gear was found to have got wholly out of working order and could not be brought back into use. “The meaning of words had no longer the same relation to things, but was changed by men as they thought proper.” The prewar hobby of being straight and not telling people lies went clean out of fashion. Anyone who could bring off a good stroke of deceit, to the injury of someone whom he disliked, “congratulated himself on having taken the safer course, overreached his enemy, and gained the prize of superior talent.” A man who did not care to use so sound a means to his ends was thought to be a goody-goody ass. War worked in that way on the soul of Greece, in days when war was still confined, in the main, to the relatively cleanly practice of hitting your enemy over the head, wherever you could find him. The philosophers in our dugouts preserved moderation when they expected as ugly a sequel for war in our age, when the chivalrous school seems to have pretty well worked itself out and the most promising lines of advance are poison gas and canards. But the survivors among them are not detached philosophers only. They act in the new world that they foresaw, and the man whose word you could trust like your own eyes and ears, eight years ago, has come back with the thought in his mind that so many comrades of his have expressed: “They tell me we’ve pulled through at last all right because our propergander dished out better lies than what the Germans did. So I say to myself ‘If tellin’ lies is all that bloody good in war, what bloody good is tellin’ truth in peace?’ ”

===Chinese===
然后，仗打赢了，等着你的便是那个新的、谎灾横行、谎毒浸染的和平世界。修昔底德（Thucydides）在他最伟大的段落之一里告诉我们：经过几年战争、几年必不可少的战争道德之后，希腊落到了什么地步。他说，就公私两界的诚实而论，人们发现和平档位已经彻底失灵，怎么也挂不回去使用了。「词句的意义与事物不再保有同样的关系，而是随各人的心意而改变。」战前那种直道而行、不对人说谎的老嗜好，彻底过时了。凡是能对某个自己厌恶的人成功施展一手好骗术的人，都「自庆拣了较为安全的路走，骗过了他的敌人，赢得了才智超群的奖赏」。不肯用这么可靠的手段达到目的的人，被看成一头假正经的蠢驴。战争当年就是这样作用于希腊的灵魂的——那还是在战争大体上仍局限于相对干净做法的年代：只要寻得着你，照他头顶一记敲将下去。我们的掩蔽部（dugout）里的那些哲学家们，当年预料到战争在我们这个时代也会有同样丑陋的下场时，倒还守着节制——在这个时代，骑士那一派似乎已经把自己经营得差不多了，最有前程的进路，是毒气与谣言（canards）。可他们当中的幸存者，并不只是超然的哲学家。他们就在自己预见到的那个新世界里行动；而那个人——八年前，他的话你信得过，如同信得过你自己的眼睛耳朵——回来了，心里带着他那许多袍泽都表达过的那个念头：「人家跟我说，咱们到底还是熬过来了，全仗咱们的宣传（propergander，歪读 propaganda）撒的谎，比德国人撒的棒。于是我自个儿寻思：『要是撒谎在战争里这么他娘的顶用，那说实话在和平里又顶个屁用？』」

## XIII The Old Age of the War / 十三 战争的暮年

### I / 一

===Original===
Men wearying in trenches used to tell one another sometimes what they fancied the end of the war would be like. Each had his particular favourite vision. Some morning the Captain would come down the trench at “stand-to” and try to speak as if it were nothing. “All right, men,” he would say, “you can go across and shake hands.” Or the first thing we should hear would be some jubilant peal suddenly shaken out on the air from the nearest standing church in the rear. But the commonest vision was that of marching down a road to a wide, shining river. Once more the longing of a multitude struggling slowly across a venomous wilderness fixed itself on the first glimpse of a Jordan beyond; for most men the Rhine was the physical goal of effort, the term of endurance, the symbol of all attainment and rest.

To win what your youth had desired, and find the taste of it gone, is said to be one of the standard pains of old age. With a kind of blank space in their minds where the joy of fulfilment ought to have been, two British privates of 1914, now Captains attached to the Staff, emerged from the narrow and crowded High Street of Cologne on December 7, 1918, crossed the Cathedral square, and gained their first sight of the Rhine. As they stood on the Hohenzollern bridge and looked at the mighty breadth of rushing stream, each of them certainly gave his heart leave to leap up if it would and if it could. Had they not, by toil and entreaty, gained permission to enter the city with our first cavalry? Were they not putting their lips to the first glass of the sparkling vintage of victory? Neither of them said anything then. The heart that knoweth its own bitterness need not always avow it straight off. But they were friends; they told afterwards.

===Chinese===
在战壕里熬乏了的人们，有时会彼此讲讲自己想象中战争的结局会是什么模样。各人有各人钟爱的图景。某个早晨，上尉会在拂晓「就位」（stand-to）时分顺着战壕走过来，竭力装出一副没什么大不了的口气说：「好了，弟兄们，你们可以过去握手了。」再不然，我们最先听到的，会是后方最近一座还立着的教堂骤然向空中摇出的一阵欢庆钟声。但最常见的图景，是沿着一条大路行军而下，走向一条宽阔闪亮的河。一大群人正在毒瘴弥漫的荒野里挣扎徐行，他们的渴望又一次钉死在前方那条约旦河（Jordan）的惊鸿一瞥上；对大多数人而言，莱茵河（Rhine）便是辛劳的现世目标，是忍耐的尽头，是一切抵达与安息的象征。

赢得你青春时代所渴求的东西，却发现它的滋味已经散尽——据说这是老年标准苦楚之一。1918 年 12 月 7 日，两个 1914 年入伍的英国列兵——如今已是配属参谋部（Staff）的上尉——心中本该盛着践约之喜的地方空着一格，从科隆（Cologne）狭窄拥挤的大街里走出来，穿过大教堂广场，第一次望见了莱茵河。两人站在霍亨索伦桥（Hohenzollern bridge）上，望着那片奔腾急流的浩荡宽阔，各自心里想必都放了行：这颗心若肯跳、若跳得动，就让它跳起来吧。他们不是靠苦干与恳求，才争得随我们的先头骑兵一同进城的许可的吗？他们此刻不正在把嘴唇凑向胜利那起泡陈酿的第一杯吗？当时两人谁也没有说什么。「心中的苦楚，自己知道」的那颗心，未必总要立刻吐露。但他们是要好的朋友；后来他们说了。

===Original===
The first hours of that ultimate winding-up of the old, long-decayed estate of hopes and illusions were not the worst, either. The cavalry brigadier in command at Cologne, those first few days, was a man with a good fighting record; and now his gesture towards the conquered was that of the happy warrior, that of Virgilian Rome, that of the older England in hours of victory. German civilians clearly expected some kind of maltreatment, such perhaps as their own scum had given to Belgians. They strove with desperate care to be correct in their bearing, neither to jostle us accidentally in the streets nor to shrink away from us pointedly. Soon, to their surprise and shame, they found that among the combatant English there lingered the hobby of acting like those whom the Germans had known through their Shakespeare: “We give express charge that in our marches through the country there be nothing compelled from the villages, nothing taken but paid for, none of the French upbraided or abused in disdainful language.”

The “cease fire” order on Armistice Day had forbidden all “fraternizing.” But any man who has fought with a sword, or its equivalent, knows more about that than the man who has only blown with a trumpet. To men who for years have lived like foxes or badgers, dodging their way from each day of being alive to the next, there comes back more easily, after a war, a sense of the tacit league that must, in mere decency, bind together all who cling precariously to life on a half-barren ball that goes spinning through space. All castaways together, all really marooned on the one desert island, they know that, however hard we may have to fight to sober a bully or guard to each man his share of the shellfish and clams, we all have to come back at last to the joint work of making the island more fit to live on. The gesture of the decimated troops who held Cologne at the end of that year was, in essence, that of the cavalry brigadiers. Sober or drunk, the men were contumaciously sportsmen, incorrigibly English. One night before Christmas I thought I heard voices outside my quarters long after curfew, and went to look out from my balcony high up in the Domhof into the moon-flooded expanse of the Cathedral square below. By rights there should have been no figures there at that hour, German or British. But there were three; two tipsy Highlanders﻿—“Women from Hell,” as German soldiers used to call the demonic stabbers in kilts﻿—gravely dispensing the consolations of chivalry to a stout burgher of Cologne. “Och, dinna tak’ it to hairrt, mon. I tell ye that your lads were grond.” It was like a last leap of the flame that had burnt clear and high four years before.

===Chinese===
那份陈旧而已然朽败多年的希望与幻象产业，其最终清算的最初几个钟头，也还不是最坏的。最初那几天坐镇科隆的骑兵旅长是个战绩不俗的军人；而他对被征服者的姿态，是快乐战士的姿态，是维吉尔（Virgil）笔下罗马的姿态，是那个较古老的英格兰在胜利时刻的姿态。德国平民分明料想会遭到某种虐待——也许就像他们自家的败类加在比利时人身上的那种。他们拼命小心地务求举止合度：既不在街上无意间碰撞我们，也不着意缩身避开我们。不久，他们又惊又臊地发现，作战的英国人中间还留着一门旧习——行事一如德国人通过他们的莎士比亚所认识的那种人：「我们严令：行军穿越乡野时，不得向各村强索分毫；凡有取用，务必照价付钱；不得以轻慢之言辱骂或欺凌任何法国人。」

停战日（Armistice Day）的「停火」令禁止了一切「亲善往来」。但凡是持剑——或持剑之等价物——打过仗的人，对此都比只拿喇叭吹响过号声的人懂得多。对于那些多年来活得像狐狸像獾、从活着的这一天闪躲到活着的下一天的人，战事一了，更容易回到心头的是那份默契同盟之感：单凭起码的体面，它也必得把所有在这颗半荒瘠的圆球上悬悬危活、随它旋过太空的人系在一起。大家全是遭难流落的人，全都真真切切困在同一座荒岛上；他们知道，无论要下多大力气才打得醒一个恶霸、守得住每人那一份贝肉蛤蜊，到头来我们总得回到那桩共同的工作上——把这座岛拾掇得更宜于居住。年底驻守科隆的那支十不存一的部队，其做派骨子里便是骑兵旅长们的做派。清醒也罢，醉醺醺也罢，士兵们倔头倔脑地一身运动家风度，不可救药地一身英国人做派。圣诞前的一夜，宵禁过后许久，我仿佛听见住处外面有人声，便走到多姆霍夫（Domhof）高处我那阳台上，俯瞰楼下月光漫漫的大教堂广场。按理，那个时辰那里不该有任何人影，无论德国人还是英国人。可是有三个人影：两个醉醺醺的高地兵（Highlanders）——德国兵旧日称那些穿褶裙的恶魔刺刀手为「地狱婆娘」（Women from Hell）——正一脸郑重地向科隆一位胖市民颁赐骑士精神的抚慰。「噢，可别往心里去，老兄。俺告诉你，你们家的小伙子们可是好样的。」那像是一场火焰的最后一跃——四年前，这火曾烧得那样清亮、那样高。

### II / 二

===Original===
For the day of the fighting man, him and his chivalric hobbies, was over. The guns had hardly ceased to fire before from the rear, from the bases, from London, there came flooding up the braves who for all those four years had been squealing threats and abuse, some of them begging off service in arms on the plea that squealing was indispensable national work. We had not been long in Cologne when there arrived in hot haste a young pressman from London, one of the first of a swarm. He looked a fine strong man. He seemed to be one of the male Vestals who have it for their trade to feed the eternal flame of hatred between nations, instead of cleaning out stables or doing some other work fit for a male. His train had fortunately brought him just in time for luncheon. This he ate and drank with goodwill, complaining only that the wine, which seemed to me good, was not better. He then slept on his bed until teatime. Reanimated with tea, he said genially, “Well, I must be getting on with my mission of hate,” and retired to his room to write a vivacious account of the wealth and luxury of Cologne, the guzzling in all cafés and restaurants, the fair round bellies of all the working class, the sleek and rosy children of the poor. I read it, two days after, in his paper. Our men who had helped to fight Germany down were going short of food at the time, through feeding the children in houses where they were billeted. “Proper Zoo there is in this place,” one of them told me. “Proper lions and tigers. Me and my friend are taking the kids from our billet soon’s we’ve got them fatted up a bit. If you’ll believe me, sir, them kiddies ain’t safe in a Zoo. They could walk in through the bars and get patting the lions.” I had just seen some of the major carnivora in their cages close to the Rhine, each a rectangular lamina of fur and bone like the tottering cats I had seen pass through incredible slits of space in Amiens a month after the people had fled from the city that spring. But little it mattered in London what he or I saw. The nimble scamps had the ear of the world; what the soldier said was not evidence.

===Chinese===
因为战斗者——他本人连同他那套骑士式的雅好——的日子，已经到头了。炮声甫歇，从后方，从各个基地，从伦敦，便涌上来那班勇士：整整四年，他们一直尖着嗓子叫嚣威胁与辱骂，其中有些人还乞免兵役，理由是叫嚣乃不可或缺的国务。我们到科隆不久，便有一位伦敦来的年轻报人火急火燎地赶到，是那群蜂拥而来者中的头几只。他看上去是个健壮的好汉。他似乎属于那种男版维斯塔贞女（Vestals）——以喂养民族之间永恒的仇恨之火为营生，而不去清扫马厩、或做别的什么适合男人做的活计。所幸他的火车恰好把他及时送到了午饭桌上。他吃得喝得满怀善意，只抱怨那酒——在我看来已属好酒——还不够好。随后他在床上一直睡到午后茶时。茶水下肚还了魂，他和和气气地说：「好吧，我得接着办我的仇恨差事了。」便回自己房里，去写一篇生龙活虎的报道：科隆的富庶与豪奢，家家咖啡馆与餐馆里的大吃大喝，整个工人阶级滚圆漂亮的肚皮，穷人家油光红润的孩子。两天后，我在他的报上读到了它。我们的士兵曾协力把德国打趴下，此刻却正因供养借宿人家的孩子们而在挨饿。「这地方有个正经动物园（Zoo），」其中一个对我说，「正经的狮子老虎。俺跟俺伙计正把借宿那家的娃娃们喂肥一点，就带他们去。先生您要肯信，那些小娃娃在动物园里可不安全。他们能从栏杆缝里走进去，拍起狮子来。」我方才在莱茵河边见过笼中的几种大型食肉兽，每一只都是一块长方形的毛皮与骨头的薄板，像那年春天城中居民出逃一个月后、我在亚眠（Amiens）见过的那些摇摇晃晃的猫——从一处处难以置信的窄缝里挤身而过。可是他看见什么、我看见什么，在伦敦都无足轻重。那班灵巧的小无赖占有着世界的耳朵；士兵说的话，不算证据。

===Original===
Some Allied noncombatants did almost unthinkable things in the first ecstasy of the triumph that others had won. One worthy drove into Cologne in a car plastered over with Union Jacks, like a minor bookie going to Epsom. It passed the wit of man to make him understand that one does not do these things to defeated peoples. But he could understand, with some help, that our Commander-in-Chief alone was entitled to carry a Union Jack on his car. “We must show these fellows our power”; that was the form of the licence taken out by every churl in spirit who wanted to let his coltish nature loose on a waiter or barber in some German hotel. I saw one such gallant assert the majesty of the Allies by refusing to pay more than half the prices put down on the wine-list. Another would send a waiter across an hotel dining-room to order a quiet party of German men and women not to speak so loud. Another was all for inflicting little bullying indignities on the editor of the *Kölnische Zeitung*﻿—making him print as matters of fact our versions of old cases of German misconduct, etc. Probably he did not even know that the intended exhibition-ground for these deplorable tricks was one of the great journals of Europe.

Not everybody, not even every noncombatant in the dress of a soldier, had caught that shabby epidemic of spite. But it was rife. It had become a fashion to have it, as in some raffish circles it is a fashion at times to have some rakish disease. In the German military cemetery at Lille I have heard a man reared at one of our most famous public schools and our most noble university, and then wearing our uniform, say that he thought the French might do well to desecrate all the German soldiers’ graves on French soil. Another, at Brussels, commended a Belgian who was said to have stripped his wife naked in one of the streets of that city and cut off her hair on some airy suspicion of an affair with a German officer during the enemy’s occupation. A fine sturdy sneer at the notion of doing anything chivalrous was by this time the mode. “I hope to God,” an oldish and highly noncombatant general said, in discussing the probable terms of peace with a younger general who had begun the war as a full lieutenant and fought hard all the way up, “that there’s going to be no rot about not kicking a man when he’s down.” The junior general grunted. He did not agree. But he clearly felt shy of protesting. Worshippers of setting suns feel ill at ease in discussion with these bright, confident fellows who swear by the rising one.

===Chinese===
有些协约国的非战斗人员，在他人挣来的那场胜利的最初狂喜里，做出的事几乎不可思议。有一位好汉，开着一辆糊满联合王国旗（Union Jack）的汽车驶进科隆，像个赶埃普索姆（Epsom）赛马会的小赌客。任凭人的心智再巧，也无法叫他明白：对战败的人民不做这种事。但经人从旁点拨，他倒能够明白：唯有我们的总司令（Commander-in-Chief）才有资格在自己车上挂一面联合王国旗。「咱们得让这帮家伙见识见识咱们的威风」——这便是每个心怀村夫气、想在德国某家旅馆里对侍者或理发师撒一把野性的人所领取的执照。我亲眼见过这样一位豪杰，以拒付酒单所标价格的一半以上，来伸张协约国的威仪。另一位则惯于打发侍者穿过旅馆餐厅，去命令一席安安静静的德国男女说话放低些声量。还有一位则极力主张对《科隆日报》（*Kölnische Zeitung*）的主编施加些小小的欺凌折辱——逼他把我们对德国人旧日劣迹诸案的种种说法当作事实刊印出来，云云。他多半压根不知道，这套可叹把戏预定的演出场地，乃是欧洲的大报之一。

并非人人——甚至连每个穿着军装的非战斗人员——都染上了那场寒碜的怨毒瘟疫。但它确在蔓延。染上它已成了一种时髦，正如在某些放荡的圈子里，害上某种花哨的病有时也是一种时髦。在里尔（Lille）的德军军人墓园，我听过一个人——受教于我们最负盛名的公学（public school）之一与我们最尊贵的大学，又穿着我们的军装——说，他觉得法国人不妨把法国国土上的德国军人坟墓统统亵渎个干净。另一位在布鲁塞尔（Brussels）则称许一个比利时人：据说占领期间，他凭一点捕风捉影的疑心——疑心妻子与一名德国军官有私——便在那座城的一条街上把她剥得精光，铰去了她的头发。到这时，对做任何骑士式事情的念头报以一声漂亮结实的冷嗤，已成风气。「我向上帝祈祷，」一位上了年纪、非战斗得彻头彻尾的将军说——他正同一位较年轻的将军讨论和约可能的条款，那位开战时不过是一名中尉、一路苦战打上来——「但愿别来那套『不打落水狗』的废话。」年轻的将军哼了一声。他不同意。但他分明怯于开口抗议。朝拜落日的人们，同这些指着朝阳起誓、光鲜自信的家伙坐而论道时，总觉得浑身不自在。

### III / 三

===Original===
The senior general need not have feared. The generous youth of the war, when England could carry, with no air of burlesque, the flag of St. George, was pretty well gone. The authentic flame might still flicker on in the minds of a few tired soldiers and disregarded civilians. Otherwise it was as dead as the half-million of good fellows whom it had fired four years ago, whose credulous hearts the maggots were now eating under so many shining and streaming square miles of wet Flanders and Picardy. They gone, their war had lived into a kind of dotage ruled by mean fears and desires. At home our places of honour were brown with shirkers masquerading in the dead men’s clothes and licensed by careless authorities to shelter themselves from all danger under the titles of Colonel, Major, and Captain. Nimble politicians were rushing already to coin into votes for themselves﻿—“the men who won the war”﻿—the golden memory of the dead before the living could come home and make themselves heard. Sounds of a general election, the yells of political cheap-jacks, the bawling of some shabby promise, capped by some shabbier bawl, made their way out to Cologne.

===Chinese===
那位年长的将军本不必担心。战争的那段宽厚的青春——那时英格兰高举圣乔治（St. George）旗，不带一丝滑稽戏的气味——已经消磨殆尽了。真火或许还在少数疲惫的士兵与无人理会的平民心里幽幽颤着。除此之外，它已同它四年前点燃的那五十万好汉一样死透了——在湿漉漉的佛兰德（Flanders）与皮卡第（Picardy）那一片片闪着水光的平方英里地面之下，蛆虫正啃噬着他们轻信的心。他们去了，他们的战争便活进了某种老耄之境，受着卑琐的恐惧与欲望的统治。在国内，我们的荣誉席位上爬满了褐压压的逃役者——他们穿着死者的衣裳乔装改扮，又领了漫不经心的当局所发的执照，顶着上校、少校、上尉的头衔，躲避一切危险。手脚麻利的政客们已经抢着动手，要在活人还乡、得以发声之前，把死者的金色记忆铸成自己的选票——「打赢战争的人们」。大选的声浪、政治叫卖贩子的嚎叫、某句寒碜许诺的吼嚷、又被一句更寒碜的吼嚷盖过，一路传到了科隆。

===Original===
“This way, gents, for the right sort of whip to give Germans!” “Rats, gentlemen, rats! Don’t listen to *him*. Leave it to me and I’ll chastise ’em with scorpions.” “I’ll devise the brave punishments for them.” “Ah, but I’ll sweat you more money out of the swine.” That was the gist of the din that most of the gramophones of the home press gave out on the Rhine. Each little demagogue had got his little pots of pitch and sulphur on sale for the proper giving of hell to the enemy whom he had not faced. Germany lay at our feet, a world’s wonder of downfall, a very Lucifer, fallen, broken, bereaved beyond all the retributive griefs which Greek tragedy shows you afflicting the great who were insolent, wilful, and proud. But it was not enough for our small epicures of revenge. They wanted to twist the enemy’s wrists, where he lay bound, and to run pins into his eyes. And they had the upper hand of us now. The soldiers could only look on while the scurvy performance dragged itself out till the meanest of treaties was signed at Versailles. “Fatal Versailles!” as General Sir Ian Hamilton said for us all; “Not a line﻿—not one line in your treaty to show that those boys (our friends who were dead) had been any better than the emperors; not one line to stand for the kindliness of England; not one word to bring back some memory of the generosity of her sons!”

“The freedom of Europe,” “The war to end war,” “The overthrow of militarism,” “The cause of civilization”﻿—most people believe so little now in anything or anyone that they would find it hard to understand the simplicity and intensity of faith with which these phrases were once taken among our troops, or the certitude felt by hundreds of thousands of men who are now dead that if they were killed their monument would be a new Europe not soured or soiled with the hates and greeds of the old. That the old spirit of Prussia might not infest our world any more; that they or, if not they, their sons might breathe a new, cleaner air they had willingly hung themselves up to rot on the uncut wire at Loos or wriggled to death, slow hour by hour, in the cold filth at Broodseinde. Now all was done that man could do, and all was done in vain. The old spirit of Prussia was blowing anew, from strange mouths. From several species of men who passed for English﻿—as mongrels, curs, shoughs, water-rugs, and demi-wolves are all clept by the name of dogs﻿—there was rising a chorus of shrill yelps for the outdoing of all the base folly committed by Prussia when drunk with her old conquest of France. Prussia, beaten out of the field, had won in the souls of her conquerors’ rulers; they had become her pupils; they took her word for it that she, and not the older England, knew how to use Victory.

===Chinese===
「诸位这边来，瞧瞧抽德国人该用哪种好鞭子！」「耗子药，先生们，耗子药！别听*他*的。交给我，我要用蝎子鞭抽他们。」「我要给他们想出些勇狠的刑罚来。」「啊，可我能替你们从这帮猪猡身上多榨出些钱来。」这便是国内报界（the home press）多数留声机在莱茵河畔放送出的那片喧嚣的要点。每个小煽动家都摆出了自己的小罐沥青与硫磺待售，专供好好地给他从未照过面的敌人下地狱之用。德国躺在我们脚下，一场举世称奇的倾覆，一个十足的路西法（Lucifer）——坠落了，破碎了，丧亲失佑之惨，远过希腊悲剧展演给你看的、加诸那些傲慢、任性而骄矜之伟人的种种报应诸恸。但这还不够我们那些小小的复仇美食家们受用。他们还要扭敌人反缚着的手腕，往他的眼睛里扎别针。而此刻，他们骑在了我们头上。士兵们只能旁观，眼看着这场下作的表演一路拖下去，直到那份最卑吝的和约在凡尔赛（Versailles）签了字。「致命的凡尔赛！」正如伊恩·汉密尔顿爵士（General Sir Ian Hamilton）替我们大家所说的：「你的和约里没有一行——没有一行表明那些孩子（我们那些死去的朋友）比那些皇帝好在哪里；没有一行代表英格兰的仁厚；没有一个字，能唤回对她儿子们慷慨胸襟的些微记忆！」

「欧洲的解放」「结束战争的战争」「推翻军国主义」「文明的事业」——如今大多数人对任何事物、任何人都信得这样少，以致他们很难理解，这些字眼当年在我们部队里被人接信时，怀着的是怎样一种单纯而炽烈的信仰；也很难理解如今已经死去的成千上万人当时怀有的那份笃定：他们若战死，他们的纪念碑将是一个新的欧洲——一个不曾被旧日的仇恨与贪欲酸蚀玷污的欧洲。为了让普鲁士（Prussia）的旧精神从此不再侵扰我们的世界；为了让他们自己——若不是他们，便是他们的儿子——呼吸上一种新的、更洁净的空气，他们甘愿把自己挂留在卢斯（Loos）未曾剪断的铁丝网上腐烂，或在布罗德塞恩德（Broodseinde）冰冷的污秽里，一个钟头又一个钟头地慢慢蜷死。如今，人所能做的一切都做了，而一切都白做了。普鲁士的旧精神正重新吹拂起来，出自一张张陌生的嘴。从好几种被当作英国人的男人——正如杂种狗、贱狗、粗毛狗、水狗、半狼统统都被冠以狗之名——那里，正升起一片尖声狂吠的大合唱，叫嚷着要超越普鲁士当年陶醉于征服法国的旧业之时犯下的全部卑劣愚行。普鲁士被逐出了战场，却在她征服者们的统治者的灵魂里赢了；他们做了她的学生；他们听信她的话——懂得怎样使用胜利的是她，而不是那个较古老的英格兰。

### IV / 四

===Original===
Sir Douglas Haig came to Cologne when we had been there a few days. On the grandiose bridge over the Rhine he made a short speech to a few of us. Most of it sounded as if the thing were a job he had got to get through with, and did not much care for. Perhaps the speech, like those of other great men who wisely hate making speeches, had been written for him by somebody else. But once he looked up from the paper and put in some words which I felt sure were his own; “I only hope that, now we have won, we shall not lose our heads as the Germans did after 1870. It has brought them to this.” He looked at the gigantic mounted statue of the Kaiser overhead, a thing crying out in its pride for fire from heaven to fall and consume it, and at the homely, squat British sentry moving below on his post. I think the speech was reported. But none of our foremen at home took any notice of it at all. They knew a trick worth two of Haig’s. They were as moonstruck as any victorious Prussian.

So we had failed﻿—had won the fight and lost the prize; the garland of the war was withered before it was gained. The lost years, the broken youth, the dead friends, the women’s overshadowed lives at home, the agony, and bloody sweat﻿—all had gone to darken the stains which most of us had thought to scour out of the world that our children would live in. Many men felt, and said to each other, that they had been fooled. They had believed that their country was backing them. They had thought, as they marched into Germany, “Now we shall show old Fritz how you treat a man when you’ve thrashed him.” They would let him into the English secret, the tip that the power and glory are not to the bully. As some of them looked at the melancholy performance which followed, our Press and our politicians parading at Paris in moral *pickelhauben* and doing the Prussianist goose-step by way of *pas de triomphe*, they could not but say in dismay to themselves: “This is our doing. We cannot wish the war unwon; and yet﻿—if we had shirked, poor old England, for all we know, might not have come to this pass. So we come home draggle-tailed, sick of the mess that we were unwittingly helping to make when we tried to do well.”

===Chinese===
道格拉斯·黑格爵士（Sir Douglas Haig）在我们到科隆几天之后来了。在莱茵河上那座宏丽的大桥上，他对我们少数人作了简短的讲话。大部分听上去仿佛这是件他不得不打发完差的活计，而且他并不怎么喜欢它。也许这篇讲话同其他明智地厌恶演讲的大人物的一样，是别人替他写好的。但有一回他从讲稿上抬起眼来，插了几句我敢肯定属于他自己的话：「我只希望，如今我们打赢了，可别像德国人 1870 年之后那样昏了头。那把他们弄到了这步田地。」他望了望头顶上德皇（Kaiser）那尊巨硕的骑马雕像——一件因其骄矜而呼喊着求天火降下焚毁它的东西——又望了望下方哨位上走动的那相貌家常、矮墩墩的英国哨兵。我想这篇讲话是见报了的。但我们国内那班工头们对它全然不予理会。他们知道一记比黑格那手高明一倍的把戏。他们同任何得胜的普鲁士人一样中了月魔。

就这样，我们失败了——打赢了这一仗，输掉了奖品；战争的花冠未及摘到手便已枯萎。虚掷的年月，毁掉的青春，死去的朋友，国内妇女被阴云笼罩的一生，那苦痛，那血汗——全都白费了，只是把我们大多数人原想从子女将要生活于其中的这个世界里擦洗掉的污渍，浸染得更加黑沉。许多人心下觉得受了骗，也彼此这样说了。他们曾相信他们的国家在背后撑着他们。进军德国的路上，他们曾想：「现在咱们要让老弗里茨（old Fritz）瞧瞧，把人打服了之后，该怎样对待他。」他们要把英国人的秘密传授给他——那条诀窍：权柄与荣耀并不归于恶霸。当他们中的一些人望着随后那场阴郁的表演——我国的报界（our Press）与我们的政客们在巴黎招摇过市，头戴道德尖顶盔（*pickelhauben*），踏着普鲁士派的鹅步来充当凯旋舞步（*pas de triomphe*）——他们不能不沮丧地自语：「这是我们干下的事。我们不能指望这场战争没有打赢；然而——倘若当初我们逃了役，可怜的老英格兰，天晓得，兴许不至于落到这步田地。于是我们拖着泥污的尾巴回了家，厌透了这摊烂污——我们本想行好，却在不知不觉间帮着搅成的烂污。」

## V The Sheep That Were Not Fed / 五 没人喂的羊

### I / 一

===Original===
“Of late years,” the novel of *Shirley* begins, “an abundant shower of curates has fallen upon the North of England; they lie very thick on the hills; every parish has one or more of them; they are young enough to be very active, and ought to be doing a great deal of good.” This blessing, conferred on the West Riding a little before Waterloo, descended on our Western Front a little after the first battle of the Marne.

===Chinese===
「近几年来，」小说《雪莉》（*Shirley*）开篇写道，「一场副牧师的丰沛骤雨落在英格兰北方；他们在山间铺得厚厚的；每个教区都有一位或好几位；他们都年轻得足以大有活动，理应做出大量的好事。」这份福分，在滑铁卢（Waterloo）之战前不久赐给了西雷丁（West Riding），又在第一次马恩河会战之后不久，降到了我们的西线。

===Original===
It was received by our troops with the greater thanksgiving because it brought with it no perceptible revival of church parades, a ministration of which the average private, *l’homme moyen sensuel* of Matthew Arnold, had taken a long and glad farewell on leaving Salisbury Plain. Like the infinite cleaning of brass-work, the hearing of many well-meaning divines in the Tidworth garrison church had been one of the tribulations through which the defender of Britain must work out his passage to France. With the final order to tarnish his buttons with fire and oil there came also a longed-for release from regular Sunday adjurations to keep sober and think of his end. “The Lorrd,” said a grim Scots corporal, a hanging judge of a sermon, after hearing the last essay of our English Bossuets before he went to the wars, “hath turrned the capteevity of Zion.” No more attendance for him at such “shauchlin’ ” athletic displays as the wrestlings of the southron divinity passman with the lithe and sinuous mind of St. Paul. “Sunday,” the blithe Highlander in *Waverley* said, “seldom cam aboon the pass of Bally Brough.” For better or worse, as a reliever from work or a restrainer of play, Sunday seldom came across the Channel during the war. A man in the ranks might be six months in France and not find a religious service of any kind coming his way, whether he dreaded or sought it.

===Chinese===
我军将士怀着加倍的感恩领受了它，因为它并未带来教堂检阅（church parade）的任何可观复兴——对这一仪节，普通列兵，也就是马修·阿诺德（Matthew Arnold）所说的「寻常感性中人」（*l’homme moyen sensuel*），早在离开索尔兹伯里平原（Salisbury Plain）时便已作过长别，别得欣然而久长。在蒂德沃思（Tidworth）驻军教堂里聆听众多好心教士的讲道，同无穷无尽的擦拭铜器一样，素来是英国的保卫者必须熬过、方能挣得开赴法国之路的诸般苦难之一。随着那道用火与油把纽扣熏暗的最后命令而来的，还有他渴慕已久的解脱——从此摆脱了每礼拜日例行的劝诫，不再有人规劝他保持清醒、惦念自己的结局。「耶和华已经翻转了锡安的被掳。」一位面目冷峻的苏格兰下士说——他评判起布道来铁面如绞刑官——奔赴战场之前，听罢我们英格兰的博叙埃（Bossuet）们最后的一番试讲，他如是说。他再不用去出席那类「拖着脚步」（shauchlin'）的竞技表演了——比如英格兰的神学及格生同圣保罗那轻盈柔韧的心智角力的场面。「礼拜天难得翻过巴利布罗（Bally Brough）山口。」《威弗莱》（*Waverley*）里那位快活的高地人说。无论好歹，无论作为劳作的宽解者还是玩乐的约束者，战争期间的礼拜天都难得渡过海峡而来。行伍中人尽可以在法国待上六个月，也不见一场任何种类的宗教仪式找上门来，无论他怕它还是求它。

===Original===
Yet chaplains abounded. Not measures, but men, to invert the old phrase. And men of all kinds, as might safely be guessed. There was the hero and saint, T. B. Hardy, to whom a consuming passion of human brotherhood brought, as well as rarer things, the M.C., the D.S.O., the V.C., the unaccepted invitation of the King, when he saw Hardy in France, to come home as one of his own chaplains and live, and then the death which everyone had seen to be certain. There was a chaplain drunk at dinner in Gobert’s restaurant at Amiens on the evening of one of the bloodiest days of the first battle of the Somme. There was the circumspect, ecclesiastical statesman, out to see that in this grand shaking-up and rearranging of prewar positions and values the right cause﻿—whichever of the right causes was his﻿—was not jilted or any way wronged. There was the man who, urged by national comradeship, would have been a soldier but that his bishop barred it; to be an army chaplain was the next best thing. There was the man who, urged by a different instinct, felt irresistibly, as many laymen did, that at the moment the war was the central thing in the whole world, and that it was unbearable not to be at the centre of things. And there was, in great force, the large, healthy, pleasant young curate not severely importuned by a vocation, the ex-athlete, the prop and stay of village cricket-clubs, the good fellow whom the desires of parents, the gaiety of his youth at the university, and the whole drift of things about him had shepherded unresistingly into the open door of the Church. Sudden, unhoped-for, the war had brought him the chance of escape back to an almost solely physical life, like his own happy youth of rude health, only better: a life all salt and tingling with vicissitudes of simple bodily discomfort and pleasure, fatigue and rest, risk and the ceasing of risk; a heaven after the flatness, the tedium, the cloying security and the confounded moral problems attending the uninspired practice of professional brightness and breeziness in an uncritical parish. He abounded so much that whenever now one hears the words “army chaplain” his large, genial image springs up of itself in the mind.

===Chinese===
然而随军牧师（army chaplain）却多得很。把那句老话倒过来说——不是法度，而是人。而且，如你所料万无一失，是形形色色的人。有那英雄兼圣徒T. B. 哈迪（T. B. Hardy）——一股焚心的同胞手足之情，在更稀罕的东西之外，还为他带来了军功十字勋章（M.C.）、杰出服役勋章（D.S.O.）、维多利亚十字勋章（V.C.），带来了国王在法国见到他时发出却被婉谢的邀请——请他回国做国王自家的牧师之一、安享性命——随后，又带来了人人早看清为必不可免的那一死。有那样一位随军牧师：第一次索姆河会战最血腥的某日傍晚，他在亚眠（Amiens）戈贝尔餐馆（Gobert’s restaurant）的晚餐席上喝得烂醉。有那谨慎周章的教会政治家，专程出马看管：在这场对战前地位与价值的大震荡、大重排之中，正义的事业——无论诸正义事业中哪一桩归在他名下——不遭抛弃，也不受任何方式的亏待。有那人，出于举国的袍泽之义（comradeship）本要投军，无奈他的主教不许；当一名随军牧师便是退而求其次。有那人，受另一种本能驱策，像许多平信徒一样不可抗拒地觉得：此时此刻，战争是整个世界的中心之事，不居其中心便难以忍受。还有浩浩荡荡的一大类：高大、健康、和善的年轻副牧师，并不曾受过神召的严催紧逼；退役的运动健将，村板球俱乐部的顶梁柱；这样的好人，被父母的期望、大学时代青春的快活、以及身边万事的整个流向，半点不曾挣扎地牧进了教会敞开的大门。战争突然而至、不期而至，给他带来了逃脱的机会，逃回一种几乎全然属于身体的生活，像他自己那粗健而快乐的青春，只是更好：一种满是盐味与刺快的生活，简单的肉体不适与快乐、疲劳与休息、冒险与冒险的止息，在其中交替嬗变；相较于那种平板、那种沉闷（tedium）、那种腻死人的安稳、以及在一个从不苛究的教区里毫无灵感地例行职业化开朗爽快所附随的种种可恼的道德难题——这简直是天堂。这种人多到这般地步：如今人们一听见「随军牧师」这个词，他高大和气的形象便自行在脑海里蹦出来。

### II / 二

===Original===
In the eyes of the men he had notable merits. He was a running fountain, more often than not, of good cigarettes. Of the exceeding smallness of Low Country beer he could talk, man to man, with knowledge and right feeling. He gladly frequented the least healthy parts of the line, and would frankly mourn the pedantry which denied him a service revolver and did not even allow him the grievous ball-headed club with which a medieval bishop felt himself free to take his own part in a war, because with this lethal tool he did not exactly shed blood, though he dealt liberally enough in contused wounds that would serve equally well. Having a caste of his own, not precisely the combatant officer’s, he had a tongue less rigidly tied in the men’s hearing, so he could soothe the couch of a wounded sergeant by telling him, with a diverting gusto, how downily the old colonel, the one last ungummed, had timed his enteric inoculation at home so as to rescue himself from the fiery ordeal of a divisional field-day. These were solid merits. And yet there was something about this type of chaplain﻿—he had his counterpart in all the churches﻿—with which the common men-at-arms would privily and temperately find a little fault. He seemed to be only too much afraid of having it thought that he was anything more than one of themselves. He had, with a vengeance, “no clerical nonsense about him.” The vigour with which he threw off the parson and put on the man and the brother did not always strike the original men and brothers as it was intended. Your virilist chaplain was apt to overdo, to their mind, his jolly implied disclaimers of any compromising connection with kingdoms not of this world. For one thing, he was, for the taste of people versed in carnage, a shade too fussily bloodthirsty. Nobody made such a point of aping your little trench affectations of callousness; nobody else was so anxious to keep you assured that the blood of the enemy smelt as good to his nose as it could to any of yours. In the whole blood-and-iron province of talk he would not only outshine any actual combatant﻿—that is quite easy to do﻿—but he would outshine any colonel who lived at a base.
